Preface Preface T his is a book for active value investors willing to make some effort to get an edge. To be sure, the value investing in the book is not the simple “value” versus “growth” investing that is common, but rather a comprehensive analysis for gauging value in a firm and how much to pay for it. Indeed, the analysis exposes traps in the simple scheme as well as in other investing styles. This is also a book with a particular focus: Valuation is a matter of accounting; the investor accounts for value. This does not mean the investor just learns how to handle accounting numbers, nor does it mean that he or she can run through the mechanics of financial statement analysis, though both are involved. Rather, it is a matter of thinking about value in an accounting framework involving balance sheets and income statements under the governance of double entry. That protects from making mistakes but also gives the prudent investor confidence in making risky investments. So, the investor keeps a book, an accounting book, maybe formally or maybe mentally. The book ahead of you explains how. The design of the accounting book embeds good thinking about how firms generate value for investors and good thinking about risk and return. That good thinking tells the investor how to challenge the prevailing price the market is asking him or her to pay, for that price might not embed the good thinking. A theme in the book warns investors about paying for growth, for growth is risky. The appropriate accounting for value not only identifies growth to be paid for but also protects against paying too much for growth. The speculative part of the market price is in the pricing of growth, so the investor has the thinking and the tools to challenge this speculation. You will find the book contrasts with many investment books. The ubiquitous beta is not of highest priority by far. The common discounted cash flow (DCF) is put aside. Indeed, the book is skeptical about valuation models in general. Perhaps surprisingly, the book takes the position that it is best to think that “intrinsic value” does not exist. For a value investor that sounds like heresy, but intrinsic value is just too hard to pin down. That requires an alternative approach to be put on the table, one that challenges the market price with confidence. Some investors see the alternative as trading on multiples, smart-beta investing, factor investing, and more. The book brings a critique to these schemes. So, for example, it explains what is involved in a P/E ratio or a P/B (fundamentally) and how to trade on them (fundamentally). These statements are just one-liners, so dig in to grasp the detail. The book builds on themes in Penman’s Accounting for Value (Columbia University Press) and, under those themes, extends the financial statement analysis in his Financial Statement Analysis and Security Valuation . Its debt to the value investing classics, Graham and Dodd, Security Analysis (1934) and Benjamin Graham’s The Intelligent Investor (1949) will be clear to the reader. But the reader will also see how the book takes the investing principles in those books and combines them with the principles of modern finance and, indeed, accounting principles. Keeping an accounting book on value is a matter of honoring all those principles. The book is a text for the student of fundamental analysis and valuation. However, we have tried to avoid using the dry style of the standard text, preferring the style of the active investor. We believe that knowledge is best gained and retained when the student sees it actively applied in a practical way. That, of course, also makes it a book for the professional investor. Afterall, we are all students, always learning! We invite you to the book and trust you will enjoy it. That joy will come from “seeing the light turn on” as you proceed. The book will make you an intelligent investor and, we hope, a successful one. You might be a student coming to the topic for the first time or a seasoned investor. Whatever the case may be, the book will lead you in becoming a continuing student of value investing. To this end, the Your Library feature on the book’s website leads you to further reading. There is also a Case Book and Exercise Book to which you are directed at the end of each chapter. For instructors, there are other materials, including PowerPoint presentations for each chapter and solutions for exercises and cases. And, for both investment professionals and students, there is a website, a blog, where we ask you to join the conversation, sharing questions and experiences with fellow readers and with us, your authors. Go to the website at penmanpope4value.com . We are grateful to several people who provided helpful comments on earlier drafts of the book: Stephen Cooper at The Footnote Analyst, Robert Gutsche and Alexandru-Septimiu Rif at the University of St. Gallen, Tom King at Case Western Reserve University, Kenneth Lee at London School of Economics and Political Science, Hyung Il Oh at KAIST, and Jack Wu at Modtris Financial Modeling and Simulation. And thanks for the fine assistance from Xuanheng Huang and MJ Song, PhD candidates at Bocconi University and Columbia University respectively. Special thanks to Myles Thompson, business editor at Columbia University Press for many years and a helpful advisor to us. Stephen Penman, New York Peter F. Pope, Milano


===== xhtml/ch01.xhtml =====
Chapter 1 Opening the Book Chapter 1 Opening the Book Price is what you pay, value is what you get S tarbucks Corporation, the operator and licensor of over 33,000 coffee shops around the world, has been a favorite of value investors. The firm is said to have “franchise value” with its brand and supply chains creating a “moat around it,” a defense against its many competitors. However, by April 2022, its share price had dropped 22.2 percent from the beginning of that year. Has the moat run dry? The firm’s imaginative founder, Howard Schultz, stepped back into the CEO position, only to see its shares decline a further 6.8 percent over the subsequent few days. Would you buy? Sell? Netflix, Inc., a leader in video streaming, traded at a P/E (price-to-earnings) ratio of 35.5 at the beginning of 2022, but was challenged by competitors. Is a P/E of 35.5 too expensive? The stock price dropped 67 percent over the following six months. Time to buy? Rivian Automotive, Inc., the upstart electric vehicle manufacturer, traded at a market capitalization of $155 billion, three times that of General Motors despite having sold only 8,000 electric vehicles. Could that price be correct? The market value of Meta Platforms (Facebook) dropped from $1 trillion to $311 billion in 2022. Was that a correction of an overpriced stock or a buying opportunity? P/E ratios generally fell below their 25-year averages in 2022. Time to buy stocks? These are the straightforward questions the value investor asks and with which this book engages. Price is what you pay, value is what you get, so how does the investor challenge the market price? One might admire Starbucks and Netflix as good firms, but good firms can be bad buys. They can also be cheap. This must be sorted out, for the primary risk in investing is not the ubiquitous beta from finance theory but rather the risk of paying too much. To challenge the market price, the value investor seeks to gain an edge over other investors. She is helped if those investors are influenced by factors unrelated to fundamentals, the so-called noise traders who form a speculative herd, overconfident or too pessimistic. They are spurred along by the buzz of social media and the talking heads on our TV screens, producing a cacophony of labels to attract the attention of investors: Alpha, beta, smart beta, value, growth, ESG, factor exposure, economic value added, momentum, meme stock, Nifty Fifty, to name just a few. They use enticing language like “value in intangible assets,” “internet real estate,” “customer share of mind,” “customer reach,” “new economy,” “regime change,” “technological frontier.” The China Securities Regulatory Commission calls for a new valuation system “with Chinese characteristics.” The U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) calls for a focus on environmental, sustainability, and governance features (ESG). The investor must cut through these distractions. To do so, she must be disciplined. Fundamental analysis supplies that discipline. Information is gold. Fundamental analysis is a matter of exploiting information to arrive at value. That information may be foot traffic in stores, bits scraped from web searchers, or so-called alternative data, which can include satellite imagery, economic data, industry analysis, climate emissions, and government regulation. Artificial intelligence (AI) and other techniques from data science may help in its discovery. However, the big question is how to pull this large array of information together, sifting out the irrelevant, to determine a value that can then be compared to price. Phew, that is quite a task! In answer to this big question, this book explains that value investing is a matter of accounting: The investor accounts for value. To do so, the investor keeps a book, an accounting book. The accounting book pulls the relevant information about a business together and translates that information into dollar numbers that can challenge a dollar price. In doing so, it compresses the many bits of information down to relatively few measures, leading to an efficient, parsimonious valuation. However, it does more than that. The accounting book also has a structure that tells us how to think about value, the disciplined thinking to get an advantage over less-disciplined investors. By thinking according to the book, the investor becomes a value investor. Value investing by the book. Being an accountant may not instantly appeal. Me, a bean counter? you might say. But it will become more appealing as you see accounting as a way of thinking about value. When you ponder it, accounting is a very natural thing. When you meet a person for the first time, you instinctively ask: Is this person an asset or a liability? Will my relationship with him or her add value? Indeed, as you come to this book, you ask: Is it an asset? Will it add value? This is called mental accounting. Perhaps there is a mental accounting for love? Here is what ChatGPT has to say (in March 2023): Love and accounting, an odd pair. But let me tell you, they can both ensnare. For love is like a balance sheet, A give and take, a constant feat. It starts with assets, all bright and new. A love so strong, it’s hard to construe. And then come liabilities, those pesky things. Arguments, misunderstandings, and other stings. But just like in accounting, there’s a remedy, A way to fix what’s broken and make it pretty. You take the debits and add them up. Then subtract the credits, and you’ll be unstuck. And so it is with love, you see. You work through the issues, and then you’re free. With patience, understanding, and lots of care. You can build a love that’s beyond compare. So don’t discount the power of love and accounting. For both can bring joy and keep you from drowning. Just keep the balance and keep it true. And love and accounting will see you through. Well, it seems that this chap may not have the full intelligence about accounting or love. One feels that ChatGPT has not had much of a love life and is not much of an accountant—though it can get the two to rhyme! Relying on a machine to account for value is tenuous. You, a human, can do better than this. AI will get smarter and maybe you can train the machine to be a partner—if not in love, then in accounting for value. A good partner challenges your overconfidence. A good partner can point you to information you do not see. But there is a premium on independent good thinking to challenge a machine’s thinking. This book attempts to get you there. With the complexities of modern business, mental accounting is just too hard. A more explicit, formal book is required. The book keeps track of assets and liabilities, recognizes relationships with customers, employees, and suppliers, and, yes, measures value added from those relationships. And it will do so all in a unit of account, whether in U.S. dollars or other currency, that is the same unit as stock prices. We will often defer to the wisdom of Warren Buffett, the Sage of Omaha, so good is he in expressing investment ideas in everyday language: “Accounting is the language of business,” Buffett said (a year ago) in response to a question from actress Glenn Close. “You have to be as comfortable with that as you are with your own native language to really evaluate businesses. Accounting tells you a lot and it can be used in many ways to deceive. You saw it at Enron for example,” he continued. “But you really have to understand what can be done with accounting when it gives you correct answers and when it gives you wrong answers.” Yahoo Finance, Feb. 29, 2016. So, yes, we will distinguish “good accounting” for value from “bad accounting” that deceives. Good accounting follows principles that foreground good thinking about value, and these are the principles on which the accounting book will be based. The two dominant standards that govern the establishment of financial statements reported by public companies—U.S. Generally Accepted Accounting Principles (GAAP) and International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS)—also feature many (though not all) of these good principles, as we will see. John Maynard Keynes understood the importance of accounting: “Once we allow ourselves to be disobedient to the test of an accountant’s profit, we have begun to change our civilization.” A bit strongly worded, maybe, but as you continue reading you will see how important accounting is to your investing. Here are some other views: What advantages does he derive from the system of book-keeping by double entry! It is among the finest inventions of the human mind. Goethe, Wilhelm Meister’s Apprenticeship , Book One (1795) In my first semester at UCLA I took Accounting and Economics . . . I found bookkeeping tedious and light on intellectual content. William Sharpe, 1990 Nobel Laureate By the end of this book, you decide which of these two quotes you like. Good Thinking, Good Practice Good thinking leads to good practice, and accounting for value embeds that good thinking. Value investing is often couched in terms of formulas and techniques, but investing is, first and foremost, about good thinking. In the book The Intelligent Investor by Benjamin Graham, the father of value investing, the reader finds few numbers and formulas to contend with; it’s all about thinking about investing. Graham’s earlier book Security Analysis (coauthored with David Dodd) does offer some quantitative analysis, but its focus is still on the thinking behind the analysis that comes off the page. So, too, for the sayings of Graham’s student, the Sage of Omaha. Following this same approach, our book will lead you into the habits of good thinking that in turn lead to good, disciplined investing practice. You will thus be assured that, when you invest, you are on solid foundations. Investing with confidence. Good thinking requires us to recognize bad thinking, of which there is much to be found in investing thinking. Bad thinking is propagated not only by some of the talking heads on our screens but also from some professional advisors who hold themselves out as experts. Keynes again: “The difficulty lies not so much in developing new ideas as escaping from old ones.” So this book necessarily engages with bad thinking to clear the decks for laying down the sound thinking. Some things may surprise you. It is said that value investors buy future cash flows and value is based on those (discounted) cash flows. That is true, but it very often does not work well; it is not good thinking for practical valuation. It is also said that investors buy future earnings: the Rothschild dictum. That is partly true, but higher earnings do not necessarily add value. Good thinking leads to an understanding of the problems with valuation formulas. Indeed, valuation formulas are dangerous unless handled carefully. With her good thinking, the value investor sees the standard Discounted Cash Flow (DCF) model to be a poor tool for challenging the market price (as we will see). She also recognizes that the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) is not very important and might even be a distraction from the task at hand (as we also will see). Bad thinking, sometimes couched in appealing language, is often the source of investment failure. It can even enter the realm of fantasy thinking. Our book is peppered with episodes that distinguish good thinking from bad thinking or untested thinking. These are called vignettes. The box here is the first. A Vignette Bad Thinking, Fantasy Thinking Bad thinking leads to faulty practice. Fantasy thinking is a particular danger in this context, and it can happen by degrees. This form of thinking creates concepts and even ideologies as a cover, often expressed in appealing language. We humans are susceptible. We see fanciful thinking in politicians who do not understand the fundamental consequences of continued deficit spending, justifying it with the likes of such concepts as Modern Monetary Theory—yes, an acronym (MMT) is required to give it force—along with associated contorted thinking of why deficits, an accounting number, do not matter. Data that the fundamental analyst sees as gold are ignored; in this case, the history of the decline of nations because of too much debt. But we also increasingly see it in investing. We saw it in the bubble of the late 1990s, with appeals to a “new age,” “Internet real estate,” conjectured intangible assets such as “organizational capital,” and other fantasies. Cryptocurrencies are the recent manifestation. The fundamental value ascribed to Bitcoin and the like is dubious, so prices are mostly based on speculation about what someone might pay for them in the future. Concepts that serve to rationalize these fantasies are embraced. In the case of crypto, for instance, we are told it is many things: a store of value providing protection from governments debasing fiat currency; a medium of exchange for avoiding credit fees and wire money fees in the banking system; a form of money that is borderless and frictionless. These are appealing ideas, and innovation is to be encouraged. But the ideas must also be examined and, as with fundamental analysis, data must be brought to test them. With Bitcoin prices falling from $68,991 in November 2021 to $18,000 in December 2022, and the subsequent demise of crypto firms and their lenders, the ideas are challenged: a store of value against the U.S. dollar? History is ignored with assumptions that upward momentum in prices will continue indefinitely. Ideology is embraced with utopian visions of technology and even a nod to virtuous capitalism. Web 3.0 to tackle the internet giants? A way to make quick money for philanthropy as the founders of the failed FTX crypto exchange maintained? Or rather a well-worn fraudster’s trick? Antiestablishment sentiments, sometimes justified against crony capitalism, attracts. Meme coins were perhaps intended as a joke, but the joke is on the investor. Fantasy thinking for the gullible. ESG investing is appealing to anyone concerned about the planet and a well-functioning society. But it is wrapped in a fog of ideas that need to be sorted out and which good fundamental information is required to challenge. Could it be a form of investor fraud cloaked in good intentions, an excuse for managers to pursue their own interests rather than that of investors? Could it actually add value for shareholders? Casinos provide a venue for those wishing to gamble, and one is of course free to gamble with speculative ideas. In a casino, one can understand the odds, but wrapping gambling in fantasy thinking obscures the odds. All investing involves risk, but fundamental analysis brings good thinking to understand the odds in betting against the house in the casino that is the stock market. This book requires you to think, to exercise your cognitive skills. If you just want a set of bullet points to tell you what to do, an investing cookbook of sorts, this book is not for you. That is for robots and robotic investing. You, on the other hand, have the necessary human attributes that are important to value investing. Although some processes can be mechanized, AI is no substitute for Graham’s intelligent investor. Quantamentals, though potentially helpful, are no substitute for fundamental thinking. If you do engage in this thinking, you will have the gratification of investing with confidence. Nobel laureate Daniel Kahneman, in his book Thinking, Fast and Slow , distinguishes “fast thinking” marked by intuitive and emotional responses from “slow thinking” that is more deliberate, more analytical. Fast thinkers make mistakes because of biased judgement. Slow thinkers avoid snap judgements, instead putting mental effort into the complexity of investing. So, go slow. Put mental effort into this book. You will become a value investor, and a valuable one at that. Good thinking is expressed through a set of investing principles. These will be laid out as we proceed, and the book that accounts for value will mirror them. Sometimes these principles come out of elegant theory, but the principles that ground the value investor are just common sense, a good check on formal theory that can be “highfalutin” but not practical. The following ten commonsense principles have long been honored by value investors. At this point, they are just “one-liners,” but you’ll see them in full play later. Commonsense Principles P.1. One Does Not Buy a Stock, One Buys a Business “Charlie (Munger) and I are not stock pickers; we are business pickers,” says Warren Buffett in his 2022 Berkshire Hathaway Inc. annual letter to shareholders. That echoes an earlier statement from the so-called father of value investing, Buffett’s mentor: Investment is most intelligent when it is most businesslike. Benjamin Graham, The Intelligent Investor , 1973 ed., p. 289. When buying a stock, think that you are buying a business, not paper. P.2. When Buying a Business, Know the Business When buying a business, think about how the business should be run, as if you were the manager. You are the potential owner, albeit usually a small one, so see yourself as thinking like one. Understand the business model, its execution, and how the management—your managers—are performing. Should Apple Inc. be valued as an IT hardware company? An innovative consumer service ecosystem with a big brand premium? An app store marketplace? There’s the risk and return. Is Honeywell International Inc. an old-style industrial firm or a technology firm that applies robotics to manufacturing and is involved in the “Internet of Things”? What is the Internet of Things? There’s the risk and return. Is a Singapore property management company generating value from collecting rents or from property appreciation, or both? Where’s the risk and return? What characterizes the business of Amazon.com , Inc.? An e-commerce company with a cloud service side business? A cloud service business supporting an e-commerce business? A subscription business bringing consumers to a wide range of offerings via Amazon Prime? What is the business model of (unprofitable) X Corp. (formerly Twitter, Inc.), or is it primarily an entrepreneur’s vanity project? How could a business be run differently? This is the question that the private equity investor or activist investment fund asks. Business analysis is first and foremost—but, to be clear, this book is not about analyzing specific businesses. That is the subject of myriad strategy, entrepreneurship, marketing, and management books. Rather, this book is about applying your knowledge of a business to the question of how much to pay for it. Many investment books tell you what to look for in a business: Honest, capable management; the people factor; the five forces of strategy; the fifteen points for sound investing; and, above all, a durable competitive advantage, that so-called “moat” protecting a business from its competitors. These points are important, but “words, words, words, I’m so sick of words; is that all you blighters can do?” cries Eliza Doolittle in My Fair Lady . An innovative consumer service ecosystem? A brand premium? The Internet of Things? Words, words, words! We need to be more concrete if we are to understand these ideas in terms that indicate their value. That is what value investing by the book does. P.3. Price Is What You Pay, Value Is What You Get Yes, this is the epigraph to this chapter. The starting point is not efficient markets but a realization that price can deviate from fundamental value. “Cynics know the price of everything but the value of nothing,” says Oscar Wilde. P.4. The Primary Risk in Investing Is the Risk of Paying Too Much Risk is depicted in many ways: as price risk with the beta measure, with pricing “factors” in formal asset pricing models, and with various fundamental measures of business risk. Some of these metrics are relevant, but the primary risk facing the investor is the risk of paying too much for shares (or selling for too little). That is what harms us most. Permanent loss of capital from paying too much is the primary fear, not the daily or monthly swings in stock prices that common risk measures capture. If price drops by 50 percent from an elevated purchase price, it then takes a 100 percent return just to get back to square one. “Risk comes from not knowing what you’re doing,” says Buffett. So, let’s know what to do. P.5. Ignore Information at Your Peril When trading on a few pieces of information, such as P/E and P/B (price-to-book) ratios, an investor runs the danger of trading with someone who has done their homework on a wider set of relevant information. Identifying that wider information and packaging it in a form to challenge the market price is the task of fundamental value investing. And that’s what accounting by the book does. P.6. Understand What You Know and Separate it from Speculation Benjamin Graham talked of value “justified by the facts.” The stock price might build in high expectations of future revenues for the electric vehicle manufacturer Rivian. That’s speculation. What you know is what revenues the firm is generating now. Separate the two. The value investor has deep respect for the facts and for the risks of speculation. P.7. Anchor a Valuation on What You Know The value investor’s task is to challenge the speculation in prices. What we know, the facts on the ground, anchor us for the challenge. Current revenues, profit margins, and a host of other information are the anchor. The investor asks: If current revenues and profit margins are low, how can the speculation in the price be justified? Graham broke value into two components: Value = Minimum True Value + Speculative Value The first component in this equation is based on hard information, value “justified by the facts.” The second is based on soft information about the future. “True Value” is nevertheless a bit too strong for, as we will see, true value is elusive. Rather, Value = Anchoring Accounting Value + Speculative Value The first component is the anchor implied by accounting, the value we can account for based on what we know. The second is the value to be challenged. Both are part of our accounting for value, but separating the two is critical. The anchoring component can challenge speculation: both the market’s and your own. This is the scientific method: establishing what we know in order to challenge speculation about how the world works. P.8. Beware of Paying for Growth, for Growth Is Risky Price speculation is about prospects for future growth. The market can get overexcited or depressed about growth, so the investor focuses on growth prospects. But, in doing so, she is warned: Buying growth is risky. Growth comes from risky investments, growth can be competed away, and leverage can promote growth but also adds risk. Recognizing that risk, the investor discounts the price to be paid. P.9. When Calculating Value to Challenge the Price, Do Not Put Price in the Calculation The circularity of violating this maxim is clear. But we will see immediately in the next chapter that some standard valuation techniques violate this principle. Indeed, we will also see that some “bad” accounting does the same. P.10. Be Patient: It Often Takes Some Time for Price to Correct Itself and Revert to Fundamentals Value investing is not day trading. Sometimes it takes several years for firms to deliver the value an investor sees, usually with realized earnings justifying the valuation. Indeed, a price that deviates from fundamental value can deviate still further in the short term, a thought to keep in mind especially when thinking about taking short positions in overpriced stocks. Be patient (and be careful in going short). “The stock market is designed to transfer money from the active to the patient,” Warren Buffett once said. This principle distinguishes the long-term fundamental investor from what we might call the short-term fundamental investor. The latter trades on the arrival of fundamental information, such as a quarterly earnings report, an analyst’s forecast revision, or a miss of an earnings number against expectation, hoping that the market might be slow in adjusting to the information (as sometimes it is). She holds the position for a month or even less. The long-term investor evaluates prospects over a much longer period and waits for the realization. These ten principles guide us. If one of them is violated, we know we are on the wrong track. They will lead us to other principles, stated in italics in the text as we progress. Modern finance has given us investing principles that have become part of “what we know.” These are important principles on which to “anchor” our investing. Finance is sometimes dismissive of the ten principles and fails to appreciate accounting. On the other hand, accounting sometimes fails to appreciate the principles of finance. This book integrates the two. Both the commonsense principles and the foundational principles of finance will lead us to principles for keeping a book with “good” accounting. Modern value investing, as you will see, is built on a three-part foundation of the commonsense principles of traditional value investing, the principles of finance, and accounting principles. This is good thinking. Market Efficiency and the Defensive Investor While we will incorporate many of the principles of modern finance, there is one we are skeptical about: market efficiency. This principle, emphasized in many business schools, says that prices incorporate all relevant information because price equals value and future price movements are largely unpredictable. Thus, it is sometimes said, the value investing endeavor is questionable. This position clearly conflicts with value investing principle 3, the heading for this chapter and the premise of this book. And in fact, we can see that this position does not make sense on some level: If prices are efficient for each and every firm, there is no incentive to do value analysis, so how can prices then reflect value and be efficient? It also goes against our natural instincts. When buying anything, be it a house, a car, or an orange, we ask: Is it worth the price? And we look for bargains. However, the value investor must be careful in discarding the efficient market principle. She might see a price that is different from value and anticipate that the price will gravitate to fundamental value, yielding her gains. This is based on the principle that prices must be efficient in the long run. But this also means that prices are efficient on average in the short run. “On average” means that some stocks might differ from the average, mispricing for the value investor to discover. But there is an important point to appreciate: It is best to start with the presumption that the price is efficient; it’s hard to “beat the market.” That puts the onus on you to demonstrate otherwise. That is the stance of active investors, in contrast to passive investors who hold the market portfolio in an index fund. But there is also the active-passive investor who concedes that the market is typically efficient in its pricing but is worried about preservation of capital. These defensive investors emphasize that the primary risk of investing is the risk of paying too much . They carry out an analysis to avoid the downside, seeking to earn steady returns rather than trying to shoot the moon. They see there is considerable money to be made (or, rather, not lost) from avoiding overpriced stocks. Rather than looking for good firms at a low price, they are satisfied with good firms at a fair price, looking to the compounding of steady returns over years to generate wealth. Charlie Munger: “The first rule of compounding is to never interrupt it unnecessarily.” What sort of investor are you? We laud the success of value investors of the past, but they were investing in a different era: there was far less information available, and it was often difficult to find. There were no computers, no Internet, no search engines. When Benjamin Graham started investing in the late 1910s and early 1920s, information on companies was limited and some did not even report revenue numbers “for competitive reasons.” This stands in sharp contrast to our immediate access to digital information today. Markets must now be more efficient. By the 1960s, Graham mused that the market had become more efficient because many others had adapted his research techniques. This highlights the importance of getting an edge through superior analysis of information. Returns to Active Investing Yes, getting an edge in investing is not easy. Study after study has shown that, after the fees they charge, the average active fund has not performed any better than its benchmark index, and often worse. That has been taken as evidence that the market is efficient: Professional investors cannot “beat the market.” The critique is a little unfair, for some active funds with the preservation of capital in mind work to protect against the downside. That inevitably loses some of the upside, yielding lower returns. Nevertheless, let’s investigate. Look at the performance of funds relative to their benchmark for 1, 3 and 5 year periods up to 2021 in table 1.1 as reported by the Standard and Poor’s SPIVA ® Scorecard. The next year, in 2022 when the total return for the Standard & Poor’s 500 index (S&P 500) was −18.11 percent, 51 percent of large-cap funds underperformed relative to the index, though better than the 58 percent underperforming in 2021. Sixty-three percent of mid-cap funds and 57 percent of small-cap funds underperformed relative to their benchmarks in 2022. Though better than what the table shows, this is not a good record. To add to this, so-called “value” funds have not performed well since the financial crisis of 2008, with the MSCI USA Value Index outperforming the Growth Index in only two years prior to 2021. During those same years, there was a flight from active funds to passive funds, as well as to exchange-traded funds (ETFs) and index funds with their lower fees. In 2021, according to reports, the total market value of U.S. index tracking funds stood at $8.8 trillion and ETFs had grown to $6.6 trillion, a 96 percent increase over 2018. Refinitiv Lipper reported that, worldwide, ETF assets stood at $10 trillion in 2021, up 98 percent over 2018, with $1.22 trillion inflows during that year. From 1995 to March 2020, $5.2 trillion flowed into passive funds versus $1.8 trillion to active funds. By the end of 2022, 52 percent of U.S. equity funds were in passive investments according to a report by JPMorgan. Table 1.1 Percentage of active funds trailing their benchmarks up to December 2021 Over 5 years (%) Over 3 years (%) Over 1 year (%) Large-cap funds 73 68 58 Mid-cap funds 59 49 76 Small-cap funds 67 55 78 Source : S&P Dow Jones Indices SPIVA ® Scorecard at https://www.spglobal.com/spdji/en/research-insights/spiva/ . What explains the performance of active funds? Well, they charge fees—sometimes 1 percent or more of assets under management (AUM)—and the performance in table 1.1 is after those fees. But also, many fund managers, while picking stocks, also diversify, not only to protect investors but also to protect themselves against a flight from their funds with drawdowns. Further, as the fund flows described above suggest, cash flows into funds when funds have performed well, forcing fund managers to buy stocks at high prices to deploy that cash. And cash exits funds when prices and performance are down, forcing the sale of stocks at low prices to meet the redemptions unless the funds have gates. That goes against the mantra “buy low, sell high.” An individual value investor avoids these issues. The average investor buys at the average price, so if the market is efficient, the average investor does not beat the market. To beat the average, the investor needs to get an edge on the competition. We hope this book leads you there. The Passive Investing Alternative These data suggest that, on average, market prices approximate value or, in the language of modern finance, markets are efficient with their pricing. There is considerable theory to suggest so: If prices differ from value, (value) investors will quickly see the profit opportunity and arbitrage the profits away, driving price to value. So, why not just buy a passive index fund with much lower fees? Indeed, it is standard advice to buy a stock index and hold it for the long term since, in the long run, stocks outperform bonds. 1 1 . The Your Library supplement to this book gives references for many of the statements in the text like this one, together with further reading on topics in each chapter. But is this good advice? Stocks might outperform bonds in the long run, on average, but that is because they are riskier, and risk should yield higher returns. There’s the rub, to borrow from Shakespeare’s Hamlet : Risk means that you can get lower returns as well as higher returns. The advice to buy stocks comes from observing the success of U.S. stocks over the past 100 years. But this success is being observed after the fact, and is charted over a long period of time for a country that was successful. Publisher Henry Luce, the founder of the iconic American magazines Time , Fortune , and Life , went so far as to name this period “the American Century.” With the yearly standard deviation of returns for the S&P 500 of about 20 percent and a risk premium of 6 percent (to be generous), one can, with reasonable probability, have periods of 25 years or more where stock returns are less than those for safe bonds. Even in the United States, bonds outperformed stocks for long periods. It was not until 1954 that U.S. stocks recovered to the level they had been at before the Great Crash of 1929. Even though stocks appreciated significantly during the bubble of the late 1990s, bonds outperformed stocks over the 20 years ending in 2018. What if you had bought stocks in 1999 when the S&P 500 stood at 1460, only to then see them fall to 880 by 2002, and then to 680 in 2009 (at a time you needed cash for retirement)? Japanese bonds outperformed Japanese stocks from 1960 to 2017, as did French bonds. The United Kingdom’s Financial Times Stock Exchange (FTSE) 100 Index stood at 6930 in December 1999, rising only 5.2 percent to 7290 by December 2021 and then only to 7,800 by 2023 (though the dividend yield was about 4 percent per year). Sometimes the long run is a long time coming. Why leave stock selection to the designers of the S&P 500 or the FTSE 100? One argument is that an index of many stocks provides diversification, and diversification reduces risk. Yes, it can, but few have made exceptional returns from diversification, earning just the average return. Diversification with an index investment is for those who do not have the capabilities of value investors. And, as the historical experience above reports, one is exposed to the risk of the market. John Maynard Keynes lost considerable wealth by holding stocks during the 1929 stock market crash. He subsequently reformed into a successful value investor. Here is his view on holding an index: To suppose that safety first consists in having a small gamble in a large number of different companies where I have no information to reach a good judgment, as compared with a substantial stake in a company where one’s information is adequate, strikes me as a travesty of investment policy. To underscore Keynes’s point, the stock prices of the seven largest stocks in the MSCI USA Index fell 40 percent in 2022. The prices for the rest fell 14 percent. Why not try to sort out stocks whose prices are more likely to fall? Or rise? Research reports that the majority of U.S.-traded stocks since 1926 yielded lifetime returns that were less than one-month Treasuries and the 4 percent best performing stocks explains the entire net gain of the entire stock market over that period. Of course, many stocks performed well for part of their lifetime, and the value investor seeks these out. From 2003 to 2022, five German companies—Siemens, SAP, Allianz, Mercedes-Benz, and Deutsche Telekom—accounted for 29.8 percent of all corporate wealth created in Germany (in stock price increases and dividends), as reported in the Financial Times on February 9, 2023. These same published reports show that 58 percent of all traded German companies created no value at all, and that many stocks, particularly financials, actually destroyed value. There is some sorting out to be done. Buffett echoes Keynes by quoting Mark Twain’s advice from Pudd’nhead Wilson: “Put all your eggs in one basket—and watch that basket.” That is the view of value investors. Benjamin Graham of Columbia University also suffered in the 1929 crash. Like Keynes, he turned to evaluating individual stocks. This does not mean necessarily ignoring exposure to the market, but instead evaluating the risk in doing so. Indeed, the value investor can build a portfolio of individual stocks based on “companies where one’s information is adequate” or can think of buying the market portfolio and stripping out stocks where analysis informs that they may be overpriced: an active-passive approach. In 2021, the S&P 500 yielded 28.71 percent in total return. But the index was heavily weighted toward large technology stocks such as Apple, Microsoft, Alphabet (Google), Meta (Facebook), and Tesla, which accounted for 25 of the 28.71 total percentage points in the 2021 return. Many value investors saw some of these firms as overpriced. The index dropped over 20 percent in 2022, with these same five large technology stocks (plus Netflix) explaining 46 percent of the decline. The passive investor takes on this risk, always worried (or should be) about whether the index is “peak passive”: Is the index about to crash? That anxiety comes from ignoring the information that value investors exploit. As they say, Ignore information at your own peril . In contrast to the returns in table 1.1 , 55 percent of actively managed large-cap mutual funds beat their benchmarks in 2022 according to a report from Goldman Sachs Group, Inc. Compared to the index, actively managed open-ended funds fell only 5.8 percent according to Morningstar Direct. This shows how actively investing can protect the downside. Of course, if an investor does not want to (or cannot) do the work to discriminate among stocks, buying the index may be the only way to share in the potential wealth generated broadly by business corporations. The market portfolio is the average of investor holdings, so the average investor holds the market portfolio. You may wish to be the average investor—there are other things in life to get on with! But you are bearing the risk of paying too much . Dabble a bit: You might find there are stocks in an index that you want to strip out. It may be that, after a value analysis of a stock, we conclude that the price is right and it is efficiently priced. Indeed, that is often the outcome. But just as it is worthwhile to kick the tires when buying a used car, it is worth poking at a secondhand stock with some analysis. You may conclude that price is value, but you now feel more secure. And you have dealt with the risk of paying too much. Call that defensive active investing. Indeed, it is good to start with the presumption that the price is right. That puts the onus on you to show that it is not. In doing so you may possibly discover an opportunity where the stock looks cheap or expensive. That’s the reward for diligent value investing. For evidence that active investing can yield rewards, look at figure 1.1 . The first panel classifies investment funds on two dimensions, tracking error and active share. Tracking error indicates the extent to which a fund’s returns differ from its benchmark index: If low, the fund has similar exposures to the index. Active share measures the composition of a fund’s investments relative to that in the index, the weight given to individual stocks in the fund relative to their weight in the index. Funds are classified into four groups in the figure. In the lower left-hand quadrant are funds that say they are active funds but are just mimicking the index, with the same investments and the same volatility (but charging you higher fees). Above them are stock pickers, but those that invest in a broad range of stocks to get diversification benefits. (The average U.S. fund holds 160 stocks, according to Morningstar.) They thus track similarly to the index, but with high active share. In the right-hand quadrant are selective stock pickers (with all eggs in one basket) who typically invest in a small, concentrated number of targeted stocks. Finally, there are funds that have high tracking error but low active share; they have similar investments to the index but somehow also have high tracking error. Let’s just call them Clowns. [IMAGE: ../images/fig1-1.png] 1.1 Funds classified on tracking error and active share and their returns. Panel A classifies funds; Panel B gives the average annual returns for the classified funds. Source : M. Cremers and A. Petajisto, “How Active Is Your Fund Manager?” Review of Financial Studies 2009: 3329–65. The second panel of the figure gives the average annual return (after fees) for these fund categories. Closet indexers earn negative returns on average, presumably the effect of higher fees relative to the passive index funds that they mimic. The Clowns are worse, and not very funny. Diversified stock pickers do better, but the highest returns are with the concentrated stock pickers. The differences may not seem large, but compounded over years they are. Further investigation tells us that the returns are yet higher for funds that hold stocks for longer periods (two years or more) and thus have lower portfolio turnover and lower trading costs. These also tend to be funds focusing on business ideas, in-depth analysis of information, and micro versus macro analysis. They also ignore volatility, instead focusing on where the price will end up. (Reported in the Financial Times , February 28, 2017). On the question of passive versus active investing, there are other issues lurking in the background that may encourage you to be a value investor. Efficient prices are necessary for the allocation of capital to its most productive use in the economy. If all investors became passive investors, how would information about value get into prices to make them efficient? Prices would truly be a “random walk down Wall Street” with passive investors then facing indiscriminate risk. A shock to the system might lead to a run on index funds with the consequent systemic risk to the economy. Someone must do the research so that prices represent value for the welfare of the economy, and that someone must be compensated for the effort with returns. The index investor can be a free rider, but we can’t have too many of them. Be gratified that, as a value investor, you are contributing to the welfare of all. Another thought: With the separation of management from shareholders in most public corporations, there is the “agency problem” of managers operating in their own interest rather than the owners’. That is dysfunctional, not only for the shareholder-owners but for the economy. Regulation ameliorates this, but a shareholder who thinks about the business and how it should be managed is a strong force in monitoring the stewardship of managers. The passive investor dismisses the agency problem while we see active hedge funds taking up the governance role. Value investing may add to your wealth, but you can see from these points that it is not just a matter of self-interest. As Adam Smith recognized in The Wealth of Nations , your self-interest creates welfare for society. Again, Keynes: “The social object of skilled investment should be to defeat the dark forces of time and ignorance which envelope our future.” So you can feel good about investing. Have you noticed that successful value investors are often also philanthropic? They feel strongly about contributing to the general welfare. Keeping an Accounting Book Value investing involves developing and maintaining an accounting book that the investor then uses to challenge the speculation in the market price: Is the market pricing according to the book? We will build this book over subsequent chapters. There are three features to the book’s architecture. The first feature is design that recognizes good thinking about value. Much like a building is designed by an architect as a configuration of rooms that will yield value to inhabitants, the “rooms” of the accounting book are accounts in a configuration that will convey values to investors. A kitchen complements a dining room, and accounts receivable complements the revenue account. More broadly, the set of accounts that make up the balance sheet complements those in the income statement. In their thinking about value, investors are thus instructed that they cannot evaluate earnings in the income statement without also incorporating the balance sheet. The second feature is structural integrity . In her design, the architect must honor the principles of sound engineering to ensure a safe building. So must the author of the accounting book for safe investing. Engineering requires obedience to certain structural relations, and so does the accounting book, for which these structural relations are called accounting equations. You are probably familiar with the one governing the balance sheet: Equity = Assets − Liabilities. The equation foregrounds safety: The balance sheet must balance, otherwise there is a flaw. This thinking is embedded in the accounting book: Shareholders’ equity in the assets and what they produce is only delivered after liabilities are satisfied. Another formula is the so-called clean surplus relation: Change in Equity = Earnings − Net Dividends. Evaluating an earnings number that does not reconcile to the balance sheet is bad thinking. These and other accounting equations tie the system into a structure that reflects the thinking about value. Once rooms are structured, the architect fills the rooms with detail. The third feature of the book is the measurement of the numbers that go into the accounts. Measurement is all-important, for that determines how dollar numbers in the accounts convey dollar value. If you know the book Moneyball by Michael Lewis, you know that baseball teams can get an edge by paying attention to performance measures that indicate success. So good measurement bears on one’s success in the investing game. Yes, this is all a bit vague. Too many strained metaphors? Let’s proceed to constructing the accounting book in detail, laying out the design with structural integrity and entering the appropriate measurements. Then let’s put the book into action to find if price is different from value. The architect designs a building for a pleasing experience for inhabitants, not just for functionality. So we trust that, as we proceed, you will be struck by the pleasing aesthetics of the book. Some Benchmarks Benchmarks are useful for understanding when a measure appears to be out of line, and we will lay down some benchmarks as the book proceeds. They will often prompt further investigation. One benchmark is the performance of the stock market as a whole. The first frame in figure 1.2 displays the total return (price change plus dividends) for the S&P 500 from 1982 to 2022. That is accompanied by related metrics to which we will continually refer. Since 2000, numbers are observed in real time (at each December 31), not from back-filled numbers after that date. Table 1.3 summarizes various measures in figure 1.2 and table 1.2 . The mean (average) total return (from price change plus dividends) for the index was 12.85 percent from 1982 to 2022 but was also highly variable, with a standard deviation of 16.73 percent and yielding 37.58 percent in 1995 but −37.00 percent in 2008. You see how risky the market can be. The trailing P/E ratio, price relative to earnings over the past year, ranges from 10.1 in 1984 to 60.8 in 2008 with a mean of 21.87. But the trailing P/E can be depressed or inflated by exceptionally low or high earnings in a particular year. Better to look at the forward P/E, price relative to the forecasted marketwide earnings for the coming year, which ranges from 7.1 in 1988 to 40.6 in 2001 with a mean of 18.10. That is an earnings yield (E/P) of 5.52 percent. The average trailing P/E over the last 50 years was 16.0 and the average forward P/E was 15.3, both due to lower multiples prior to 1982. Those numbers translate into an earnings yield of a little over 6 percent. The mean price-to-book ratio (P/B) is 2.78 and ranges from 1.25 in 1982 to 5.05 in 1999. The average P/B ratio over the last 50 years has been 2.8. The mean trailing book return of equity (ROE) is 13.89 percent and ranges from a low of 2.81 percent in 2008 to a high of 21.05 percent in 2021. The corresponding forward ROE has a mean of 16.25 percent. Table 1.3 S&P 500 metrics, 1982–2022: Summary statistics 1982–2022 Mean Median Std. dev. Introducing the Case Book Nothing works better than seeing investing “live.” We’ll be taking you to cases where you can see investing principles in action, most from the recent past. These are posted in a Case Book on this book’s open website, with the full solutions available under a password. These cases will be updated regularly so the numbers for the year in the cases introduced at the end of each chapter might not be the numbers in the updated case in the Case Book. Here is a case to get your juices flowing. It involves discounted cash flow (DCF) valuation that is common in practice. It involves keeping a cash book on valuation. But you will see that it does not work well; it is not based on “good thinking.” That sets up the rest of the book: How does one engage in valuation through “good thinking”? How does one keep an effective book for valuation? Case 1: Starbucks Corporation: Keeping A Cash Book You’re Got To be Kidding! Let’s try our first valuation. For Italians, Starbucks coffee is not their cup of tea. But for a good part of the rest of the world, Starbucks coffee is the morning wake-up call, and its stores are a place to spend a quiet hour in the evening with a book—a book on value investing, perhaps. There are over 38,000 company-operated and licensed Starbucks outlets in 86 countries, serving who-knows-how-many cups of coffee and other products each day. Sitting there, sipping your caffè macchiato swirl razzle-dazzle, you might well ask: What could all of this be worth? A quick check on Yahoo Finance or Google Finance tells you that the firm has a market capitalization of $109 billion or $92.99 per share at December 31, 2023. Is it worth it? Should you pay the current forward P/E of 24.1? Bill Ackman, the activist investor with a fundamental slant, has favored Starbucks for some time. In October 2018, he bought a 1.1 percent stake in Starbucks. It was a good call by Ackman. Starbuck’s stock price increased from $58.63 in October 2018 to $88.89 by the end of 2019, with an added annual dividend yield of 1.65 percent. Then the COVID pandemic hit its stores hard, with the price dropping back to $65.74 in March 2020. At that point, Ackman increased his position in the stock. Again, a good call. But is the price of $92.99 at December 31, 2023 worth it, particularly after the troubles that Starbucks has run into? Working through this case will introduce you to the idea of keeping a book for valuation. The book in the case is a cash book, and you are asked whether a cash book does the job. A cash book is cash accounting for value, as in the accounting in the cash flow statement. It gives you the inputs into the most orthodox method for valuation: discounted cash flow analysis. It seems to make sense; after all, we invest to get cash returns, so the expected cash should be the basis for valuation. Let’s check. Introducing Your Library To reduce clutter, this book does not have a lot of references or footnotes. We see these as often distracting from the message. However, consistent with value investing principles, nothing in the book is speculative; it is backed up by authoritative research. This book’s library directs you to sources behind the ideas to give you confidence in what you are reading in this book. The writings are part of “what we know,” or at least what we tentatively know from research to date. Anchor on what you know . The list is representative but not comprehensive. Some papers are technical, a challenge for many readers. But at least read the abstracts. Annotations introduce the books and papers. They also sketch out the history of the generation of ideas and the investing techniques that come from them. You’ll get a sense of investing on the shoulders of those who came before. A good value investor is a student of value investing.

Chapter 2 Cheap Value Investing Chapter 2 Cheap Value Investing Ignore information at your peril: when calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price in the calculation T o set the stage, this chapter lays out two simple valuation methods based on price multiples that are quite common in investment practice. They are cheap, for they do not require much analysis. But they are too cheap. A saying attributed to Albert Einstein tells us to keep things as simple as possible but not too simple. These methods are simple, but too simple. Some of the methods are seductively branded as value investing but are not: They violate the fundamental principles of chapter 1 and can get the investor into trouble. Indeed, they expose the investor to a value trap, and this chapter explains why. The underlying problem is the failure to keep a book. Examining this problem sets the stage for introducing accounting for value in the next chapter. These cheap methods invest with pricing multiples such as price-to-earnings (P/E) and price-to-book (P/B), but without the links to the fundamentals underlying these ratios. Without that understanding, the investor is exposed to the trap. So, in reviewing these common techniques, we will come to an appreciation of what determines P/E and P/B ratios, fundamentally. It is that which must be incorporated in the accounting book. A machine might point you to these ratios: ChatGPT: “If I had to point to one valuation metric, I would choose the forward price to earnings (P/E) ratio. The forward P/E ratio is based on the market’s expectation for the company’s future earnings performance, which is arguably more relevant than its past performance. As a general rule of thumb, a lower forward P/E would indicate that a stock is relatively cheaper and potentially more attractive from a valuation perspective.” Man Group, “The Road Ahead: Investment Tips from ChatGPT,” April 2023 As with the discussion of love in the previous chapter, this artificial intelligence is a little artificial. Machines are good for searching and tabulating information but pulling the information together in a coherent and efficient way requires more thinking, and that is what accounting for value involves. Before we proceed, a teaser. We’ll begin most chapters with some statements or questions—yes, to tease you, but also to get you thinking about what’s to follow. Sometimes the teasers will show up your mistaken thinking (sorry!), sometimes they will mystify you, and sometimes they might even shock you. But you will see the resolution as the chapter proceeds. Teasers The average forward P/E in the United States has been about 16. Why are the forward P/E ratios for these automobile companies so low? (August 2023) General Motors Ford Stellantis Volkswagen Honda Toyota 4.29 6.33 2.79 4.16 7.41 9.60 Why are the forward P/E ratios for these automobile companies so high? Tesla Ferrari 65.79 44.05 Too Simple: Valuation with Multiples This method is so simple a machine could do it, and so could a fifth grader. It has two forms: valuation with historical multiples and valuation with multiples of comparable firms. But first, let’s be sure of the calculations. A trailing P/E is the current share price over the most recent annual earnings per share (EPS). A rolling P/E updates with price per share divided by the sum of the most recent four quarters of earnings (or two half years, if in a country without quarterly reporting). This is labeled P/E ttm (trailing 12 months). A forward P/E is the price per share over a forecast of EPS for the next fiscal year (which is the current year in progress). Typically, the multiple is reported using a consensus forecast of sell-side analysts who follow the stock. The P/B is the equity price over the most recently published book value of common equity on the balance sheet. Valuation with Historical Multiples Analysts put in considerable effort to understand a business in order to develop forecasts of sales, earnings, and cash flows relevant for valuation. Good work. That then leaves the task of converting these forecasts into a valuation. Too often we see analysts taking their forecast (say, of earnings) and applying an average P/E for the firm or its industry to arrive at a valuation that they call the “target price”: Value = Forecasted Earnings × Historical P/E. In 2022, analysts were forecasting a consensus forward EPS of $0.75 for Infosys Limited (INFY), the Indian technology outsourcing and consulting firm. With an average P/E over the prior 10 years of 22, the estimated value is $16.50. That surely is simple, but could it also be simple-minded? The shares traded at $23.05. Sell? Valuation with the Method of Comparables Rather than applying a historical multiple, this method applies the multiples of comparable firms (in the same line of business) to the target’s numbers to get a value. This is similar to estimating the value of an apartment by applying the price per square foot (or meter) in the same area to the square feet (or meters) in the target apartment. Macy’s, Inc. (M), a department store, traded at a P/E of 7.1 and a P/B of 1.91 (in 2022). To value Nordstrom, Inc. (JWN), a similar department store, apply Macy’s P/E to Nordstrom’s EPS of $0.08: Value = $0.08 × 7.1 = $0.57. Or apply Macy’s P/B to Nordstrom’s book value per share (BPS) of $3.65: Value = $3.65 × 1.91 = $6.97. Nordstrom shares traded at $24.10 at the time. Sell? Do you feel confident? These metrics are sometimes plotted on a football field chart, leaving you quite confused about the game play. There are a number of issues with these valuations. Firms change over time, so a historical P/E might not be indicative of current growth prospects or risk. Taking averages over a number of “comps” perhaps gives a more precise multiple to apply, but exact comparables are often hard to find. Breweries might be similar, but what is the comparable for a start-up with a unique product? Similar firms, like Macy’s and Nordstrom, are in competition, with one possibly dominating. The process can be refined by controlling for differences between the comps, but there are more fundamental reasons to be insecure about these methods: They violate the value investing principles of the last chapter. Ignore information at your peril . In using a P/E ratio, or even adding further comparative multiples like P/B or Price/Sales, one is using only a small amount of the available information. You have not identified all the relevant information, so you are in danger of trading with someone who has done their homework and is on the lookout for naive traders. When calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price in the calculation . If the traded price is an “efficient” price, OK. But, if the comp is overvalued or undervalued, so is your valuation; you don’t have the benchmark for determining value. Price is what you pay, value is what you get . Dangerously, pricing with comparables can contribute to a price bubble, the very thing that value investing is designed to check. See the vignette in the box below. A Vignette Initial Public Offerings and Price Bubbles You have probably noticed that initial public offerings (IPOs) come in batches, typically in hot markets with high multiples. We had the personal computer and software IPOs in the 1980s, the telecom, tele-servicing, Internet, and theme restaurant IPOs in the 1990s, followed by the many tech start-up and biotech IPOs of this century. Most occurred when there was a lot of speculation about these companies’ prospects. An investment banker would say to a client: Look, similar firms are going to market at high prices. You should also, and I can justify a high price based on the pricing multiples they receive. That creates another high multiple which then becomes the benchmark for the pricing of the next IPO, and so it escalates into a price bubble. But bubbles pop, as in 2000 after the many high-priced IPOs for internet, e-commerce, and telecom start-up companies. And so again in 2022, as the high IPO prices for start-up tech firms in 2021 dropped significantly and firms failed. Warren Buffett called the 1990s a chain letter, with the investment bankers the postmen. Pricing with multiples is probably the most common valuation technique on Wall Street and other financial centers around the world. And it is sometimes justified: The investment banker works to get the highest price for the issuer. So her best advice is: If the multiples of the comps indicate the market is willing to overpay, then go IPO. But that is the sell side. In this book, we are on the buy side, either investing our own money judiciously or as fiduciaries with others’ money. We are possibly buying the IPO rather than selling. Beware when someone is asking you to buy. Beware of buying with comparables . Indeed, the investment banker has a problem in pushing overpriced shares: He or she must dump the stuff on other clients. One multiple is particularly dangerous: Price/Sales. See the box below. Value Investing in Action Beware of the Price/Sales Ratio The average price-to-sales ratio (P/S) is about 1.0. So, it is tempting to see a P/S less than 1.0 as a cheap stock. Beware. One might think that a P/S ratio is based on expected sales growth in the future, so a high ratio indicates high sales growth. But be careful: A firm can make sales but incur losses from those sales. The old advertising gimmick may pull in customers to the discount retailer: “We make a loss on every sale but make it up in volume.” However, if literally true, that loses value. Sales are necessary to add value, but they must be made at a profit. So the P/S ratio also prices the profit margin, that is, how much the firm earns from sales. Walgreens Boots Alliance, Inc. (WBA), the retail pharmacy chain, traded at a P/S ratio of 0.31 in 2022, looking cheap. But its net profit margin was just 2.39 percent of sales. The net profit margin is calculated as [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn01.png] So, [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn02.png] Thus, with the profit margin from sales recognized with a P/S, the multiple is the P/E ratio. For Walgreens, the combination of a P/S ratio of 0.31 and a profit margin of 2.39 percent yields a P/E of 13.0. That gives insight into the P/E ratio: It is determined in part by forecasted sales and profit margins. But that is not the full story, as we will see. There is another issue. P/S is based on the equity price, but sales are generated by assets and those assets can be financed by debt as well as equity; those assets yield higher sales but with no added equity. So, the appropriate P/S ratio is an unlevered price-to-sales ratio, also called the enterprise price-to-sales ratio: [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn03.png] Enterprise price, the price of the business that generates sales, is equity price plus net debt. Walgreens’s unlevered P/S was 0.54. We deal with unlevered measures in chapter 5 . Too Simple: Value versus Growth Investing Turn it around: Rather than seeing a pricing multiple as indicating value, see it as indicating mispricing. That is, see a low P/E or a low P/B as a cheap stock, and a high P/E or high P/B as too expensive. This stock screening makes some sense: If the market price is low relative to earnings and book value, the market might be underpricing these fundamentals. So buy low multiples and sell high multiples. But heed the warning: Ignore information at your peril . There is a variation on this strategy called Value versus Growth Investing. Low multiples are “value stocks” and the “value investor” buys stocks with low P/E and P/B. High multiples are “growth stocks” because a high P/E stock has a lot of potential growth priced into it, like Tesla (TSLA) trading (in 2024) 81 times forward earnings, or a forward P/E of 32 for Microsoft (MSFT), 32 for Netflix (NFLX), and 33 for Amazon (AMZN). But the thinking changes with a growth stock. Rather than a high multiple being an indication of an overpriced stock, these stocks are viewed as a buying opportunity: They can generate growth, but the market is underpricing that growth. So ubiquitous is this approach to investing that the Morningstar™ Style Box classifies investment funds by shading cells in a matrix based on these investment styles and the size of firms in which the fund invests: [IMAGE: ../images/c02_ufig001.png] Here the classified fund invests in large growth stocks. You might well ask what the large, medium, and small distinctions mean for a value investor (we will come to that), but here the focus is on the value-growth designations. How have these strategies performed? Table 2.1 reports the mean annual returns from investing on P/E and P/B over the years. Each year, firms are grouped into five portfolios based on their P/E or P/B. The returns are the average from repeating the strategy every year, 1982 to 2020. Table 2.1 Average annual returns from investing on the basis of price-to-earnings (P/E) and price-to-book (P/B), 1982–2020 (in percent) Multiple Portfolio 1 Portfolio 2 Portfolio 3 Portfolio 4 Portfolio 5 Low–High (Low) High P/E 13.01 11.90 10.83 9.92 7.98 5.03 P/B 14.28 12.17 10.75 9.75 5.18 9.10 Source : Compustat ® accounting data and price and returns from the Center for Research in Security Prices. Note : Earnings are before extraordinary items and P/E excludes loss firms. Returns are in excess of those on the U.S.10-year (risk-free) bond. You can see that investing in low P/E (value) yielded a higher average return than high P/E (growth), with a difference of 5.03 percent per year, sometimes called the value-growth spread. Similarly so for low and high P/B with a return difference of 9.10 percent per year. These are significant differences. If investing is that easy, there is no need to read further in this book, but this just looks too easy. A basic law of economics says that higher risk yields higher returns on average, so these differences in returns could be due to differences in risk. Indeed, this Value versus Growth strategy lures the investor into the so-called value trap: It can go against the investor with growth yielding higher returns than value. Figure 2.1 depicts annual returns for the MSCI USA Value and Growth Indexes 2003–2020. While value continued to outperform growth up to 2006, growth has outperformed value in most years since. Similarly, from 2009 to 2021, the Russell 1000 Value Index outperformed its Growth Index cousin in only two of those years. What had looked like easy money from 1982 to 2009 turned out to be otherwise. In 2021, the MSCI World Value Index was up 19.3 percent, but the MSCI World Growth Index was up 24.8 percent. In 2022, it flipped again, with value outperforming growth. This is not secure investing. [IMAGE: ../images/fig2-1.png] 2.1 MSCI U.S. Value versus Growth Index Annual Returns We are flying blind here, with little understanding of the fundamentals we are buying. When you have just a few bits of information, ignore information at your peril . This is value investing mislabeled: Most analysts feel they must choose between two approaches customarily thought to be in opposition: “value” and “growth.” . . . We view that as fuzzy thinking . . . Growth is always a component of value [and] the very term “value investing” is redundant. Warren Buffett, Berkshire Hathaway Annual Report, 1992 Almost all stocks involve growth (positive or negative), so the investing question is how to buy growth under the mantra: Beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky . That is the task for the value investor, and that is why the Value versus Growth distinction is “fuzzy thinking.” Sometimes you should buy low P/E, sometimes high P/E; or low P/B or high P/B, but only if the value from growth is attractive. That is the focus of this book. Let’s understand what determines P/E and P/B. Again, we ask you to exercise your cognitive skills. That involves an appreciation of concepts we will expand upon as the book progresses. But at the core is the idea that investing is about buying future earnings growth and taking in the risk to that growth. An expensive P/E or P/B is one that overprices that growth or fails to recognize the risk to growth. Carry these key ideas with you. What Determines a P/E Ratio? P/E and P/B multiples are commonly seen as expectations of future earnings growth, with a high P/E or a high P/B called a growth stock. But that is not the full picture. We begin with the P/E ratio. Consider investment in a conventional bond: By the effective interest method of accounting, bond interest (earnings) is equal to the bond yield, and the bond yield indicates the risk of the bond and the required return that compensates for that risk. Thus, the expected forward earnings yield for one period ahead (the reciprocal of the forward P/E) is [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn04.png] where r is the required return (yield to maturity) and P 0 is the bond price. That is, the accounting tells us that the E/P (or P/E) ratio conveys information about risk. Could that also be the case for a stock? Well, there is a complication: A bond has no growth, but a stock can produce growth. Consider a simple pricing model for a stock: [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn05.png] and thus [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn06.png] The simple model only works with positive earnings and full payout of those earnings in dividends, but it serves to illustrate that there are two components to a P/E ratio: risk and growth, r and g . The g increases price and decreases E/P, but r decreases price (a discount for risk) and increases E/P. When buying growth, you are also taking on risk and P/E incorporates both. You might think that higher growth necessarily yields a higher price and thus a higher P/E ratio, and that is indicated by the negative sign on growth in the E/P ratio expression: growth adds to price. But beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky . If growth is risky, that requires a higher r in the model and that reduces the price, just as it does with a bond. Thus, a given P/E can be one with high g and high r , or low r and low g . There is some sorting out to do. Consider the following (numbers are from 2022): Johnson & Johnson (JNJ) trades with a forward P/E =15.8 (E/P = 6.3%) Is this a stock with average growth, or a stock with high growth but risky growth? Starbucks Corporation (SBUX) trades with a forward P/E = 27.6 (E/P = 3.6%) Is this a stock with high growth that is not very risky? Or is it overpriced? China Petroleum & Chemical Corporation (SNP) trades with a forward P/E = 5.6 (E/P = 17.9%) Is this a stock with low growth or high growth that is very risky? Or is it underpriced? Alibaba Group Holding Limited (BABA) trades at a forward P/E = 12.0 (E/P = 8.3%) We might think of Alibaba, the e-commerce giant of China, as having high growth prospects. But the P/E is only 12.0. Is this a stock with high earnings growth but growth that is very risky? Or is this stock underpriced? These are the questions that the value investor must sort out. Assuming a stock is underpriced might lead you into a trap. A common mistake in valuation is to insert a required return, r , in a valuation model—perhaps one delivered by the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM)—and then to add a growth rate. But that is a mistake. Growth and risk are intrinsically entwined; typically, a firm cannot add growth without adding risk. It is r − g that is important, not r and g as unrelated inputs. Adding g typically also means adding to r . Go to the action box on the next page where you will see that the average E/P is equal to the risk-free rate, the yield on the 10-year U.S. government bond. That is striking: on average, growth is just the risk premium such that r − g = risk-free rate . Growth adds to risk and not to price, so adding g does not change r − g . On May 31, 2023, the forward E/P for the S&P 500 stood at 5.49 percent and the one-year U.S. bond yield was almost the same, 5.25 percent. Value Investing in Action Buying Growth Is Typically Risky By the simple P/E model, [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn07.png] The required return is the risk-free rate plus a risk premium for risk borne: r = risk-free rate + risk premium Suppose, in the extreme, all growth is priced as risky such that g = risk premium . Then, r − g = risk-free rate + risk premium − risk premium = risk-free rate and [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn10.png] Now look at the E/P ratios for the S&P 500, the representative index for all U.S. stocks, at year end from 1962 to 2022: Mean Median Trailing P/E for the index 19.22% 17.96% Trailing E/P for the index 5.20% 5.57% Yield on the 10-year U.S. bond 5.81% 5.70% The average E/P for stocks is approximately equal to the risk-free rate. At the core, on average, all growth is priced as risky. This, of course, is just on average; E/P ratios (and P/E) vary widely relative to the bond yield. On May 31, 2023, with a P/E of 12.8 the S&P 600 small stock index had a yield of 7.81 percent, some 2.32 percent higher than the S&P 500 index. Small stocks typically have higher growth prospects but growth at risk, thus the higher yield and a lower P/E. That prompts the fundamental analysis question: For a given firm, does forecasted growth go into the price, increasing the P/E ratio, or does it just add risk? Should I pay for growth?
