The box on the next page comes back to the Teaser questions at the beginning of the chapter. What explains those P/E ratios? The Teasers P/E ratios can reflect mispricing, whether due to biased forecasts of future earnings growth or from mispricing the risk to that growth. However, before jumping to the mispricing interpretation, it’s best to consider why the pricing might be correct. So, let’s look at the analysts’ consensus estimates for earnings growth rates for the automobile companies below. Their forecasts are annual earnings growth rates (g) over the next five years. General Motors Ford Stellantis Volkswagen Honda Toyota P/E 4.29 6.33 2.79 4.16 7.41 9.60 Forecast g 1.42% 9.74% 1.58% 0.99% 13.20% 3.69% PEG 3.03 0.65 1.77 4.21 0.56 2.60 Tesla Ferrari P/E 65.79 44.05 Forecast g 26.00% 14.54% PEG 2.53 3.03 The low P/E firms have lower forecasted growth rates than the high P/E firms. Within low and high P/E firms, P/E ratios are typically higher with higher growth forecasts. However, Ford, Honda, and Toyota are a little out of line. It might be tempting to buy Ford and Honda where the P/E is low relative to the estimated growth rates, and sell Toyota. However, that could be a mistake. The other feature of a P/E ratio is missing: A P/E should be lower if price is discounted for the risk to growth. Ford and Honda may be firms with more growth, but growth with higher risk of not being realized. You see a PEG number under the growth rates. That is the P/E divided by the growth rate. This is the topic of the next section. (Note: We have assumed that analysts’ growth forecasts are those embedded in the market price and are unbiased. But analysts can be wrong.) The PEG Ratio, Growth and Risk Our Beware principle warns us about buying growth and requires an analysis built on that principle. That is for a later chapter, but there is a simple metric that contains the idea. In the teaser box above you see the measure called PEG. The PEG ratio, price-earnings-growth, is a common metric used to screen stocks. It compares the P/E to a growth forecast: [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn11.png] Rather than simply screening on P/E, the screen compares the P/E to a growth forecast. Growth forecasts are often taken as sell-side analysts’ five-year EPS growth rate. So, Johnson & Johnson, with a P/E of 15.8 and an EPS growth forecast of 9.9 percent per year, has a PEG ratio of [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn12.png] , while Starbucks’s PEG is [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn13.png] , and China Petroleum’s PEG = [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn14.png] . Baidu, Inc. (BIDU), prominent in Internet search engine services in China, has a P/E of 20.0 on growth expectations of 14.1 percent per year, so its PEG = 1.4. This is quite a range. As benchmarks, the average PEG for U.S. stocks in 2022 was 1.9, 1.7 for EU stocks, and 1.3 for UK stocks. The PEG calculation makes some sense: If the traded P/E ratio indicates growth, challenge the P/E with growth expectations. A low PEG indicates the P/E is low relative to growth expectations, so buy the stock; a high PEG indicates growth is overpriced. So, buy China Petroleum and Baidu (low PEG) and sell Starbucks (high PEG)? This is the standard interpretation of the PEG ratio. However, something is missing: Beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky . If the growth in the denominator of PEG is risky, the P/E should be lower if the price discounts for the risk. It is r as well as g that determines a P/E ratio. With a low PEG, you might think you are getting a cheap stock, but you are just buying risky growth that depresses the P/E. Case 2 (at the end of the chapter) makes the point vividly. To demonstrate, figure 2.1 tracks PEG ratios for ten portfolios formed on beta. The year is 2022, but the pattern is similar in other years. Beta is not a sufficient measure of risk for a value investor, but you see a pattern here: High beta, low PEG; low beta, high PEG. That is, the higher the beta risk, the lower PEG is. So, buying a low PEG that looks cheap is dangerous: The investor might just be buying risk. Another trap. [IMAGE: ../images/fig2-2.png] 2.2 Relationship between PEG and beta, March 2022. Source : Morningstar and Yahoo Finance What Determines a P/B Ratio? Whereas the P/E ratio prices earnings, the “bottom line” of the income statement, the P/B prices the book value of shareholders’ equity, the “bottom line” number in the balance sheet. As Equity = Assets − Liabilities by the balance sheet equation, it also prices the net assets on the balance sheet. What explains the pricing of these net assets? Price in the numerator of the P/B ratio prices the expected future earnings to come from these assets, so P/B is expected earnings from net assets relative to the net assets. That is the expected book rate of return, ROE, probably the most common accounting performance measure: [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn15.png] (If book value has changed significantly over the year, the denominator should be average book value over the year, for earnings are generated from book values over the entire year.) This makes sense: The pricing of book value depends on what the book value will earn. Higher expected earnings on book value, a higher ROE, demands a higher P/B. Table 2.2 reports the forward ROE for selected stocks along with their P/B ratios. Forward ROE is the forecast of next year’s earnings divided by current book value. The forecasted earnings here are analysts’ consensus forecasts. You see that high P/B has higher ROE, and P/B declines as ROE declines. Deutsche Bank (DB) has a forward ROE of only 5.6 percent and the corresponding P/B is 0.4. Case 2 at the end of this chapter demonstrates further. Table 2.2 P/B and return on equity (ROE) for selected stocks, March 2022 Stock Share price Book value P/B Forward ROE % Apple 158.52 4.24 37.4 145.3 Microsoft 285.89 20.57 13.9 42.3 Cisco Systems 54.71 9.43 5.8 36.5 Meta Platforms 195.21 42.44 4.6 29.3 Newell Brands 21.96 9.15 2.4 20.9 Alibaba 92.90 48.89 1.9 16.8 Bank of America 40.67 27.11 1.5 12.1 Citigroup 54.88 78.40 0.7 9.1 Deutsche Bank 10.74 26.85 0.4 5.6 However, that is not the full picture. Just as earnings growth in a P/E ratio is discounted for risk, so must forecasted ROE: The forecast might not be realized. That leads us to an important metric. Value-added Earnings on Book Value Expected ROE adds to book value only after it has been discounted for risk. That discount is incorporated in a measure called value-added ROE, where ROE adds value if it is higher than the required return (sometimes called the cost of equity): Value-added ROE t = ROE t − r ROE is the rate-of-return per dollar of book value, but value added depends also on the amount of book value (net assets) earning at the rate of return. That is a dollar earnings number, Value-added Earnings t = (ROE t − r) × Book Value t− 1 By simple math, Value-added Earnings t = Earnings t − (r × Book Value t- 1 ) Value-added earnings is more commonly referred to as residual earnings (or residual income ) because it is the residual in earnings after charging the book value (net assets) for the cost of capital. The measure says that value is added to book value only if the earnings earned on book value is higher than the required return. This, too, makes sense: A firm only adds value to its net assets if those net assets generate earnings that cover the required return for risk. So, charging the ROEs in table 2.2 with a required return and recognizing the amount of book value earning at that ROE yields the value-added earnings in table 2.3 . Here a required return of 10 percent is applied, but this will differ across firms according to their risk. Table 2.3 P/B, ROE, and value-added ROE for selected stocks, 2022 Stock Share price Book value P/B Forward ROE % Value-added earnings Apple 158.52 4.24 37.4 145.3 5.737 Microsoft 285.89 20.57 13.9 42.3 6.644 Cisco Systems 54.71 9.43 5.8 36.5 2.499 Meta Platforms 195.21 42.44 4.6 29.3 8.191 Newell Brands 21.96 9.15 2.4 20.9 0.997 Alibaba 92.90 48.89 1.9 16.8 3.325 Bank of America 40.67 27.11 1.5 12.1 0.569 Citigroup 54.88 78.40 0.7 9.1 −0.706 Deutsche Bank 10.74 26.85 0.4 5.6 −1.181 Note : The required return (cost of equity), r , is set at 10 percent. The value-added earnings for Apple is $(1.453 − 0.10) × 4.24 = $5.737 per share, while that for Deutsche Bank is ($0.056 − 0.10) × 26.85 = $–1.181 per share, and so on. Firms with P/B greater than 1.0 have positive value added while those such as Citigroup and Deutsche Bank with P/B less than 1.0 have negative value added: They are not covering the cost of capital. Now, with this discount for risk, you still see the P/B ranking value-added earnings. That is because both price in the P/B ratio and value-added ROE discount for risk. The ranking is not perfect, with Cisco Systems and Alibaba out of line. Of course, by applying the same required return, we have not allowed for differences in risk. And P/B is based on expected value-added earnings more than one year ahead. But the inconsistency raises a question: Is Cisco Systems overpriced and Alibaba underpriced? That is the absorbing question the value investor asks. The analysis here suggests one way of getting a handle on the question, which we will pursue: Is value-added ROE being priced correctly by the market? Value investors look for value added. We now have a measure for it, one that is risk-adjusted. So we are ready to go to the next chapter on value investing. But, to whet your appetite, consider the value investing action box. Value Investing in Action Newell Brands Inc. (NWL) traded at a P/B of 0.70 in 2018, yet the forward ROE was 15.6 percent. Do you find this interesting? Buy? Sell? China Eastern Airlines Co., Ltd. (CEA:NYSE) traded at a P/B of 1.02 in 2018 with a forward ROE of 10.7 percent. Does this look reasonably priced? In May 2022, General Motors Company (GM) traded at a P/B of 0.84 but with an ROE(ttm) of 16.03 percent. Do you find this interesting? Buy? Sell? In May 2022, the stock price of The Goodyear Tire & Rubber Company (GT) was down 40 percent over the past 12 months. It traded at a P/B of 0.64 with an ROE(ttm) of 19.93 percent. Do you find this interesting? Buy? Sell? In June 2022, Goldman Sachs Group, Inc. (GS) traded at a P/B of 1.01 with a 17.4 percent ROE(ttm). Do you find this interesting? Buy? Sell? These are just cases to prompt your interest. A full value analysis is necessary to reach an investment decision. And note: P/B can be low despite a high ROE because expected ROE is at risk, discounting the price. Keeping a Book Applying P/E and P/B as two separate screens fails partly because it does not keep a book, nor does it follow the thinking in the book. In an accounting book, earnings and book value are not independent features to function as two separate screens. Rather, they work together: In double-entry accounting , one cannot book earnings without also affecting book value (assets minus liabilities), so in evaluating a P/E ratio, one must also consider the effect on book value and the P/B ratio. For example, if an expense is booked to earnings but not paid for, a liability (accounts payable or an accrued expense liability) is recorded. If revenue is booked to earnings, the firm has an asset, accounts receivable or cash. However, if a customer pays cash but revenue is not recognized, a deferred revenue liability is recorded, netting out the increase in cash (no income, no change in the balance sheet). Double entry accounting is part of the structure of the accounting book introduced at the end of chapter 1 . This structure is also dictated by accounting equations that tie elements of the book together. As Book Equity = Assets − Liabilities, book value in the P/B ratio is determined by assets and liabilities and these accounts are, in turn, affected by earnings with double entry. That tells us: Keeping a book for valuation must involve both the income statement and balance sheet. That is a double-entry book. You will see how keeping a double-entry book is highly effective in accounting for value. The accounting system that served the Venetians so well for centuries also serves the contemporary value investor. We will delve into the properties of the accounting book as we proceed. For the moment, note that, with P/E and P/B considered together, the forward E/P multiplied by P/B yields the forward [IMAGE: ../images/ch02eqn19.png] . Now, that is interesting, for forward ROE is part of the value-added earnings measure above. Chapter 1 explained that measurement is also an important component of the book we are keeping. Given price, P/E and P/B are determined by the accounting for earnings and book value; they are accounting phenomena. Does the measurement of earnings and book value, and the resulting ROE, convey the fundamentals of growth, risk, and profitability underlying P/E and P/B? There is much to explore. The next chapter introduces you to the accounting book and begins to show how thinking by the book guides value investing. Value Investing Principles Chapter 1 laid down ten principles that guide value investing. As the book progresses, we will add to these principles. Here are those from this chapter. As with the principles in chapter 1 , these are principles to be honored for your investing to have integrity. If they are ignored, you are in danger of making a mistake. “Value versus Growth” investing is fuzzy thinking. A P/E multiple embeds both growth and the risk to growth. Beware of buying low P/E: You may be buying risky growth. A P/B multiple embeds both ROE and the risk to ROE. Beware of buying low P/B: You may be buying ROE at risk. Earnings and ROE add value only if they cover the required return. The Exercise Book For each chapter, you will find exercises in an Exercise Book on the website. These exercises will not only test your understanding of the material in the chapter but will show you how it is applied in practice. Learning by doing: By working through these exercises, the light will go on. Solutions to the exercises are available under a password for those who have access. The Case Book There are two cases in the Case Book that bring out the ideas in this chapter. Here are the introductions to the cases. Case 2: P/E, PEG, Growth and Risk Beware of Paying for Growth, for Growth Is Risky The stock market often gets excited about growth prospects, particularly in good times. Indeed, much of the market value of many stocks is based on earnings growth expectations. Value investors, however, are cautious about paying for growth. They understand that growth may not be realized; it can be eroded by competition. They challenge the market price: Is the growth expectation built into the market price worth paying for? The P/E ratio indicates earnings growth: price (in the numerator) is the expectation of future earnings, so a higher P/E indicates future earnings growth from current or forward earnings (in the denominator). The PEG ratio—the ratio of the forward P/E to a forecast of future earnings growth (sometimes also called the price/earnings-to-growth ratio)—is said to provide a challenge to the market’s growth expectation: If the P/E is high relative to a forecast of growth, the stock must be overvalued. So, if P/E = 10 and earnings growth forecast is 10 percent per year (for example), PEG = 1.0 and the stock is considered to be reasonably priced. But, if P/E = 20 with a 10 percent growth forecast, the PEG of 2.0 indicates overpricing. Is this the correct interpretation of a PEG ratio? Is PEG a good screen? This case asks you to consider this question. In doing so, take note of the fundamentalist dictum: Beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky . Case 3: The Fed Model Is It Any Good? In the late 1990s, investors experienced what came to be recognized as a price bubble, with the forward P/E for the S&P 500 reaching 33 at one point against an historical average of 15. Here are the returns for that index during those years: 1995 1996 1997 1998 1999 Return 37.6% 23.0% 33.4% 28.6% 21.0% Forward P/E (Dec 31) 17.3 19.5 23.1 30.6 32.1 Bubbles burst, as indicated by the subsequent returns for the S&P 500 (far worse for NASDAQ stocks): 2000 2001 2002 −9.1% −11.9% −22.1% The Federal Reserve (the Fed) works to control consumer price inflation, but it has often been accused of promoting asset price inflation; while money easing is designed to stimulate the economy, the increased money stimulates asset prices instead—and even bubbles (or so it is said). The Fed has resisted this charge, maintaining that controlling asset prices (rather than consumer prices) is outside its mandate. Nevertheless, when asset bubbles burst, there are real effects on the economy. In 1996, Alan Greenspan, then-Chair of the Federal Reserve, came out with his now-famous “irrational exuberance” speech, presumably to “talk down” stock prices in the context of the dot-com bubble: Stock prices are too high, he claimed, fed by investor fever—exuberance—that pushed prices above rational value. Fed by speculation, not by the Fed. His benchmark was the Fed Stock Valuation Model (FSVM), known simply as the Fed model, apparently used internally at the Federal Reserve to evaluate the general level of stock prices. The model is sometimes referred to as the “Greenspan Model” because of the Fed Chair’s 1998 speech. Is it any good? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.


===== xhtml/ch03.xhtml =====
Chapter 3 Keeping a Book for Value Investing Chapter 3 Keeping a Book for Value Investing Understand what you know and separate it from speculation: anchor a valuation on what you know T his chapter begins to design the accounting book for value investing. The book tells us what we know, what we can anchor on to challenge the speculation in market prices. The book embeds “good thinking” about investing, instructing the investor to follow that thinking. The traditional value investing principles of chapter 1 are part of that thinking, so the accounting book incorporates those principles. However, since the days of traditional value investing, “what we know” has been expanded by modern finance theory, so the principles of finance are also honored in designing the book. But first, some teasers. Teasers Dividends Are the Fundamentals Investors invest to get dividends. So, dividends are the fundamental to focus on in valuation. True? A firm that pays dividends is worth more than one that does not. True? Cash is king! So, value is based on the cash flow a firm can generate. Use a discounted cash flow (DCF) valuation model. Good advice? We begin by introducing some principles of modern finance, keeping in mind that good theory must be practical; it must lead us to an investing product that works, and works well. First, a foundational principle of modern finance: No arbitrage. The No-arbitrage Principle No arbitrage is a key principle in finance because all valuations must obey the principle. It is a key principle for value investors because it states that a “true” price, equal to value, is one that cannot be arbitraged. If copper trades in London and New York, no arbitrage implies that the price cannot be arbitraged to make a profit by buying at a lower price in London and selling at a higher price in New York; the same price in London and New York is a no-arbitrage price. That is simultaneous trading no-arbitrage. In value investing, no-arbitrage refers to informational arbitrage: A no-arbitrage price is one that cannot be arbitraged (to make a profit) by calculating another price with additional information or better analysis. The value investor endeavors to discover that price which, when compared to the traded price, indicates an arbitrage opportunity: She sees that investors setting the market price are overlooking information or do not apply the appropriate analysis of that information. The market efficiency doctrine says that market prices are no-arbitrage prices; an investor cannot find information to establish an alternative price that provides a profit opportunity. Thus, an efficient market price “incorporates all available information.” Valuation Models Modern finance goes the route of building formal mathematical models that incorporate good thinking based on the no-arbitrage principle. That comes from a desire to be formal and precise. A valuation model has the following form: Value = Forecast of payoffs in the short term, discounted for time and risk + Forecast of a terminal value, discounted for time and risk The discount rate = the risk-free rate (compensation for time) + a risk premium. The terminal value is a forecast for payoffs beyond the short term, captured by a growth rate, g . The no-arbitrage price for an investment is determined by the cash flows that the investment is expected to deliver. That comes from economic theory which says that, when making an investment, the investor gives up cash that could buy consumption (now) in hope of getting higher consumption later (say, for children’s education or for retirement). Future cash flows buy that future consumption, so the investor buys future cash flows and pays a price for them. However, the investor understands that those cash flows are at risk; the cash flows and thus the consumption they buy might not materialize. She thus discounts them for risk, reducing the price she is willing to pay for the investment. The no-arbitrage price is that discounted price. However, models must be viewed with some skepticism. Financial economists are sometimes accused of “physics envy” by trying to get the mathematical precision of the formulas in physics. But that can be false precision: The concept of future prospects and particularly of continued growth in the future invites the application of formulas out of higher mathematics to establish the present value of the favored issue. But the combination of precise formulas with highly imprecise assumptions can be used to establish, or rather justify, practically any value one wishes, however high, for a really outstanding issue. –Benjamin Graham, The Intelligent Investor, 4th ed., p.315 Some of the worst business decisions I’ve ever seen are those with future projections and discounts back. It seems like the higher mathematics with more false precision should help you but it doesn’t. They teach that in business schools because, well, they’ve got to do something. –Charlie Munger, 2012 Berkshire Hathaway annual meeting Models are important: Imagine the meteorologist forecasting the path of a storm without a model or an epidemiologist forecasting the spread of a virus without a model. Those models must be built on the good thinking of the relevant science. That is also true of a valuation model, otherwise the inputs would only be a meaningless string of numbers. But models must not be taken literally. This is particularly true of valuation models, as the quotes above point out. But this also applies to meteorological and epidemiological models, as weather forecasters well know and as the experts studying the COVID pandemic in its early stages acknowledged. A small error in a model can have a large effect, sometimes called the butterfly effect. We will see that in modeled growth rates about which Graham and Munger are so skeptical. Rather, models serve as an efficient guide to our good thinking. We learn from models without been seduced by their seeming precision. View valuation models as such, and ask whether they contain good thinking. That good thinking is in conformity to the fundamental principles of chapter 1 , the principles of modern finance, and sound accounting principles. However, even with these principles, a model must be practical. It must work! We have referred to the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) as a precise formula for the required return, but it does not work in practice. And so we will see with some valuation models. So let’s embark on an exploration of standard valuation models. Are they based on good thinking? Do they work? These are important questions for our endeavor, for a valuation model directs the design of the book on which value investing is based. The Dividend Discount Model For an equity investment, the cash flows that buy consumption are the dividends to be received. The Dividend Discount Model (DDM) captures the idea. Indeed, it expresses the idea concisely, but one must be careful of a model, as always. The DDM for the no-arbitrage price (now, at time 0) is [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn02.png] The numerators are the expected dividends for periods ahead, with the ellipsis indicating these continue for a very long time for a going-concern business. The denominators are the risk discounting at the investor’s required return, r , for bearing the risk. (This can vary each period, but let’s leave that complication aside.) There is one thing to keep in mind as we proceed. Valuation theory is about buying future dividends for consumption. Investors might buy a firm for other reasons—for the joy of running a firm, or to pursue environmental, sustainability and governance objectives to save the planet. In theory terms, these investors have other features in their utility functions. This is particularly so for managers of firms, which is OK if they own the firm. But other investors in the firm must be on the lookout if managers pursue these objectives in conflict with the investors’ desire to save (for consumption in retirement, say). That is called the agency problem or the stewardship issue in investing. Accordingly, we look to the accounting book to provide measures that monitor management in conjunction with other governance mechanisms. Keeping in mind Graham’s and Munger’s warnings, is the DDM a good model for value investing? Dividend Irrelevance and the Dividend Conundrum You might respond to this question by observing that the model requires us to forecast very far into the future, and forecasting the long term is very difficult. With the average annual dividend yield of 2.5 percent of price, one would have to forecast dividends for well over 40 years to explain the price. The extreme case is the firm that currently pays no dividends, although it will do so in the long run, perhaps even in liquidation. It was many years before Apple, Microsoft, Cisco Systems, and many more well-known companies started paying dividends. Berkshire Hathaway has never paid dividends but that surely has not stopped investors from buying it. We have a practical problem: Forecasts for a distant future are highly uncertain, not something to hang a secure valuation on. That’s the speculation that we seek to avoid. Graham’s and Munger’s skepticism is justified here. However, there is another problem even if the firm pays dividends. The Nobel prize winners Miller and Modigliani (M&M) showed that, up to the liquidating dividend (in the very long run), dividends don’t matter; more dividends now just means lower dividends later, and vice versa, with the present value of dividends in the DDM, Value 0 , unaffected by the timing of the dividends. This is the dividend irrelevance principle , the foundation of M&M dividend theory. Another way of saying it: If a firm pays a dollar of dividends, the price drops by a dollar because the dollar is no longer in the firm. You see this with the calculation for the dollar stock return: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn03.png] That is, the dollar return is the price change plus the dividend. If Dividend t increases, Price t decreases by the same amount to leave Stock Return t unchanged. Berkshire Hathaway pays no dividends; no matter. Nor (as of 2023) do Meta Platforms (Facebook), Alphabet (Google), Alibaba, Baidu; no matter. If they do, no matter: The shareholder is no better or worse off; the dollar is no longer in the firm, but it is in his pocket. Apple, Microsoft, and Cisco Systems did not pay dividends for 30 years, but no matter: They generated value. The 0.55 percent dividend yield that Apple now pays (in 2023) is irrelevant; the price, cum dividend, would be the same if the dividend yield were the 2.78 percent that Cisco Systems now pays. On this point, traditional value investors were wrong; they believed a firm is more valuable if it pays a dividend. M&M corrected them. The first and second Teaser questions are answered. We have a conundrum called the dividend conundrum : In theory, price is based on expected dividends, but dividends don’t matter! A model that is correct in theory is useless in practice! That has lessons for the value investor: Don’t use this model. Dividends are not the fundamental for pricing. Dividends are the distribution of value, not the generation of value. They are not the valuation anchor. We need to look to something more fundamental to anchor on, an accounting for the generation of value. To do so, we need to keep a book. You might respond: But what if the shareholder needs cash from the investment? Well, he can sell some of the stock to get the desired cash, with the same effect as the dividend: Higher cash, lower price. This is called making homemade dividends. If the shareholder does not want the dividend paid, she can buy the stock with the dividend. The dividend is foregone, but her claim on future dividends is increased. In either case the investor is no better off with or without the dividend. Of course, this requires a liquid stock market to be able to trade the stock, an assumption of the M&M analysis. For a non-traded family firm, a dividend might matter for the family has to be able to eat. (That, incidentally, illustrates the importance of capital markets: Shareholders can generate the cash flows they need for consumption without damaging the business by taking cash from it.) Of course, dividends might be correlated with something that really matters, and it is that “something” we need to focus on rather than dividends. Dividend stocks are sometimes less risky stocks, but that is due to underlying fundamental risk, not the dividend. In 2022 when the Dow Jones Industrial Average index fell 6.9 percent, the top 10 percent of dividend-paying stocks in the index—the so-called Dogs of the Dow—rose 2.2 percent (including dividends). Dividends are viewed as conveying information about stable earnings: Stock prices are hit if a firm reduces its dividends, so maintaining or increasing the dividend can be an indication of the firm’s ability to generate future earnings to pay future dividends. This is the so-called signaling theory of dividends, which involves thinking of dividends as information, not as value creation. That information is in the fundamentals revealed by an accounting for value. Firms can run Ponzi schemes in dividends: Issue stock to the gullible or borrow, then pay out the proceeds to the gullible without any real value creation. Accounting by the book is a check on the Ponzi scheme. The dividend irrelevance principle also has lessons for those buying stocks offering dividend yield. See the following action box. Value Investing in Action Chasing Dividend Yield For 15 years after the financial crisis of 2008, interest rates on U.S. government bonds were low, less than 2 percent for the 10-year bond. This was in part due to the great price manipulator in the sky, the U.S. Federal Reserve Bank, pegging interest rates. The Fed’s action tempted investors to find stocks with a higher dividend yield, Dividend/Price, than the bond yield: The humble dividend is reclaiming its rightful place as the arbiter of stock-market value . . . To investors desperate for income, the argument for buying equities is, well, duh. Who wouldn't want a higher income? James Mackintosh, The Wall Street Journal , May 9, 2016 The commentator is directing investors to buy high dividend yield. Indeed, fundamentalists used to advocate high dividend stocks: A bird in the hand is worth two in the bush, they said. But M&M dividend theory revised that advice: A firm cannot create value by writing checks to shareholders. If writing checks increased stock prices, that would be a Ponzi scheme—and Ponzi schemes ultimately collapse. China Petroleum & Chemical Corporation (SNP) paid a high annual dividend yield of 9.15 percent. On June 12, 2023, the share price dropped by 6.2 percent on one day. Oh no! What a hit! Well, no, in fact: The semiannual dividend went ex on that day, so the price dropped by approximately the amount of the semiannual dividend yield, as shareholders no longer were entitled to the dividend. The dividend and the price drop canceled each other out. It is all well and good to be cynical about dividends, but what should guide a firm’s dividend policy? The next box takes up the issue. Dividend Policy When Should a Firm Pay Dividends? “Dividends don’t matter” is a good mantra to keep in mind when valuing firms. But firms do pay dividends, so what determines payout? A firm pays dividends for the following reasons: A firm pays dividends when it has accumulated cash from operations but has no investment prospects for the cash. It can invest in interest-bearing deposits or T-bills. But that does not add value (as we will see). The firm should pay out the cash to shareholders who might have investment opportunities that the firm does not have. The shareholder might also invest it in T-bills. There is no difference between shareholders investing in T-bills or the firm investing in T-bills on their behalf; the shareholder is no better or worse off by having the cash directly. But the shareholder might have other opportunities for this money. Active investors have been known to force a dividend when they see they have investment opportunities that the firm does not or cannot exploit. A firm pays a dividend when it has particular shareholders who require cash dividends. This is called the clientele effect on dividends. Most shareholders can generate homemade dividends if a firm does not pay dividends, but some are constrained. University endowments and other nonprofit organizations require cash for their operations but can be restricted in selling off their endowments. Of course, in paying these dividends, the firm should not sacrifice value-added investments; rather, they should borrow against the value of those investments to do so. Regrettably, a private firm may also be forced to pay dividends if shareholders require cash but there is no market in which shares can be traded to make homemade dividends. If this means the firm must forgo value-adding investment, that’s a real shame: Value lost. Dividends or Stock Repurchases? Cash can be passed out to shareholders with stock repurchases rather than dividends. What determines the choice? If the personal tax rate on dividends is higher than that on capital gains, a stock repurchase is more advantageous for the shareholders. If the firm wishes to make a cash distribution and it assesses that the stock is below value, it should choose a stock repurchase: Buying stock cheap adds value (see chapter 5 ). But to the shareholders opting to have their stock repurchased: Beware. Your value is being eroded. Accounting for Value: Cash Accounting Accounting reports a statement that accounts for the cash flows during a reporting period. Table 3.1 reports the 2023 Cash Flow Statement for Starbucks Corporation but with only the summary totals for each section of the statement. Table 3.1 Summary cash flow statement for Starbucks Corporation, 2023 ($millions) Cash flow from operations 6,472 Cash investments 2,261 Free cash flow 4,211 Cash in financing activities 3,464 Change in cash 747 As you see, this accounting explains the change in the cash account. Cash Flow from Operations is the net cash from running the business; that is, cash from customers net of cash paid out to suppliers. Cash Investment is cash expended to invest in business assets like property, plant, and equipment (PPE), sometimes referred to as CapEx (capital expenditures). The difference is called Free Cash Flow, labeled as such because it is cash that is “free” (after fresh investment in the business) to distribute to debt and equity investors, recorded in the third section of the statement, Cash in Financing Activities. Or, if there is still surplus cash after doing that, it is added to Cash in the bottom line of the statement. Free Cash Flow can be negative with Cash Investment greater that Cash Flow from Operations. In that case, the firm must get the cash out of the cash account or from fresh infusions of cash by debtholders or shareholders reported in the Cash in Financing section of the statement. 1 1 . The cash flow statements under U.S. GAAP and IFRS don’t quite honor these clean distinctions. For example, they typically classify cash paid for interest as cash flow from operations rather than a financing cash flow. Bad accounting. IFRS allows some flexibility in the classification. Cash accounting for value focuses on these cash flows. Instead of accounting with the cash dividends distributed, it uses the cash flows generated within the firm that can pay the dividends. The formal model, the Discounted Cash Flow model (DCF), is quite familiar and commonly used. It is the basic model taught in the business schools that Charlie Munger derides: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn04.png] The discount rate here, r Firm , is the required return for the firm. This is because the risk for the firm is different from that for equity, which has added risk from leverage. You may have seen it as the weighted average cost of capital (WACC), referring to the method for calculating it. The present value of expected free cash flow gives the value of the firm, but that must be shared with debtholders. So the value of the net debt is subtracted to yield the value of equity. As we will see in chapter 5 , the debt amount is net debt because debt obligations net against debt assets held. Accountants do a pretty good job of reporting the value of the net debt, and that is available on the balance sheet. Again, we have a long horizon forecasting problem. In practice, this is (supposedly) accommodated with a finite horizon “terminal value,” sometimes called a “continuing value”: Forecast free cash flow (FCF) for T years ahead, then add a continuing value to capture the value for years after T. So, for T = 4, [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn05.png] The final term is the continuing value. The g in that term is the rate at which FCF 4 is forecasted to grow subsequently (and g = 0 is no forecasted growth). You see that this really puts nothing on the table: The problem of forecasting the long run is just replaced with the problem of determining a growth rate, g , that gives us the value from the long run, and a long-run g is just as indeterminate as the long-run cash flows. This is the “continued growth in the future” inserted into valuation formulas that Graham was skeptical about in the first quote in this chapter. With his skepticism about forecasting the long run, John Maynard Keynes quipped: “The long run is a misleading guide to current affairs; in the long run we are all dead.” There will be a final “judgment day” but waiting for judgment day is a long wait. The American singer Prince chimed in: “Forever is a mighty long time.” That applies here, with emphasis. Free Cash Flow Is Not Value Added However, a deeper issue lurks below the surface: FCF does not capture value added. Look at the free cash flows for the firms in table 3.2 . Table 3.2 Free cash flow, earnings, and share prices for selected firms ($millions except per share) Netflix, Inc. 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 Cash from operations 8,020 10,363 11,029 14,206 18,095 Cash investment 9,979 13,217 14,170 12,285 18,543 Free cash flow (1,959) (2,854) (3,141) 1,921 (448) Earnings 559 1,211 1,867 2,761 5,116 Price per share 270 340 499 521 602 General Electric Co. 2000 2001 2002 2003 2004 Cash from operations 30,009 39,398 34,848 36,102 36,484 Cash investment 37,699 40,308 61,227 21,843 38,414 Free cash flow (7,690) (910) (26,379) 14,259 (1,930) Earnings 12,375 13,684 14,118 15,002 16,593 Price per share 399 288 196 281 267 Netflix, Inc. (NFLX) has been at the forefront of streaming video to households, growing rapidly over the years with increasing cash flows from operations. Those cash flows surely add value. But, at the same time, the firm has been investing in what it calls streaming content assets: buying licenses for films and video and making expenditures on production of original content. Those assets stood at $30.9 billion on its 2021 balance sheet. That requires considerable cash investment, resulting in negative free cash flows in table 3.2 , except in 2020. Here is the issue with free cash flow: Investment is typically seen as generating value, but the free cash flow calculation treats it as a “negative,” penalizing the (value-adding) cash flow component of measure that is generated in operations. Investment reduces free cash flow and the liquidation of investments increases it. That is perverse. You see in the table that, while Netflix had negative free cash flows, its stock price was rising. The market was not treating negative free cash flow as a loss of value; quite the opposite, in fact. The perversity of the free cash flow measure is further demonstrated in table 3.2 with General Electric (GE) back in its better days, from 2000 to 2004. The firm was investing heavily during this period, generating large negative free cash flows while maintaining its stock price. The exception was 2003 when it had a positive free cash flow of $14.259 billion. But you can see that this was due to a drop in investment; this was the year the firm planned to acquire Honeywell International Inc., but then was denied by European Union competition regulators. The measure of free cash flow is perverse: Lower investment, higher free cash flow. The third Teaser question is answered. The DCF model works in theory and yields the same valuation as the DDM, provided FCF is forecasted for the very long run. That is because the investments for Netflix and GE will eventually (and hopefully) generate cash inflow. But that “hopefully” is troublesome. Changing hope to value from the long run is the issue; in the long run when we are all dead. To get those long-run cash flows, firms must expend cash in the short run. Some value is calculated for a firm with positive cash flows in the near term, albeit typically only a small part of the value. Most of the calculated value rides on the continuing value calculation, and that is a high percentage of the calculation. Starbucks Corporation generates high free cash flow but, if you worked case 1 in chapter 1 , you will have seen that a forecast of free cash flow for three years into the future captures little of the value: Over 95 percent of the value was in the continuing value. Prices can’t be negative so, when estimating the value of Netflix or GE, negative free cash flows mean that over 100 percent of the value is in the speculative continuing value. Not something to anchor a secure valuation on. The continuing value is determined by the inserted growth rate, g , which is highly uncertain and very speculative. If we are challenging the speculation in market price, this will not work. Beware of paying for growth for growth is risky. Anchor a valuation on what you know and not on speculation. The problem is particularly acute with growing companies. Walmart Inc. (WMT), with its continual expansion throughout the world, reported negative free cash flows for its first 40 years. The Home Depot, Inc. (HD), the expanding warehouse retailer, also did so up to 2000. We seek to value the growth that generates value, but DCF methods leave us hanging because investment to get that growth decreases free cash flow. Strange, this, for cash is what we want! But it’s cash that the firm can ultimately generate that we want and free cash flow in any one period does not tell us much about that. Cash flow from operations, the first number in the free cash flow calculations, is sometimes promoted as a performance measure rather than accrual accounting earnings. It sounds good: Who does not like more cash? But it is a dangerous number. See the vignette. A Vignette Cash Flow for Snap Inc. Snap Inc. (SNAP) offers Snapchat, a camera application that allows users to communicate visually through short videos and images. The speculative stock traded with high valuations but continually reported losses. For 2021, the company reported a much-heralded positive cash flow from operations for the first time, $292.9 million, up from a negative $167.6 million for 2020. But that ignored many non-cash expenses, including $1,092.1 million of stock compensation expense. The net loss in the income statement that included these expenses was $488.0 million. Operating cash flow ignores costs that the shareholder must incur to add value. Being cash, the measure is often touted as a quality number, but that is suspect. A company converting cash compensation to non-cash stock options increases cash flow but not profits. And cash flow ignores the cost in depreciation of plants, pension obligations, and much more. Beware. Keeping a Cash Book The cash book is a double-entry book delivering a cash flow statement and a balance sheet. The bottom line of the cash flow statement is the change in cash, and the balance sheet has only the cash account, nothing more. The two are joined together under a structural relation (think of your monthly reconciliation of your checking account explained by deposits and withdrawals): [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn06.png] And, with a balancing balance-sheet equation, [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn07.png] Shareholders’ equity is given by the amount of cash in the firm, and the change in equity is the change in cash. An expanded cash book keeps track of debtholders’ and shareholders’ share of cash: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn08.png] Is free cash flow value to be added to equity? As debt is typically larger than the cash balance, equity is typically negative. Does this look like good accounting for value? Accounting for Value: Accrual Accounting Nonprofits and governments generally use cash accounting. That is a problem because there is no balance sheet to formally track government debt and pension obligations for which the “shareholders,” the taxpayers, are on the hook. No wonder some politicians don’t care about the national debt they are adding to; there is no formal book to discipline them, to hold them to account. Politicians sometimes see debt as revenue (to cover current expenditure)! However, business firms use accrual accounting. That produces a balance sheet along with an income (profit or loss) statement. In table 3.1 , free cash flows are negative but Netflix’s and GE’s earnings from the income statement are positive and align with stock prices. Could earnings be a better measure of value added than free cash flow? Accounting principles tell us the answer is yes. Among those principles are the structural equations that govern how the accounting is done; they must be honored, otherwise we are in error. One such “good thinking” equation adjusts free cash flow to earnings from operations (before interest), often called enterprise income or Operating Income (OI): [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn09.png] and [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn10.png] Operating income includes cash flow from operations, the “good stuff” in free cash flow. But it adds back cash investment, the offending “bad stuff”: Cash investment does not affect earnings. In addition, earnings add so-called accruals. These are value added (or lost) that is not a cash flow. So, if sales are made on credit rather than cash, they are deemed to add value and are thus accrued to the income statement as revenue. If wages, rent, insurance, and other expenses are incurred but not yet paid for, they are accrued to the income statement. Income is thus a value-added measure: value received in revenues over value given up in expenses. Financial statements for U.S.-listed firms can be searched through the Electronic Data Gathering, Analysis, and Retrieval (EDGAR) system on the following Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) web page: https://www.sec.gov/edgar/searchedgar/companysearch.html . Table 3.3 presents Netflix’s 2021 income statement from its annual 10-K filing. Revenue of $29,698 million is netted against $23,503 million of operating expenses to report $6,195 million in operating income. We will examine the integrity of the accounting involved later, but you can see the principle: Netflix is keeping a book on value added from trading with customers (value gained) and suppliers (value given up). The resulting operating income number is quite different from the free cash flow number for 2021 in table 3.2 . If you go to the 2021 Cash Flow Statement, you will see the cash investment and added accruals that explain the difference between income and free cash flow. (Strangely, Netflix records its investment in streaming assets as cash flow from operations rather than cash investment.) Table 3.3 Netflix, Inc. income statement for 2021 CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS OF OPERATIONS - USD ($) shares in thousands, $ in thousands 12 months ended Dec 31, 2021 Dec 31, 2020 Dec 31, 2019 Income statement [abstract] Revenues $ 29,697,844 $ 24,996,056 $ 20,156,447 Cost of revenues 17,332,683 15,276,319 12,440,213 Marketing 2,545,146 2,228,362 2,652,462 Technology and development 2,273,885 1,829,600 1,545,149 General and administrative 1,351,621 1,076,486 914,369 Operating income 6,194,509 4,585,289 2,604,254 Other income (expense) Interest expense (765,620) (767,499) (626,023) Interest and other income (expense) 411,214 (618,441) 84,000 Income before income taxes 5,840,103 3,199,349 2,062,231 Provision for income taxes (723,875) (437,954) (195,315) Net income $ 5,116,228 $ 2,761,395 $ 1,866,916 Earnings per share Basic (in USD per share) $ 11.55 $ 6.26 $ 4.26 Diluted (in USD per share) $ 11.24 $ 6.08 $ 4.13 Weighted-average common shares outstanding Basic (in shares) 443,155 440,922 437,799 Diluted (in shares) 455,372 454,208 451,765 While this accounting corrects the free cash flow measure to capture value added, it also has the property of bringing the recognition of value forward in time: Earnings are usually recognized earlier than the cash flows. The costs of investment are not recognized immediately, but as investments depreciate over time. That yields earnings typically higher than free cash flow. Revenue from sales on credit is recognized before cash is received, expenses before they are paid for. If a firm pays its employees with a pension after they retire, DCF valuation requires us to forecast 30 or so years ahead to include the cash flow. Accrual accounting recognizes the wage expense immediately. Further, accrual accounting recognizes expenses for which there are no cash flows (ever), as with stock compensation. With this earlier recognition of value added, there is less weight on a terminal value in a valuation. In generating an income statement, the accounting also generates a balance sheet with more than the cash account on it. Investment in inventory and plant is not booked to the income statement but rather as an asset on the balance sheet. This is an asset to generate future revenue, not an expense for calculating value added from current revenues. So too for investment in plant and equipment. Further, the accruals to measure value added in the income statement are booked to the balance sheet; revenue from credit sales is booked as an Accounts Receivable and expenses not yet paid for are booked as Wages Payable, Accounts Payable, Accrued Expense Liabilities or a Pension Liability. An additional accrual is recognized as assets depreciate, reducing both assets on the balance sheet and recording Depreciation Expense in the income statement. Time to get back to the text for your introductory accounting class! Table 3.4 reports Netflix’s 2021 balance sheet. Under cash accounting, only the cash account appears on a balance sheet. But accrual accounting adds Netflix’s Content Assets and Property, Plant, and Equipment (PPE) that are part of Cash Investment in the Free Cash Flow calculation. These are designated “net” because they are after accumulated depreciation of the PPE and amortization of Content Assets (accruals) against the revenue they produce. The remaining items result from assets and liability accruals for recording revenues and expenses. Table 3.4 Netflix, Inc. balance sheet at December 31, 2021 CONSOLIDATED BALANCE SHEETS – USD in thousands Dec. 31, 2021 Dec. 31, 2020 Current assets: Cash and cash equivalents $ 6,027,804 $ 8,205,550 Other current assets 2,042,021 1,556,030 Total current assets 8,069,825 9,761,580 Content assets, net 30,919,539 25,383,950 Property and equipment, net 1,323,453 960,183 Other non-current assets 4,271,846 3,174,646 Total assets 44,584,663 39,280,359 Current liabilities: Current content liabilities 4,292,967 4,429,536 Accounts payable 837,483 656,183 Accrued expenses and other liabilities 1,449,351 1,102,196 Deferred revenue 1,209,342 1,117,992 Short-term debt 699,823 499,878 Total current liabilities 8,488,966 7,805,785 Non-current content liabilities 3,094,213 2,618,084 Long-term debt 14,693,072 15,809,095 Other non-current liabilities 2,459,164 1,982,155 Total liabilities 28,735,415 28,215,119 Commitments and contingencies Stockholders’ equity: Preferred stock, $0.001 par value; 10,000,000 shares authorized at December 31, 2021 and December 31, 2020; no shares issued and outstanding at December 31, 2021 and December 31, 2020 0 0 Common stock, 0.001 par value; 4,990,000,000 shares authorized at December 31, 2021 and December 31, 2020; 443,963,107 and 442,895,261 issued and outstanding at December 31, 2021 and December 31, 2020, respectively 4,024,561 3,447,698 Treasury stock at cost (1,564,478 shares at December 31, 2021) (824,190) 0 Accumulated other comprehensive income (loss) (40,495) 44,398 Retained earnings 12,689,372 7,573,144 Total stockholders’ equity 15,849,248 11,065,240 Total liabilities and stockholders’ equity $ 44,584,663 $ 39,280,359 Accounting for value with both income statements and balance sheets is exhibited with the accounting for inventory. Under cash accounting, the cash expenditures to produce or buy inventory reduce free cash flow. But, under accrual accounting, such expenditures are booked as inventory on the balance sheets. Then, as inventory is sold (and no longer an asset), the asset is reduced to become cost of goods sold to be matched against sales of the inventory. That yields the value added from selling inventory. Accounting for value. Keeping a Book In contrast to the cash book, the accrual accounting book is structured as follows: Equity t = Equity t−1 + Earnings t − Net Dividends t This equation explaining the change in equity is sometimes called the clean-surplus equation. Value-adding earnings are added to equity and dividends are paid out of equity. The net dividend is cash dividends plus share repurchases less cash invested by shareholders with share issues. Now add the balance sheet equation, Equity t = Assets t − Liabilities t The equity is simultaneously explained by assets net of liability claims on the assets. Double entry accounting for value. Thinking by the Book This accrual accounting system tells us how to think about value: The balance sheet reports the book value (net assets) that generate value. The income statement reports value added from these assets. Valuation involves both the balance sheet and the income statement. The book has four features: First, the accounting book tracks the shareholders’ equity over time. That is what interests the value investor. Second, shareholders’ equity is updated by the value added each period with earnings. That tells us: To think about value, think about earnings to be added to shareholders’ equity: Value = Balance Sheet Value + Value Added in the Income Statement Third, the generation of value (earnings) is separated from the distribution of value (net dividends). Never are the two mixed. Isn’t that good? Dividends are irrelevant to value and that is just how the accounting works! Dividends are paid out of book value so, just as dividends reduce equity price dollar for dollar, they reduce equity book value dollar for dollar, with no effect on earnings. In short, the accounting recognizes dividend irrelevance to value generation. So, by investing by the book, you are avoiding the mistake of valuing dividends. That’s built in. Fourth, the equations embed a further feature of accounting, double-entry accounting : One cannot record earnings in the income statement, revenue and expenses, without affecting the balance sheet assets and liabilities. So, the value-added measure yields assets that project further future earnings and the liabilities that the shareholders are obliged to satisfy in gaining value added. For example, a pension expense recognized in the income statement requires the complementary recognition of a pension liability in the balance sheet. This an important principle: Valuation involves both an income statement and a balance sheet. The governing accounting equations are part of the accounting principles for keeping a book on value. To you at this point, they might just be words, one-liners. Their importance will become clearer as we proceed with investing by the book. We will hark back to them continually. We leave one issue aside for the moment. These accounting equations do not tell us how assets, liabilities, and value-added earnings (and thus shareholders’ equity) are measured. This is the issue of the quality of the accounting. In the next few chapters, we will use U.S. GAAP and IFRS accounting and will then examine the quality of that accounting in chapters 9 and 10 . Now, can we put this thinking into a valuation model? Valuation by the Book: Anchoring on the Balance Sheet The principles at the beginning of this chapter tell us to anchor on what we know rather than the speculation of DCF analysis. The accountant has already done some accounting for value in the book value number on the balance sheet. So, Anchor on the book value. Then add value that is not yet included in book value. By the clean surplus relation above, that is earnings forecasted to be added to the balance sheet in the future. However, those earnings are not yet realized; they are still at risk. So, Discount those earnings for risk at the investor’s required return, r . The risk-discounted earnings are the residual earnings (or residual income ) of chapter 2 : Earnings are charged with a required rate of return applied to book value. Investment must cover the required return, so residual earnings are those in excess of earnings from book value (net assets) earning at the required return: Residual Earnings t = Earnings t − (r × Book Value t− 1 ) = (ROE t − r) × Book Value t− 1 The future residual earnings are the “earnings power” that traditional value investors are advised to look for, but now with a discount for risk. These ideas with their three steps can be captured in a model: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn15.png] B indicates book value as in the P/B ratio. Indeed, the model explains that the difference between the (no-arbitrage) price and book value is the present value of expected residual earnings. 2 Stating the model in terms of expected ROE = Earnings t /Book Value t −1 , 2 . With a valuation based on annual periods, the initializing book value is the last annual number. The forward year is that for the current fiscal year in progress so, if the forecast is after the first quarter of the year, earnings includes quarterly earnings already published and may include as much as three-quarters of earnings. Strictly speaking, any dividend paid quarterly or half-yearly should be reinvested (at the risk-free rate) for the remainder of the year and added to the earnings for the year, but this is usually a small amount that can be ignored. [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn16.png] You can see the balance sheet and income statement working together to give the value. This is a no-arbitrage valuation, provided the investor exploits all information about future earnings and book value. That is, it yields the same value as the DDM if one were to forecast all future dividends up to liquidation. Further, it embeds the no-arbitrage theory of dividends: Dividends reduce book value (the first term in the model) one-for-one just as they do price, but dividends do not affect residual income, the value-added measure. We have a valuation that is dividend irrelevant ! We have a valuation that is consistent with the theory of valuation but is practical. We have finessed the dividend conundrum. We spare you the mathematical proofs, but you can proceed with the assurance that you are on a sound theoretical basis. We can now state some further principles of value investing: If a firm is forecasted to earn an ROE equal to the required return, it must be worth its book value: Price = Book Value. If a firm is forecasted to earn an ROE greater than the required return, it must be worth more than its book value: Price > Book Value. If a firm is forecasted to earn an ROE less than the required return, it must be worth less than book value: Price < Book Value. We saw these principles explaining the P/B ratios in table 2.3 of the last chapter. They are always true: You can anchor on them. If you forecast that a stock will earn an ROE above your required return consistently in the future but it trades at P/B < 1.0, it must be a BUY. If a stock trades at P/B > 1.0, but your analysis sees it earning an ROE less than your required return, it must be a SELL. Of course, the accounting for book value and earnings must be “good” accounting, an issue to which we will return. However, we still must forecast the long run (represented by the ellipsis in the formulas above). We often have information about earnings in the near term, for the forward year and perhaps two years ahead. Indeed, analysts typically give point estimates of earnings for the next two years (but no more). To exploit that information, set up the model with a continuing value: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn17.png] Ah, you say, we still have the speculative g to deal with. Yes, and indeed we will broach it in the next chapter. For now, let’s see how this valuation works for Netflix in the following box. Value Investing in Action Accrual Accounting Valuation for Netflix (NFLX) March 15, 2022 Netflix, the early leader in video streaming to households, was priced for a number of years with so much speculation that value investors avoided it. However, with increasing competition from the likes of HBO Max, Apple TV, Amazon Prime Video, and Disney+, subscriber growth declined in 2022 from 24 percent per year from 2016 to 2021. This is an example of speculative growth challenged by competition. The stock price stood at $343.75 on March 15, 2022, down 42 percent from the beginning of that year and 50 percent down from its October 2021 high. Was that a good time to BUY? The value investor Bill Ackman and his Pershing Square Capital Management fund thought so, buying heavily in early 2022. Netflix reported a book value of $15,849 million for 2021 with 443.963 million shares outstanding (in table 3.4 ). Book value per share (BPS) was thus $35.70, so its P/B was 9.63. The pro forma below that starts with the BPS at the end of 2021 then adds the consensus earnings per share (EPS) forecasted by sell-side analysts for 2022 and 2023 and the indicated dividend per share (DPS) of zero for those years. Let’s set your hurdle rate to cover your risk at 8 percent and calculate a value based on these inputs. Required return = 8 percent; Price = $343.75. Source of analysts’ forecasts : Yahoo Finance, March 15, 2022. 2021A 2022E 2023E EPS 11.01 14.40 DPS 0.00 0.00 BPS ** 35.70 * 46.71 61.11 ROE *** 30.84% 30.83% Residual earnings (8%) **** 8.154 10.663 [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn18.png] * Book Value per Share (BPS) = Book Value/Shares Outstanding. Shares Outstanding = Issued Shares − Shares in Treasury. ** BPS t = BPS t−1 + EPS t DPS t *** ROE t = EPS t /BPS t−1 **** Residual Earnings t = Earnings t − (r × Book Value t−1 ) = (ROE t − r) × Book Value t−1 The valuation in the action box accounts for value with book value and short-term forecasts. But we are left with value from the long term given by the growth rate, g . You might speculate and insert a growth rate of say, 4 percent, arguing that this is the historical GDP growth rate. Thus, [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn19.png] Based on your speculation about long-run growth, you might conclude that the stock at $343.75 is overpriced: SELL. But you are violating a basic precept by putting speculation into the calculation: Don’t anchor on speculation. The only thing we understand from this exercise is that the market, with a price of $343.75, is either speculating with a growth rate higher than 4 percent or has a required return much lower than yours. That is important: We are understanding the speculation in the market price that we wish to challenge. This is our cue for the next chapter. Note that we have considerable value calculated from book value and near-term forecasts so that, while the continuing value was over 100 percent of the value for Netflix with DCF valuation, it is lower here. With accrual accounting we have reduced the speculative component in the valuation. That is usually the case, though not always. That will enter our evaluation of risk for, when a speculative component for which we cannot account is high, we might charge a higher return. More to consider, and we will. Protection from Paying Too Much for Growth One of our fundamental principles says: Beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky . The accrual accounting valuation has built-in protection from paying too much for growth. Tyco International Ltd., founded in 1960, marketed a range of products including high-tech materials, fire prevention services, electronics, healthcare products, and packaging. See https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tyco_International . Many of its products were acquired by acquisition. Acquisitions accelerated in the 1990s under CEO Dennis Kozlowski, known for his self-aggrandizing parties and extravagant lifestyle. His aggressive acquisition strategy is a showcase of empire building that ended in 2002 with a financial scandal that put him in jail. Table 3.5 tracks several accounting measures over the acquisition period and the firm’s subsequent demise. Table 3.5 Earnings, book values, pricing multiples, and residual earnings for Tyco International Ltd., 1997–2005 1997 1998 1999 2000 2001 2002 2003 2004 2005 Earnings ($billions) $(0.39) 1.17 1.02 2.76 * 3.46 (9.18) 0.98 2.88 3.02 EPS $(0.96) 2.96 2.48 6.54 * 7.68 (18.48) 1.96 5.76 6.04 Book value of equity ($billions) $3.43 9.90 12.37 17.03 31.73 24.16 26.48 30.4 32.6 Stock price per share $90 151 156 222 236 68 106 143 115 Price/Book (P/B) 4.8 6.2 6.1 5.6 3.4 1.4 2.0 2.3 1.8 Trailing P/E - 53 74 34 31 - 54 24 19 Net debt ($billions) $2.7 4.5 8.9 10.3 20.4 18.6 17.4 12.8 9.9 Residual earnings (at 10%) ($billions) $(0.73) 0.50 (0.09) 1.27 1.03 (11.96) (1.55) 0.04 (0.13) * Excluding gains on asset sales; earnings including those gains were $4.52 billion. Per share numbers are adjusted to 2010 shares for subsequent stock splits. From 1997 to 2001, EPS grew from a loss of 96 cents to a profit of $7.68. But is that earnings growth you want to pay for? Well, the market thought so with the per-share price increasing from $90 to $236 and the P/E ratios as high as 74, building in considerable growth expectations. Acquisitions yield earnings growth when earnings from acquired firms come into the income statement. But those acquisitions have to be paid for, so book values with the growing investment increased from $3.43 billion to $31.73 billion. After charging these book values at a reasonable required return at the time, the residual earnings (in the last row of the table) were minimal: Little value was added. An investor focusing on residual earnings rather than earnings would not have paid for the growth. She would have been protected from paying too much for growth. And she would have been protected from the huge drop in stock price in 2002 when the investments on the balance were written down with a consequent loss of $18.48 per share, wiping out most of the profits of the previous years. The lesson here: Do not buy earnings growth, buy residual earnings growth . Earnings growth can be generated by investment, but that does not necessarily add value. A firm must cover the required return to add value for the investor, and that is captured in the residual earnings calculation. The case also reinforces another principle: Always use both balance sheets and income statements in valuation. They combine to indicate value. A Shortcut, Too Cheap A common valuation technique combines the P/B ratio with the book rate of return, appealing to the following model: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn20.png] This model says that firms with higher earnings relative to book value (that is, a higher book rate of return) are worth more. Indeed, we saw in table 2.2 in the previous chapter that there is a positive relation between P/B and ROE. However, this does not quite do it. This chapter informs that the relevant input is not earnings but rather residual earnings: The firm must cover the required return to add to book value. Further, it is not only ROE that adds value but also growth. The short-form model here is correctly stated as [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn21.png] with g , future growth in residual earnings, the added input. The model commonly used is a no-growth valuation, yet growth must be considered. We look for simple schemes, but here is another that is just too simple. In fact, the no-growth model just gives the P/E ratio, and a particular one at that: [IMAGE: ../images/ch03eqn22.png] So, value is given by a forward P/E ratio = 1/ r . The analysis is that for the P/E ratio with a given solution: If the required return is 10 percent, the forward P/E should be 10 with no growth. That, again, is too simple. Ignore (growth) information at your peril. Value Investing Principles Here are some additional investing principles from this chapter: The dividend irrelevance principle: Dividends are the distribution of value, not the generation of value. Free cash flow is not a measure of value generation. Accrual accounting with balance sheets and income statements capture value generation, at least in principle. Anchor a valuation on book value, then add value-added earnings. Beware of buying earnings generated by investment. Do not buy earnings growth, buy residual earnings growth. Valuation involves both an income statement and a balance sheet. The Exercise Book As for each chapter, exercises in the Exercise Book test your understanding of the material in this chapter and your ability to apply it. The Case Book The next case in our Case Book applies the accrual accounting valuation discussed in this chapter. Here is the introduction. Case 4: Starbucks Corporation: Investing by The Book Anchoring on the Balance Sheet OK, the DCF valuation for Starbucks in case 1 didn’t work out too well. Sorry for teasing you. So now for something completely different. Apologies to Monty Python: This is no joke. Cash is not value, as we discovered with Starbucks in case 1. That seems strange, because cash back is what we are looking for when investing. However, firms have to invest in order to get cash, and investment requires that cash be consumed. The net cash flow—free cash flow—confuses the cash return from investment with that expended to get the cash. Investments are made to add value, yes—but the higher the investment, the lower the free cash flow. That seems perverse. We need a better accounting for value. So, anchor on the balance sheet and apply accounting for value by the book. Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and present some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.
