Chapter 4 Accounting for the Market Price Chapter 4 Accounting for the Market Price Beware of paying for growth, for growth is risky T he previous chapter began our design of the accounting book but left us with a critical issue. Because we wish to challenge the speculation in the market price, we are instructed not to base a valuation on speculation. Yet the accounting model from keeping a value book, despite its admirable features, includes a speculative growth rate for the long run, g : [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn01.png] Benjamin Graham and Charlie Munger expressed severe reservations about valuation models in the previous chapter, with a particular complaint about inserted growth rates. Indeed, there is a pervasive skepticism about valuation models today. What is the value of g and, for that matter, what is the value of r ? Varying g by 1 percent or r by 1 percent has a big effect on the calculated Value 0 , yet we don’t know g and r with any level of precision. One can always insert one’s own hurdle rate for r , but our skepticism about a model like the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) delivering r is widely shared. In short, a valuation model does not lead to secure investing. With this skepticism, investors have turned to investing with multiples or to the pervasive Value versus Growth investing styles, which fell short in chapter 2 . Or they have ventured into “smart beta” investing or opaque “factor” investing. Is there any way that a value investor can deal with these issues? The answer in this chapter is: Yes. This chapter will orient the investor to a different way of thinking, good thinking. Don’t think of valuation with a valuation model. Rather, think about the person asking you to trade: How is that person thinking about value and does that agree with your thinking? What is “good thinking”? This chapter shows how investing by the book yields answers. Teasers Some Shockers! Here are some statements that might shock you. We’ll show that they are actually wise. There is no such thing as intrinsic value. You don’t have to know the intrinsic value! Valuation models are not for valuation. “What is the Cost of Capital?” This is a trick question. Well, these statements certainly require explanation! Separating What We Know from Speculation Following this dictum, chapter 1 separated value into two components: Value = Anchoring Accounting Value + Speculative Value Anchoring accounting value is what we know, what we can account for. Speculative value is what we are more uncertain about. It is represented by the uncertain, speculative g in the valuation model. So, separate it out. If we have information that gives us a confident forecast of earnings two years ahead, [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn03.png] Here we are anchoring on the book value, which we know from the balance sheet, and on forecasts in which we have confidence. But we have refused to enter the speculative g into the model, so the model now contains only the value we can account for. With g = 0, that accounting yields a no-growth value, the value if there were no growth after the second year ahead. The box below calculates the no-growth value for Netflix, separating accounting value from speculative value at a point in 2022 when the speculative share price had dropped from $597.37 to $343.75. The no-growth accounting value is $166.66 per share. With this number, the price that the market is placing on growth can be calculated: $343.75 − 166.66 = $177.09. We now understand the valuation the market is putting on growth. People talk of value as being composed of steady-state value plus the present value of growth opportunities. That is rather vague. The analysis here puts concrete numbers on the two components. And we are beginning to understand how the market is thinking. Value Investing in Action Separating Accounting Value from Speculative Value for Netflix (NFLX) March 15, 2022 Required return = 8 percent; Price = $343.75 Source : Analysts’ consensus forecasts on Yahoo Finance 2021A 2022E 2023E EPS 11.01 14.40 DPS 0.00 0.00 BPS 35.70 46.71 61.11 ROE 30.84% 30.83% Residual earnings (8%) 8.154 10.663 Growth in residual earnings 30.77% The valuation: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn04.png] Figure 4.1 gives a graphical depiction of the components of the market price. The $343.75 is composed of book value of $35.70, value from the short term of $130.96, and $177.09 in speculative value about the long term. It is the latter that we wish to challenge: Should the investor pay $177.09 for growth? We have changed the valuation problem: Rather than challenging the market price, we now have the market’s valuation of growth to challenge. [IMAGE: ../images/fig4-1.png] 4.1 Building block diagram for the market price of Netflix (NFLX), March 15, 2022. If we feel that a forecast two years ahead is too speculative, we can anchor on book value and a forecast for only the forward year: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn05.png] Or, if we are uncertain of earnings one year ahead, we could insert the currently observed residual earnings, Earnings 0 − r.B −1 (what we know), into this valuation instead of the forward residual earnings. This would exclude information about forward earnings being different from current earnings, and would also exclude any speculation in the forward earnings forecast. Figure 4.2 displays the building block diagram for Nvidia Corporation (NVDA) with the no-growth value based on current residual earnings. At the time, June 2023, the stock price had soared to $438.08 and a forward P/E of 57.51 on speculation that the firm’s chip technology would be important for ChatGPT and other AI applications. The value in the pricing of long-term growth, $420.51, was 96 percent of the current price. That is to be challenged. It will also be challenged by competitors: AMD and Intel are not sitting idle, and even Nvidia’s customers Google, Microsoft, and Amazon are building custom AI chips. And that growth pricing will also be challenged by investors. Nvidia is a speculative stock and part B of the figure contrasts the market’s pricing of growth in June 2023 with that at earlier dates. One sees that the high pricing in October 2018 and November 2021 was subsequently revised downward. Beware of paying for growth . [IMAGE: ../images/fig4-2.png] 4.2 Market’s pricing of growth for Nvidia Corporation (NVDA). (A) Building block diagram at June 2023 with the no-growth value based on current residual earnings, RE 0 . (B) Growth pricing in earlier episodes (percent of price). Source : S. Penman, R. Gutsche, L. Gorkun-Voevoda, and A. Rif. “Buying Risky Growth and Returns to Trading on P/E Ratios,” Journal of Portfolio Management , April 2025. At ssrn.com/abstract=4537327 . The active investor applies this analysis to screen for mispriced stocks. The defensive investor focused on preservation of capital applies it to identify overpriced stocks to avoid, as was seemingly the case for Nvidia in 2018 and 2021. Such an investor might be happy to buy a market index like the S&P 500 while stripping out those stocks in the index that appear to be overpricing growth. The primary risk of investing is the risk of paying too much . Step back and see what we are doing here. It’s not the math or technique that is primarily important. What is important is understanding the person asking you to trade. As a first step, we are understanding the value that the person on the other side of a possible transaction is putting on growth. That is the person Benjamin Graham called Mr. Market. Let’s continue talking to him to better understand his thinking. Continuing the Conversation with Mr. Market: The Market’s Growth Rate A dollar value for growth does not quite give us a handle. But we can be more explicit. Instead of Value 0 on the left-hand side of the valuation model, enter the current market price: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn06.png] With the other inputs accounted for, the market’s g can be calculated in a reverse engineering exercise. We now have an explicit growth forecast to challenge. The box on the next page conducts the exercise for Netflix. In March 2022, the implied growth rate in the market price of $343.75 is 4.71 percent per year, down from the 6.22 percent at the beginning of the year with a market price of $597.37 and the same forecasts. A 6.22 percent growth rate could look a bit high. Does 4.71 percent look more acceptable? This is what the value investor must challenge. Although the difference between the two growth rates may not seem large, they are growth rates over the very long run. With compounding, this could mean a sizeable difference in wealth. Think of the difference between wealth in 20 years for an economy growing at a GDP rate of 4 percent versus 3 percent. For Netflix, the difference in present value terms is the difference between the January 1 price of $597.37 and the March price of $343.75. In terms of total market capitalization, that difference is $112.598 billion. The investor tracks a stock with an implied growth rate, looking for a point where investing might become attractive (or not). By May 1, 2022, Netflix’s price had dropped to $180.97. The g had fallen to 0.83 percent. Looking more attractive? Value Investing in Action The Market’s Growth Rate for Netflix (NFLX) March 15, 2022 Required return = 8 percent; Price = $343.75. Source of analysts’ forecasts : Yahoo Finance. 2021A 2022E 2023E EPS 11.01 14.40 DPS 0.00 0.00 BPS 35.70 46.71 61.11 ROE 30.84% 30.83% Residual earnings (8%) 8.154 10.663 Growth in residual earnings 30.77% Reverse engineering the market’s growth rate: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn07.png] For comparison, calculate the growth rate at January 1, 2022 when the price stood at $597.37 before the 42 percent drop: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn08.png] A Declining Growth Rate The implied growth rate of 4.71 percent for Netflix in March 2022 is the average over years to the long term. But that does not mean that the growth rate for 2024 and 2025 will be at 4.71 percent. Indeed, in the pro forma for Netflix, the forecasted residual earnings growth rate for 2023 is 30.77 percent, so it is implausible that it will drop to 4.71 percent in 2024. However, growth rates do decline under the forces of competition, and the 4.71 percent tells us that there will be a decline from 30.77 percent with an average of 4.71 percent over subsequent years. This can be plotted. To do so, there is something else we can anchor on: Though varying over firms, the average residual earnings growth rate is roughly equal to the GDP growth rate. That makes sense: Residual earnings is value added for firms and GDP is value added for the economy; firms on average perform at the average for the economy. So, we can expect firms’ growth rates to be at the GDP growth rate in the very long run as they become more like the average. The historical GDP growth rate for the United States is about 4 percent. See table 1.2 in chapter 1 . Accordingly, see Netflix’s 4.71 percent growth rate as an average of a growth rate of 30.77 percent declining ultimately to 4 percent. The growth rate for 2024 is a weighted average of the growth rate in 2023 and the 4 percent: Growth rate for 2024, g 2024 = [ w × 30.77%] + [(1 − w ) × 4%] The growth rate for the next year ahead, 2025, is a weighted average of the growth rate in 2024 and the 4 percent: Growth rate for 2025, g 2025 = [ w × g 2024 ] + [(1 − w ) × 4%] And so on into the future. The problem thus becomes one of estimating the market’s weight, w , for the weighted average calculation. Like the estimate of g , that is the implied w in the market price of $343.75. It can be estimated by trial and error with successive w inserted into the long-run residual earnings valuation until the valuation converges to the market price. The Goal Seek function in Excel solves for it. An engine using this feature to produce a plot can be found on the website for this book. The mathematically inclined will recognize this plot as growth with geometric decay. Figure 4.3 plots the declining growth path for the $343.75 price at March 15, 2022. This is the market’s forecasted growth path that we can now challenge. If we assess that Netflix can maintain growth above this line, the stock is a BUY. If we assess that the firm cannot meet this benchmark, it is a SELL. Of course, we cannot evaluate the distant future on this plot, but can do so for the intermediate term after 2023. The forecasts for 2024 to 2027 from the plot are also given in the figure. Can Netflix produce growth above the market’s forecasts for 2024–2027? Or will the increasing pressure from competitors result in growth below the line? (If you think that the historical GDP growth rate will be lower in the future, play with a 3.5 percent or 3.0 percent rate.) Figure 4.3 also plots the path for the market price of $597.37 at January 1, 2022. The subsequent 42 percent price drop significantly changed the projected path. In January, the projected residual earnings (RE) for 2027 was $21.85, growing at 14.85 percent. By March, the RE was projected to be $15.56, growing at a slower 5.51 percent and quickly reverting to the 4 percent GDP rate. [IMAGE: ../images/fig4-3.png] 4.3 The market’s growth paths for Netflix, Inc. (NFLX). (A) Growth path over next 20 years: Price = $343.75. Market w = 0.487. (B) Growth path over the next 20 years: Price = $597.37. Market w = 0.798. These are snapshots at specific points in time. The investor with an interest in Netflix (or any other company) tracks the growth implied by the market as the price changes, looking for a point where she considers an investment to be attractive becomes attractive (if it ever arrives). See the box below. But the exercise is still, as yet, incomplete. We just have the market’s growth path to challenge. Fundamental analysis challenges the growth rate itself, and this is the subject of the following chapters. The analysis focuses on the question: What determines residual earnings and its growth? Dynamic Tracking for Investing Opportunities Netflix, Inc. (NFLX) The comparison of Netflix’s price on January 1 and March 15 saw a drop in the market’s pricing of growth, making the stock look more attractive. The investor might continue to track Netflix to a point where she sees the firm is a buy (or, if holding the stock, a sell). By June 2022, the price had dropped to $175.50. Here are the comparative implied growth rates with the same hurdle rate of 8 percent at each date, along with that on February 1, 2023 when the price had recovered somewhat. The market’s pricing of growth is a percent of the market price for the stock. Date Share price No-growth value Market’s pricing of growth Implied g Jan 1, 2022 $597.37 $166.66 72.1% 6.33% Mar 15, 2022 $343.75 $166.66 51.5% 4.71% Jun 15, 2022 $175.50 $161.46 8.0% 0.84% Feb 1, 2023 $361.99 $167.60 53.7% 5.05% For this tracking, the investor updates the book value and forward earnings forecasts, of course. The June 15, 2022 price looks particularly attractive. But the investor has yet to bring fundamental analysis to her evaluation of the market’s pricing of growth. Competitive Advantage Period There is another way to look at this: How long will it take before the company’s growth rate gets close to the 4 percent GDP growth rate? That resonates with the idea of the competitive advantage period (CAP), the amount of time a firm can generate excess returns from investments; here it correlates with the number of years of growth in excess of the GDP growth rate. The declining growth graph informs our analysis. For a given initial residual earnings growth rate, the higher the value of w the longer the CAP implied by the market price. At a price of $597.37 for Netflix, growth gets close to 4 percent after about 13 years on the graph. For a $343.75 price, it takes only 5 years. Value investors look for firms with “durable competitive advantage” and ask if the market is pricing this in. With the drop in stock price, the market is saying that the CAP has dropped significantly. The concept of “fade rate” is similar to CAP; it refers to how quickly the growth rate fades to the average GDP growth rate. CAP is sometimes calculated with cash flows or with the long-run rate set as the book rate of return equal to the required return. However, chapter 6 will tell us that the book rate of return is not a good measure of the economic return that competition challenges. Negotiating with Mr. Market Benjamin Graham characterized investing as a negotiation with Mr. Market, the hypothetical person setting the market price. That is what we are doing here: Mr. Market is naming his ask price and we are entering into a discussion about it. An important point in negotiation is understanding how the other person thinks, whether it be a street vendor, an employer or employee, or even (or especially) one’s spouse or partner. That is our starting point in our negotiation with Mr. Market: What, sir, are you thinking about growth prospects? Graham saw Mr. Market as a moody fellow, subject to swings in speculative optimism and fearful pessimism, inclined to join the herd mentality of the moment rather than anchoring on information. The negotiation asks about his state of mind. In that negotiation, it’s good to have benchmarks. The historical GDP growth rate is about 4 percent. So, with a long-run g of 4.71 percent in March 2022, one asks: Can Netflix maintain an average growth rate above the 4 percent? With a g of 0.83 percent in May 2022, can growth be that low, particularly with what we see now: The forecasted growth rate for 2023 is 30.77 percent in the pro forma. We do not yet have the complete analysis to challenge the growth rate to ensure that we are on the right side of the trade. The negotiation with Mr. Market will continue as we begin our fundamental analysis of growth in subsequent chapters. Like all negotiations, this continuing discussion with Mr. Market is a mental exercise, but one based on an accounting book that tells us how to think and translates that thinking into a challenge to Mr. Market’s thinking. At all points in the discussion, we ask: What do we know that he does not see? If his price differs from our analysis, it is also good to turn the question back on ourselves: What could he possibly know that we do not? We use the analysis to challenge our own thinking. Look at yourself in the mirror. We are now able to explain the “shockers” in the Teasers at the beginning of this chapter. There Is No Such Thing as Intrinsic Value Though value investors often talk about intrinsic value, we began this chapter by pointing out that calculating intrinsic value is a doubtful activity: We are concerned with the intrinsic value of the security and more particularly with the discovery of the discrepancies between intrinsic value and price. We must recognize, however, that intrinsic value is an elusive concept. In general terms it is understood to be that value which is justified by the facts, e.g., the assets, earnings, dividends, definite prospects—as distinct, let us say, from market quotations established by artificial manipulation or distorted by psychological excesses. But it is a great mistake to imagine that intrinsic value is as definite and as determinable as is the market price. Benjamin Graham and David Dodd, Security Analysis , 1934, p. 17 A scientist goes hunting for something that exists in nature, to discover something like a distant galaxy, molecular structure, the DNA of the tsetse fly, or the geology of ocean sediments. But the value investor should not think of the endeavor as trying to find intrinsic value as if it exists out there somewhere. It’s best to work from the basis that it does not exist. The reason, of course, is that firms are going concerns continuing indefinitely into the (infinite) future—and that distant future is hard (impossible?) to predict. With weather in the distant future so indeterminant, meteorologists bring their science to predict weather only in the near term. So does the value investor. You Don’t Have to Know the Intrinsic Value! Investing is a not a game against nature. That is the scientist’s pursuit. Rather, value investing is a game against other investors. Rather than finding intrinsic value, all we have to do is agree or disagree with other investors, playing a game that confirms or rejects the price set by them. After all, we can only BUY, SELL, or HOLD. That comes as a relief: The burden on the investor is not to find the “true” value. That is why we approach investing by understanding Mr. Market’s thinking about growth. And that is the reason why the fundamental analysis of subsequent chapters is not applied to determine intrinsic value, but rather to challenge the thinking of other investors. “Successful investing is anticipating the anticipation of others,” said John Maynard Keynes. In tennis, one must be good at one’s game, but it is said that most points are won by waiting for the opponent to make mistakes. So the value investor maintains her discipline and waits for Mr. Market to be too excited or too pessimistic about growth, or to ignore information. Valuation models are not for valuation Valuation models are for understanding other investors’ thinking, as we have started to do, not for valuation. This requires the valuation model to embed good thinking, the good thinking in keeping an accounting book. A DCF model that just accounts for a cash book does not do it. The thinking of a double-entry book with a balance sheets and income statement does, provided the accounting in that book is “good accounting.” The prevalence of DCF models in practice may give us pause. But that is the valuation model of the sell side. An investment banker trying to get the best price in an IPO uses the method of comparables presented in chapter 2 during hot IPO markets. But he is then left with the problem of justifying a high price with the “due diligence” requirement. For that he needs a bad model: Choose your g and r to fit, and you can justify practically any price. The DCF model suits him—with so much of the valuation in a long run, it is difficult to validate the valuation. But the value investor is on the buy side, considering whether to buy the possibly overpriced IPO. “What Is the Cost of Capital?” This Is a Trick Question Up to this point, we have ignored the other unknown in the model, the required return, r . If that is your personal hurdle rate to cover risk, that is OK. But the question, “What is the Cost of Capital?” is one that absorbs many business school courses in valuation and security analysis. But in vain. After 70 years of trying to find the cost of capital, with Nobel Prizes awarded for the attempt, there is a dirty little secret in academia: We do not know the cost of capital. The value investor anchors on what we know and reaches the same honest conclusion: We do not know the cost of capital. The question is a trick question. That said, there is no objection to your putting in your own personal hurdle rate in the reverse engineering exercise. Finessing the Problem with the Cost of Capital We return to this issue in chapter 8 where we bring fundamental analysis to the issue and discuss risk in more detail. But here we show how to finesse the problem of not knowing the cost of capital. To do so, we turn to a familiar concept with traditional value investors: the margin of safety. To avoid the risk of paying too much, those investors are advised not to invest in slightly underpriced stocks, but rather to look for those that are significantly underpriced, thus providing a margin of safety. We can apply r to assess our margin of safety. Our reverse-engineering exercise has a particular property: The higher the inserted r , the higher the g output needed to justify the same price. That makes sense: A higher hurdle rate requires higher growth to compensate for the higher risk. And it also aligns with the notion that risk and growth go together: Higher growth is riskier growth. So, to build in a margin of safety, insert an r in the reverse-engineering exercise that is seemingly excessive. That will yield a higher g , but if your analysis says that the firm can produce this higher growth, you have a margin of safety. The action box on the next page demonstrates this with a revised required return of 10 percent for Netflix. At the $343.75 price, the market’s implied growth rate is now 7.06 percent, higher than the 4.71 percent obtained with an 8 percent required return. This could also be converted to a declining growth path: The higher implied long-term growth rate suggests that Netflix should be able to maintain growth above the GDP growth rate for a longer time. If the fundamental analysis of the following chapters indicates that Netflix can achieve this higher growth trajectory, you have a margin of safety. There is an added bonus: You have a better understanding of your uncertainty about buying Netflix, the risk to buying growth. Value Investing in Action The Market’s Growth Rate for Netflix (NFLX) with a Required Return of 10 Percent Required return = 10%; Price = $343.75. 2021A 2022E 2023E EPS 11.01 14.40 DPS 0.00 0.00 BPS 35.70 46.71 61.11 ROE 30.84% 30.83% Residual earnings (10%) 7.440 9.729 Growth in residual earnings 30.77% Source of analysts’ forecasts : Yahoo Finance. Reverse engineering the market’s growth rate: [IMAGE: ../images/ch04eqn11.png] Screening on the Market’s Pricing of Growth To this point, we have not done any fundamental analysis. That will follow in subsequent chapters. What we do have is the target for the analysis, the market’s view on the growth rate: Does fundamental analysis indicate that the market’s view is correct? The identification of the market’s growth rate is a starting point; the value investor needs to select firms for analysis, and the implied g provides a screen. Value investors are often advised to screen stocks for investigation on their P/E or P/B ratios: If investing long, screen out low P/E and P/B stocks; screen out high P/E or P/B if looking to short. However, chapter 2 pointed out the dangers of screening stocks on these multiples, advising the investor to understand how earnings and book value work together and to bring in other information. Ignore information at your peril . The reverse engineering exercise joins book value and earnings and brings in other information in the g with an accounting for value that incorporates a discount for risk. That g is the market’s g , which can serve as a screening filter for the value investor: Investigate firms with a particularly low or high g , rather than screening simply based on P/E or P/B. Fundamental analysis will then lead her to ask whether she or the market is correct. A lazy investor might decide to go no further, buying firms with low g and avoiding firms with high g . That does add information to the P/E and P/B but, again, ignores information that might tell him whether a low market g is justified or actually too high. The diligent investor proceeds to the fundamental analysis discussed in subsequent chapters to bring more information to challenge the g . Nevertheless, screening stocks on g alone appears to differentiate on returns. See table 4.1 . A low market valuation of growth yields higher returns, and a higher market valuation of growth yields lower returns on average. The lower returns for high pricing of growth might indicate the danger of buying growth. But we must be careful: The higher growth pricing could be due to a lower discount for risk, and the lower growth pricing could be due to a larger price discount for risk. If so, higher risk should yield higher returns. And overvaluation or undervaluation could be different in different years, depending on Mr. Market’s moods. There is much to be sorted out, and that involves bringing fundamental analysis to challenging the market’s growth valuation. Stocks are ranked into 10 portfolios each year. The returns are for the 12 months after portfolios are formed. Portfolio 1 consists of firms with low speculative growth pricing, portfolio 10 with high speculative growth pricing. (HPQ, formerly Hewlett Packard) in 2022. Value investors are wary of buying growth. So the question is: What was the markets’ forecast of growth at the time? Did these look to be attractive growth rates for investors? You do not have enough information yet to answer that question since you have not carried out the fundamental analysis of later chapters to challenge the market’s growth rate. We have set a hurdle rate in these examples. Investors’ rates might differ, of course. Short-term forecasts are analysts’ consensus forecasts. These may not be the forecasts that the investors were using. There is no pretense these cases are from a random sample. Value investors do make mistakes! HP Inc. stock price, October 31, 2022 = $28.05, with analysts revising 2022 EPS down to $4.07 and 2023 EPS down to $3.31. Value Investing Principles There is no such thing as intrinsic value. You do not have to know intrinsic value. Investing is not a game against nature, but rather a game against other investors. Valuation models are not for valuation. Value investing challenges the growth rate implicit in the market price. The Exercise Book Exercises on the website apply the material in this chapter, part of your preparation to become an active value investor. The Case Book The Case Book has two cases inviting you to begin a discussion with Mr. Market about pricing. We’ll continue this discussion in the subsequent chapters. Case 5: Challenging Starbucks Stock Price Does a Drop in Price Provide a Buying Opportunity? At the beginning of this book, at the lead-in to chapter 1 , we asked whether a significant drop in stock price for Starbucks Corporation was a buying opportunity. Starbucks had long been a favorite of value investors. It was said to have “franchise value” with its brand and supply chains creating a “moat around it,” a defense against its many competitors. Case 1 documented value investor Bill Ackman’s success in investing in the company. We looked at Starbucks in case 4. You were left with the question of an appropriate growth rate. Chapter 4 tells us that this is not an appropriate question: Inserting a speculative growth rate just yields a speculative valuation. We should instead turn it around and ask: What is the market’s growth rate? This case asks you to track the implied growth rate in the market price as Starbucks’ stock price declined over 2022. At the beginning of the year, the stock traded at $109.00. What was the market’s growth rate at the time? The case then asks you to move on in time to September 30, 2022, Starbuck’s fiscal year end. By that time, the stock price had dropped by 22.7 percent to $84.26 on news of higher labor costs, unionization, and, yes, challenge from the competition. The firm’s imaginative founder, Howard Schultz, had stepped back into the CEO position on April 1. On his past record, he is no April Fool. His strategy was for the long run, he said, the same strategy that paid off handsomely in the past. He canceled a proposed stock repurchase, planning instead to plow that money back into the business. Is the drop in price a buying opportunity? Case 6: Challenging The Market Price for Microsoft How Much Am I Being Asked to Pay for Growth? Microsoft Corporation was one of those technology stocks that became highly overpriced in the late 1990s. Following a high of $58 in December 1999 (on a split-adjusted basis), the stock price fell to $22 in 2000 and remained around $25 for the next decade, returning to the $58 level only in 2016. That long wait for a return is a warning to those buying over-priced stocks. For the patient, the stock price reached $323.01 by December 1, 2021, with a forward P/E or 35.1 and a P/B of 17.1. For the investor anxious about overpricing, that calls for an investigation, and that investigation focuses on the growth expectations in the market price. The primary risk in investing is the risk of paying too much. By the end of 2022, the stock price had fallen 25.8 percent to $239.82, along with other technology stocks. With such a large drop, the value investor asks whether that presents a buying opportunity. Was the drop a correction to an overpriced stock or a deviation from value? Price is what you pay, value is what you get . Are you a buyer? Case 7: Netflix or Paramount? A Comparison of Alternative Stock Investments On May 17, 2022, Berkshire Hathaway disclosed that it had acquired 69 million shares in Paramount Global (PARA) for $37.68 each. Paramount’s stock price rose over 15 percent on the announcement. This added to Berkshire’s purchases of $51 billion in stocks during the first quarter of 2022 when the market was down considerably with higher inflation and fears of a recession. Apparently Berkshire was following Buffett’s dictum to be “fearful when others are greedy and greedy when others are fearful.” But why Paramount and not Netflix? Netflix was the pioneer and leader in video streaming with a good foothold in the business. Paramount was a recent entrant with its Paramount+ service, arriving with many others like Apple TV+, Amazon Prime Video, and Disney+. This chapter has examined Netflix at about the same time as the Paramount stock purchase. Follow the same analysis for Paramount and make comparisons. Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 5 Accounting for Leverage Chapter 5 Accounting for Leverage Beware of paying for growth created by leverage, for that growth is risky B uying growth is risky, but buying growth that is leveraged up with debt financing is riskier still. This chapter is about handling financing leverage in value investing. When a firm is partially financed with debt in addition to equity, shareholders are required to pay off the debt before sharing in the firm’s value. It is well recognized, both in theory and from grim experience, that leverage is risky—just ask the shareholders of Lehman Brothers Inc., who lost all in the 2008 financial crisis! When things go well with leverage they go very well, but when things go badly with leverage they go very badly. This chapter shows that leverage increases expected earnings growth in the g of the last chapter. Is this growth that we wish to pay for? Typically, the answer is No: While leverage increases earnings growth, it also adds risk. The alarm bell reminding us to beware of paying for growth tolls louder when leverage is involved and can even become the death knell. For this reason, value investors typically avoid leverage. However, there are situations when an investor will buy leverage, and the chapter points to these. In doing so, it examines stock issues, stock repurchases, and transactions involving the issue and redemption of debt, for these change leverage. Do these transactions add value? There is another, more subtle issue: Leverage affects several accounting measures that feature prominently in value analysis, such as return on equity (ROE), P/B, and P/E. Therein lies danger, for investing based on these measures can involve buying leverage. In buying low P/E firms, as in the simple investing of chapter 2 , the investor might think he is buying a cheap stock, but he might actually be loading up on leverage risk. Yet another trap! This chapter exposes the trap and shows how to avoid it. These teaser questions nearby get you thinking. Teasers Executive Compensation You are a member of the Compensation Committee of the Board of Directors. The CEO comes to you with a proposal: In exercise of our fiduciary duty, we are all focused on maximizing returns to the shareholders. Therefore, I recommend tying my compensation to Return on Equity (ROE). I will work hard to increase this return to shareholders. Will you go along with the proposal? Leverage and P/E Financing leverage increases earnings growth, and thereby increases the P/E ratio. Is this true? Stock Repurchases The CFO comes to the board with a proposal to repurchase some of the firm’s outstanding shares. This action will increase EPS, thus adding value and increasing the stock price, she argues. Will you go along with the proposal? Leverage Principles As in previous chapters, we start with the theory that puts us on a firm foundation. Leverage and Risk No-arbitrage theory states that the required return for equity, which we will denote as r Equity , has two components. One is determined by the risk in the firm’s business operations, r Firm , and one is from additional risk if the firm is levered. There is a formula that always holds: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn01.png] where r Net Debt is the (after-tax) required return for the net debt. Here, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn02.png] measures the amount of leverage which, when applied to the spread between the required return for the firm and that for debt, gives the return premium required for leverage. Simply put, the investor takes on the risk in the business operations but adds more risk with higher debt financing, and that risk requires a higher return. This leverage formula may be more familiar to you in another form. With a little math that moves r Firm to the left-hand side, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn03.png] where [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn04.png] is the balance sheet equation but now stated in terms of values rather than accounting book measures r Firm is the weighted average cost of capital (WACC) so often calculated in investing classes, though with market prices substituted for values in the expression (inappropriately, if price is different from value). In the WACC formula it looks like the required return for the firm is determined by the required return for equity and debt, but it is the first equation that is the causal equation: The equity return is determined by the required return for the firm plus that for leverage. Leverage and Value Another foundational principle in the theory of finance is the financing irrelevance principle , established by the same Modigliani and Miller of the dividend irrelevance principle. The principle states that financing transactions do not add value. So, a firm cannot add value by issuing a bond (debt) at a fair value price or redeeming a bond at fair value. In the parlance of finance, these are zero net present value transactions: Value received is value given up. Similarly, issuing or repurchasing shares at fair value adds no value. It makes sense: A firm cannot add value by buying and selling paper. In the case of a bond, the financing irrelevance principle can be demonstrated with our measure of value added, residual income. Suppose a firm purchases a $1,000 bond at par with a required annual return of 5 percent. If this bond is efficiently priced, the bond will yield a return of 5 percent or $50 per year. The annual residual income on the bond is $50 − (0.05 × 1,000) = 0: The bond adds no value, yielding interest income just at the required rate. If this is a bond that is issued by the firm, the interest is an expense and the debt is an obligation. So, the residual income to the shareholder is −$50 − (0.05 × −1,000) = 0: No value added, nor value destroyed. Of course, if that $50 were then invested in the business by buying assets to produce revenue, that could generate value. But that is an operating activity, not a financing activity. For equity transactions, consider the following. Three people own a firm worth $150 million with equal shares, so the value per share is $50 million. One of the partners wishes to be bought out for $50 million. What is the effect of this share repurchase on the value per share? Well, with $50 in cash passed out of the firm, the value in the firm is now $100 million and, with now only two shares, the value per share is $50 million: No value added. You see the same result if another person is invited to join the firm for $50 million. These examples demonstrate that financing activities generally do not add value. But they also point to situations when they do: when the financing transaction is not at fair value. So, if the partner of the $150 million firm is bought out for $40 million, below value, the value of the firm is $110 million and the remaining shares are each worth $55 million: value added to the remaining shareholders. The scenario points to the need to understand value, something that the buyout partner here did not. In these special situations, the value investor sees value in financing activities, and we will explore them. But the best way to start your thinking is to embrace the idea that financing typically does not add value. A firm adds value through its business, trading in input and output markets with suppliers and customers, not from trading securities in the capital market. Banks that “make money” from the interest rate spread between borrowing and lending rates are an exception, as are debt arbitrageurs who look for mispriced debt. Leverage Measures With theory to ground us, we turn to measuring leverage. The theoretical measure of leverage above, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn05.png] , is usually estimated with Market Leverage, with the value of net debt approximated by its book value and the value of equity set at its market price: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn06.png] . Under the mantra, Price Is What You Pay, Value Is What You Get , the investor is concerned about this measure: If the equity is overpriced, the stock looks less levered and less risky, a trap. See the following vignette. The investor uses the accounting book as an anchor. A Vignette Beware of Market Leverage: Netflix, Inc. (NFLX) In 2021, Netflix traded at a price that value investors viewed as speculative. Indeed, the share price dropped from $597.37 at the end of 2021 to $343.75 by March 2022, and then to $180.97 by May 2022. The implied growth rate calculated in chapter 4 also fell. With the high price at the end of 2021, the market leverage measure, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn07.png] , makes leverage look less risky whereas the investor is at risk of the speculative price falling. The primary risk is the risk of paying too much . The following table tracks the market leverage for Netflix as the price dropped and compares it to the book leverage measure that follows immediately in the text: Dec 31, 2021 Mar 15, 2022 May 1, 2022 Stock price $597.37 $343.75 $180.97 Market price of equity $265,240 million $152,788 million $80,400 million Implied growth rate , g 6.22% 4.71% 0.83% Net debt $9,335.1 million $8,525.7 million $8,525.7 million Book equity $15,849.2 million $17,544.0 million $17,544.0 million Book leverage 58.9% 48.6% 48.6% Market leverage 3.5% 5.6% 10.6% One asks: Does the 3.5% leverage at the end of 2021 understate leverage risk? Book equity does not capture all value that backs up debt, as investing by the book reveals. So, if an analysis indicates equity is reasonably priced, a market leverage measure has integrity. But beware of market leverage . We understand leverage by keeping a book. In the double-entry system, leverage affects both the balance sheet and income statement, so leverage is conveyed through both. Unfortunately, both U.S. GAAP and IFRS muddle the presentation of these financial statements. In order to keep a book that tracks leverage for the investor, these statements must be reorganized into what are called reformulated financial statements to uncover the leverage. As we proceed, delve into the respective annual 10-K reports to see where the numbers are found. This information is compiled through the U.S. Securities and Exchange Commission’s online program for Electronic Data Gathering, Analysis, and Retrieval (EDGAR). Go to: https://www.sec.gov/search-filings and enter the firm’s ticker symbol or name. Keeping a Book: The Reformulated Balance Sheet The balance sheet equation governing the balance sheet in both GAAP and IFRS states equity in terms of assets and liabilities: Equity = Assets − Liabilities However, assets and liabilities are a mixture of those involved in the business with those involved in financing the business. The book kept by the value investor separates the two: Common Shareholders’ Equity = Net Operating Assets − Net Financing Debt = NOA − ND Net operating assets are the net assets used in the business, sometimes called enterprise assets or invested capital: Net Operating Assets (NOA) = Operating Assets (OA) − Operating Liabilities (OL) Firms issue debt but also hold debt assets. Net debt is the firm’s net position in debt: Net Debt = Debt Liabilities − Debt Assets OA are all assets on the balance sheet other than the debt assets. OL are all liabilities other than the financing debt liabilities, those occurring in operations such as accounts payable, accrued compensation, and unearned (deferred) revenues. The restatement appropriately separates business activities from financing activities, a separation we will see as crucial to determining the value generated by the business. Balance Sheet Leverage The measure of balance sheet leverage is [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn12.png] . This measure requires us to identify net debt and equity in the balance sheet. Why net debt? Well, firms can issue debt in the form of bonds or bank loans, creating a liability, but they can also buy debt such as a bond or a deposit in an interest-bearing bank account, creating a debt asset. Both are part of debt financing activities, and net debt is the difference between the two. A firm (other than a debt arbitrageur) is not in the business of making money from buying and selling debt. Rather, debt is used for raising cash from operations or for disposing it. If the firm has excess cash from the business (free cash flow), it can apply it to buying debt assets—a Treasury bill or an interest-bearing bank deposit, for example—or it can apply the cash to reducing its debt obligations. If it needs cash to invest in the business, it can issue debt obligations or it can liquidate the debt assets it holds. In both cases, net debt is not affected by whether the cash affects debt obligations or debt assets. In effect, debt assets defease debt obligations, so it is net debt that is relevant. A firm with more debt assets than debt obligations is long in debt, a net creditor, while one with more debt obligations than debt assets is short in debt, a net debtor. Leverage conveys this net debt position. Here is the net debt calculation: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn13.png] Table 5.1 presents the comparative 2023 GAAP balance sheet for Microsoft Corporation with the relevant debt numbers circled. Net debt is calculated with the numbers corresponding to the items above: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn14.png] With net debt and common equity identified, the leverage measure falls out easily. The 2023 reformulated statement here is in summary form, with totals only. Net operating assets of $157,341 million is the common equity of $206,223 million plus the net debt, −$48,882 million, obeying the (reformulated) balance sheet equation. Yes, Microsoft is in a negative net debt position, with its debt assets greater than its debt obligations. You can prove that the NOA is equal to the total of all assets net of liabilities on Microsoft’s balance sheet that are not in net debt. Microsoft’s equity is represented as an investment in the business of $157,341 million and an investment in net debt of $48,882 million. A disclosure issue complicates this calculation. Capital leases are included in long-term debt of $41,990 million and the current maturities of long-term debt of $5,247 million; and long-term operating leases of $12,728 million are line-itemed on the balance sheet. However, short-term operating lease obligations of $2,409 million are not. They are recorded within other current liabilities, but the number is found in the Leases footnote to the financial statements (Note 14), as indicated. Debt assets, sometimes simply referred to as “cash,” are within cash and cash equivalents as well as short-term investments, totaling $111,262 million for Microsoft. Investment footnote 4 reveals that short-term investments includes net derivatives valued at $6 million and assumed to be for operational purposes, so this is subtracted. Occasionally, you will find that firms have debt assets under long-term investments in the non-current asset section of the balance sheet. But not here. Debt obligations total to $62,374 million. So, Microsoft’s net debt is −$48,882 million: Microsoft is long in debt, a net creditor. With the equity of $206,223 million on the balance sheet, book leverage is: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn20.png] Microsoft is negatively leveraged. Note that this calculation differs from the standard calculation of the debt/equity ratio, which ignores debt assets. Cash and cash equivalents are typically debt assets. But there are situations where they are treated as part of operations. See the Value in Debt Assets action box below. When accounting is introduced in this book, we give a summary of how the accounting works in a side box. This provides an overview but does not substitute for the full treatment that you find in an accounting text. The accounting for most items is similar under U.S. GAAP and IFRS but we will note major differences. A nearby Accounting Primer deals with the accounting for debt. As the accounting for leverage involves leases, another Accounting Primer box below briefly introduces lease accounting. Accounting Primer Accounting for Debt Debt assets are measured at fair value if they are “available for sale,” which is the case with short-term debt available for liquidity. Fair value is typically current market value (called a Level 1 fair value input) though, for less liquid assets, it is sometimes estimated from comparables (Level 2 input) or with valuation models (Level 3 input). The fair value footnote gives the details. On marking debt assets to fair value, an unrealized gain or loss is recorded within other comprehensive income in the comprehensive income statement (not within net income in the income statement). Financing debt liabilities are recorded at their initial historical transaction amount, adjusted for amortization of any bond discount or premium. While debt liabilities are usually not marked to fair value, their book value is typically close to it unless there has been a big change in credit worthiness. There is a “fair value option” to mark these liabilities to fair value, but few firms choose this option. Accounting Primer Lease Accounting Firms can purchase assets or lease them, as airlines often do with aircraft. Purchased assets are, of course, booked to the balance sheet, usually as property, plant, and equipment (PPE), sometimes also called fixed assets. However, a firm that leases an asset, although it does not technically own the asset, also books the asset if the lease is for more than one year. In effect, the firm is deemed to have the right to use the services of the asset. The lease contract requires the firm to pay regular lease payments to the lessor, which is a financing liability, equivalent to when a firm buys an asset and finances it with a bank loan. In short, the leased asset is part of the operating assets of the business, and the lease liability is a financing liability. Prior to 2017, firms could classify leases as finance (or capital) leases, which were substantively deemed a purchase; or as operating leases (such as monthly rent paid on a warehouse). The latter were not booked to the balance sheet, resulting in an off-balance sheet liability for contractual payment obligations on the lease. Since 2017, all leases of more than one year duration must be on the balance sheet, an improvement in the accounting. However, firms still distinguish operating leases on their balance sheets, as does Microsoft. Treat them no differently than debt. Under IFRS, there is no distinction between finance leases and operating leases. The disclosure of lease liabilities differs by firm. Some disclose capital leases (also called financing leases) on the balance sheet. Others include them as debt or in the category of other liabilities. Always read the footnotes thoroughly. For Microsoft’s leverage calculation, there is no preferred equity and common equity is therefore the total equity, read from its balance sheet in table 5.1 . However, when there is preferred equity, U.S. GAAP and IFRS include it in total equity. It is the common shares whose price the value investor is challenging, and preferred equity is not part of the common equity. Rather, it is an obligation for the common shareholders to pay other claimants—it is effectively debt from the common shareholders’ point of view. So treat it as such, subtracting it from total equity and adding it to net debt to appropriately measure the leverage for the common shareholders. There is another complication: Common ividends payable to shareholders are classified as a liability in U.S. GAAP and IFRS. Value Investing in Action Value in Debt Assets Debt assets in cash and cash equivalents and short-term investments typically enter the net debt calculation as negative debt. Like debt obligations, they yield zero residual income so do not add value: If a firm has $100 million in debt assets earning at an interest rate of 5 percent, residual earnings = $5 − 0.05 × 100 = 0. But there are exceptions: If cash is required for operations (for example, cash in the till for a retail store), a small amount may be classified as an operating asset. However, many transactions these days are cashless. Balances are required in business checking accounts so that checks and electronic charges are honored, but these balances are routinely swept into interest-bearing accounts at the bank. In any case, these small amounts are difficult to estimate and typically do not have a material effect on net debt calculations. They can therefore be ignored. Restricted cash required under a contract in the course of business is an operating asset (for example, a bond posted as a guarantee to a supplier). More significant is the case where the firm holds precautionary cash as insurance against a lack of liquidity in downturns when it might have difficulty borrowing. These “rainy day funds” to support operations are classified as operating assets. Since they are at risk in operations, they are charged at the required return for operations in calculating the value-adding residual income they earn. Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) ran into difficulties during the COVID pandemic as flights were canceled. Thanks partly to a cash infusion from the U.S. government, its debt assets increased by $11.2 billion to $14.096 billion to provide liquidity to get through the crisis. Much of this was applied to pay employees to retain them for the (hoped-for) recovery. With these debt assets earning at an (after-tax) interest rate of 0.8 percent ($112.77 million) and with a (risky) required return for operations of 11 percent, Residual income = $112.77 − 0.11 × 14,096 = −$1,437.79 million With the assets at risk in operations, they were yielding negative residual income, the cost of insurance against a further downturn. After the Covid pandemic, Airbus, the European aircraft manufacturer, announced in May 2022 that it was amassing €10 billion against future crises. “I would call it a kind of insurance policy,” its chief financial officer told the Financial Times . However, shareholders cannot have a liability to themselves. They are part of the shareholders’ claim. Table 5.2 corrects the reported equity for JPMorgan Chase & Co. (JPM). The corrected equity is not only that for the leverage calculation, but also that for the book value in the residual income calculation for valuation. Dividends payable are usually quite small and often can’t be found in footnotes; if they cannot be found, they can be ignored. Common equity 15,143 [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn22.png] Keeping a Book: The Reformulated Income Statement The reformulated income statement separates income statement items pertaining to a business’s operating activities from those pertaining to the financing of the business, as with the reformulated balance sheet. The U.S. GAAP income statement is typically a lot closer to what we want than the balance sheet. Table 5.4 gives the 2023 GAAP income statement for Microsoft followed by a reformulated statement. Table 5.4 Microsoft Corporation GAAP and reformulated income statement for 2023 GAAP income statement INCOME STATEMENTS – USD ($) shares in millions, $ in millions 12 months ended Jun 30, 2023 Jun 30, 2022 Jun 30, 2021 Revenue $ 211,915 $ 198,270 $ 168,088 Cost of revenue 65,863 62,650 52,232 Gross margin 146,052 135,620 115,856 Research and development 27,195 24,512 20,716 Sales and marketing 22,759 21,825 20,117 General and administrative 7,575 5,900 5,107 Operating income [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn23.png] 83,383 69,916 Other income, net 788 333 1,186 Income before income taxes 89,311 83,716 71,102 Provision for income taxes [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn24.png] 10,978 9,831 Net income $ 72,361 $ 72,738 $ 61,271 Earnings per share Basic $ 9.72 $ 9.70 $ 8.12 Diluted $ 9.68 $ 9.65 $ 8.05 Weighted average shares outstanding Basic 7,446 7,496 7,547 Diluted 7,472 7,540 7,608 Reformulated summary income statement for 2023 Operating income before tax, reported 88,523 Other operating income (788 − 1,286) * (498) Operating income 88,025 Taxes on operating income (16,950 − 291) 16,659 (18.82% effective tax rate) Operating income after tax ** 71,366 Net financing income before tax, reported 1286 Tax on financing income (at 22.6% *** ) 291 995 Net income 72,361 * Portion of other operating income that is not net interest income (footnote 3) ** Also called net operating profit after tax (NOPAT) or enterprise income *** Combined federal and state statutory rates from tax footnote The reformulated income statement for Microsoft starts with the operating income in the GAAP statement containing operating items: $88,523 million in 2023. We know Microsoft must have interest income and interest expense due to financing activities because it has both debt assets and debt liabilities, but neither is on the income statement. Footnote 3 reports that they are recorded under other income, along with a net losss on derivatives, currency remeasurements and other operating items totalling $498 million. The latter is added to operating income while the former, interest income minus interest expense, is the net interest income before tax of $1,286 million. This is called net financing income in the reformulated statement because it may not just be net interest. Just as preferred stock is debt in the shareholders’ view, so preferred dividends are a financing expense to be included if there is preferred stock. The number is net financing expense if the firm has positive net debt. Both the operating and financing components of the income statement draw taxes, but there is only one tax expense number, $16,950 million, in the income statement. That must be allocated between the income numbers for operating and financing activities so that they both include the tax that they incur. Interest income and interest expense draw tax and reduce tax, respectively, at the statutory rate. On $1,286 million of net financing income, the tax is $291 million for Microsoft at the statutory tax rate of 22.6%. Thus, the after-tax net financing income is $995 million. This is referred to as the effective interest income or expense. The tax rate of 22.6% is a combined federal tax rate of 21.0% and state tax rate of 1.6%, discovered from the tax footnote in table 5.5 where the federal and state rates are highlighted. With total tax of $16,950 million on the income statement, the tax allocated to the operating income then is $16,950 − 291 = $16,659. This results in an after-tax operating income of $71,366 million, which is sometimes also called net operating profit after tax (NOPAT) or enterprise income. (Note that a new tax of 1% on stock repurchases in the United States in 2023 is a tax on financing activities.) Table 5.5 Microsoft Corporation tax rate from tax footnote, 2023 Year ended June 30, 2023 2022 2021 Federal statutory rate [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn25.png] 21.0% 21.0% Effect of: Foreign earnings taxed at lower rates (1.8)% (1.3)% (2.7)% Impact of intangible property transfers 0.0% (3.9)% 0.0% Foreign-derived intangible income deduction (1.3)% (1.1)% (1.3)% State income taxes, net of federal benefit [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn26.png] 1.4% 1.4% Research and development credit (1.1)% (0.9)% (0.9)% Excess tax benefits relating to stock-based compensation (0.7)% (1.9)% (2.4)% Interest, net 0.8% 0.5% 0.5% Other reconciling items, net 0.5% (0.7)% (1.8)% Effective rate 19.0% 13.1% 13.8% We now have the net income of $72,361 million separated into two components. The shareholders have earned $71,366 million from the business and $995 million on net debt, both after tax. Note that, while the tax rate is 22.6% on the net financing income, it is only 18.82% on operating income. That incorporates the value added from the firm’s tax planning to reduce taxes (legally) by taking advantage of tax credits and shifting operations to low-tax jurisdictions, for example. Beginning in 2025, U.S. firms will be required to detail how the effective tax rate differs from the statutory tax rate. Income Statement Leverage Net interest on net debt is reported in the reformulated income statement, giving rise to another leverage measure. Income statement leverage is the amount of net income that is determined by net financing expense. Reading from the reformulated statement for Microsoft, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn27.png] With negative after-tax net financing expense, Microsoft has negative income statement leverage: Financing activities add to the net income rather than reducing it. If the firm has positive net debt, the measure is positive and its reciprocal, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn28.png] gives an indication of the risk of financing expense from leverage reducing net income, with a lower measure indicating higher risk. In the extreme, net financing expense could be larger than operating income so that net income becomes a loss. This is similar to the interest coverage measure for debt analysis, but in a form amenable to equity analysis. Table 5.6 gives the reformulated income statement for Chesapeake Energy Corporation whose balance sheet we saw in table 5.3 . Note that preferred dividends (that are not tax deductible) are treated as a financing expense and added to net interest expense. Table 5.6 Reformulated income statement for Chesapeake Energy Corporation, 2014 Operating LOSS before tax 18,919 Taxes on operating income (−4,463 + 66) ( 4,397 ) (negative taxes) Operating LOSS after tax 14,552 Net interest expense before tax 179 Tax on interest expense (37.1%) (66) 113 Preferred dividends 171 Net LOSS to common equity 14,806 [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn30.png] Since net income is a loss, the standard leverage measure cannot be calculated. However, the inverse, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn29.png] , can. But keep in mind the different interpretation: A negative amount indicates higher leverage, not lower, as the leverage increases the loss. During the year, the stock price declined by 30.2 percent. Ouch! Separating Operating Profitability from Financing Profitability Reformulated statements do more than identify financing leverage. They also separate profitability of the business operations from that for financing activities. For operations, this is done by matching the after-tax operating income (OI) in the reformulated income statement to the net operating assets (NOA) in the reformulated balance sheet to yield the return on net operating assets (RNOA): [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn31.png] As with ROE, if NOA has changed significantly over the year, the denominator should be the average NOA for the year. For financing profitability, the after-tax net financing expense in the reformulated income statement is matched to the net debt in the balance sheet to yield a percentage net borrowing cost (NBC): [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn32.png] If the firm has net financing income (on negative net debt), the latter is the return on net financial assets: net financing income relative to net financial assets. With this reformulation, we get a clean measure of the profitability of the business (RNOA) uncontaminated by financing items, and a clean measure of the net borrowing cost (NBC). Clean measures are vital to understanding the value generated in the business, as we will see. This is our first step in fundamental analysis and its integrity rests on having reformulated financial statements. The first part of the action box below calculates these measures for Microsoft and Chesapeake, along with the overall profitability of the equity (ROE). Value Investing in Action Measuring Profitability for Microsoft and Chesapeake The following measures convey the profitability for the equity (ROE), the profitability for the operations (RNOA), and the profitability of the financing activities. Microsoft 2023 Chesapeake 2014 [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn33.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn34.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn35.png] = 43.45% = −97.77% [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn39.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn37.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn38.png] = 56.96% = −56.44% [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn36.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn40.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn41.png] = − 2.41% = 2.67% Average balance sheet numbers over a period would be used in denominators if there have been significant changes over the period. The ROE financing leverage equation (introduced in the next section) ties these profitability measures together and explains the ROE: Microsoft, 2023: ROE = 56.96% + (-0.248 × [56.96 % −2.41%]) = 43.45% Chesapeake, 2015: ROE = −56.44% + (0.703 × [–56.44% − 2.67%]) = −97.77% Note that leverage is at the beginning of the period, not the end. We now turn to how leverage affects accounting numbers. The effect can be pernicious. Beware of accounting measures affected by leverage . We’ll look, in turn, at the effect on ROE, earnings growth, EPS, P/E, and P/B. Leverage and ROE Risk and return go together so, as leverage increases equity risk, one would think it should also increase the ROE. And so it does. But is that return to be paid for? The answer is: NO. ROE is determined by financing leverage, the profitability of operating activities (RNOA) and the profitability of financing activities (NBC). An ROE leveraging equation that is always true ties these together: ROE = RNOA + LEV × [RNOA − NBC] (1)  (2)    (3) The equation says that ROE is determined by three factors: Profitability of operations: RNOA Financial leverage: LEV = Net Debt/Common Equity, at the beginning of the period Operating spread: RNOA − NBC If a firm has zero leverage, its ROE equals its RNOA. But added positive leverage increases ROE, provided RNOA is greater than the net borrowing cost. The proviso is important: If the spread [RNOA-NBC] goes negative, ROE is less than RNOA. This is so-called unfavorable leverage. That is the risk with leverage: the risk that the firm can’t cover its borrowing cost. You see the leverage equation in action in the second part of the action box above. For Microsoft, leverage is negative, so ROE is less than RNOA. You might think that an ROE of 43.45% is impressive, but that obscures a much higher return of 56.96% in the business. ROE can be seen as the return on a portfolio with an investment in the business and an investment in net financial assets, with the lower return on net debt weighing down the overall return. RNOA is the number to focus on when evaluating value added, not ROE. With Chesapeake, you see leverage working unfavorably. An RNOA of −56.44% is bad enough, but that is magnified with an ROE of −97.7% due to an unfavorable spread over the borrowing cost. Leverage risk in action. Ouch! Microsoft has $111,256 million of debt assets (“cash”) on its balance sheet. Firms should not hold cash invested in T-bills and the like if they have no use for it in making business investments. Any dummy can buy a T-Bill, so pass the cash out to shareholders who might have better use for it. What would be the effect on ROE forecasted for 2024 if Microsoft maintained the same RNOA as in 2023 but decided to pay a special dividend or buy back stock with $50 billion from this cash pile? The next action box shows how the leveraging equation is applied to give the answer. With ROE 2024 increasing from 44.03% to 57.35%, Microsoft looks more profitable. But that is due to payout that increases leverage but does not add value. RNOA remains at 56.96%. That is the number that counts. Value Investing in Action Analyzing the Effect of a Special Dividend or Share Repurchase on ROE Microsoft Corporation Here are the summary 2023 balance sheets before and after the payout: BEFORE AFTER NOA 157,341 157,341 Net debt ( 48,882 ) 1,118 Equity 206,223 156,223 Book leverage −0.237 0.007 With the net debt and equity increased by $50 billion, the leverage turns positive. The leverage equation gives the effect on a forecast of ROE for 2024 if RNOA and NBC remain the same: BEFORE [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn44.png] AFTER [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn45.png] Now to the first Teaser at the beginning of the chapter: If you were on the compensation committee of a corporate board and the CEO proposed that you reward her for achieving higher ROE, would you agree? Maximize return on equity: Sounds good, doesn’t it? Well, NO. The next day the CEO would borrow and apply the funds to a stock repurchase, increasing leverage and ROE. She should be incentivized to add value in operations. Leverage and Valuation But hold it! ROE enters our valuation model: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn46.png] where r E is the required return for equity. So, an increase of ROE should increase Value , right? Well, again, NO: An increase in leverage also increases the required return for equity, r E . We see this through the leveraging equation: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn47.png] With both ROE and r E increasing, they cancel out to leave Value unchanged. This is financing irrelevance in action. Beware of paying for ROE generated by leverage . The next action box demonstrates the point with “no-growth” valuations of Microsoft at the end of 2023, before and after the $50 billion cash payout. The valuation is based on the forecast of ROE for 2024 with the growth rate set to zero. This is the no-growth valuation to challenge the market price in the last chapter. While ROE increases from 44.03% to 57.35%, the required equity return increases from 7.77% to 8.01%. Thus, the calculated cum-dividend value is unchanged. It finesses another point: Leverage increases growth, as we will soon see, but it is not growth to be paid for. Value Investing in Action Financing Leverage Increases Expected ROE but Does Not Increase Value No-growth Valuation for Microsoft BEFORE $50 billion payout: Inputs [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn48.png] The no-growth equity value is $1,169,148 million so leverage with this value is [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn49.png] Set your hurdle rate for the firm, r Firm , to 8.0%. Thus, with negative leverage: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn50.png] Residual Income 2024 = (0.4403 − 0.0777) × $206,223 = $74,785 million No-growth valuation [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn52.png] AFTER $50 billion payout: Inputs [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn53.png] Residual Income 2024 = (0.5735 − 0.0801) × $156,223 = $77,088 No-growth valuation [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn55.png] Shareholders also have the dividend, so Cum-dividend Value 2023 = $1,119,148 + 50,000 = $1,169,148 (Allow for rounding error) Leverage and Earnings Growth In the last chapter we targeted growth expectations in the market price. But we must be careful, for leverage increases growth but it is not growth to be paid for. Beware of paying for growth generated by leverage . There is a growth leveraging equation that is similar in format to that for ROE: g Net Income,t = g OI,t + [ Earnings Leverage t −1 × ( g OI,t − g NFE,t )] The leverage here, earnings leverage , is the income statement leverage measure we saw earlier in this chapter. That leverage adds to net income (earnings) growth, with the amount of the increase determined by the spread between the growth in operating income from the business relative to the growth in net financing expense. The action box on the next page demonstrates this. To keep it simple, the example is for a hypothetical firm, Micky Mouse Corp. The example starts with the firm with no leverage where expected earnings growth and EPS growth is at 10 percent per year. Since forecasted residual earnings are zero, the firm is worth its book value, or $10 per share. But then a share repurchase, to buy back half of the 10 million shares outstanding at a fair value of $10 each, is financed by borrowing. This increases the book leverage from zero to 100 percent. The added leverage increases the equity required return from 10 percent to 15 percent. The value per share of $10 does not change, an illustration that share transactions at fair value do not add value. But the growth rate in both earnings and EPS increases from 10 percent to over 11.5 percent. Remember: Leverage increases earnings growth but does not increase value. Beware of buying earnings growth generated by leverage . In the last chapter, active investing was cast as a matter of challenging the growth rate implicit in the market price. But that growth rate was partly affected by leverage, and that is growth we don’t want to pay for. The finesse is demonstrated in the unlevered valuation section below and in case 8, which repeats case 6 that elicited the market’s growth rate for Microsoft, but now strips out the effect on leverage on the growth rate. Value Investing in Action Leverage and Earnings Growth for Micky Mouse Corp.
