But a borrowing applied to repurchase stock also increases EPS, as the increase of forward EPS from $1.00 to $1.50 for Micky Mouse Corp. demonstrates. That can be deceiving: Surely, more earnings for each share is good? No: Our Micky Mouse Corp. example shows that there is no change in the price per share. Beware of an increase in EPS from a share repurchase . In a 2003 survey of top managers, 76 percent of respondents said that increasing earnings per share was a factor in making stock repurchases. Those managers are either ignorant of the value implications of a stock repurchase or are playing games with the naive investor who thinks that more EPS is more value. A value investor should not be one of those investors. Leverage and P/E People often claim that leverage increases the P/E ratio. With leverage increasing earnings growth, you might think so. But the opposite is true: Leverage deceases P/E. That is demonstrated with the drop in the forward P/E from 10.0 to 6.67 with the increase in leverage for Micky Mouse Corp. To appreciate this, we must understand the difference between the common levered P/E and an unlevered P/E (sometimes called the enterprise P/E): [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn60.png] OI is operating income (after tax) on the reformulated statement, sometimes called enterprise earnings. As with the ROE and growth leveraging equations, there is a P/E leveraging equation expressed in terms of E/P, the reciprocal of P/E: Forward levered E/P = Unlevered E/P + [Market Leverage × (Unlevered E/P − NBC)] Leverage (now defined as market leverage) increases the levered forward E/P and thus reduces the P/E. The action next box goes to Chesapeake Energy in 2017 when it had returned to profitability. Value Investing in Action Levered and Unlevered P/E for Chesapeake Energy Corporation, 2017 Inputs: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn62.png] Forward earnings (from analysts’ forecasts) $0.707 billion Net financing expense 0.417 Net borrowing cost = 4.29% Forward operating income 1.124 billion P/E ratios: Levered Forward P/E = 5.9/0.707 = 8.35 (E/P = 11.98%) Unlevered (enterprise) P/E = 15.6/1.124 =13.88 (E/P = 7.20%) Reconciling leveraging equation: Levered forward E/P = 7.20 + [1.644 × (7.20 − 4.29)] = 11.98% (a P/E of 8.35) With high leverage of 1.644, Chesapeake’s levered P/E is 8.35. It looks cheap. But its unlevered P/E is 13.88. The simple investors of chapter 2 might buy this “cheap” P/E of 8.35, but they would be loading up on leverage risk. Beware of buying low P/E ratios . In 2013, value investors favored Apple, Inc. (AAPL) and were duly rewarded with high subsequent returns. Its (levered) P/E at the time was a low 11.3. No worries—this is not due to leverage. As Apple had no debt obligations at the time (only debt assets), the firm was negatively levered. And so, the unlevered P/E was even lower at 7.6. Now, that is tempting! The second Teaser question at the beginning of the chapter is answered: Leverage reduces the P/E. A low P/E due to leverage is a trap. In buying this P/E, you are likely loading up on risk. Leverage and P/B The levered and unlevered P/B ratios are calculated as: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn64.png] The P/B leveraging equation explains the difference: Levered P/B = Unlevered P/B + [Book Leverage × (Unlevered P/B − 1)] So, if the unlevered P/B is greater than 1.0, the levered P/B is higher. It looks as if you should pay more for book value, but under the financing irrelevance principle, leverage does not increase the price. If the unlevered P/B is less than 1.0, the levered P/B is lower, but this does not change price. This, again, is leverage giving a false impression. The action box below calculates the two P/B ratios for Microsoft in 2023 and their reconciliation according to the leveraging equation. The Micky Mouse Corp. box demonstrates a special case where leverage does not affect the levered price-to-book ratio: unlevered P/B = 1.0. You see that in the leveraging equation, but for all other levels of unlevered P/B, leverage affects the levered P/B, the usual number bandied about. Value Investing in Action Levered and Unlevered P/B Multiples: Microsoft Corporation, 2023 ($millions) Book Market P/B NOA 157,341 2,437,642 15.5 Net debt (48,882) (48,882 ) 1.0 Equity 206,223 2,486,524 12.1 Book leverage −0.237 The Reconciliation: Levered P/B = 15.5 + [–0.237 × (15.5 −1.0)] = 12.1 Protection from Buying Leverage: Unlevered Valuation Our leverage principles at the beginning of this chapter told us that the value-added earnings from net debt (residual income from debt) is zero. So, let’s strip out the residual income on debt from our valuation model. The valuation model we have embraced thus far is a levered valuation model: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn67.png] The unlevered valuation model substitutes value-added earnings in the operations, residual income from operations, for the levered residual earnings: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn68.png] But, as NOA − Net Debt = Equity Book Value, [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn69.png] As the risk is now for the firm without leverage, the required return is r Firm rather than r E and the growth rate is that for the residual income from operations, OI t − r Firm .NOA t −1 . As with the levered valuation, the model can be restated in terms of profitability relative to the required return: [IMAGE: ../images/ch05eqn71.png] We are now protected from buying growth due to leverage. Further, r E changes each period as leverage changes, so a levered valuation requires us to change r E each period ahead as leverage changes. We can do that with the leverage equation, but that is a pain. Now we don’t have to worry about it: Changes in leverage are irrelevant to value. Again, we use this model not to do valuations but instead to challenge the market price. The market price, inserted on the left-hand side of the model, elicits the market’s implied growth rate, which is now the relevant value-adding growth rate from the business. Case 8, below, goes through the same exercise for Microsoft, yielding a similar declining growth path as in the last chapter. Can Leverage Add Value? Yes, sometimes. Just as our leverage principles tell us that leverage does not add value when financing transactions are made at fair value, they also tell us that value is added (or lost) when that is not so. Stock Repurchases and Stock Issues If a firm repurchases its shares at a price less than fair value, it adds value to the shares of the remaining shareholders. The example at the beginning of this chapter shows this: A shareholder with a share worth $50 is bought out for $40, adding $5 to the value of each of the remaining shares. Just as we, as shareholders, add value to our own portfolio when we buy a cheap stock, so too can our firm, on our behalf. A warning goes to those who sell their shares in a stock repurchase: Be careful when anyone asks to buy your shares, but be especially careful if it is the firm’s management. They might have more information than you! Many share repurchases just pass out cash (at fair value), but the value investor can check this by getting a grasp on value. If the firm repurchases shares at more than fair value, it destroys value for the shareholders who remain. Sadly, this is not uncommon. Again, the value investor is aware. Correspondingly, issuing shares at a price greater than value adds value for existing shareholders. After the financial crises and some of its own missteps, Deutsche Bank (DB) was required by regulators to issue shares to strengthen its capital ratios. It did so at low prices, with P/B only 0.3. Ouch! Nikola Corporation (NKLA), the clean energy truck maker, announced a stock issue of $100 million in 2023 at a 20 percent discount from the market price, a dilution for the existing shareholders who had already experienced a 70 percent decline in the stock price over the previous year. Ouch! See the first vignette on the next page. The third Teaser question at the beginning of the chapter: Yes, share repurchases increase EPS but they do not add value unless the shares are repurchased cheaply at below fair value. If overpriced, the share repurchase destroys value. See the following vignettes. A Vignette Reverse Dilution with Stock Repurchases? During the bubble of the late 1990s, many firms paid employees with stock options, resulting in the issue of shares at less than market price when the options were exercised. That dilutes the equity of existing shareholders. In response, firms repurchased the same number of shares “to reverse dilution,” they said. Microsoft, a big issuer of stock options, followed the practice with the same justification. But do the repurchases reverse dilution? Certainly not! Yes, the exercise of stock options dilutes equity (see chapter 9 ) but, unless the repurchase is made at less than fair value, a repurchase cannot reverse the dilution effect. The number of shares outstanding stays the same, but a repurchase at fair market value has no effect on equity value. In the 1990s, the repurchases were made at very high prices (greater than fair value), which actually increased dilution with consequent loss to shareholders. This goes against the simple principle “buy low, sell high.” Rather, this is selling low then buying high. Microsoft made these repurchases when its per-share stock price (pre-split) ranged from $80 to $100, only to fall over 50% subsequently. In the Inflation Reduction Act of 2022, the U.S. Congress put a 1% tax on stock repurchases (above the capital gains tax already in place). An exception was made for repurchases that “reverse dilution from stock options.” Bad thinking. Will we now see more diluting with stock options to avoid the 1% tax? A Vignette Dilution from Issuing Shares: AMC Entertainment Holdings, Inc. (AMC) AMC is a large movie theater chain, with multiplex cinemas in many cities. With theaters closed during the COVID pandemic, the firm became cash constrained, generating negative cash flow from operations in 2020 and 2021. Despite this, AMC became a speculators’ “meme stock” whose price became quite inflated. In response, the firm issued $2 billion in stock, a sensible thing to do when a stock is overvalued. The firm sought even more cash by issuing shares but ran out of authorized shares to do so. It also sought shareholder approval to increase authorized shares but, with the share price in decline, the shareholders disapproved, presumably fearing dilution. Still cash constrained, the firm decided to pay dividends to common shareholders with preferred shares. That is curious: Preferred shares are obligations that common shareholders must satisfy, so common shareholders now have a claim on themselves: no value added. Further, preferred dividends are not tax-deductible at the corporate level in the United States, but shareholders must pay tax on the dividends at the individual level. Stranger still: The stock price jumped 50 percent on the next trading day. The preferred share issue does not raise cash to elevate the firm’s severe liquidity, nor does it add value for shareholders. So, what’s going on? The best guess would be that the firm plans to issue additional preferred shares to other investors for cash. This requires preferred dividends to be paid, a cost to current shareholders, and it reduces their equity—all the more so if the preferred stock comes with conversion privileges. Debt Arbitrage Until 2010 Microsoft had no debt obligations, only debt assets of $36.8 billion in cash and short-term investments. But it issued $5 billion of debt at an after-tax borrowing rate of only 0.75 percent, almost the same rate as U.S. Treasuries, and continued to do so until 2016. With $36.8 billion in “cash” and strong free cash flow, Microsoft had no need of cash, it seemed. Perhaps the firm saw an arbitrage opportunity, issuing debt when it was cheap with a low interest rate. If interest rates subsequently went up, Microsoft could buy back the debt at a lower price, booking a gain. Or, having borrowed at a lower rate, it would be saved the higher interest rate if it needed to borrow in the future. Debt arbitrage. The firm then bought back its stock at a low $24 per share: A double arbitrage. Investors gave credence to this scenario, with the stock price jumping 5.4 percent on the announcement of the debt issue. There is a more distasteful way of gaining from debt: Issue debt to make the firm riskier (thus forcing down the debt price), and then redeem the debt at the lower price for a gain. This is called ripping off the debtholders. Debt investors beware: debtholders and shareholders have a conflict of interest. Covenants protecting existing debtholders may offer protection. Should a Value Investor Ever Buy Leverage? For reasons that should be abundantly clear from this chapter, value investors shun leverage. However, there is a situation where they might take on leverage. If convinced that a stock is underpriced, they might borrow to invest in the stock, levering up on private account. But a firm can lever on the shareholder’s behalf: So an investor can buy an underpriced firm with its leverage, with the same upside potential as levering on private account. But, beware. Stick to the adage that buying leverage is risky. There is also a downside that is magnified with leverage. Indeed, if the investor thinks the shares are cheap but the firm is highly levered, she might buy the firm’s debt to hedge on private account. This is active risk management to which we will return in chapter 8 . Value Investing Principles Beware of paying for leverage, for leverage adds risk. Financing activities do not generate value (except in special circumstances). Beware of buying accounting numbers generated by leverage: ROE, earnings growth, EPS, P/E, P/B . You may be falling into a trap. Leverage generates earnings growth, but not growth to be paid for. The value investing book keeps balance sheets and income statements that separate operating activities from financing activities. Always start fundamental analysis with reformulated financial statements that separate operating activities from financing activities. Building a Value Investing Engine This book is for practical value investing based on sound thinking. The Value Investing in Action boxes urge you in that direction. But you look for a comprehensive value investing tool that incorporates all the features involved. Now it is time to begin building such a tool to which you can add and make refinements as you progress through the rest of this book. You can do that within a spreadsheet program or another preferred platform; you can bring Python programming to the task. The architectural structure of your tool will obey the accounting equations we have laid out thus far and to which we will keep adding, for obeying those equations is critical for investing with integrity. When the tool is completed, by the time you get to the end of the book, you will be enormously proud of your creation. Building the tool will yield a product that will be of significant help to you in your investing. Value investing in action. To begin: Identify a location in the spreadsheet for a share price and one for shares outstanding so that total equity market price (per-share price x shares outstanding) can be calculated. This is the price you are challenging. Build in the architecture of reformulated balance sheets and income statements. This is the starting point in keeping a book: If you fail to separate operating from financing activities, you will fail in identifying the value generation in a business. With this design in place, see if you can develop a program to download the financial statements from the SEC’s EDGAR site into the template for reformulated statements. Of course, this can also be done manually. Are there programs to scrape footnote information such as tax rates into the spreadsheet? You can also use the XBRL filings. Enter the formulas for combining the relevant cells in the reformulated statements for calculating the various profitability measures in this chapter. Add the formulas for the various leveraging equations, again connecting the relevant cells. With these equations built in, you can get answers by the press of a button. And you will be able to address What If questions, such as Microsoft’s $50 billion special dividend or the Micky Mouse stock repurchase. Such sensitivity analysis becomes important as we proceed. Set up the reverse engineering exercise to get the market’s growth rate for residual income, now on an unlevered basis. Case 8 focusing on Microsoft will guide you. Use the Goal Seek feature in Excel to infer the market’s weights for a declining growth rate. There is Python code to build engines available through the website for the book. More generous authors might provide the completed product you are developing. You could then just “do it” with the press of a button. But, sorry, that would not really be generous. There is no substitute for building your own product and taking possession of it. In learning by doing, you will see value investing in action. You can also add further bells and whistles, whether they be substantive features or, for many of you, the recent innovations in data science for data extraction and analysis. That will move you toward what is sometimes referred to as quantamentals. A word of caution: Automation helps with the mechanical aspects of fundamental analysis but is no substitute for personal interaction with the business and the data that comes from it. Good judgment is important, and a machine cannot provide it. Do not work by pressing buttons on the machine without good thinking. There are many machine methods that can be added to your engine, including machine learning and artificial intelligence. But do not let the machine take over. The responsibility for investing rests on you, the investor. The Exercise Book With exercises on the website, you see more value investing in action and the traps in buying leverage. The Case Book Case 8 conducts the same reverse engineering exercise for Microsoft as in case 6, but now on an unlevered basis. That removes the leverage effects that have concerned us so much in this chapter. Case 8: Microsoft: Challenging The Market Price with Unlevered Valuation What Is the Market’s View of the Future of the Business? Leverage affects the growth rate but typically does not add value. Leverage also adds risk, to leave price unchanged in most cases. For a levered firm, the growth expectation extracted from a market price is growth that we don’t want to buy: Beware of buying growth generated by leverage. It is value from growth in the business (from operations) that we wish to buy, and it is the market’s forecast of that growth that we wish to challenge. In case 6 focusing on Microsoft, we extracted the market’s expectation of growth from the market price. However, Microsoft is significantly negatively levered, so that is a levered growth rate. So now, challenge the market’s implied expectations of growth in the business! Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely. They will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 6 Accounting for Profitability Chapter 6 Accounting for Profitability Beware of paying for profitability T his chapter continues the fundamental analysis begun in the previous chapters. In the spirit of chapter 4 , the analysis does not seek to determine a valuation but instead to challenge the market’s growth forecasts. Remember the caveats about buying leverage in chapter 5 and focus on growth in value added from operations. The profitability of operations is a determinant, so this chapter focuses on the accounting for profitability. The analysis continues over subsequent chapters, asking the question: Does an understanding of fundamentals tell us that the market’s pricing of growth is appropriate, too high, or too low? That triggers a BUY or SELL decision. This is done under the mantra: Beware of Paying for Growth, for Growth Is Risky. The epigraph to this chapter may puzzle you: Beware of Paying for Profitability. Sounds strange! Surely more profitable firms are worth more? Don’t value investors insist on buying profitable companies? Well, there are subtleties involved. The first issue is to have an appropriate, clean measure of profitability, so the chapter begins by differentiating the return on net operating assets (RNOA) measure introduced in the last chapter from others often used. It shows how this measure elicits the value added in a business. But, even with this clean measure, there are dangers in buying profitability. The value investing book has both an income statement and a balance sheet, and both are involved in identifying value added. By keeping to the discipline of the book, the investor not only uncovers value but is also protected from mistakes, including the mistake of paying too much for profitability. The teasers here cue us. Teasers Return on Assets (ROA) ROA is the most common measure of the profitability of a business, and features prominently in textbooks, finance portals such as Yahoo Finance and Google Finance, and in analysts’ equity research reports. Is ROA a good measure of profitability? Return on Tangible Assets Analysts and commentators sometime strip out intangible assets such as goodwill when calculating profitability to get to return on tangible assets. Is this appropriate? Profitability from Acquisitions A firm acquires another firm in a mergers and acquisitions (M&A) transaction. Subsequently, its profitability declines. Was that a failed acquisition? Earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) A good measure of value added? The Profitability Driver, RNOA Value-added residual income from operations (ReOI) is calculated as ReOI t = (RNOA t − r Firm ) × NOA t−1 That is, ReOI is determined by operating profitability, RNOA, and the amount of net operating assets (NOA). It is [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn02.png] on which we focus in this chapter. As in the previous chapter, the measure is identified from reformulated income statements and balance sheets. Measurement is all-important if value is to be captured through sound analysis. It is essential that we use the appropriate measure of profitability. To stress the importance of the RNOA measure, let’s compare it with other profitability measures, all of which miss out on some aspect of value generation. The first is the popular measure of return on assets (ROA), which features prominently in textbooks and on internet finance portals: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn03.png] In the last 50 years in the United States, the average ROA was about 6.8 percent. The average rate on the 10-year Treasury bond was slightly lower: 6.4 percent. Surely, firms earn more on their operations than the risk-free rate? Yes, they do: It is the ROA measure that is the problem. From the reformulated income statements in the last chapter, you know that the numerator here, earnings before tax-adjusted net interest expense, is correct, and is equal to the after-tax operating income in the numerator of RNOA. However, the total assets in the denominator include debt assets, which are not part of operations. They are part of net debt in the financing activities. They earn a relatively low return at the prevailing money market rate, and typically lower than the return from the business. Further, total assets exclude operating liabilities in the business. If a firm holds $500 million in inventory but has accounts payable in the same amount to the suppliers of the inventory, there is zero net investment in inventory. We will see how operating liabilities can generate value. We now have the answer to the first teaser question at the beginning of the chapter: ROA is not a good profitability measure. The nearby boxes below explain the ROA and RNOA calculations for Tesla, Inc. (TSLA) and compare the two measures for selected firms. ROA and RNOA for Tesla, Inc. EBIT is sometimes also used in an enterprise P/E ratio, [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn06.png] , and as a measure of performance. Operations draw taxes, but this measure fails to capture the value lost from income taxes. Sorry, we must all pay our taxes! And it fails to capture value-adding tax planning activities. With tax planning, Microsoft had an effective tax rate of only 18.82 percent in chapter 5 , yielding higher after-tax income than tax at the higher statutory rate. However, EBIT is unaffected. If the corporate tax rate changes from 35 percent to 21 percent, as it did in 2017 in the United States, EBIT is unaffected—but surely, firms add more value from their operations with lower taxes on those operations. The value investor shuns EBIT. An even more pernicious measure is earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation and amortization (EBITDA). This measure is commonly used for an enterprise multiple: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn07.png] . However, depreciation and amortization are as much of an expense, a value loss, as wages: When plant and equipment depreciate physically and become obsolescent, they lose value. Here, again, is Warren Buffett: References to EBITDA make us shudder . . . does management think the tooth fairy pays for capital expenditures? —Warren Buffett, 2000 letter to Berkshire Hathaway shareholders Trumpeting EBITDA is a particularly pernicious practice. Doing so implies that depreciation is not truly an expense, given that it is a “non-cash” charge. That’s nonsense. In truth, depreciation is a particularly unattractive expense because the cash outlay it represents is paid upfront, before the asset acquired has delivered any benefits. Imagine, if you will, that at the beginning of this year a company paid all of its employees for the next ten years. . . . Would anyone care to argue that the recording of the (amortized) expense in years two to ten would simply be a book-keeping formality? —Warren Buffett, 2002 letter to Berkshire Hathaway shareholders The next box illustrates the danger of EBITDA and answers the fourth Teaser question. A Vignette EBITDA and the WorldCom Con In the 1990s, telecom firms were laying down large amounts of fiber to accommodate the increasing transmissions from growing internet traffic. These were capital expenditures to be depreciated against the revenues earned from these transmissions. Telecom analysts of the day focused on EBITDA, ignoring the depreciation of this fiber and the threat of obsolescence brought by competing (wireless) transmission. Price was said to be a multiple of EBITDA. WorldCom, the second-largest long-distance telephone company in the United States, played an accounting trick. They capitalized $3.8 billion in normal operating expense to the balance sheet, increasing pretax income by the same amount. WorldCom thus looked more profitable, becoming a darling of analysts. But there was a secondary effect: Those capitalized expenses then had to be amortized, which reduced future earnings—yet, as analysts focused on multiples of EBITA, those amortizations did not enter into analysts’ valuations. WorldCom executives went to jail. As with all the vignettes in this book, check Wikipedia for more detail (and perhaps donate to that wonderful nonprofit service?). But do exercise a bit of caution: with volunteers making entries there, occasionally the entries are not quite correct. Other measures carry similar dangers. For example, [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn08.png] Though definitions vary, tangible assets are those with a physical form such as plant, equipment, inventory, and assets with monetary value such as cash and near-cash items on the balance sheet. However, the important point is what tangible assets miss. They do not include goodwill and intangible assets acquired in mergers and acquisitions. The measure is increasingly popular in evaluating banks, with 61 percent of banks reporting the measure in 2020, up from 10 percent in 2001. Nevertheless, including the profits from acquisitions but not the cost of the investment in those acquisitions is perverse, and may well encourage value-destroying acquisitions. Our value-added measure, residual income, says that earnings from investments must be charged at the cost of capital before value is added, and goodwill and acquired intangibles are part of the purchase price of an acquisition. And they are part of the investment base that generates the earnings in a profitability measure. Otherwise, it looks like the firm is getting earnings from nothing. Remember Tyco of chapter 3 . The second teaser question at the start of this chapter is answered. Never strip out goodwill or other intangible assets. The action box below compares the RNOA profitability measure calculated for Microsoft in 2021 with pseudo-profitability measures often used. ROA is way too low. EBIT increases the numerator, but in error. The omission of goodwill with tangible assets increases the profitability, but also in error. A measure sometimes used, return on capital (ROC), yields very high profitability with errors in both the numerator and denominator. RNOA is the measure with integrity, 111.13 percent. This follows from the discipline of keeping a book on the business that separates operating activities from financing activities. Another measure is return on invested capital (ROIC). This the same as RNOA, provided it is calculated correctly. But often it is not. A common calculation is [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn09.png] This adjusts EBIT for taxes, but at the statutory tax rate rather than the effective rate. And the denominator, while taking out cash and equivalents, leaves in other debt assets in short-term investments. More significantly, it does not incorporate operating liabilities. That leads us to an important feature of RNOA. Value Investing in Action RNOA and Alternative Profitability Measures for Microsoft, 2021 [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn10.png] Operating Liability Leverage One feature of RNOA, notably missing from the alternative measures, is the role played by the operating liabilities of a business. These are in the base of RNOA: Net Operating Assets (NOA) = Operating Assets − Operating Liabilities. As you see, including operating liabilities in the base increases RNOA, which captures the economics of the business. The box on the next page has balance sheets for two companies that make this point. They are simple companies with only one asset, inventory—just your kid’s lemonade stand. Both companies have suppliers, but the suppliers of Company A require cash on delivery for shipments. With $400 NOA (in inventory), the shareholder must invest $400 into the business. The suppliers of Company B allow inventory purchases on credit up to $200. Consequently, NOA is $200 and the shareholders invest only $200 in the business. Company A earns operating income of $48, so its RNOA is 12 percent. With the same business, you might calculate RNOA of company B as $48 on $200 of NOA. That is an RNOA of 24 percent, demonstrating the potential leverage from operating liabilities (and the advantage to shareholders if suppliers partly finance the business). But there is no free lunch, as they say. The smart supplier will say: Well, if you want credit, it is going to cost you. Rather than paying $2 per pound for sugar, I will now charge you $2.05 per pound; I, after all, will lose interest on the cash I would have received on earlier payment. That reduces operating income, and you see in the box that income for Company B is $44 rather than $48. Thus RNOA = 22 percent, not 24 percent. Accordingly, operating liabilities lever up RNOA via a denominator effect but reduce RNOA via a numerator effect. The numerator effect is implicit interest: The supplier charges the firm interest for the financing in the form of higher prices for supplies. The overall effect depends on which dominates. Like the leverage equations in chapter 5 , there is a leverage equation in the action box that explains the effect of operating liability leverage on RNOA. Operating liability leverage (OLLEV) increases RNOA, provided that the profitability without the leverage is greater than the implicit borrowing cost. Value Investing in Action Effect of Operating Liability Leverage on Profitability Company A Assets Liability and equity Inventory 400 Liability 0 Equity 400 400 400 Income earned on the income statement = 48 RNOA = ? Company B Assets Liability and equity Inventory 400 Liability 200 Equity 200 400 400 Income earned on the income statement = 44 RNOA = ? Profitability Calculation: Company A: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn11.png] Company B: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn12.png] Operating liabilities lever up RNOA via a denominator effect but reduce RNOA via a numerator effect. The leverage equation is: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn13.png] ROOA, Return on Operating Assets, is the return without operating liabilities. [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn14.png] , Operating Liability Leverage, is the amount of operating liabilities as a component of net operating assets. (Do not confuse OLLEV with operating leverage, the amount of fixed costs relative to variable costs in the income statement.) Financing leverage does not add value if debt is efficiently priced, so the big issue is whether operating liability leverage does. It certainly can, for credit terms with suppliers are part of the pricing that affects value-added operating income. We saw that in the price charged for sugar when credit was granted. But a buyer might have power over suppliers in setting prices, adding value. That could be in negotiated low prices but also in credit terms: Give me 120 days’ credit, says the buyer, and don’t raise your prices! That buyer is called a monopsonist (in contrast to a monopolist setting prices for customers rather than suppliers). Think of Walmart charging suppliers to get their goods onto Walmart’s shelves. Or U.K. supermarkets putting price pressure on dairy produce (it is said) because they have the power of large buyers. Operating liabilities leverage the profitability of operations and can add value . Of course, the operating liabilities equation in the box says that the leverage comes with risk: Just like financing leverage, the spread term could turn negative. For examples of the use of operating liability leverage, see the following vignettes. Vignettes Using Customers’ Money Airbnb, Inc. (ABNB) Airbnb advertises home rentals for travelers and collects payment when customers reserve the home, sometimes months in advance of the rental. The firm then pays the host 24 hours after the guests arrive. Meanwhile, the upfront cash is invested, usually in interest-bearing accounts and short-term bonds. The company reports that it processes about $80 billion in customer deposits annually. It reported $58.5 million in interest in its third quarter of 2022. Correspondingly, it reported a liability, “Funds payable and amounts payable to customers,” of $4.8 billion—59 percent of its operating liabilities. Essentially, the firm is “playing the float” using other people’s money. Expedia Group, Inc. (EXPE) Expedia runs a website for flight, hotel, car, and cruise reservations, collecting cash from customers as they make reservations. For the third quarter of 2022, it held $7.6 billion of travelers’ funds on its balance sheet as a deferred revenue liability and reported $20 million of interest revenue. Starbucks Corporation (SBUX) Starbucks, the coffee house chain, runs an awards program whereby 31 million users (as of 2023) pay cash into an account for coffee purchases by purchasing the Starbucks Card which can then be presented at a store or accessed through an app. That results in a $1.8 billion “Stored value card liability and current portion of deferred revenue” operating liability on its balance sheet (as of 2023), effectively creating a float. It generates sales and interest income for Starbucks, and also “breakage” income from cards not redeemed. The latter amounted to $196 million in income in 2022. As with most operating liabilities, the float does not come without cost: Starbucks gives discounts and other perks to holders of the Starbucks Card. Now look at Apple Inc., a company with extreme operating liability leverage. Table 6.1 reports Apple’s net operating assets from 2015 to 2018 and part of reformulated income statements from 2016 to 2018. (The Apple case below takes this through 2024.) Below the statements, the table makes the calculations that enter the accounting for value to challenge the enterprise market price of $691,396 million. Net operating assets are negative. This is a company with minimum investment in inventory, plant, and other assets; it outsources production of its computers, phones, and other products to suppliers in Asia who invest in the manufacturing. It has large accounts payable to these suppliers, yielding high operating liability leverage. Deferred (unearned) revenues also contribute to the operating liabilities. These are amounts due to customers who have paid or have been billed upfront, but the firm has not yet delivered the goods or services. This is cash that Apple can use but, as with payables, it is part of the value added that comes into the pricing of the product sold; the customer might pay less if cash is required in advance, but the firm might have powers of a monopolist. With high operating liabilities, Apple essentially plays the float, using OPM, “other peoples’ money,” and the negative NOA is the measure of that float. Customers are paying up front and payments to suppliers are delayed, providing cash for Apple to use elsewhere. That is a feature of financial firms like banks, but rare in manufacturing firms. With negative NOA, RNOA cannot be calculated, and neither can OLLEV. But that does not matter: It is value-added earnings that matter. With negative NOA in that calculation, residual income from operations (under the financial statements in table 6.1 ) is greater than operating income! This makes the point that the float earns money for the corporation by enabling them to invest in other assets or pass it out to shareholders in dividends and repurchases so they can invest it at a risk-equivalent 7 percent. Thus, the charge against a negative NOA is actually income: 0.07 × $19,168 = $1,344 million added to residual income in 2018, and so on in the earlier years. The market at the time did not seem to understand this value generation. The market’s growth rate was a negative −1.29 percent, and it saw Apple in decline. Mr. Market had it wrong. A firm requiring cash up front for services generates a float; deferred revenue, an operating liability, captures the float, as seen in Apple’s balance sheet. There is $44.141 billion in (short- and long-term) unearned revenue in Microsoft’s 2021 balance sheet. Below is the Form 10-K footnote that explains the number. Microsoft Corporation: Unearned Revenue Footnote to 2021 Balance Sheet Unearned Revenue Unearned revenue comprises mainly unearned revenue related to volume licensing programs, which may include SA and cloud services. Unearned revenue is generally invoiced annually at the beginning of each contract period for multi-year agreements and recognized ratably over the coverage period. Unearned revenue also includes payments for consulting services to be performed in the future; LinkedIn subscriptions; Office 365 subscriptions; Xbox Live subscriptions; Windows 10 post-delivery support; Dynamics business solutions; Skype prepaid credits and subscriptions; and other offerings for which we have been paid in advance and earn the revenue when we transfer control of the product or service. (Note: SA is Software Assurance) The Apple demonstration introduces an important point that we now explore further: Valuation involves both income statements and balance sheets. Valuation is often seen as a matter of pricing earnings, as in determining a P/E multiple. With Tyco in chapter 3 , we saw one mistake in pricing earnings without involving the balance sheet: The balance sheet must be charged at the cost of capital to understand the value added from earnings. The Apple case demonstrates another reason to involve the balance sheet. In the income statement, it is a question of value added in revenues over expenses, value gained over value given up in trading with customers and suppliers. But value is also determined by how the firm structures its business to add that value, as revealed by the reformulated balance sheet. Operating liability leverage does that with low operating assets and high operating liabilities. However, there are further reasons for “anchoring on the balance sheet.” Economic Profitability and Accounting Profitability An economic profitability measure compares the income from investment in the numerator of a rate-of-return measure to the investment that generates that income in the denominator. That is the “real” return on investment. However, accounting measures typically do not do this: Investments are often missing from the balance sheet. Those are the so-called intangible assets, including brands that result from investing in advertising and promotion, and knowledge assets from investing in research and development (R&D). They also include investments in employees (so-called human capital), in building supply chains and distribution systems, and in customer loyalty programs. These investments are written off against earnings immediately. For the modern company, these are a large portion of their investments. This would appear to be a corruption of a profitability measure: Investments to generate earnings are booked to the numerator, confusing investment with the return to investment. That triggers a warning. Beware: Do not interpret accounting profitability as a measure of real, economic profitability. But is this a problem for valuation? The action box on the next page gives the answer: With assets missing from the balance sheet, accounting profitability is higher but has no effect on value calculated. The Denominator Effect of the Accounting In the box, investment in the two-year project is accounted for under two different accounting treatments. In the first treatment, $1,000 of investment is booked to the balance sheet and depreciated (straight-line) against revenues, yielding an RNOA of 10 percent each year. With a required return of 10 percent, residual income is zero and the project is worth its book value of $1,000. In the second treatment, $200 of the investment is not booked to the balance sheet, but rather expensed to the income statement. (See it as advertising to generate the subsequent revenue.) That reduces depreciation and, on a lower book value, increases subsequent RNOA significantly. The higher RNOA on a lower book value also increases residual income. But does that mean a higher value? No: The higher residual income has a present value of $200 but, when combined with a lower book value of $800, yields the same value of $1,000. The higher RNOA and lower book value cancel out to leave value unchanged. Value Investing in Action Effect of Alternative Accounting Methods on Profitability and Value Investment in a project Initial investment = $1,000; project life = 2 years; r Firm = r Firm = 10% Accounting treatment 1: Book investment to the balance sheet Year 0 1 2 Sales 600 550 Depreciation 500 500 Operating income 100 50 Net operating assets 1,000 500 0 RNOA 10% 10% ReOI (10%) 0 0 Present value of ReOI 0 Firm value 1,000 Accounting treatment 2: Expense $200 of investment Year 0 1 2 Sales 600 550 Depreciation 400 400 Operating income (200) 200 150 Net operating assets 800 400 0 RNOA 25% 37.5% ReOI (10%) 120 110 Present value of ReOI 200 Firm value 1,000 Always use both balance sheet and income statement in valuation. Now look at the next vignette on The Coca-Cola Company. A Vignette The Coca-Cola Company (KO): The Effect of its Brand Missing on the Balance Sheet Much of Coke’s value comes from its ubiquitous brand. This asset has been generated and maintained through more than a century of advertising and promotion. All that expenditure has been expensed to Coke’s income statement. Consequently, the brand asset is not on the balance sheet, so Coke had a levered P/B of 11.3 and an unlevered P/B of 5.2 at the end of 2022. That might suggest that the analyst should correct the balance sheet by booking this asset. Not so: While the missing asset in the denominator increases RNOA, this has no effect on value calculated. Let’s take Coke’s reformulated balance sheet and see what happens when the brand asset is booked. At the time, analysts’ earnings forecasts implied forward operating income for 2023 of $12.06 billion. Let’s set the brand value at $60 billion. Balance Sheet As reported $ billion Capitalizing the brand asset ($60 billion) NOA 55.41 115.41 Net debt 30.55 30.55 Equity 24.86 84.86 RNOA = $12.06/55.41 = 21.77% RNOA = $12.06/115.41 = 10.45% With the brand on balance sheet, RNOA is surely lower: 10.45 percent against 21.77 percent without it. Now calculate an unlevered value with an 8 percent required return and zero growth: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn18.png] Value based on the reported balance sheet: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn19.png] Value based on the adjusted balance sheet: [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn20.png] No difference! The lower RNOA is offset by a higher book value. Always carry the balance sheet with you when valuing profitability. This will protect you from buying profitability generated by the accounting. The Profitability Trap: Protection from Paying Too Much for Profitability Both the simple example and the Coke vignette point to a trap for the unwary: Buying high profitability can be a mistake. You might just be buying a firm with low book value due to the accounting. Profitability (RNOA), by itself, is not an indication of value. It depends on the balance sheet that produces the RNOA. Beware of paying for profitability . The example also reinforces the important principle we saw with Apple: Always use both income statement and balance sheet together to avoid falling into the profitability trap. Anchor on the balance sheet to protect from paying too much for high profitability . Buying profitability is common, even among value investors. They often adhere to the mantra: Only buy profitable companies. That seems to make sense and is a good principle if one has real (economic) profitability in mind. But we don’t see real profitability; we see accounting profitability, and buying accounting profitability is dangerous. Stay on the platform: Value is generated by RNOA in conjunction with anchoring book value. Accounting Primer Accounting for Business Combinations To consolidate the two businesses: (1) Assets and liabilities on the target firm’s balance sheet are brought in at fair value. (2) “Identified intangible assets” are recognized at fair value. – definite-lived intangibles (subject to future amortization) – indefinite-lived intangibles (subject to possible future impairment) (3) Goodwill = Purchase price − ((1) + (2)) (subject to possible future impairment) (1) + (2) + (3) = Purchase price that goes onto the consolidated balance sheet Profitability from Acquisitions We will be evaluating value added in mergers and acquisitions in chapter 11 but, as improvement in profitability is often a gauge of the success of an acquisition, the issue is introduced here. A study by S&P Global Market Intelligence, reported in The Economist in October 2017, found that, in acquisitions from 2001 to 2017, profitability typically fell after an acquisition. Does this tell us that acquisitions typically do not add value? The action box lower down this page investigates this through the acquisition of The Gillette Company by The Procter & Gamble Company (PG). But first review the accounting for business combinations in the Accounting Primer above. With their array of consumer brands, both companies had been attractive to value investors. The action box gives the summary balance sheets for Procter & Gamble before and after the acquisition. You can see a significant increase in PG’s net operating assets and equity from bringing Gillette’s net assets, along with the goodwill and acquired intangibles recognized on the purchase, onto its balance sheet (as in the Accounting Primer). You also see an increase in operating income as the two firms’ earnings are combined with presumed added synergies. However, you also see a big decline in RNOA after purchase, from 25.8 percent to 11.5 percent, supporting the S&P Global Market Intelligence report’s finding. Indeed, residual operating income declined. Oh, no! The acquisition lost considerable value! Well, actually no. The valuations before and after are approximately the same, with the small difference probably due to added integration costs in the first year after the acquisition. Yes, RNOA declines in mergers, but there is more value in the balance sheet with the increase of net operating assets from the acquisition. That’s the answer to the third Teaser. Once again, we see that accounting profitability is not real profitability. But that does not matter for valuation: Anchor on the balance sheet so you will not be misled by the decline in profitability. Always carry the balance sheet with you. Value Investing in Action Value Added in an Acquisition The Procter & Gamble Acquisition of Gillette, 2005 ($millions) Procter & Gamble balance sheets before and after the acquisition: BEFORE AFTER Net operating assets 34,770 93,306 Net debt 16,519 30,561 Equity 18,251 62,745 Operating income, RNOA, and ReOI before and after: BEFORE AFTER Operating income 8,980 10,730 RNOA (on beginning NOA) 25.8% 11.6% Residual operating income (r Firm = 7.0%) 6,537 4,292 Enterprise valuation before acquisition (with a 4 percent GDP growth rate and required return of 7 percent): [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn21.png] Enterprise valuation after acquisition (with a 4 percent GDP growth rate and required return of 7 percent) : [IMAGE: ../images/ch06eqn22.png] The valuations are not quite the same but the difference can be explained by one-off integration costs booked to operating income in 2006. However, the big drops in RNOA and ReOI after the merger do not in themselves indicate a big loss of value. Always use both balance sheet and income statement in valuation. The Numerator Effect of the Accounting Assets are omitted from the balance sheet because they are expensed to the income statement. These expenditures, in R&D and brand building, for example, are investments to gain future revenues, not expenses incurred to gain current revenues. Thus, reported earnings are not a good measure of value gained from current revenues. In accounting terms, these investments are mismatched expenses. The effect is to reduce the numerator of RNOA; it is not a measure of income from past investments. That compounds the denominator problem. Twitter, Inc. (now X Corp.) went to IPO in November 2013, closing on its first trading day priced at 26 times estimated 2014 sales, a price embedding significant growth expectations. The firm was reporting losses (and a negative RNOA) due largely to the expensing of R&D, advertising, and promotion that amounted to 80 percent of revenue. In 2017, profitability continued to be low. Is this a stock to be avoided? (The firm has since been taken private.) Table 6.2 presents the 2017 income statement for Twitter, Inc., along with the reformulated statement. Net income is still negative and the calculated RNOA is −8.02 percent. However, you can see that, on revenue of $2,443 million, R&D and selling and marketing expenses together total to $1,259 million. That is mainly investment to gain future revenue, not the current top-line revenue. The reformulated statement separates out this investment to report a profit margin from current sales (before tax) of 53.1 percent: Twitter is adding value from sales. The analysis task is to evaluate whether the R&D and marketing will add further sales and profit margin in the future. Table 6.2 Income statement for Twitter, Inc., 2017 and reformulated statement Published income statement CONSOLIDATED STATEMENTS OF OPERATIONS – USD ($) shares in Thousands, $ in Thousands 12 Months Ended Dec 31, 2017 Dec 31, 2016 Dec 31, 2015 Income statement [abstract] Revenue $ 2,443,299 $ 2,529,619 $ 2,218,032 Costs and expenses Cost of revenue 861,242 932,240 729,256 Research and development 542,010 713,482 806,648 Sales and marketing 717,419 957,829 871,491 General and administrative 283,888 293,276 260,673 Total costs and expenses 2,404,559 2,896,827 2,668,068 Income (loss) from operations 38,740 (367,208) (450,036) Interest expense (105,237) (99,968) (98,178) Other income (expense), net (28,921) 26,342 14,909 Loss before income taxes (95,418) (440,834) (533,305) Provision (benefit) for income taxes 12,645 16,039 (12,274) Net loss $ (108,063) $ (456,873) $ (521,031) Net loss per share attributable to common stockholders: Basic $ (0.15) $ (0.65) $ (0.79) Diluted $ (0.15) $ (0.65) $ (0.79) Weighted-average shares used to compute net loss per share attributable to common stockholders: Basic 732,702 702,135 662,424 Diluted 732,702 702,135 662,424 Reformulated income statement, 2017 Revenue 2,443 Cost of revenue 861 General and administrative expense 284 1,145 Operating income from sales (before tax) 1,298 Investment: Research & development 542 Sales and marketing 717 1,259 Operating Income (before tax) 39 Profit margin from current sales (before tax) = 1,298/2,443 = 53.1% Now, the calculation of the profit margin is a little tricky. Some of the selling and administrative expenses will be generating current sales. And general and administrative expenses cover a multiple of expenditures, such as bonuses and stock options to employees to retain them to generate future revenue. Unfortunately, disclosures are not sufficient to ascertain what is an investment and what is a current expense. Nevertheless, Twitter is not an unprofitable firm but one that is potentially very profitable should these expensed investments pay off. If so, it will have high income on a low denominator, like Coke. The investor should not automatically avoid it but evaluate that potential for growth. The following are questions to ask when you see expensed investment: What is the science of the R&D program? How likely is it to pay off? An increasing revenue-to-R&D number indicates payoff to R&D. Are the marketing and promotion expenses building a brand or just maintaining the current brand? An increasing revenue-to-advertising expenses ratio indicates payoff. Is the firm building human capital by investing in employees? Staff training programs? Employee incentive schemes? How likely are these to pay off? Will the trained employees leave and go to a competitor? Is the investment in software (expensed) generating customers—Twitter’s investment in its social media platforms, for example? Is the firm developing customer loyalty programs? Rebates for purchases? Frequent flyer miles? A customer loyalty program like Amazon Prime? Are these paying off? Is the development of product distribution systems paying off? Is the firm gaining shelf space at large retailers like Walmart, for example? Is the firm investing in developing supply chains? Will that investment pay off? See the Starbucks vignette. You will have noticed that each of these questions comes with a warning: Will the investments pay off? Each may deliver additional growth but that growth is risky. Beware of buying growth, for growth is risky —and all the more so for investments that are particularly risky. We have the platform for dealing with growth at risk: The place to start is to ask what the market’s view on growth is, as in chapter 4 , then ask if the firm can deliver that growth. Or is the risk to buying that growth just too high? Growth from these investments is particularly speculative and the market can get overexcited, as during the tech bubble of the 1990s, in hot IPO markets, and in the pricing of tech start-ups in 2021. Commentators claim that the problem with accounting rates of return can be solved by booking the expensed investment to the balance sheet, solving both the numerator and denominator problem. Well, there is method in the accounting madness: These investments are particularly speculative and thus risky; the payoffs might not be realized. Does a firm really have an asset that can be booked as collateral? And what would the amortization rates be with such uncertainty? Rather, see the accounting treatment as conveying risk. Chapter 8 takes you there. A Vignette Starbucks Corporation: Investing in Speculative Assets During the 1990s, Starbucks Corporation was trading with considerable growth expectations built into its market price; the P/E was 51 in 1999. Indeed, the firm was opening coffee shops worldwide at a fast pace. However, it was reporting a book rate of return on its operations of only 9 percent, suggesting the firm was not particularly profitable: With a required return of 9 percent, the firm was not adding value with residual income, calling a P/E of 51 into question. However, by expanding stores aggressively, Starbucks was expensing store-opening expenses, advertising, employee training costs, and its development of coffee supply chains from distant premium growers. The book rate of return was depressed by these investments. The question to ask was: Will these investments pay off? The expensing indicated that the growth strategy was risky. As it happened, the strategy paid off, with the book rate of return rising to 52 percent by 2018. But the risky strategy could have gone the other way. Protection from Paying Too Much for Manipulated Earnings You may be thinking: With the emphasis on accounting numbers, I am worried that those numbers might be manipulated. How can I then anchor on them? You are right to be concerned. But working with the accounting book has built-in protection. The final action box of this chapter below picks up on Micky Mouse Corporation from chapter 5 to make the point. Value Investing in Action Protection from Paying for Earnings Generated by Accounting Micky Mouse Corporation r Firm = 10% Pro forma before accounting write-down of inventory: Year 0 1 2 3 4 Net operating assets 100.00 110.00 121.00 133.10 146.41 Common equity 100.00 110.00 121.00 133.10 146.41 Operating income 10.0 11.00 12.10 13.31 RNOA 10% 10% 10% 10% ReOI (10%) 0.0 0.0 0.0 0.0 PV of ReOI 0.00 Value 100.00 Pro forma after accounting write-down of inventory: Inventory written down by 10 means cost of goods sold in Year 1 is 10 lower. Year 0 1 2 3 4 Net operating assets 90.00 110.00 121.00 133.10 146.41 Common equity 90.00 110.00 121.00 133.10 146.41 Operating income 20.00 11.00 12.10 13.31 RNOA 22.2% 10% 10% 10% ReOI (10%) 11.00 0.0 0.0 0.0 PV of ReOI 10.00 Value 100.00 The creation of higher future income by a pure accounting maneuver does not change the valuation. Protect by anchoring on the balance sheet. The box involves a write-down of inventory that is solely an accounting trick with no economic content. The pro forma in the box before the accounting gimmick is the same as that for Micky Mouse Corporation in the last chapter. With an RNOA equal to the required return of 10 percent, future residual income is zero, and thus the equity is worth its book value of 100. The accounting mischief involves writing down inventory by 10, reducing book value to 90. But today’s inventory becomes future cost of goods sold, now reduced by 10. So forward operating income is now up from 10 to 20, and RNOA is now 22.2 percent, up from 10 percent—perhaps that is the CEO’s bonus year, or the firm plans to go IPO. The write-down shifts income for the current year to the next. A diligent analyst would be correct in revising her forecast up to 20. But beware of earnings generated by accounting methods. That same diligent analyst valuing by the book would not be fooled. While both forward RNOA and residual income increase, the value based on the present value of residual income and book value remains at 100. The lower book value cancels the added earnings. Value investing by the book protects from paying for earnings generated by the accounting. The example, once again, demonstrates the importance of valuation using both income statement and balance sheet. In this case, there is an underlying accounting principle that says that one cannot generate earnings with accounting methods without affecting the balance sheet. Anchor on the book value. Value Investing Principles We continue to accumulate principles of value investing. Here are those to be added from this chapter, coming now at a fast pace. They all involve investing by the book. Beware of buying profitability. Operating liabilities lever the profitability of operations and can add value. Accounting profitability is not a measure of real, economic profitability. Value is generated in both the income statement and balance sheet. Anchoring on the balance sheet protects from paying too much for profitability. Anchoring on the balance sheet protects from paying for earnings generated by accounting. Building a Value Investing Engine This chapter assures you that the tool you are building has integrity; it has the built-in features that are required for a good product. Although there is not much in the chapter to build into the tool, your tool should be able to calculate operating liability leverage that potentially adds value. The Exercise Book The exercise book adds examples where you observe the RNOA appropriately calculated and applied. The Case Book Case 9: Microsoft: Trading on Profitability with A Magic Formula What Profitability Measure Do You Want to Use? The profitability of operations drives value, so determining profitability is an important aspect of fundamental analysis. However, chapter 6 has emphasized two points. First, profitability must be appropriately measured. Second, the appropriate measure must always be used in conjunction with the balance sheet: Anchoring on the balance sheet protects from paying too much for profitability . This case deals with these points in the context of a common trading strategy. The trading strategy is one popularized in a book by Joel Greenblatt, a value investor of some note: The Little Book That Beats the Market . The trading scheme is straightforward: Buy stocks with high enterprise earnings yield (low enterprise P/E) and high profitability. That, of course, is a modification of the earnings-to-price strategy discussed in chapter 2 , focusing on operations and adding profitability to the screen. It makes sense: Firms with high profitability are worth more, so if the market is pricing earnings with a low price, it might be a BUY. But is it a good strategy? You are asked to answer this question with the two points above in mind. Do you think the Magic Formula is a good one for screening stocks? What is it missing? How would you modify it? Case 10: Value Added in Apple’s 2024 Balance Sheet A Study in Operating Liability Leverage Pull down the 10-K for Apple, Inc. (AAPL) for fiscal year 2024 from the SEC filings. Now there is an uncomplicated balance sheet and income statement (though be sure to check the footnotes!). To what extent has the value added by the enterprise come from earnings or from the balance sheet? What are the sources of value added from the balance sheet? Has the value added from the balance sheet increased or declined since the 2018 analysis in this chapter? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 7 Accounting for Growth Chapter 7 Accounting for Growth Beware of overpaying for growth M arket prices are speculative, and it is that speculation that the value investor challenges. Chapter 4 focused the task: By introducing accounting for value, we saw that the market’s speculation is about future growth in value-added residual income. That gives the investor something specific to challenge. She does so with fundamental analysis. She asks: Does my analysis of the drivers of residual income growth lead me to agree with the market’s speculation, or do I disagree? That fundamental analysis is the topic of this chapter. Evaluating growth is crucial for the contemporary value investor. It is said that Benjamin Graham refused to pay for growth, recognizing that growth is risky and can be competed away. He would only buy stocks where the price was less than the no-growth value we calculated in chapter 4 . In his day, Graham could find companies trading at less than their no-growth value. Today, this is rarely the case. Indeed, Graham has been criticized: He probably would not have bought the great growth companies of the last half of the twentieth century: Dell, IBM, Microsoft, Cisco Systems, Apple, and many more (though he came around to dealing with growth in his later years). This was something that distinguished Philip Fisher from Graham. Fisher saw value in buying growth when he assessed it as undervalued through fundamental analysis. We must grapple with paying for growth while always holding to the mantra: Beware of overpaying for growth . The analysis in this chapter does so. Indeed, investing by the book builds in protection from paying too much for growth. But first our usual teasers. Teasers A growth company is one that can grow earnings. True? Earning higher return on net operating assets (RNOA) is the way to generate growth. This is only partially true. Why? Growing sales with an increasing profit margin is all that is needed to grow value. There is something missing here. What is it? What Is a Growth Company? The term “growth company” is often bandied about by investors. But you may wonder: What is a growth company? Is it a company that can grow sales? earnings? assets? employees? The Value versus Growth investors of chapter 2 see high P/E or high P/B as a growth company. We have concluded that is not necessarily correct, but how should we identify a growth company? At this point, the book will have led you to the answer: Growth that adds value is growth in value-added earnings; that is, growth in residual earnings. It is not earnings growth that adds value, but rather earnings growth that covers the cost of capital on net assets on the balance sheet. The Tyco example in chapter 3 , with high earnings growth but little residual earnings growth, demonstrates this. A firm can have a high P/B because it is expected to have positive residual earnings in the future, but that could be with declining residual earnings in the future: g < 0. A high P/B is not necessarily a growth company. It is mislabeled by the naive Value versus Growth investor dichotomy. Table 7.1 gives examples of growth companies and a counterexample. Nike, Inc. (NKE) has reported growth in sales and growth in earnings, but the relevant metric that defines it as a growth company is its upward trend in residual earnings. That contrasts with a once-great growth company, General Electric Company (GE), for which residual earnings have recently declined. Microsoft Corporation (MSFT), in the last panel of the table, has had a couple of value-destroying years (with the failed acquisition of Nokia in 2015–2016), but has reported residual earnings growth in most of the last several years. We have the answer to our first Teaser: Growth in value-added residual earnings defines a growth company, not growth in earnings. Table 7.1 Growth and no-growth companies A. Nike, Inc. Microsoft Corporation (MSFT): A growth company Microsoft ($ billion) 2016 2017 2018 2019 2020 2021 2022 2023 Sales 85.3 96.6 110.4 125.8 143.0 168.1 198.3 211.9 Earnings 16.8 21.2 16.6 39.2 44.3 61.3 72.7 72.4 Book value 72.0 72.4 85.6 102.3 118.3 142.0 166.5 142.0 Residual earnings (7.5%) 10.8 15.8 11.2 32.8 36.6 52.4 62.1 59.9 Growth in RE −8.0% 46.4% −29.3% 193.5% 11.7% 43.1% 18.5% −3.6% Unlevered basis: Operating income 17.0 20.1 25.3 28.1 44.2 60.5 72.7 71.4 Net operating assets 13.4 25.6 30.2 46.9 54.4 81.5 125.3 157.3 ReOI (8%) 15.5 19.0 23.3 25.7 40.4 56.1 66.2 61.4 Growth in ReOI −6.7% 22.8% 22.2% 10.5% 57.4% 38.8% 18.0% −7.3% Growth is From the Business Be reminded that, first and foremost, it is the business that generates value. So, as always, analysis starts with an understanding of the business. Charlie [Munger] and I are not stock pickers; we are business pickers. —Warren Buffett, Berkshire Hathaway 2022 annual letter to shareholders A “business picker” evaluates the ability of a business to generate growth by assessing the firm’s position in its product and supplier markets, and the competition it faces from others. It is not just an issue of growth but of maintaining or increasing growth. In (manufacturing) companies of the postwar era, growth tended to decline over time and business modeling was a matter of tracking the expected decline. Today, many firms continually add to growth through innovation, adaptation, and a focus on services. Think Apple, Microsoft, Amazon. So, business modeling focuses on the persistence of growth and, indeed, the ability to increase growth. That involves the stability of product market share, supply chains, relative efficiency in production, brand dominance, and much more. Think Deere & Company, Walmart, Caterpillar Inc. Value investors say these are firms with “franchise value” that maintains growth. Value-adding business is captured with residual income, if not now then prospectively in the future. So, residual income from operations is the focus, and the valuation question is whether that is likely to decline, persist, or increase. Residual income, in turn, is determined by its drivers, so it is residual income drivers that sharpen the focus. These drivers connect to the business. While sales per square foot is a determinant of value in a retail store, the accounting measure of asset turnover quantifies that, and asset turnover determines residual income. While product features such as market share and production technology determine value, sales and profit margins quantify their effect on residual income; this, in turn, connects to value in our accounting for value. This chapter lays all of this out in a systematic way to demonstrate how the many drivers jointly determine value. Residual Income Drivers With the perils of leverage understood, we focus on residual income for the business, the value-adding residual income from operations on the last two lines in the table for Microsoft. We’ll call it ReOI. It has two drivers: operating profitability (RNOA) and net operating assets (NOA): [IMAGE: ../images/ch07eqn01.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch07eqn02.png] is the return per dollar of net operating assets, the profitability. A firm grows residual income by increasing RNOA. But residual income is dollar value added, so the firm also grows residual operating income by investing in NOA to earn at the RNOA, provided RNOA is greater than r Firm . Again, balance sheets and income statements working together. The unlevered valuation model we have been applying values this growth: [IMAGE: ../images/ch07eqn03.png] The g here is growth in ReOl capturing growth in operations, to be distinguished from the g for levered residual earnings seen in prior chapters. For the growth company, g > 0. For the firm with negative growth, g < 0. And for the no-growth company, g = 0. In the reverse engineering of chapter 4 , the model is applied to understand the market’s g . With leverage effects taken out, it is the market’s growth rate for ReOI that fundamental analysis challenges. That is appropriate; as chapter 5 explained, while leverage does generate growth in (levered) residual earnings, it is growth from the business that adds value, and that is growth in residual income from operations. That is driven by growth in RNOA and growth in NOA, so it is the analysis of these drivers that challenges the market’s growth rate. The Base for Growth: Sustainable Profitability Growth refers to the future, but it depends on the now: the base from which growth builds. Current (residual) income can be affected by one-time items that will not be sustained in the future, and so are not a base for growth. The base is income stripped of these one-time items. This is sustainable income, sometimes referred to as persistent income or core income. One-time items are called unsustainable income or transitory income. They have a P/E of 1.0, a dollar of price for a dollar of earnings; as they will not be repeated, they do not attract a multiplier. Sustainable income can be repeated and indeed can grow, so is priced with a P/E multiplier greater than 1.0. The following are examples of unsustainable income items. The items are divided into income or expenses from one-time events and those that repeat. The latter may surprise you, because these gains and losses (from mark-to-market equity investments, for example) often occur every year. But a gain in the current year does not predict a gain in the future; it could be a loss as prices fluctuate. In statistical terms, the expected gain in the future is zero; one can’t predict it: This year’s gain or loss is unsustainable. Unsustainable income items Nonrepeating items * Repeating but unsustainable items Asset impairments Unrealized gains and losses on equity securities Restructuring charges Gains and losses on asset sales Special charges Gains and losses on derivatives Losses from lawsuits Currency gains and losses Regulatory penalties Tax adjustments Extraordinary items Discontinued operations Changes in accounting estimates * Some items can repeat (for example, asset impairments), but the timing and amounts are typically not predictable. Table 7.2 presents Microsoft’s income statement that we saw in chapter 5 , but now along with its comprehensive income statement. The income statement foots to net income, and the comprehensive income statement then adds other comprehensive income (OCI) to report comprehensive income. The table reformulates the income statement to separate unsustainable income from sustainable income.
