Chapter 8 Accounting for Risk to Value Chapter 8 Accounting for Risk to Value The primary risk in investing is the risk of paying too much M uch of the book up to this point deals with the risk of paying too much. Indeed, one can pay too much by not understanding that risk. However, once a share is purchased, risk is from holding the share going forward. The price to be paid should take account of risk, so risk is to be evaluated. Standard risk analysis sees risk in betas and price volatility. Value investors see that the risk is in the fundamentals driving value (sales, profit margins, asset turnovers, and more). This chapter deals with this risk. Because risk and return are two sides of the same coin, the amount of risk to your returns determines your hurdle rate for investing, sometimes referred to as the required return or the cost of capital. Earlier chapters invited you to insert your own hurdle rate ( r ) into your challenge to the market price, but have had little to say about how you might come to it. This chapter remedies that. Teasers U.S. government bonds are risk free. True? Diversification is protection from risk. Always true? Growth is risky. Always true? Stock and bond returns are negatively correlated, so protect from the risk in stocks by allocating some investment funds to bonds. Good advice? Good Thinking About Risk and Return As in all aspects of value investing, the analysis of risk starts with good thinking. And, when it comes to risk, there is a lot of thinking to do. Here are some thoughts. The Determinants of the Required Return for Risk The required return is the return required if there were no risk plus a risk premium: Required Return, r = Risk-free Rate + Risk Premium The risk-free rate, sometimes just called the interest rate, compensates for the time value of money. Because the value investor is buying value added earnings earned over many years, the risk-free rate is typically measured by the yield on a long-term U.S. government bond; the United States is seen as always honoring its debt obligations (though, with current deficit fiscal policies, we are becoming increasingly concerned about that!). Risk is not just about the cash to be received from an investment, but also the purchasing power of that cash. So expected inflation also determines the required return: Required Return, r = ( Real Risk-Free Rate + Expected Inflation Rate) + Risk Premium The real risk-free rate is that required with no expected inflation, but the investor must also be rewarded for having her wealth destroyed by inflation. In a bond market, the risk-free rate (the expected yield) should compensate for expected inflation: Investors demand an expected return in real (purchasing power) terms. However, the Federal Reserve (Fed) and other central banks, when setting interest rates, interfere with policy objectives other than controlling inflation. Further, while bond rates might include expected inflation, actual inflation can differ from expectation, adding another component to risk that goes into the risk premium. Governments have been known to reduce the real amount they have to repay on their debt by printing money that increases inflation and reduces the purchasing power of bond maturities. We must ask, then: Are U.S. government bonds really risk-free? That’s the first Teaser question. You now have the answer. During the high inflation of the 1970s, U.S government bonds were jokingly called “certificates of confiscation.” With shares priced to yield the required return r , they are in part determined by interest rates and inflation. Figure 8.1 depicts the relationship between the trailing P/E ratio for the S&P 500 and U.S.10-year bond yields and inflation rates. The measures are in table 1.2 in chapter 1 . The figure first plots P/E ratios for each year on the bond yields for that year, and then the P/E against bond year-over-year. It then does the same for P/E and inflation. You see a downward sloping relationship between P/E and both interest rates and inflation. You also see the same negative correlation in the graphs tracking P/E over time: With higher interest rates and expected inflation, investors discount the price, the numerator of P/E. Add 2022: In that year, the forward P/E for the S&P 500 declined from 22.7 to 18.9, while the U.S. 10-year bond yield increased from 1.52% to 3.88% and annual inflation came in at 8.0%. That accords with our simple depiction of the P/E ratio in chapter 2 : [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn03.png] [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-1.png] [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-1a.png] 8.1 Trailing P/E for the S&P 500, bond yields, and expected inflation, 1960–2021. The years in the plots are those where the P/E on the vertical axis was observed. The P/E is then plotted against U.S. bond yields and inflation over time. (A) and (B): P/E and bond yields. (C) and (D): P/E and inflation. For given growth expectations, P/E is reduced by r , and r incorporates the risk-free rate and expected inflation. The same correlation is observed with P/B ratios. The investor pays less (more) for a stock when interest rates and inflation expectations are high (low). We saw the effect of inflation in the great inflation period in the United States from the mid-1960s to 1982, when the Dow Jones Industrial Average dropped from 1000 to 777. The real loss was significantly higher as the purchasing power of stock prices also declined; inflation-adjusted, that is a 74 percent decline. In another burst of inflation to 8.5 percent in the first four months of 2022, the S&P 500 declined 10.9 percent. Of course, other factors may have contributed to these price declines, but clearly the risk of inflation is a concern to the value investor. So she adds a premium for expected inflation in setting her required return. She also looks for protection from this curse. A fixed-rate bond in highly susceptible: If interest rates and expected inflation rise, the bond price falls, as we saw in the late 1970s and early 1980s, and from 2021 to 2022. No refuge there. A variable-rate bond, on the other hand, adjusts the interest rate as inflation changes, with no effect on price. That is an inflation hedge. But such long-term bonds are not readily available, although short-term government bills and interest rates on bank accounts adjust for inflation (slowly and imperfectly in the case of bank accounts). That opens the question: Is an equity investment more like a fixed-rate bond or a variable-rate bond? The displays in figure 8.1 indicate the former. But the simple P/E formula says that if a stock can increase earnings growth ( g ) with the increase in r , the price and P/E is unaffected: r − g remains the same. Thus, if you find a stock that can increase earnings with inflation—say, by raising selling prices without cost increases or lower demand—that stock is a hedge against inflation risk. So-called duration also affects the required return. Just as bond yields are typically increasing in the time to maturity (an upward-sloping yield curve), so too should the required return for equity be higher the more distant the expected earnings are to be realized. That distance is duration. Simply put, the ultimate realization is susceptible to more risk shocks with a longer duration period. Interest rate risk and inflation risk contribute to this. That was evident in the large drop in the stock prices of many technology firms in 2022. Their prices and P/E ratios were typically based on expectations of earnings in the long term. As interest rates and expected inflation increased, the present value price of these payoffs decreased (as did bond prices with increasing bond yields). Can the value investor find an equity investment that provides a hedge against interest rate risk, inflation risk and duration risk, one that acts more like a variable-rate bond than a fixed-rate bond? With gold prices typically increasing with inflation, one might expect gold to provide a hedge (as indeed it often does, along with other commodities). But perhaps some equities provide a hedge. This is a feature to investigate, which we will do. The Expected Return Also Determines the Risk Premium The risk premium is, of course, determined by the probability of not receiving an expected payoff. Sometimes, investors see this as the risk to expected payoffs. However, theory informs us that the risk premium (and thus the required return or discount rate for risk) is determined also by the expected payoff itself. Roughly speaking, [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn04.png] This is known as the Lintner ratio or the Fama-Lintner ratio. For a given level of risk, be it beta, variance of return, or fundamental measures of risk that we will introduce, the amount by which we discount price to be paid also depends on what we expect to get from the investment. If beta is our risk measure and we see an investment with a beta of 1.2 and an expected payoff of 100, that is preferred to an investment of the same amount with the same beta but an expected payoff of 50; the downside from the 50 expected return is far worse. Beta is not the sole determinant of risk, of course, but if we see a firm with risk to the fundamentals and good prospects in our analysis, that is to be preferred to one with the same risk but lower prospects. This makes sense: If an investment has a high expected payoff with lower risk, it is to be preferred. It also resonates with value investing: A firm deemed underpriced has a higher expected return, making the risk to that investment more attractive to take on. But the risk could also be higher. That requires us to understand risk. As a point of departure, consider the standard models. Models of the Required Return Modern finance has developed models to determine the required return. These are called asset pricing models or risk factor models. The most common is the (single factor) capital asset pricing model (CAPM): [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn05.png] The value investor’s skepticism about models certainly applies here. Here are some reservations about the CAPM: The risk-free rate is usually the U.S. Treasury bond rate. But is this, or any government rate, really risk-free? While nominally it incorporates expected inflation, the government can print money, causing more inflation that will yield lower purchasing power when the bond matures. That seems to be a bad habit with governments. With the Fed keeping U.S. interest rates low during 2010–2022 while printing money with its “quantitative easing” policy, surely inflation was on the horizon, particularly with the government running increasing budget deficits. To think that the 10-year rate of 1.7 percent at the time would perpetuate for 10 years was a bit of a stretch (as later became evident). Silicon Valley Bank (the sixteenth largest U.S. bank) failed in 2023, not because it had made risky loans but because it held U.S. Treasury bonds that dropped in value as inflation increased. If one holds a U.S. government bond to maturity, it is presumably risk free in monetary terms (though, with increasing budget deficits, one sometimes wonders). But it is not risk-free in purchasing power terms. Stocks have no maturity date: The investor is always exposed. The market risk premium is the expected return on the market over the risk-free rate. The model says we need the expected return on the market in the future to get the expected return on an individual stock, but who knows that? Professors often tell their students that the market risk premium is 5 percent, knowing it is not knowable. Surveys are made on peoples’ estimate of the market risk premium. Recent surveys (in 2022) have the market risk premium at 5.2 percent in Switzerland, 5.5 percent in the United States, 5.6 percent in the United Kingdom, 5.8 percent in France and Germany, 6.0 percent in Italy, 6.2 percent in China, 6.4 percent in Australia and Spain, 6.9 percent in Greece, 17.7 percent in Argentina, and 19.4 percent in Venezuela. In terms the value investor is familiar with, this is speculation, and the value investor does not build in speculation. The beta risk can be estimated from past data, but it is the beta going forward that the investor is interested in: What risk will I be subjected to while holding the stock? And those historical betas are estimated with a standard error of 0.2 or more. So, if the estimated beta is 1.0, the true beta could easily be 0.8 or 1.2. Apply that difference to the guess for risk premium, and you will see that you do not have a reliable measure. The CAPM assumes return distributions following a normal distribution (though it can be derived with other doubtful assumptions). This is not so empirically sound, as we will see. For these reasons, the CAPM does not work. That is the oft-repeated “beta is dead” conclusion. There have been attempts to redeem the model by adding other factors in so-called multifactor models. The Fama and French model is perhaps the most famous, but it fails to yield a required return that works in real time for the investor. While the failure of these models is disappointing, you should be relieved that you do not have to engage in the extensive “beta bashing” of many investment texts and security analysis courses. It is not where the action is. The required return for operations unlevers the required return for equity in the weighted average cost of capital (the WACC): [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn06.png] As we saw in chapter 5 , this is a valid calculation in theory. However, bringing it to practice is fraught with problems. First, we must know the required return for the equity ( r Equity ) and as we have seen, that is elusive. But second, it requires us to know the weights, and that means we have to know Value Equity . But this is what we are trying to find! This problem is supposedly finessed by setting value equal to price: [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn07.png] However, the value investor does not concede that price is value without thorough investigation. Indeed, she strives to find value different from price. If price is not value, the WACC calculation reflects mispricing, not the required return. When calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price in the calculation . Here is the value investing view: For owners of a business—and that’s the way we think of shareholders—the academics’ definition of risk is far off the mark, so much so that it produces absurdities. For example, under beta-based theory, a stock that has dropped very sharply compared to the market—as had Washington Post when we bought it in 1973—becomes “riskier” at the lower price than it was at the higher price. Would that description have then made any sense to someone who was offered the entire company at a vastly reduced price? In fact, the true investor welcomes volatility. Ben Graham explained why in chapter 8 of The Intelligent Investor. There he introduced “Mr. Market,” an obliging fellow who shows up every day to either buy from you or sell to you, whichever you wish. The more manic-depressive this chap is, the greater the opportunities available to the investor. That’s true because a wildly fluctuating market means that irrationally low prices will periodically be attached to solid businesses. It is impossible to see how the availability of such prices can be thought of as increasing the hazards for an investor who is totally free to either ignore the market or exploit its folly. —Warren Buffett, Letter to Berkshire Hathaway Inc shareholders, 1993 In 2022 when the S&P 500 was down 19.4 percent, the index had 122 daily price moves more than 1 percent, the most in fourteen years according to a Wall Street Journal report. The investor might see this volatility as risk and shy away. But, to Buffett’s point, the lower prices at year’s end could be a buying opportunity. Prices do vary with risk, so Buffett’s statement is maybe a bit too strong, but the point is made: We must question the underlying assumptions of asset pricing models that the market is efficient and price movements indicate risk. The value investor approaches it differently: If a stock is underpriced, it has a higher expected return, and it is that which he focuses on rather than an elusive required return that an efficient market supposedly prices. Price is what you pay, value is what you get . Nevertheless, that does not relieve the investor of considering risk, for prices drop when the firm becomes riskier. It does, however, change the thinking: Risk is in the fundamentals, not in beta risk in prices. Here is another value investor, Seth Klarman: I find it preposterous that a single number reflecting past price fluctuations could be thought to completely describe the risk in a security. Beta views risk solely from the perspective of market prices, failing to take into consideration specific business fundamentals or economic developments. The price level is also ignored, as if IBM selling at 50 dollars per share would not be a lower-risk investment than the same IBM at 100 dollars per share. . . . Beta also assumes that the upside potential and downside risk of any investment are essentially equal, being simply a function of that investment’s volatility compared with that of the market as a whole. This too is inconsistent with the world as we know it. The reality is that past security price volatility does not reliably predict future investment performance (or even future volatility) and therefore is a poor measure of risk. —Seth Klarman, Margin of Safety , 1991 The opening sentence here leads to the next point. The Quest for the Required Return is a Fool’s Errand The quest for the cost of capital has absorbed academic finance for decades. However, business risk is a complicated matter. There is risk from changing customer tastes, risk from technological change, risk in labor relations, risk in supply chains, risk in distribution systems, risk in exchange rates, commodity risk, country risk, political risk, regulatory risk, litigation risk, and more—not to mention the strategies of potential competitors. Can this complexity be funneled into one number to say the cost of capital is 8.0 percent, not 7.5 percent or 8.5 percent? We think not. Modern finance has admirably tried to do so but it is probably a fool’s errand. The desire to have a formula to describe the world, like physicists have, and reduce it to one number is understandable. But the complex business world does not behave in strict, immutable ways. Models can be developed with assumptions about investors and the world they face. This is typically done in finance by assuming we know the set of possible events and outcomes in the future that affect prices. And we know the probability to assign to each of those “states.” That is a tall task for we human beings of limited rationality. The easy way out is to assume a normal distribution or some similar statistical convenience. Or, more paternalistically, we can assume the investor’s utility in each of those states (somewhat a private matter). Even the notion of risk is suspect, for there is a classic distinction between risk and uncertainty. Risk modeling says that we know the event/outcome states that can occur, and their probabilities. Uncertainty says that, in this changing world, we do not know the set of all possible things that can happen, let alone their probability. This is the “black swan” concern: Things happen which were not even in our imagination. The upshot of all this: Abandon the question: What is the cost of capital? We just cannot look to a formula to bail us out. The onus is on us to evaluate risk so that we can establish our own hurdle rate. In the discipline of value investing, we bring any available information to do so. That we do in this chapter. The Required Return Is a Personal Matter The vignette below tells us that the return that each of us requires to compensate for risk is a personal matter. The different reactions of students and the professor in the vignette is due to differences in tolerance for risk. A person in debt with no secure job prospects is very risk-averse in uncertain times. In challenging the price, this investor requires a high return to invest and, as we saw in chapter 4 , that in turn requires high growth to cover the high required return. A secure person has a lower hurdle rate; he is more disposed to investing in down markets. The risk premium is in the mind (or heart) of the beholder. Consult with your spouse or partner in setting your hurdle rate, not with the CAPM. Think of your children and their security. Don’t think that there is one required return, r , to be estimated, a return from a model that you must be enslaved to. A Vignette Investing in the Financial Crisis of 2008 In the depths of the global financial crisis in 2008–2009, when the S&P 500 dropped below 750, Warren Buffett wrote a piece in The New York Times titled “Now Is the Time to Buy Stocks.” He presumably saw that stocks were cheap, depressed by the run on the market, one of Mr. Market’s fearful episodes. This is just the type of situation that a value investor craves. After reading the article to his students, one of your authors asked the class: Are you also buying? The answer in the classroom was almost universally: No. They said their prospects for employment after graduation were now quite uncertain. They were leveraged with student debt up to the hilt. Many were actually selling stocks, running away from risk with the herd at the time. All well and good for Buffett to buy with wealth to back him up, but not for me, they said. With the uncertainty of how the crisis would play out, buying into the crash was far too risky for them. Turning the question around, one student asked: Professor, are you buying? Well, yes: I have a job; indeed, my university is silly enough to give me tenure. I have an investment portfolio with relatively safe equities and little debt. I can take on the risk. It’s my time to buy. As an aside, there is something troubling here: If only secure investors buy in down markets, they are the ones that get wealthier if stocks are cheap. It is usually the already-wealthy who can afford to take on the risk. They can be gratified that their buying creates liquidity for those selling to flee from risk. But the prospect of a more unequal society is worrying. It breeds resentment, social unrest, political polarization, and turmoil. We have seen much of that since the financial crisis. Yes, we might take advantage of Mr. Market’s mood swings, but this is something to keep in mind. Perhaps that is why many successful value investors are also successful philanthropists. With the required return such a personal matter, how should an investment manager establish the hurdle rate for his fund? If that manager has the correct thinking, he should see investing as a partnership with investors in the fund, with the hurdle rate being that of those investors. That requires communication about the risks in the fund to attract investors with that same disposition toward risk. One way to do this is to set the hurdle rate for the manager’s incentive fee at the investors’ presumed hurdle rate; the manager does not share in the profits of the fund until he covers their cost of capital. Yes, you could think of the investment manager himself being rewarded on the basis of his fund’s own residual earnings. That differs from the more common fee structure where the manager’s fee includes a significant percentage of assets under management (AUM), rewarding him irrespective of the performance of the fund. Diversification as Protection from Risk One of the foundational principles of modern finance says that diversification reduces risk. And indeed, it often does. Diversification with a portfolio does not change the average return for the stocks in the portfolio. But it does reduce the standard deviation from the average, the risk. See figure 8.2 : As the investor adds stocks to the portfolio, the standard deviation declines, typically trailing off with fifteen or more securities. This is the insight of Harry Markowitz that earned him a Nobel Prize. Before his paper, risk was thought of as the volatility of a stock’s return. No: that volatility can be reduced by forming a portfolio. [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-2.png] 8.2 Typical effect of adding securities to a portfolio on the standard deviation of returns. The idea is simple: In a portfolio, the negative return on one stock can be offset by a positive return on another stock in the portfolio, provided that returns in the portfolio are less than perfectly positively correlated. Here’s the rub: The typical correlation between stocks’ returns is about 0.65. However, in down markets, the correlations move toward 1.0. In 2008, in the big down market during the financial crisis, the correlation between stocks, bonds, oil and gas, and real estate investment trusts (REITS) moved toward 0.95. The investor lost the protection of diversification in a time when she needed it most. In the down market of 2022, U.S. stocks, international stocks, supposedly safe government bonds, and real estate investments moved down together, and quite dramatically. “Nowhere to hide,” as it is said. That is not what an investor wants. In up markets, correlations become lower, but that is when the investor wants stocks to move together. These are the considerations in answering the chapter’s second Teaser question. It is said that the ultimate diversification is in holding the market portfolio of all assets. But that exposes us to market risk; we are hit with large market drawdowns that are not uncommon, as we will see in the next section. The value investor looks for protection from this risk, which we will explore. Charlie Munger again: “The goal of investments is to find situations where it is safe not to diversify.” Diversification is a way of handling risk for the passive investor unwilling to dig into the underlying fundamentals. But diversification embeds a trap . Ignore information at your peril. What We Know About Risk The value investor eschews standard estimates of the required return as just too imprecise and just too speculative. But she requires an assessment of the risk she faces to formulate her own personal hurdle rate. So, to provide background for that assessment, let’s return to our basic principles: Understand what you know and anchor on what you know rather than speculation . So, what do we know about risk? Risk Is in the Tails Return outcomes do not behave like the normal bell-shaped distribution underlying the CAPM. Rather, “risk is in the tails.” That is, the investor is subject to more extreme returns, large positive and negative returns, than indicated by a normal distribution. Figure 8.3 gives the distribution of returns for the S&P 500 from 1973–2021 with the normal distribution superimposed. The mean return over this period was 12.45 percent per year with a standard deviation of 17.16 percent, the two statistics that fully describe the normal distribution. But you can see that the actual returns are not normally distributed. There are more large negative returns in the left tail than expected under the normal distribution, and a higher frequency of positive returns greater than 25 percent than indicated by the normal distribution. This depiction is actually from a period when investors fared well, data with an ex post bias. The U.S. stock market dropped 39 percent over two days in 1929, followed by a 25 percent drop for 1930, 43 percent for 1931, and 35 percent for 1937. But even then, the large negative returns came with large positive returns: The market returned 34 percent in 1933 and 48 percent in 1935. [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-3.png] 8.3 Returns for S&P 500, 1973–2021, relative to the normal distribution. The figure exhibits what we know about the risk in buying stocks: We are subject to the possibility of large negative returns, a real concern for the investor worried about the preservation of capital. However, we are compensated with a significant probability of large positive returns. Risk is in both tails; downside risk is compensated with upside potential. This directs the value investing endeavor: The investor seeks exposure to the upper tail while minimizing exposure to the lower tail. First and foremost, that exposure is determined by the BUY decision, where the risk is the risk of paying too much . To realize the upper tail, she looks for stocks where value is greater than price, buying cheap stocks to yield a high return. To avoid the lower tail, she shuns stocks that are overpriced. However, to further avoid the lower tail, she must also be aware of the further downside risk while holding the stock. That is where the fundamental analysis of risk comes in. The returns for the S&P 500 underestimate this risk because this is a portfolio and portfolios diversify risk. The spread of returns is higher with individual stocks; one can lose 100 percent on the downside if the firm goes bankrupt, but it is not uncommon for a stock to deliver upside returns of 300, 400, or 500 percent (or more) in a year. Leverage Adds Risk If we ever had any doubts about leverage risk, chapter 5 dismisses them. With tail risk the concern, the investor is wary: Leverage adds to tail risk, increasing the probability of higher (levered) positive returns but also more negative returns. Buying an airline is risky, for it is shocked during recessions, wars, and pandemics. But buying a levered airline is even more risky, adding higher left-tail risk. Bankruptcy, with a typical 100 percent shareholder loss, is due to firms not covering their debts. Growth Is Risky Well, the answer to this third Teaser question is a definite Yes, it is always true. This theme pervades this book. The fundamental analysis of risk focuses on the risk to growth. Since growth is growth in residual income, the analysis is of risk to residual income. Fundamental Risk, Price Risk, and Information Risk The risk to growth is the subject of analysis, but there are two other exposures to be aware of. The first is price risk , in a different sense. The value investor looks for mispriced stocks, then waits for the price to “gravitate to fundamentals.” But stocks that deviate from fundamental value can deviate further. Investors are reminded of the 1990s bubble experience during which many value investors judged as early as 1996 that stocks were overpriced and sold or went short. But they became even more overvalued with the return for the S&P 500 of 33.4 percent in 1997, 28.6 percent in 1998, and 21.0 percent in 1999, and with its P/E ratio topping 33.0. Those that sold in 1996 missed out on these returns. Those that went short had a very uncomfortable time with margin calls, experiencing Keynes’s observation that “markets can remain irrational longer than you can remain solvent.” This is the price risk that concerns value investors. The episode has two informative lessons about price risk. First, prices can go against the value investor in the short run. Value investors buying into the large price drop during the financial crisis experienced further price declines. Second, prices are driven not only by fundamentals but also by the “animal spirits” that Keynes also referred to. So, while the investor focuses on fundamentals, she might also consider how prices are driven by factors other than fundamentals such as investor “sentiment” and possibly take advantage. This is the subject of behavioral finance, the study of “irrational” investing based on psychology and sociology rather than economics, bringing more definition to “animal spirits.” With apologies, your authors are not well trained in this area, so we will not pursue it. In coping with price risk, the investor might heed the tenth principle in chapter 1 : Be patient: It often takes some time for price to correct itself and revert to fundamentals . Patience pays off. Post-bubble in 2000–2002, the S&P 500 dropped 49 percent. Selling stocks in 1996 and investing in bonds paid off in the long run. The second issue is information risk due to a lack of information that would be helpful in reaching a more confident judgement. It takes two forms. The first is the risk that others have more information than you. One should not trade with the better informed, but trading on exchanges is anonymous. In corrupt countries, an investor might refuse to trade at all. Insider trading laws ameliorate the problem, but they are difficult to enforce. Disclosure regulation, more forcefully enforced, provides the same information to all. Superior analysis of these disclosures counters any information disadvantage, and indeed can provide an advantage. However, lack of disclosure adds to information risk. You will find that disclosures are not always detailed enough for the information you need. You are left estimating or worse still guessing: you are taking a risk. Information risk also refers to the quality of the information being analyzed, the risk that the information is misleading. An accounting quality analysis, as in chapters 9 and 10 , helps mitigate this risk. The audit provides some assurance, though not completely. Auditors in the United States assure that financial statements conform to GAAP (generally accepted accounting principles), but GAAP is deficient in some respects, as chapters 9 and 10 explain. The same goes for IFRS. And audit failures are not unknown. Nevertheless, read the audit opinion accompanying the annual financial statements to see if there is anything of note. Of particular interest is the integrity of internal controls. With the collapse of cryptocurrency firms FTX and Celsius Network in 2022, it was observed that several crypto firms had reported significant internal control weaknesses in areas like related-party transactions, commingled customer funds, and general recordkeeping. A speculator in crypto might brush this aside, especially with social media influencers like Vitalik Buterin hard at work, but internal control weaknesses alarm the more considered investor. Information risk requires a higher hurdle rate to invest; or, in the extreme, you walk away. That applies especially to firms you don’t understand: Others probably have more information than you. The Analysis of Fundamental Risk Phew! That was a long discussion about risk. But it’s a complex issue. Now to the core issue in this chapter. The following quote introduces it: Beta is a more or less useful measure of past price fluctuations of common stocks. What bothers me is that authorities now equate the beta idea with the concept of risk. Price variability yes; risk no. Real investment risk is measured not by the percent that a stock might decline in price in relation to the general market in a given period but by the danger of a loss of quality and earnings power through economic changes or deterioration in management. —Warren Buffet in his 1993 letter to shareholders Buffett is saying risk is in the fundamentals, not in prices. Prices can be quite volatile daily, monthly, and yearly, but the value investor is concerned about where the price will end up, not the variation along the way. The investor forecasts future residual earnings and their growth—earnings power, in Buffett’s words—and prices ultimately settle up against those earnings. It is those realized earnings that determine the return to the investment. But those anticipated earnings might not be realized; they are at risk. With residual earnings determined by its drivers, the risk to residual earnings and to value is in the risk to these drivers. So, risk analysis simply follows the driver analysis of the previous chapter with sensitivity analysis added. Risk is assessed by asking the What If questions of that chapter. What if sales remained at the current level? What if sales declined with a corresponding effect on asset turnover? What if the profit margin declines due to higher production costs? What if competitors undercut the firm on pricing? What if government regulations are imposed? Supply chain shocks during the Covid pandemic were shocks to profit margins, along with shocks to operating liability leverage. As in the last chapter, the focus is on key drivers, those where the firm is more susceptible. If you have built the sensitivity analysis into your analysis engine, you are ready to go. You have the path of future residual income under each driver scenario, calculated with the shortcut formula: [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn08.png] All that remains is to convert each path to a valuation. You then have a valuation of the operations under each scenario. The set of possible valuations is the profile of outcomes that displays value at risk. Of course, you only proceed to this analysis if, after challenging the market’s growth rate, you have concluded that the risk of paying too much is low, for that is the primary risk in investing. If that risk is high, you walk away. The action box below generates a value-at-risk profile for Microsoft. Do not confuse it with the VaR metric, that estimates the left tail risk to price using price volatility rather than fundamentals. To the no-growth value, add growth scenarios and calculate the value under each. Each scenario implies an equivalent declining growth path. Outcomes along that path are determined by the sensitivity analysis for the value drivers, as in the last chapter. The value under each scenario, as a percentage of the current market price, gives the expected loss if that scenario is realized. We see that a growth rate over 6 percent is required to avoid a loss if the stock is purchased at the current price. The assessment of risk is completed by adding probabilities to each value scenario. These are not provided in the box; that is up to you. With your assignment of probabilities, you can plot a probability distribution, one that replaces an assumed distribution. Only your knowledge of the business can supply these probabilities, reinforcing the dictum that business analysis precedes valuation analysis: One does not buy a stock, one buys a business ; when buying a business, know the business. What is the likelihood of a negative growth rate—say, of −2 or −1 percent? Is the no-growth value a possibility? Of particular interest is the probability of growth of 6 percent, for only in this scenario is value greater than price. The important thing is to think contingently along alternative possible paths. The ability to weigh alternatives strategically defines intelligence generally, but is certainly the mark of an intelligent investor. This value-at-risk profile, along with the probabilities you attached, might induce you to change your required return, given as 8.5 percent in the analysis here. As we observed in chapter 4 , an increase (decrease) in the required return means higher (lower) growth to cover the required return. Value Investing in Action Value at Risk for Microsoft, 2023 Market price = $2,428,712 million; r Firm = 85.% Accounting for value: The initializing no-growth value [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn09.png] Risk to value: Value from adding growth rate scenarios to no-growth value [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn10.png] Growth, g Value Value/Price Probability −2.0% $802,794 33.1% ? −1.0% $865,591 35.6% ? 0.0% $943,164 38.8% ? 1.0% $1,041,423 42.9% ? 2.0% $1,169,915 48.2% ? 4.0% $1,598,223 65.8% ? 5.0% $1,995,937 82.2% ? 6.0% $2,711,823 111.7% ? It’s up to you to assign probabilities to the alternative scenarios . This will be based on your understanding of the business . Driver Structure and Value at Risk The last chapter characterized firms and industries by their driver structure. This structure determines value at risk, with key drivers the focus. In the case of Coca-Cola with constant profit margins and asset turnover, sales growth was the key driver, so value at risk comes down to largely to sales at risk. For an airline or wireless carrier, sales are also a key driver at risk but, with its fixed asset base and cost structure, sales also drive profit margins and asset turnovers, so these are also at risk. To illustrate the effect on driver structure on value at risk, the nearby action box looks at Delta Air Lines, Inc. in 2019. The year is particularly relevant for a scenario analysis as the airline was hit hard in 2020, with considerable value lost as the COVID pandemic spread. Risk in action. Only two scenarios are examined in the box. They are downside scenarios, always a concern with airlines. With a fixed cost structure, the upside delivers well because added sales go straight through to operating income and profit margin (PM). That is the effect of operating leverage and net operating assets (NOA) flexibility introduced in the last chapter. With a fixed asset structure, added sales also increase asset turnover (ATO) without a need to increase NOA. But, with upside potential comes downside risk: A drop in sales with no drop in fixed expenses means fixed costs might not be covered: The amount of fixed to variable cost, the operating leverage, adds risk. Further, with no adjustment to NOA on a sales drop, ATO is susceptible. This is demonstrated in the two scenarios. The second scenario is particularly interesting, as that was roughly the condition in 2020 as the pandemic hit. Value goes to zero. Government assistance, government debt guarantees, and employee cooperation brought the airline through. The scenarios are with a no-growth valuation. In scenario 1, that benchmark is approximately the market valuation at the time: The market saw no growth in the future. That begs looking at a growth scenario. Is the market not seeing upside potential? But, in looking at upside growth, keep in mind the downside scenarios. Value Investing in Action Value at Risk for Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) Summary financial statements, 2019 ($millions) Balance sheet Sustainable income statement Net operating assets 29,731 Sales 47,007 Net debt 14,373 Fuel expense 8,519 Equity 15,358 Salaries 11,225 Depreciation 2,581 Maintenance 1,751 Landing fees 1,762 Passenger service 1,251 Contracted services 6,225 Other expense 7,075 40,389 Operating income 6,618 Tax expense 1,494 Operating income (after tax) 5,124 No-growth value: OI 2020 same as sustainable income in 2019, r Firm = 10 % [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn11.png] Risk scenarios: Scenario 1 : Sales fall to $43,000 million with no change in (fixed) expenses except taxes [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn12.png] Scenario 2 : Sales fall to $17,095 million with fuel expense decreasing to $3,176 million and passenger services expense to $323 million [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn13.png] A Margin of Safety Driver scenarios reveal possible outcomes for a firm’s operations. But risk is determined by the probability of those outcomes occurring, particularly the probability on the downside. So, after finding stocks where the challenge to the market price indicates the price is attractive, the investor then seeks the probability of the downside for the value drivers she is anchoring on: sales, profit margins, and asset turnovers. If the price is attractive and she deems that probability to be low, she has a margin of safety. Or, put differently, if she sees that the upside potential for these drivers is considerably higher than the downside risk, she invests comfortably. Railroads and airlines, with their high fixed-cost structure and low NOA flexibility, have similar drivers and sales (growth) is especially important in determining both profit margin and asset turnover. But one might see an airline (with its exposure to downside drops in revenue) as less attractive than a railroad (for which there is a persistent demand for carrying freight), even though their current sales, profit margins, and asset turnovers look similar. One looks for a margin of safety. Theory tells us that the required return, the discount rate, is determined, not only by risk, but by the risk relative to the expected payoff: High expected payoff for a given risk is discounted less. That is in the Fama-Lintner ratio presented earlier in this chapter. So, an investor seeing a firm with good prospects and low downside probability likes that firm (provided, of course, the “price is right”). Current driver levels, on a sustainable basis, are usually a good starting point for projecting expected payoffs. So, if one sees a firm with a consistent annual sales growth at 10 percent (say), handsome gross margins of 40 percent (say), operating profit margins of 20 percent (say), and a steady asset turnover, that firm is to be preferred to one with low sales growth and thin profit margins, provided the downside risk to the attractive fundamentals is not high. Indeed, anemic sales growth and thin profit margins indicate susceptibility to the downside. Accounting Conveys Risk and Uncertainty Value investors always adhere to the idea of prudence: In the face of risk, be conservative. Fortunately, accrual accounting as practiced largely embraces such principles. This section shows how the numbers resulting from prudent accounting convey risk. Risk is in speculation about future growth, so the accounting is of particular relevance for value investors wary of buying growth. Fair value accounting is not widely practiced beyond financial institutions, but is a point of departure for understanding how accounting handles risk. We will have more to say about this accounting in the next chapter, but the basic idea behind fair value accounting is to put value—speculative value—into the balance sheet: Net assets (book value) are measured at the present value of the cash flows that they are expected to yield (at least as an ideal). That sets price equal to book value; that is, P/B = 1.0. Thus, all the investor must do is look at the book value and the valuation task is complete. No analysis is required; sounds wonderful. However, this puts speculation into the balance sheet, and that is something the value investor does not want. The investor says to the accountant: Tell me what you know and leave the speculation to me. When it comes to assessing risk, she sees fair value accounting as unhelpful: With fair value accounting we see risk only after it hits us, when the balance sheets on which the investor relies suddenly collapse. That was the experience in the crash of 1929 and with banks’ (fair value) assets in the financial crisis of 2008–2009. We look for accounting that conveys risk in advance. Accounting, as practiced, has this feature. It is embedded in the basic principle of accrual accounting: Under uncertainty, earnings are not booked until the uncertainty has been resolved. This is the so-called “realization principle” for recognizing (value added) earnings in the income statement. The stock price (appropriately) builds in speculation about future earnings, but accountants will not book them. They are telling us that those earnings are still at risk, they have not yet been realized. Thus, the price should be discounted for that risk. This, of course, is reflected in our accounting for value: Expected future earnings are discounted for risk in the residual earnings calculation. As earnings add to the balance sheet, this principle is also one for determining the balance sheet. In contrast to fair value accounting, value is only added to the balance sheet when risk is resolved and realized earnings are added to book value. Thus, assets are recorded at their cost (no value added), with value added only when they realize earnings. This accounting is typically referred to as historical cost accounting but might better be called realization accounting. Two subprinciples underlie the realization principle. The Revenue Recognition Principle Revenue, the top line of the income statement, is governed by the revenue recognition principle in the accompanying accounting primer. It says that revenue (sales) is booked only when a firm nabs a customer with a firm contract and most risk of not gaining value from that customer has disappeared. The customer is legally bound to pay and, even then, the accountant discounts the resulting accounts receivable (with an allowance for bad debts) for the chance the customer will not pay. On the firm’s side of the contract, revenue is recognized only when the customer has no claim on the firm for further performance under the contract. If the firm does have future performance obligations, and the customer has performed, the revenue is not booked but rather recorded as deferred (unearned) revenue, a liability to perform. Accounting Primer Accounting for Risk: Revenue Recognition Revenue is recognized only after two conditions are met: The contract between the firm and the customer has been satisfied by both parties to the contract. Receipt of cash from the customer is highly certain. This is a principle that requires that all performance risk, from both the customer and the firm, be eliminated. The authoritative statements are ASC 606, established by the Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB); and IFRS 15, established by the International Accounting Standards Board (IASB). In short, recognized revenue is risk resolved and speculative revenue from future customers is value still at risk. This suits the investor well: If the stock price builds in a lot of value for expected revenue but it has not yet been realized, the investor discounts that price. In the late 1990s, when the market was putting high valuations on the prospects of new internet companies with little revenue as yet, the value investor discounted them (often ignoring them completely). That turned out to be wise, as many of these firms did not survive. The high valuations in the early 2020s of speculative firms like Tesla and Netflix led to disappointment. See the vignette box on the next page. Beware of buying unrealized revenue growth . Conservative Accounting for Investment On the expense side of the income statement, expenses incurred to gain revenue are matched to that revenue to report value received over value given up, that is, value added. Matching the cost of goods sold to revenue from selling the inventory is a good example. Depreciation expense matches part of plant costs to those revenues. But there is an exception. When many investments are made, they are booked to the balance sheet at their historical cost. But, when the outcomes for the investment are particularly uncertain, the investment is expensed immediately to the income statement. See the next Accounting Primer which also gives examples of the type of expenditures subject to this accounting treatment. These are not expenditures to gain current revenue, but rather to gain future customers and revenue. Accounting Primer Accounting for Risk: Conservative Accounting for Investment When outcomes to investment are particularly uncertain, the investment is expensed to the income statement rather than booked to the balance sheet. Research and development (R&D) is the marquee example, but this accounting also applies to other areas, including advertising and promotion to build brands and attract customers, investment in human capital, expenditures to build supply chains and product distribution networks, and customer loyalty programs. Investment in software that has not yet shown technical feasibility is also expensed. The criterion for expensing is the probability of future economic benefits. In FASB Statement No. 2, R&D is expensed because of “the uncertainty of future benefits.” In IAS 38, IFRS distinguishes research (which is expensed) and development (which is booked to the balance sheet). The two are distinguished by the probability of future benefits. Even for property, plant, and equipment, IAS 16 requires economic benefits to be “probable” for the asset to be booked to the balance sheet. The guiding principle, again, is risk or uncertainty. Inventory that is likely to be sold in the near future is booked to the balance sheet as an asset. So is plant and equipment that produce a known product for sale. And so is oil and gas in the ground that can be extracted for sale, or a wind turbine that can generate power for sale. The outcomes to these investments are somewhat uncertain, but investment in R&D where there is no product as yet (and might never be), let alone customers for the hypothetical product, is more uncertain by degrees. If R&D has only a 10 percent probability of success, that is a really risky investment. So, the principle tells us: Don’t add it to other assets on the balance sheet pretending it provides collateral. The underlying accounting doctrine is prudence: In the face of uncertainty, be prudent. The accountant is obeying the dictate of the value investor: Tell me what you know; don’t speculate . Vignettes Speculative Revenues Not Yet Realized Amazon.com , Inc. (AMZN) pricing in 1998–1999 During the Internet bubble of the late 1990s, the price of the new e-commerce company Amazon soared on speculation about future profits, even though the firm was reporting significant losses. Its market capitalization was a multiple of that of Walmart, even though its revenues were a fraction of those for Walmart. With the bursting of the bubble in 2000, Amazon’s stock price fell from over $400 (pre-split) to $7.00. Amazon survived, but it was not until 2014 that the firm realized a profit. Priceline.com During the 1990s bubble, another e-commerce start-up called Priceline.com launched an online site for airline reservation and ticketing, displacing traditional travel agencies. The stock price soared on speculation about its profits even though the firm was reporting losses. At one point, its market capitalization was more than the total for all U.S. airlines. The firm was subsequently acquired by Booking Holdings Inc. (BKNG), which also operates Booking.com and KAYAK. Netflix, Inc. (NFLX) and anticipated streaming revenues Netflix was a dominant firm in video streaming in 2020–2021, gaining subscribers at a strong pace. That recognized revenue justified a strong valuation. But its stock price built in even higher subscriber growth in the future with a price-to-sales (P/S) ratio over 4 and a trailing P/E of 36 on the anticipated sales, not yet realized. At the first signs of declining subscriber numbers in 2022, the stock price declined by 70 percent from its 2021 high. Tesla, Inc. (TSLA) and the pricing of electric vehicle manufacturers In 2020, in the face of increased awareness of the danger from climate change, automobile manufacturers began moving to electric vehicles (EV). With EV seen as the path to the future, Tesla, the innovative leader, was priced at a high P/S of 16 and a trailing P/E over 100. But the speculated revenue and earnings had not been booked yet; they were unrealized and at risk. Automobile manufacturing is a highly competitive business and historically not particularly profitable. Not all could be successful. Indeed, in 2022 Tesla’s stock price dropped 55 percent from its 2021 high. Rivian Automotive, Inc. (RIVN) and Nikola Corporation (NKLA) One of those EV firms was Rivian. It went IPO in November 2021, reaching a stock price of $172 and a market valuation of $153 billion. But it had only delivered 156 electric trucks, reporting losses. Speculative earnings remained unrealized. The stock price declined to $16.66 by the end of June 2023, an 89 percent decline. Another EV and hydrogen-cell vehicle firm that reported losses, Nikola Corporation (NKLA), saw its stock price decline from $65.90 in June 2022 to $1.38 a year later. Nvidia Corporation (NVDA) Between January and October 2024 the shares of Nvidia Corporation soared 148.07 percent to $122.85 on speculation that it would supply the chips for AI applications. Was this speculation or rational pricing? Was the resulting forward P/E of 63 appropriately discounted for the risk to future earnings growth? This conservative accounting conveys risk to the investor, as its name implies. If expensed R&D (or R, under IFRS) is high relative to current revenue, the firm is risky: The firm is investing a lot to gain future revenues, but is able to realize relatively low revenues now. A start-up biotech with high R&D but low revenues as of now is risky. A mature pharmaceutical with a proven record of successful R&D generating revenues is less risky. Turn to the action box below on Twitter, Inc. (now the privately owned X Corp.). Value Investing in Action Expensed investment for Twitter, Inc. (TWTR) Twitter, Inc. consistently reported losses. However, R&D and sales and marketing expenses are over 51 percent of revenue, contributing to the overall net loss. These expenditures were made to generate future revenue and earnings growth, but there was uncertainty about whether the expected revenues and earnings would be realized. This firm is risky. By 2021, revenue had grown to $5,077 million but Twitter (now X) was still reporting losses, with R&D and marketing expenses still 47 percent of revenue. Will these investments ever pay off? Will Twitter ever be profitable? On a price of $31,389 million, it was trading at a P/S of 7.5 in 2021 and a P/B of 6.5: risky, indeed. Risk in speculative values With forecasts of negative residual operating income for 2018 and 2019, the setup for Twitter’s valuation in 2017 with r Firm = 12 percent was as follows (in millions): [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn14.png] The speculative value is value to be added in years after 2019. Clearly this must be a large number if it is to explain the price of $31,389 million, at 6.5 times book value of $4,829 million. This is a feature of a risky firm: realization of value-added earnings is well into the future, speculative and uncertain. That contrasts with a firm with considerable value in book value and near-term earnings. Note that the realization principle is conveying this risk: If value in price will take some time to be realized, it’s risky. Beware of paying for risky long-term growth in speculative values. With Twitter as an example, chapter 6 detailed the effect of conservative accounting on the book rate of return (RNOA), with warnings about accepting RNOA as a measure of profitability. We are now showing how the accounting conveys risk: The investor should recognize these accounting features in setting her hurdle rate. It tells her to separate the expensed investments in the income statement from other expenses when determining the profit margin from current sales, as with Twitter in chapter 6 . It tells her to consider the payoffs from these investments but also to discount those forecasted payoffs for risk. Further examples are found in chapter 12 where we take fundamental analysis to explain the value trap in so-called Value versus Growth investing: This is a trap from not understanding the risk conveyed by the book rate of return. Risk and Uncertainty Investors usually speak of risk, and we have done so for much of this chapter. But accountants talk of uncertainty, as in the basic principle of accrual accounting above. There is a difference. Finance theory of required return refers to risk: The investor knows the set of possible outcomes—the so-called state space—and the probability of each outcome. That leads to measurable risk where the gamble can be represented by a probability distribution over the set of (assumed) outcomes. But the ability to know all possible outcomes is a big ask of an investor; it is beyond our rationality. We are too frequently surprised by “black swan” events that cannot be predicted or even imagined, and even more so in this rapidly changing world. The events of 9/11 and its aftermath? Locked up because of a pandemic? What a shock! And there will be more black swans swimming in. Uncertainty, on the other hand, says we don’t know the set of all possible outcomes, let alone the probabilities to assign to those outcomes; we are faced with a future where unimagined events can materialize. That reinforces the point that chasing a measure of the required return ( r ) is a fool’s errand. The common saying, “it’s an uncertain world” is not to be taken lightly. When we invest, we are buying uncertainty, not risk. Whether by serendipity or purpose, accounting uses the word uncertainty: We are dealing with risk that cannot be measured. While exploring value drivers under various outcome scenarios is helpful, quantification cannot always be brought to the accounting under uncertainty. Uncertainty requires that we replace such quantification with heuristics: prudent judgment based on experience in similar circumstances. So, the accountant expenses R&D in a pharmaceutical firm not because she has a probability distribution over outcomes but, because with no product as yet and little understanding of whom the drug might serve or what competitive drugs might emerge, there are just too many unknowns. Prudent judgment says to expense the expenditure immediately under this uncertainty. These judgments come from experience over a long history, from the early nineteenth century at least. They were not imposed by regulation, but instead arose as instinctive human behavior. Returns to Buying Growth: Growth/Return Profiles There is another way of evaluating risk. When considering a bond investment, one can discount the expected cash flows to present value at one’s hurdle rate, and then compare that value with the price the market is asking. Or, one can instead ask: What is the return I expect to get by buying at the current price? That is the bond yield, calculated as the (internal) rate of return that reconciles the cash flows to the price. If the investor sees the yield as higher than his hurdle rate, he finds the bond attractive. Can the same approach be taken with equities? Well, unlike bonds, equities have growth, so that complicates the exercise. However, given a growth expectation, one can extract the expected return from buying at the market price with that growth rate ( g ). This is the reverse engineering exercise of chapter 4 but, rather than inserting a required return ( r ) to get the market’s g , insert a g to get the expected return to buying the stock with that g. There is a simple formula that does the job. For buying the firm (unlevered), [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn15.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn16.png] is the unlevered (enterprise) book-to-price ratio and RNOA 1 is for the forward year. Like a bond yield, ER Firm is not the required return for risk, but rather the expected return from buying at the enterprise market price, P NOA . If the market is underpricing the stock, the expected return is higher. The formula is called the weighted average expected return formula (not to be confused with the weighted average cost of capital, or WACC) because the expected return is a weighted average of profitability and growth, with the enterprise book-to-price ratio supplying the weights that add up to 1.0. Similarly, the levered expected return from buying the firm and its net debt is given by [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn17.png] where growth is now levered growth. But, you object, we don’t know the growth rate! That point has been driven home in this book! However, the calculation facilitates scenario analysis. Go to Microsoft in the action box on the next page. There a growth-return profile is developed for different growth scenarios. Read it as follows: If Microsoft delivers 5 percent growth, you can expect to receive a return of 8.71 percent per year for investing at the current price. On the upside, you can expect a return of 10.64 percent if the firms delivers 7 percent growth. For every percentage increase in the growth rate, there is a 0.965 percent increase to the expected return. But downside return is also quantified: If there is negative 2 percent growth, you can expect only a 1.95 percent return. The small differences between different growth rates might not seem much, but these are expected returns over the long run; with compounding, that makes quite a difference. You do not have to wait for the long term, however. These are just indicators; if the market is mispriced and subsequently corrects itself, the return you expect to earn is equivalent to a compounded return over the long run. The first component of ER E is [IMAGE: ../images/ch08eqn18.png] which is the forward E/P ratio, the inverse of the P/E ratio. It is common to screen on P/E ratios as in chapter 2 , but this does not accommodate P/E ratios with different growth. The addition of growth in the second term in the ER E formula modifies the screen. Once again, it is up to you to bring your knowledge of the business to apply probabilities to scenarios, as indicated in the box. That might give low probabilities to some scenarios, higher probabilities to others. Now you understand your risk and, with your personal disposition for risk, you can reach a judgment as to whether you want to invest in this profile. Like the set of values in the value-at-risk profile, the return-growth profile provides some quantification of risk. However, it does not get to the elusive required return. As we have suggested, that is probably not attainable. And it involves a risk premium that is personal to the investor, something a calculation should not paternalistically assume. The best we can do is develop the risk profile here. However, there is added benefit from admitting that the market may be inefficient: Risk is not in the required return, but rather it is in the risk that the expected return from buying value different from price might not be achieved. That is what the value investor is looking for, not the required return. The value investor appreciates that growth is at risk, and the growth-return profile conveys this. The profile shows how well you will do investing in the firm under different scenarios. It gives your upside and downside expected returns. What are your probabilities for these scenarios? If firms with the same fundamental risk are efficiently priced, they should have the same growth-return profile. So, if one firm in a risk class (with the same fundamental risk) has a higher return profile, choose that one because it has higher expected returns for the same risk. You might put firms in the same risk class by industry or, more diligently, those with the same risk scenario analysis above. If you are bold, you could engage in pairs trading: Go long on the firm with the dominating profile and short the other. Risk cancels in that zero-net-investment position, with the return then due to the relative mispricing and not risk. But be careful to do your homework so that you feel secure, for shorting stocks can be risky. The exercise for the levered expected return is in the next action box. But rather than for an individual stock, it is for the market as a whole, approximated by the S&P 500. Value investors do not buy the index, of course; that is for passive investors investing in an index fund. But maybe we can help that passive investor. She does face the question of when to buy the index and when to exit. She entertains the question: Is the market too high at present? Is it peak passive? And it helps the investor allocating investment to stocks and bonds: Is it time to move from stocks to bonds or vice versa? Expected returns for the market are driven by gross domestic product (GDP) for the economy, so a good benchmark is the growth rate at the average historical annual GDP growth rate of 4 percent. At this rate, the growth-return profile informs us that the expected return is 7.55 percent. But, if average growth in the future is only 1 percent, that falls to 5.18 percent. On the upside, a growth rate of 6 percent comes with an expected return of 9.12 percent. The passive investor tracks the profile over time as the index price changes to evaluate moves into and out of an index fund. She must be careful, however: If the risk to the market is higher, it might be priced to yield a higher return in compensation, so a move into the index fund might be moving to risk. The move might be evaluated with macro fundamentals that indicate uncertainty about future GDP growth. Does this really work? Figure 8.4 plots the actual returns over the following three years, annualized, on the expected return (ER E ) estimated each December 31, 1982–2018 with a 4 percent growth rate. The actual returns are correlated with the expected return, with the correlations given below the figure. These correlations increase with the return horizon, with a correlation of 0.454 for 3-year returns. (Those for longer periods are based on fewer observations.) As an example, the relatively low expected return of 6.61 percent at the end of the 1990s price bubble forecasted a return over the next three years of −14.5 percent. At the end of 2008, after a drop of 37.0 percent for the year, the index indicated a high expected return of 10.09 percent. The return for the index over the next 3 years was 14.0 percent per year, with a 26.5 percent return for the following year, 2009. In 2011, the ER E was 10.44 percent, followed by a return over 3 years of 20.5 percent per year. However, the correlation is not perfect. [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-4.png] 8.4 Returns to investing on the expected return (ER) implied by the S&P 500 Index with a 4 percent forecasted GDP growth rate. Figure plots the actual returns over 3 years (annualized) from investing in the S&P 500 (left hand vertical axis) against the ER estimated for the index at the end of each year (right hand vertical axis), 1982–2019. The actual return plots for each year are the subsequent 3-year return. Below the figure are the correlations between ER and actual returns over following years. Again, we must be careful, for a high expected return might just imply more risk. The high ER E at the end of 2008 was very much associated with uncertainty about the future of the economy. The ER E must be read alongside macro indicators. Correlation between ER E and subsequent actual returns: ER E (g=4%) Correlation with 1-yr forward return 0.268 Correlation with 2-yr forward return 0.341 Correlation with 3-yr forward return 0.454 Correlation with 4-yr forward return 0.480 Correlation with 5-yr forward return 0.517 Managing Risk The value investor looks for returns from mispriced stocks but, from his fundamental analysis, understands that those returns are at risk. He seeks returns in the upper tail but knows that returns in the lower tail are possible. Is there any way he can protect his returns? Standard advice recommends diversification, and that may protect the investor somewhat. However, we have seen that diversification embeds a trap, losing the benefits of diversification when prices fall and protection is most needed. Standard advice also counsels the investor to allocate a certain amount of his investment dollars to bonds for safety (often a 60/40 split). That typically has reduced risk. Peter Bernstein, an American investment theorist and historian, called the 60/40 allocation “the center of gravity” for long-term investors. However, even nominally risk-free bonds are not risk-free; they too are susceptible to interest rate risk and inflation risk. Stocks are also susceptible, as figure 8.1 tell us, but bonds are not always a refuge. Stocks and bonds typically move together; as interest rates increase, bond prices fall but, as that interest rate goes into the discount rate for stocks, so do stock prices. This is particularly the case when inflation is over 3 percent, as recently. The correlation between returns on the S&P 500 and the 10-year U.S. government bond from 2000 to 2021 was negative (−0.65), but from 1966 to 2000 the correlation was positive (0.37). Over all these years, the correlation was 0.12. In 2022, U.S. stocks dropped 19 percent and bonds 13 percent, but long-dated U.S. Treasuries were down 29 percent. Global bonds lost 31 percent and UK bonds lost 39 percent. A 60/40 allocation dropped 17 percent. Bonds can also be tax-disadvantaged. In the United States, interest on bonds is taxed at ordinary income rates rather than the lower rate on “qualified” dividends and capital gains on stocks. The answer to the final Teaser question is clear. Is there nowhere to hide? That is a good question for the defensive investor concerned with the preservation of capital. Safe Equities Could selected equities provide protection? A safe stock, usually a large profitable firm with sustainable earnings but not a lot of risky earnings growth, was once called a “blue chip.” That term has gone out of fashion, but the idea of buying safe equities persists. Safe equities are sometimes identified by their industry—consumer staples, for example. In 2022 the S&P 500 was down by 18.1 percent, but stocks of consumer staples in the index were down only 2.7 percent, utility stocks down only 0.5 percent, and healthcare stocks down only 3.2 percent; consumers must still buy groceries, pay their electricity bills, and take their prescription medicines even in bad times. But industry membership is just a rough proxy for risk. In 2022, Campbell Soup (food, beverages, and snacks) and General Mills (foods) were up 30 percent and 25 percent for the year respectively—but Clorox (cleaning supplies, disinfectants, and bleaches) was down 20 percent. A fundamental analysis of risk uncovers safe equities, and that involves an assessment of earnings growth at risk. Financing leverage is the first stop in evaluating a firm’s risk: Although leverage adds growth, that growth is at risk, as we saw in chapter 5 . With this risk, the investor is faced with the question: Will the firm meet its debt commitments in downturns? ( Chapter 13 takes you there.) However, there is also risk in the operating activities of the business. Coming to grips with that risk complements the analysis of leverage risk. Financing risk and operating risk multiply: High operating risk with high financing risk can produce the perfect storm, landing the investor in the left-hand tail of return outcomes. Table 8.1 reports on a risk metric based on a combination of fundamentals that indicate earnings growth at risk. The table sorts firms into ten portfolios each year based on this risk, with low-numbered portfolios classified as low risk and high-numbered portfolios as high risk. Panel A of the table reports fundamental features for these portfolios showing averages over the years 1981–2012. Panel B reports the returns associated with the risk that these fundamental features convey.
