Penman and J. Zhu. “An Accounting-Based Asset Pricing Model and a Fundamental Factor.” Journal of Accounting and Economics 73.2-3 (2022): 101476. Our valuation platform prices book value by adding (discounted) future earnings, so price should be lower if the risk to those earnings is higher. Panel A reports that P/B is indeed lower (B/P is higher) the higher the risk: Price discounts for risk to yield a lower P/B. Low-risk firms also have the following features: high realized sales growth; increasing profit margins from those sales; declining selling, general, and administrative expenses (SG&A) as a percent of sales; declining R&D relative to sales; and growth in NOA on the safe balance sheet. The fundamentals change as risk gets higher (as we move down the columns of the table), with high-risk firms exhibiting fundamentals quite different from the low-risk ones. These fundamentals involve the realization principle and conservative accounting discussed earlier. At the far right of the table, earnings growth (two years in advance) increases with risk: Higher-risk portfolios have higher earnings growth, but that is growth at risk. That is indicated by the earnings betas in the table—sometimes called fundamental betas—that measure the sensitivity of a firm’s earnings against shocks to market-wide earnings. Growth is risky, and that explains the discount to risk in the P/B ratio. It also warns of buying low P/B ratios in the simple value strategies of chapter 2 : You might just be buying risk. The safe, low-risk portfolios are those with high realized sales and profit margin growth, but not high future growth at risk. It is the opposite for high-risk portfolios. Panel B of the table reports the returns for these portfolios and some associated risk features. The returns that these firms earn in the following year are increasing in risk. Yes, risk is rewarded with higher returns (and safe firms earn lower returns). And, yes, those increasing returns are associated with increasing risk: The higher-risk portfolios have higher betas. Significantly, they have high downside beta: High-risk firms, though exhibiting higher growth, experience risk to that growth in down markets: Growth is risky. They also experience a much larger range of return outcomes, and the kurtosis—a measure of the extent of extreme return realizations in the tails—is higher for high-risk firms. Low-risk firms have less of this risk and, significantly, low downside beta: Safe. Altogether, Panels A and B tell us that fundamental analysis of risk conveys the risk to investing. The financial measures, while informative, are not sufficient. The investor is well advised to evaluate the quality of management, their history for honesty and good execution in serving investors rather than themselves. Disclosures should be reviewed for any indication of concern. And, of course, understanding the business, the competitive landscape, and the sustainability of the business is, again, primary. The fundamental features for portfolios 1–3 indicate safe firms that typically yield lower returns (as lower risk should). But the diligent value investor always investigates further. Some of these firms could be underpriced, potentially yielding high returns, and some could be overpriced. Indeed, by the end of 2022, the forward P/E ratios of the consumer staples, utilities, and healthcare stocks whose relative returns (above) during the 2022 down market were so impressive, were 21, 19, and 18, respectively according to FactSet—higher than the S&P 500 P/E of 17. That could be due to lower risk, but it is worth checking for overpricing. This is done through our analysis that indicates price is different from value where it is the risk in the growth that is being mispriced. With fear of risk and a desire to preserve capital, the investor might concentrate his efforts on firms in portfolios 1–3. If he finds bargains here, he is very happy; he is investing with a margin of safety. Let’s call that investor the defensive investor: Higher returns with low risk. A more adventurous investor might also seek returns in the high-risk firms, understanding that she is taking on more risk in doing so. Again, it’s your personal attitude toward risk that determines your positioning. Table 8.2 reports that firms with low fundamental risk provide safety in downturns. It gives the returns on a market portfolio for both up- and down-market periods. The market portfolio is widely diversified, so the returns here are those with diversification. During the period 2000–2002, safe equities dropped only −1.70 percent per year compared with −14.73 percent for the diversified market portfolio. In the parlance of modern finance, safe equities provided a hedge. Or, in more common language, safe equities provide insurance against bad times. However, buying insurance comes at a cost, and the cost to buying insurance with safe equities is earning lower returns in up markets, as happened in 1995–1999. There is a side note: Safe equities did not provide protection during the financial crisis of 2008–2009. During the down months, September 2008 to February 2009, the market was down 50.5 percent while safe equities were down 65.3 percent. However, in the subsequent up months (March to December 2009, when the market was up 45.7 percent), safe equities were up 95.1 percent. A strange time. Table 8.2 Protection from risk: Annual percent returns for safe equities in up markets and down markets Years Mean market return Mean return for safe equities 1995–1999 (Up) 27.75 16.29 2000–2002 (Down) −14.73 −1.70 2009–2017 (Up) 16.16 16.33 Source : S. Penman, J. Zhu, and H. Wang. “Returns on Risky Portfolios Are Explained by a Two-Factor Model,” 2022. At https://ssrn.com/abstract=4020541 . Figure 8.5 tracks the cumulative return from investing in 1967 with a 60/40 split between the S&P 500 index and safe equities, updating it each year up to 2021. For comparison, the cumulative return for the classic 60/40 split between the index and the 10-year U.S. bond is also plotted. For the more conservative investor, there are also returns for a 40/60 split between the S&P 500 index and safe equities. By the end of 2021, the cumulative return from the 60/40 split with safe equities was 236.5 percent, while that from the split with bonds was 149.5 percent. The cumulative return from the 40/60 safe equity allocation was 195.7 percent. Much of the differences occur after 2000. [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-5.png] 8.5 Cumulative returns, 1975–2021 for allocations between stocks and bonds and between stocks and safe equities. The return on stocks is that for the S&P 500. The return on bonds is that for the 10-year U.S. bond. Source : S. Penman, J. Zhu, and H. Wang. 2025. Safe Equities: An Alternative Allocation to Bonds. Financial Analysts Journal , forthcoming. At ssrn.com/abstract=4696945 . Hedging Unwanted Risk Fundamental analysis of risk identifies risk drivers. When we applied this to Delta Air Lines, we saw that one of those risks was exposure to fuel prices. An investor might see value in investing in an airline but does not want exposure to fuel prices. An airline might hedge fuel prices, satisfying this investor. But, if not, the investor can buy her own hedge, eliminating the risk. That applies to all unwanted risks in a firm, provided hedging instruments are available. These could be risk in commodity prices, currency risks, and interest rate risk, provided the firm is not hedging on investors’ behalf. Create your personalized value-at-risk profile. Hedging is buying insurance, and insurance comes at a cost—in this case, the cost of buying these hedging instruments. That’s OK; the best insurance, as they say, is insurance one does not have to claim on. But be sure of your instrument. Beware of WMD, Weapons of Mass Destruction. See the following vignette box. A Vignette Weapons of Mass Destruction Pension funds are always concerned about having sufficient assets to pay retirees. In recent years, UK pension funds adopted a strategy called liability-driven investment (LDI), buying interest-rate swaps and other derivatives tied to U.K government bonds (gilts) to hedge against the risk of falling interest rates increasing the present value of their future obligations. The LDI strategies also use leverage to try to close the gap between pension assets and their future pension promises, buying interest rate exposure using far fewer assets. According to the International Monetary Fund (IMF), by 2022 there was £1.5 trillion invested in LDIs (about two-thirds of UK GDP). LDIs free up assets to close pension fund deficits. If all goes well, OK. But, if asset values fall, the collateral backing up the LDIs falls. That happened in September 2022 as bond pieces dropped significantly on the rise of interest rates with inflation and economic uncertainty. The pension funds faced margin calls. With not enough cash to meet those calls, they were required to sell off investments in collateralized debt, private equity, and venture funds at significantly discounted prices to meet the calls. Mass destruction. Fortunately for the pension funds, the Bank of England (BOE) stepped in to buy bonds but, in doing so, reversed its policy of reducing inflation by selling bonds (and reducing the money supply). More inflation, more mass destruction. Upon the later announcement by the governor of the BOE that the central bank was ending its bond buying, the UK and U.S. stock markets dropped significantly on fears of financial instability. So did the pound. So did the collateralized debt that pension funds held as collateral. More mass destruction. The episode is reminiscent of the crisis in credit default swaps (CDS) during the 2008 financial crisis. Investors thought these instruments would protect them against losses in corporate bonds but counterparties, notably American International Group, Inc. (AIG), could not honor the commitments. Again, the U.S. government stepped in with bailouts to reduce the systemic risk of mass destruction. Even with a nonspeculative hedge instrument, beware of counterparty risk. Hedging Inflation Risk In discussing the effect of inflation on P/E ratios in figure 8.1 , we asked whether the investor can find equities that provide a hedge against inflation, just as a variable-rate bond does. Figure 8.6 shows some promise. This is the same depiction as in figure 8.1 but now the P/E is the forward P/E based on expected earnings from firms adjusting to inflation. The clear negative correlation between inflation and the trailing P/E in figure 8.1 is not evident here, indicating that firms’ earnings adjust to inflation, providing at least a partial hedge. [IMAGE: ../images/fig8-6.png] 8.6 Forward P/E for the S&P 500 and expected inflation, 1960–2021. However, this depiction is for firms on average. Some firms can provide more of a hedge than others. Look for firms with the following characteristics: Firms with the ability to increase sales prices without a corresponding increase in expenses. These are firms with low price elasticity of demand for products, either because those products are essentials for the consumer or there are high barriers to entry from competitors. They thus increase PM during inflation. An examination of gross margins and their variability in inflationary times gives an indication. Firms without these features but with susceptibility to higher prices for their inputs, both commodity prices and wage rates, are at the other extreme. They are typically firms with little market power and thus low profit margins. With the burst of inflation in 2022, the stock prices of retailers whose costs increased more than revenues dropped significantly on their first quarter profits reports: Walmart by 6.8 percent, Target by 24.9 percent, Home Depot by 5.2 percent, and Best Buy by 10.5 percent. Firms with licensing revenues as a percentage of other firms’ sales. Credit card companies that charge merchants a fixed percentage of sales transactions are an example. Firms with the ability to expand revenues and earnings without excessive investment. These are firms with expanding ATO during inflation. Firms with low depreciation charges because they bought plant and equipment at lower prices in the past. They are more of a hedge than firms who must replace plant at higher prices in the near future. Firms with fixed-rate debt obligations for which the repayment declines in real terms with inflation. Firms with variable-rate debt assets, which are protected as interest rates increase with inflation. A bank can have fixed-rate debt obligations but variable-rate mortgage assets. On the other hand, a bank with fixed-rate mortgages is susceptible. Banks typically benefit as interest rates rise, with the spread between lending rates (on loans) and borrowing rates (on bank deposits) increasing. The four large UK banks (Lloyds, Barclays, HSBC, and NatWest) benefited as the BOE raised interest rates in 2022 to fight inflation, earning higher interest on deposits of £900 billion at the central bank and increasing lending rates. Just as the investor can hedge risk more generally on private account, she can also do so with inflation risk, provided instruments are available. Commodities such as gold and copper, and real estate are typical hedges. Just like a firm, an investor can issue fixed-rate debt, but the only way for an individual to do so is by buying real estate with a fixed-rate mortgage. That yields protection with both increases in real estate prices and the decline in the real value of the debt: the investor is replicating the bank with fixed-rate debt and variable-rate mortgages. Value Investing Principles Here are the principles from this chapter to add to your collection: P/E and P/B ratios are negatively correlated with interest rates and inflation. The CAPM does not work. Abandon the question: What is the cost of capital? Diversification can reduce risk but embeds a trap. The required return is a personal matter. Risk is in the tails. The primary risk of investing is the risk in paying too much, but buying at value also involves risk. Fundamental analysis identifies value at risk. Investing by the book profiles risk to growth. There is protection from risk in safe equities. Building a Value Investing Engine There is much in this chapter to embellish your value investing tool. The sensitivity analysis incorporated in the previous chapter can now be applied to depict value-at-risk profiles. Growth-return profiles can also be developed. Creative graphical display will vividly enhance the output. The Exercise Book Risk analysis exercises bring out the points in this chapter. The Case Book Case 13: They Say Patience is A Virtue Will Amazon Ever Add the Value in the Market Price? After many years of losses, annual EPS had been in the black for seven years in a row up to 2021. But EPS for 2022 was a loss of $0.27, down from a profit of $3.30 in 2021, even though sales revenue was up 11.5 percent. With the firm reporting losses from its founding in 1994 up to 2014, the subsequent profits appeared to reward shareholders for their patience. But, with the 2022 loss, patience is wearing thin, particularly with the drop in stock price of 35.7 percent over the previous 12 months with no dividend. With a per-share price at $98.38 after the 2022 10-K filing, a forward P/E of 69, and a price-to-book ratio of 7.24, there seems to be considerable speculation in the market price. Shareholders well remember the late 1990s when Amazon was a star of the new tech companies and traded at $4.25 per share (on a split-adjusted basis), only to fall to $0.30 when the tech stock bubble burst. Ouch! The increase in price to $98.38 over the subsequent 23 years was reward for the patient, but is that justified by the fundamentals? Or is it speculation? Case 14: Buying Microsoft What is My Expected Return from Buying the Firm? We have spent a bit of time with Microsoft. We calculated an expected levered growth rate in the market price in case 6, then understood in case 8 that we should work with the unlevered growth rate—the growth in the business—to avoid paying for growth generated by leverage. In chapter 7 , we investigated the drivers of that growth rate, the diagnostics to challenge the market’s assessment of the growth path. Now, let’s turn things around and calculate the expected return from buying Microsoft at the current market price. Case 15: Buying The Market Challenging the S&P 500 The S&P 500 is accepted as representative of the stock market as a whole since it consists of 500 stocks weighted by market value (considerably more than the 30 stocks in the Dow Jones Industrial Average). A large number of passive investors buy the index via index funds. That gives them broad exposure to stocks, which is sensible for someone who wishes to get the benefits of wealth creation in corporations but does not wish to (or cannot) do the analysis to selectively pick stocks. But that comes with risk of course, underlined by John Maynard Keynes’s observation after he got stung buying stocks in the 1920s: To suppose that safety first consists in having a small gamble in a large number of different companies where I have no information to reach a good judgment, as compared with a substantial stake in a company where one’s information is adequate, strikes me as a travesty of investment policy. —Keynes to the Chairman of Provincial Insurance Company, February 6, 1942, quoted in P Bernstein, Capital Ideas , NY: John Wiley and Sons, 1992 The S&P 500 returned a handsome 28.7 percent total return in 2021, with a forward P/E of 22.7 at December 31. That led some analysts to claim that the market is overvalued. Peak passive? Indeed, the return on the index for 2022 was −18.11 percent. Carry out an analysis to ask the question: Is the S&P 500 currently overvalued? What is the expected return from buying the S&P 500? Remember the weighted-average return formula. Is the market “peak passive”? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 9 Keeping the Book Chapter 9 Keeping the Book The Anchoring Balance Sheet A safe anchor secures ships. So must a balance sheet that anchors the value investor—but it must be a safe balance sheet. This chapter describes the accounting necessary for a safe balance sheet. That is a benchmark for investigating whether balance sheets under U.S. GAAP, governed by the Financial Accounting Standards Board, or balance sheets under International Financial Reporting Standards (IFRS) are safe anchors. To what extent must the value investor be concerned about these published balance sheets? Must these balance sheets be corrected to serve the value investor? The question is one of accounting quality, a question we will continue to pursue in the next chapter on the income statement. There are a few points to keep in mind as we proceed. First, you do not have to become a forensic accountant to feel comfortable; U.S. GAAP and IFRS accounting practices are fairly good as is. And there are auditors, boards of directors and their audit committees, regulators, and the threat of legal sanctions to maintain this quality. Second, that said, the discovery of bad accounting typically shocks the stock price; the so-called “accounting torpedo,” though rare, adds to our risk. Be vigilant. Third, accounting quality analysis detects “red flags” that signal possible danger, and we will raise several flags. But don’t get too scared: Red flags are just prompts to be careful and undertake further investigation. Most of the time, they are found to be due to operating factors, or are immaterial, or are easily accommodated by adjusting the accounting. In short, while we cover quite a number of potential accounting issues to check out, don’t be alarmed. The investor typically gets to a comfort level with the balance sheet quickly. To evaluate balance sheet quality, we must introduce more accounting. So here is a chance to sharpen up your accounting knowledge. We can only provide an overview of selected key issues in the Accounting Primers, so keep an intermediate or advanced accounting text open beside you as we proceed. We hope that, as you see accounting at work in the book, you will gain a keen interest in “good accounting” and an appreciation of “bad accounting.” Several vignettes serve as examples, as in other chapters. The reader can appreciate these episodes better by googling the episode or checking out Wikipedia or Investopedia. The Safe Balance Sheet Once again, return to our platform for accounting for value: [IMAGE: ../images/ch09eqn01.png] Future (residual) earnings are discounted for risk, for they have not yet been realized. However, the current book value is not discounted; it goes into Value one-for-one without any discount for risk. That tells us: There should not be risk in the balance sheet. This is what defines a quality balance sheet, a safe balance sheet. In more colloquial terms, a safe balance sheet is one that cannot come back and hit you later. A risky balance sheet is one that exposes you to those hits. Off-balance-sheet liabilities will come back to haunt you. Speculative asset values can collapse. Manipulated assets will have future consequences when payoffs are not realized, as will manipulated liabilities. A safe balance sheet does not have these risks. Like a safe anchor, it holds firm. Of course, we look to the balance sheet to convey risk to future earnings (from leverage, for example), but it cannot be a balance sheet that adds risk because of the accounting. There is a history to the value investor’s demand for a safe balance sheet. The boom times of the 1920s saw accountants writing up assets toward the speculative market values prevailing at the time. But in a few short days in October 1929, those values collapsed. As we have said, that is when value investing came to the fore with the likes of Benjamin Graham and John Maynard Keynes. These investors cried: No more water in the balance sheet! Water can evaporate! In the United States, the Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) was founded in 1933 under that mantra, a position held until the flirtation with fair value accounting in the 1990s. But history repeats: The SEC granted Enron Corporation permission to use fair value accounting, marking up its assets for long-term energy supply contracts before earnings were realized. But, once again, the water evaporated. So, the value investor tells the accountant: Don’t give me a speculative balance sheet. Tell me what you know and leave the speculation to me. Fortunately, accounting is typically practiced under a doctrine of prudence: When there is risk, be conservative. We saw the resulting accounting principles in the last chapter. That suits the investor who is confronted with risk. A Safe Balance Sheet Means a Safe Growth Rate to Challenge This book has cast value investing as a matter of challenging the imputed growth forecast in the market price. A safe balance is important in the endeavor. If the balance sheet is overstated, either from overstated assets or omitted liabilities, the growth rate ( g ) inferred from the market price is higher than otherwise. Look at the action box below where the market’s growth rate is calculated with and without a portion of Delta Air Lines’s off-balance sheet liabilities. These are aircraft purchase commitments and guarantees to regional carriers reported in footnotes but not on the balance sheet. (These contingent liabilities will be discussed later in the chapter). This is a risky airline, so we set the required return to 11 percent. With the liabilities off balance sheet, the growth rate is 1.98 percent per year, but 1.27 percent with the liabilities booked to the balance sheet, a lower growth hurdle to cover in order to buy the stock. Why is this so? Well, the 1.97 percent growth rate without the booked liabilities is a rate that does not incorporate the hit to growth if the contingent off-balance-sheet liabilities come due. If they do, growth will be lower than estimated. The safe balance sheet yields a growth rate protected from such hits, a safe growth rate to challenge. This example does not mean that the contingent liabilities should be booked to the balance sheet. That depends on the probability that they will have to be honored, as discussed later. Value Investing in Action Balance sheet and the market’s growth rate, Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) The market’s implied growth rate is estimated with two balance sheets at December, 2019, with and without off-balance-sheet liabilities (amounts in $millions). These are operating liabilities, so booking them to the balance sheet reduces net operating assets. Net operating assets 18,365 Net debt 14,373 Common equity 3,992 [IMAGE: ../images/ch09eqn03.png] An overstated balance sheet yields a higher implied growth rate . Features of a Safe Balance Sheet The safe balance sheet has the following features: No significant off-balance-sheet liabilities. Book value (Assets − Liabilities) is not written up until risk is resolved. Book value increases with earnings, so this means that earnings are not booked (and added to the balance sheet) until a realization principle has been satisfied (e.g., a customer has been securely won). Expected earnings from future customers are not booked, deemed speculative, or at risk. There is no speculation in the balance sheet. See the Accounting Primer on the topic of revenue recognition in the last chapter. Assets are recorded on the balance sheet at their cost, with no valued added until earnings are recognized. This principle has given this accounting system the name “historical cost accounting,” but a better name is “realization accounting”: Wait for realization with a customer able to pay before writing up the balance sheet from historical cost. If the carrying value of an asset on the balance sheet is deemed to be higher than the realized value it will generate, it is written down with an impairment. This removes the potential negative surprise in the future from losses from overvalued assets; the balance sheet cannot hit you later. Under conservative accounting, investments that are particularly risky are not booked to the balance sheet. Such assets include investment in research and development (R&D), or investment in research under IFRS, and brand-building expenditures where there is significant uncertainty about whether the investment will result in realized earnings. Thus, speculation with high risk is removed from the balance sheet. See the Accounting Primer on the topic of conservative accounting for investment in the last chapter. This is prudent accounting in the face of risk. It is “good accounting” for the value investor. The Safe Balance Sheet: How to Get There U.S. GAAP and IFRS largely have these features, so the investor can proceed with the assurance that these balance sheets are basically OK. But issues remain, to which we now turn. In a double-entry system, accounting in the balance sheet also affects the income statement, so many of these issues will come up in the next chapter on the income statement. There are three issues to address to ensure that the balance sheet is a quality one: Accounting for operating and financing activities Accounting for off-balance-sheet items Accounting for on-balance-sheet items Well, we have dealt with the first issue in chapter 5 : Been there, done that. The issue is of foremost importance to a quality balance sheet: If we don’t separate operating and financing items, we cannot get a clear view of value generation in the firm from the net operating assets, nor of the financing risk. Analysis always begins with the reformulation of balance sheets. The other two issues require our attention. We will work with the 2020 balance sheet of Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) in table 9.1 , along with the summary reformulated balance sheet. We choose this balance sheet because 2020 was the pandemic year when airlines were hit hard, with Delta reporting a comprehensive loss of $13.4 billion. With Delta so much at risk, the investor is most concerned, and the risk of buying its balance sheet is part of that concern. Now is the time for you to get more familiar with the SEC’s Form 10-K. This might be hard going at first but, as you become more familiar with these and other filings, you will be more at ease, knowing where to go for answers to questions and able to separate substance from boilerplate. Indeed, you will see how enlightening a dry-looking document can be. Not the racy detective novel but, all the same, with much to detect. In table 9.1 , you see the large drop in shareholders’ equity is due to the large loss in 2020. You also see the significant increase in net debt and leverage. This is, indeed, a risky balance sheet! See chapter 5 . Prelude: Understanding the Business As always, the investigation into a balance sheet starts with an understanding of the business. That comes from reading the front end of the 10-K and the Management Discussion and Analysis (MD&A) report, along with any other commentary you can find in industry publications, analysts’ reports, and the press. Here is the composition of the business gleaned from the 10-K: — passenger and cargo air transportation — mileage-credit sales, including a co-branded credit card with American Express (see note 1) — refinery operation (Monroe Energy, 100 percent owned), largely to supply Delta — joint ventures with antitrust immunity: Virgin Atlantic, Virgin Australia, Air France–KLM, Alitalia, Korean Air Lines — investments in Hanjin KAL (13 percent), China Eastern (3 percent), Air France–KLM (9 percent), Virgin Atlantic (49 percent) — service agreements with regional carriers: Delta Connection — other business: Delta Vacations and MRO Delta, a maintenance service to other airlines We understand that, with the deregulation of airlines, the business is very competitive. On the other hand, government-approved airline alliances have created a form of oligopoly that protects from competition. We also understand that airlines are such an important part of the travel infrastructure that governments may see them as “too big to fail.” This was the case during the pandemic: U.S. airlines received significant government assistance to survive. Accounting Primer Accounting for contingencies Under ASC Topic 450 issued by the Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) ASC Topic-450, a liability is booked to the balance sheet if it is “probable” and estimable. If only “possible” or not estimable, it must be disclosed in footnotes without quantification. However, if “remote” it is ignored. IAS 37 issued by the International Accounting standards Board is similar, but there are some differences in the guidance. This is vague language. Warranty liabilities and liabilities for refunds to customers are booked, as are unpaid insurance claims, but many other contingent liabilities get footnote disclosure (unless remote). Accounting for Off-balance sheet Items Off-balance sheet Liabilities An unrecorded liability can come back and hit you later. Most off-balance sheet liabilities are contingent liabilities; that is, the firm might or might not be obligated, depending on a contingency. They arise from product liability, labor, investor, and tax lawsuits; environmental remediation regulations; and commitments in contracts. The issue for the investor is whether to book these to her reformulated balance sheet. Watch for emerging regulations that could create liabilities or contingencies related to environmental or social performance. To get an insight to the accounting for these liabilities see the Accounting Primer above. Then see the Coca-Cola vignette below. A Vignette Tax Liability for The Coca-Cola Company (KO), 2021 Coca-Cola Co., Whirlpool Corp., and Eaton Corp. all lost to the Internal Revenue Service in the U.S. Tax Court over the two years up to 2021. But none of the companies had yet booked the bulk of those tax expenses and liabilities in their financial statements. Coca-Cola disclosed that total potential liability from its tax dispute with the Internal Revenue Service could reach $12 billion. The company lost its tax court case in November 2021 but, with the case on appeal, had so far recorded only $438 million as an accrued liability. Wall Street Journal , February 24, 2022 Coca-Cola’s tax court loss stems from the worldwide locations in which it reports profits, much of it in foreign subsidiaries with low corporate tax rates. The tax case relates to the years 2007–2009. Let’s scan Delta’s 2020 10-K for contingencies. (Do you have it on the screen before you?) Here is what we collect: Aircraft purchase agreements (Note 12) $13.88 billion in commitments to buy 224 aircraft from 2021–2025. What if Delta’s operations decline with the pandemic, and they have no need for the aircraft? What are the cancellation fees? Can the commitments be restructured? Capacity purchase agreements (Note 12) $7.725 billion in revenue guarantees over 2021–2025 to regional carriers bringing passengers to Delta hubs. What is the probability of exercise? Product, labor, or environmental lawsuits? (Note 12) “Will have no material effect,” says Delta. Indemnification of lessors of aircraft and real estate (Note 12) “We cannot reasonably estimate our potential payments under indemnities.” Each of these raises the question of whether the contingent liability should be booked to the balance sheet. Each depends on the probability of realization and whether the amount is estimable. Understanding the business will help you to determine both. There are four alternatives for the value investor. First, book the liability (if there is a dollar number) as if it is a firm obligation. This is very conservative, but protective. With such a conservative balance sheet, our reverse engineering excise will return a lower growth rate to be justified. Second, book the liability with a haircut if you have some grasp on the probabilities (less conservative). Third, increase your required return for investing in the firm: You have information on risk. Fourth, walk away: Thanks, but not today, Mrs. Murphy. With the contingent liability under aircraft purchase agreements, there is also a contingent asset (to be supplied with aircraft). Thinking conservatively, realize that this asset is not booked, just as a potential asset for retaining employees with stock options is not booked. But the investor will still keep this asset in mind: If the manufacturer performs, the firm has additional aircraft it might need locked in. Off-balance sheet Vehicles A particularly dangerous off-balance sheet liability is the variable interest entity (VIE), aka a special purpose entity (SPE) or a special investment vehicle (SIV). These are limited partnerships where the firm is not the controlling partner, so it does not bring the entity’s assets and liabilities onto its balance sheet; nevertheless, the firm has substantial beneficial interest. The issue is whether the vehicle has recourse to the firm for liabilities, for example because the firm guarantees the debt of the SPE. Delta’s footnote 10 tells us that the firm has an interest in a VIE, the lessor of Terminal 4 at JFK airport. As Delta is the predominant lessee, this does not appear to be an issue. Enron Corp., the spectacular corporate failure in 2001, was a classic example, though banks and others have also played with these instruments. The firm capitalized the special vehicles with its own stock, taking a note, with recourse to the firm with complex financial instruments and with options for the vehicle to have their Enron stock repurchased. As Enron’s stock price declined, calls were made on Enron to repurchase the stock at a higher fixed strike price. Enron was faced with recourse liabilities. Compounded by Enron’s fair value accounting (below), this was a perfect storm. The best way to deal with these structures is to consolidate them, bringing the liabilities on balance sheet if there is recourse. The vehicles are often complex structures that are hard to figure out even with the footnote disclosures. Beware of financing structures like back leverage, net asset value (NAV) financing, and subscription financing. Indeed, beware of all opaque financing structures. Beware of liabilities to be settled in cryptocurrencies. Again, if you see something that you cannot penetrate, run. Stock Option Overhang Compensating employees with stock options often creates an off-balance-sheet liability called the option overhang. To understand, recall from chapter 5 that when a firm issues stock to new shareholders at less than fair value, it loses value for current shareholders, just as it does when repurchasing stock at more than fair value. To drive home the point, there is no economic difference between paying an employee $60 in cash and issuing stock worth $100 to the employee for $40; paying employees in the shareholders’ paper is the same for the shareholder as paying in cash. That loss is not recorded under U.S. GAAP or IFRS. Nor is the anticipated loss when options are outstanding but not yet exercised. That is the off-balance sheet liability, called the option overhang. The following Accounting Primer lays out the accounting for stock options. Accounting Primer Accounting for Stock Options There are four significant events with stock options granted to employees: Grant date: Options are granted, with the exercise price usually set at the stock price on that date. The value of the options granted is set at the option value at that date, usually with a modified Black-Scholes pricing formula. Vesting date: The date up to which the employee must work to be entitled to exercise the options. The option value at grant date is amortized over the vesting period and charged to the income statement as compensation. Exercise date: The employee exercises the option if the stock price exceeds the exercise price. However, the difference is a loss to shareholders that is not recorded if more than the grant-date option value booked. Lapse date: The option does not go into the money, so the option lapses. Accordingly, compensation recorded earlier is overstated and operating income understated: The employee was not paid. Go to Delta’s footnote 14 to review its equity compensation. The overhang is calculated in the action box on the next page. The option overhang is the difference between the current market price ($49.06 per share) and the exercise price for the outstanding options, which is $52.37 (here out-of-the-money), plus the further time value because they might go further into the money. That is reduced by the amount already recognized at grant date and amortized to the income statement as compensation. (This is difficult to unravel, thus an estimate.) The loss to shareholders is tax-deductible, so the after-tax liability is after applying the tax rate in the tax footnote. Phew! This is a difficult calculation. Sorry, but that can happen when U.S. GAAP or IFRS accounting is bad: A mess to clean up. Did you see in the Accounting Primer box that, when options lapse, the firm has over-expensed for compensation that was never paid? Value Investing in Action Option Overhang for Delta Air Lines, 2020 “As of December 31, 2020, there were 5.4 million outstanding stock option awards with a weighted average exercise price of $52.37 of which 2.4 million were exercisable.” Footnote 14. Market price = $49.06 × 5.4 million = $264.92 million Exercise price = $52.37 × 5.4 million = 282.80 In-the-money amount (17.88) Option time value 83.00 Stock option valuation 65.12 Grant-date expense booked (estimated) 28.00 Option overhang (pre-tax) 37.12 Tax at 23.1% (Note 13) 8.57 Option overhang liability 28.55 million An option pricing model will render the $65.12 option valuation. That is typically a Black-Scholes model modified for some special features of employee stock options. See the option liability as the expected loss the shareholders will incur when they pay the employees with their paper. An investor evaluating whether to invest in a firm must be aware of this liability, for he could be hit as exercise date approaches. Delta’s liability is small with so few options that are well out of the money. But the number can be large. See the Microsoft case at the end of this chapter. Many firms now pay with restricted stock units (RSUs) rather than options. These are stock grants restricted until vesting. They are deemed compensation and recorded as such; there is no accounting issue. Compensation from stock appreciation rights (SARs), whereby the employee is paid cash equivalent to the stock price appreciation after a grant date, also is recorded correctly. Stock options are not the only contingent security claim against Delta. See the next action box. Value Investing in Action Outstanding Warrants for Delta Air Lines, 2020 In exchange for a $5.6 billion bailout during the COVID crisis, the U.S. government was issued warrants to acquire 6.7 million shares (approx. 1 percent of shares outstanding) at an exercise price of $24.39. The market price of the common shares was $49.06 at balance-sheet date in 2020. The at-the-money component of the resulting liability is calculated as: Market price (at $49.06 per share). $328.70 million Exercise price (at $24.39 per share). 163.41 Current in-the-money loss liability 165.29 million To this amount, the option time value is added. The resulting off-balance-sheet liability should be booked to the balance sheet as a financing liability. Put Option Overhang A firm can issue put options or put warrants as well as calls. A put option gives the holder the right to have shares in the firm repurchased by the firm at a set price. If the stock price declines below this strike price, the option is exercised, forcing the firm to buy back the stock at a price greater than the current price. Chapter 5 informed us that this is a loss to shareholders, just like the issue of stock at a price less than market price with a call option. Similarly, an off-balance-sheet liability exists when these put options are outstanding. The Microsoft case at the end of the chapter involves both put and call options. Also consider at the following vignette involving the China Evergrande Group. A Vignette Recourse with Share Purchases, China Evergrande Group China Evergrande Group was one of the largest property firms in China, but highly levered. It had difficulty paying its debts in 2021 and was ordered by the Chinese government to reduce its leverage. In April 2021, it announced it would issue $2 billion of equity through its subsidiary Fangchebao Group (FCB), an online platform for home and car sales. If there were no initial public offering (IPO) for that firm within 12 months, investors were entitled to have their shares repurchased at a 15 percent premium. Is this deleveraging? Not quite: A liability was created to repurchase shares at less than market price should the IPO not take place. In August 2023, Evergrande filed for bankruptcy and, in January 2024, a Hong Kong court ordered it to liquidate. Source : Wall Street Journal , April 2, 2021 Convertible Bonds and Convertible Preferred Stock Stock options are not the big problem they used to be, but not so other convertible securities such as convertible bonds and convertible preferred stock. Review the accounting in the Accounting Primer below. When convertible securities are outstanding, there is an omitted liability, just as in the case of stock options: The shareholder is obligated to issue shares at less than market price on conversion, a loss of value. As with stock options, this is not recorded. Further, with the interest rate or preferred dividend lower due to the conversion privilege, the firm appears to have low financing costs. Or none: See the vignette on the next page. Accounting Primer Accounting for Convertible Securities Convertible bonds are recorded at their issue price, with interest on the bonds recorded in the same way as a plain vanilla bond. However, the interest on the bonds is lower because of the conversion privilege: The bondholder can convert the bond to common equity if the conversion price is less than the market price of the common shares. That conversion is a loss to shareholders but is not recorded under U.S. GAAP or IFRS. So it looks like the firm is getting cheap financing. The same accounting applies to convertible preferred stock where only the low preferred dividend is recorded. Firms are required to report both basic and diluted earnings per share (EPS) with their income statements. Basic EPS is earnings net of preferred dividends divided by the weighted-average shares outstanding during the year. Diluted EPS is the EPS as if all convertible securities were converted. It is tempting to view the difference as the cost to the shareholder from the dilution from exercise of the convertible securities. That would be a mistake. The contingent shareholders do not share in current earnings, only in future earnings and even then only contingently. And current and contingent shareholders share future earnings differently: When shares are converted, existing shareholders lose, the new shareholders gain. Diluted EPS, while giving an indication of the potential dilution of the current shareholders, does not tell us the loss to shareholders from the potential conversion. A Vignette Convertible Bonds with No Interest Rate In 2021 during the pandemic, a number of firms issued convertible bonds with a zero interest rate. They included Airbnb, Inc. ($2 billion), Twitter, Inc. ($1.25 billion), Spotify ($1.3 billion), Beyond Meat, Inc. ($1.0 billion), DraftKings Inc. ($1.3 billion), and Affirm Holdings, Inc. ($1.7 billion), as well as Snap Inc., Dish Network L.L.C., and SoFi Technologies, Inc. Conversion premiums were as high as 55 percent. With no interest recorded, it looks like these firms are getting financing for nothing. But the shareholders will bear that cost when the convertible securities are converted into common stock. Yet that cost is not recorded under U.S. GAAP or IFRS. Borrowing without cost? No, bad accounting! The accounting for the liability on the balance sheet is the expected loss on conversion at the expected market price of the common stock at the time. Based on the current market price as an estimate of that price, Market value of common stock $989 million Carrying value of convertible 532 Liability for loss on conversion $ 457 million (no tax deduction) To this, add option value. The liability should be continually remarked as the market price of the common stock changes (but not below zero), with the change in the expected loss recorded as a financing expense in the income statement. The liability is extinguished on conversion. Why are these firms doing this? Perhaps management and their investment bankers are knowingly playing the accounting trick, offensive to the shareholders they serve. Perhaps they are deluded in thinking they are getting cheap financing. But could they be thinking that their stock is overpriced so the bonds will not be converted as the stock price falls? A financing arbitrage play as seen in chapter 5 ? Peloton Interactive, Inc. raised $1 billion in convertible bonds with a strike price of $240. The shares dropped to $13 in 2022. Spotify issued its zero-coupon bonds with a strike of $515, then its shares dropped to $109. These firms were indeed getting financing for nothing. Convertible bond buyers: Caveat emptor . Factored Accounts Receivable with Recourse Firms sometimes sell their accounts receivable, with or without recourse. If with recourse, the firm must make good on any bad debts. That is a contingent liability. Reverse-factored Accounts Payable Firms can exchange their short-term accounts payable for secured long-term debt with a financing firm, freeing up cash that would otherwise be applied to service payables. This so-called supply-chain financing increased to $1.8 trillion globally in 2021 according to a report from BCR Publishing Ltd. Even though that debt has higher priority, the obligation is still reported in (lower-priority) accounts payable. This was an issue in the demise of supply financing companies, Carillion plc (in 2018) and Greensill Capital (in 2021). In 2022, the FASB and IASB required firms to disclose the terms and amounts of their supply chain financing programs. Off-balance sheet Assets: Built-in Protection from Missing Assets Commentators often claim that accounting is in error when it omits assets from the balance sheet. As tangible assets are booked to the balance sheet, they have intangible assets in mind, like a brand asset. However, they miss an important point: There is also an income statement, and value is conveyed through the income statement as well as the balance sheet. The Coca-Cola Company (KO) is a company with a large asset, its brand, missing from its balance sheet. The action box below demonstrates that booking its brand to the balance sheet makes no difference to its valuation . . . in 2021. We saw also saw this in Chapter 6 in Coke’s no-growth valuation for 2022. But the point bears repetition here to emphasize that a balance sheet without the brand is not a quality issue—omitted assets due to expensing of risky investments are not an accounting deficiency. It reports the reformulated balance sheet for 2021 and the forecast of operating income for 2022 based on analysts’ consensus forecasts. These numbers yield an RNOA for 2022 of 21.19 percent with no brand on the balance sheet. The box then reports a revised balance sheet with estimated brand value of $60,000 million added to NOA (and thus to equity). That reduces the RNOA to 10.13 percent and residual operating income from $7,803.4 million to $3,603.3 million. Would you then shun Coke on this drop in profitability? No: The valuations are exactly the same in the box. The higher RNOA with the brand off balance sheet is cancelled by the lower book value, and the lower profitability with the brand on balance sheet is compensated with higher value on the balance sheet. Missing assets from the balance sheet are not a concern. The instruction to the value investor is: Balance sheets and income statements work together to convey value. And, as in chapter 6 : Do not buy profitability, for it depends on the balance sheet. Always carry the balance sheet with you. The last chapter explained why assets are missing from the balance sheet: They are expensed to the income statement rather than booked to the balance sheet, as with R&D and advertising and promotion expenditures to build brands. However, the last chapter also explained that, as these assets are particularly risky, that accounting conveys risk. The accounting has a purpose, so don’t put very risky assets on the balance sheet with the pretense they provide collateral. Doing so reports a lower debt-to-assets ratio making the firm look less risky. But, with an investment in particularly risky assets, it has higher risk. The Coca-Cola example tells us that will not help your valuation, but it will lose the information about risk so important to determining your hurdle rate. Maintain the prudent accounting. Value Investing in Action Valuation with Missing Assets from the Balance Sheet The Coca-Cola Company, 2021 ($millions) An off-balance-sheet brand estimated to be worth $60 billion is added to the balance sheet: Brand off balance sheet Brand on balance sheet Net operating assets 54,996 114,996 Net debt 30,136 30,136 Equity 24,860 84,860 Operating income 2022 : $11,653 RNOA 2022 21.19% 10.13% ReOI 2022 (7% required return) 7,803.4 3,603.3 No-growth value based on the brand off balance sheet: [IMAGE: ../images/ch09eqn05.png] No-growth value based on the brand on balance sheet: [IMAGE: ../images/ch09eqn06.png] Missing assets don’t matter if the earnings from those assets are coming through the income statement . Stick to the discipline: Carry the balance sheet with you for valuation . As the Delta example that books a missing liability to the balance sheet demonstrates, adding a missing asset to the balance sheet will change the implied growth rate in the market price. But, with that different growth rate, it will not change the price. Accounting for Items on the Balance Sheet As with off-balance sheet liabilities, on-balance sheet assets are risky if they can come back and hit you later. That can happen if the balance-sheet asset value is in danger of falling. U.S. GAAP and IFRS are generally prudent: If the carrying values are above the expected value to be realized, they are written down with an impairment. But we must be wary. Fair Value Accounting Of major concern is the use of fair value accounting. The idea with this accounting is to get value into the balance sheet. The accompanying Accounting Primer explains how fair values are measured. Accounting Primer Fair Value Accounting There is a hierarchy for determining fair values, known as leveling: Level 1: Mark to market. If the asset is traded in a liquid market, fair value is its market price. Level 2: Mark to comparables. If a market price is not available, fair value is determined by the price of a similar asset for which its market price is available. Level 3. Mark to model. If no market price is available, fair value is estimated with a valuation model. Clearly, the integrity of the fair value declines as the measurement goes down the leveling from 1 to 3. However, there are issues with all three levels. Fortunately, the application of fair value accounting is limited to certain investment securities as outlined in chapter 7 , to derivatives, and to certain financial assets and liabilities in banks and other financial firms. In banks, mortgage assets that are “available for sale” are fair valued. Fair values are promoted as being forward-looking, but forward-looking numbers can be speculative. Fair value accounting defies the realization principle that the prudent investor holds to; value is recognized before it is realized. As a result, balance sheet fair values can collapse, surprising the investor. He sees risk only after it hits him. That is not a safe balance sheet. Remember: Water in the balance sheet can vaporize. There are other issues with this accounting: In business, assets do not have stand-alone value; they generate value by using them jointly in operations. Thus, a fair value for an individual asset is irrelevant unless its value is independent of other assets. Investment securities have stand-alone value; the price of an equity share in an investment portfolio does not usually depend on the price of other shares in the portfolio. But a brand has value only in conjunction with other assets, as do plant and most intangible assets. IFRS allows an option for fair value accounting for nonfinancial assets that are not stand-alone assets, though it is rarely used. Under fair value accounting, expected earnings are equal to the required return and expected residual earnings are zero. As a result, there is no concept of value-added over the required return that the investor needs to evaluate, and information about that gets lost. The accounting defies one of our fundamental principles: When calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price into the calculation. Level 1 (mark-to-market) fair values, while precise in their measurement, are illustrative of the previous point: If the market price is a mispricing, we are bringing mispricing into the balance sheet. Indeed, mark-to-market accounting can bring price bubbles into the balance sheet. See the vignette below on fair values for speculative cryptocurrencies and the following box on fair value collapses. Level 2 and 3 fair values are subject to error, particularly Level 3. We have seen the problem with valuation models in this book: One can calculate any value with speculative inputs. Mark-to-guesstimate? Forward-looking fair values are appropriate when shareholder wealth changes one-to-one with prices. That is the case with a stand-alone bond or an equity holding that is not part of the firm’s business strategy. So, for a share trader, fair value is appropriate because her wealth comes directly from changes in the price of the shares in which she is speculating. It is also appropriate for a real estate company speculating in property, but not for one holding property to generate rental income. Fair value accounting is also applicable for assets held to supply liquidity; the fair value indicates the amount of cash that can be generated. That applies to the short-term debt investments typically held by companies to store cash to be used for paying dividends, making investments, retiring debt, and more. It also applies to investments held by insurance companies that must be sold to satisfy insurance claims, and to investments in investment funds that must be sold to meet redemptions from the fund. And it applies to securities held by banks to meet withdrawals of deposits. In the second-largest bank failure in U.S. history, Silicon Valley Bank (SVB) failed in 2023 when the value of Treasury bonds it held dropped significantly on rising interest rates and depositors rushed to withdraw. A large amount of those Treasuries were held at cost, since they were classified as “held to maturity.” If they had been classified as “available for sale,” a loss of $15 billion would have been reported in the 2022 financial statements (as estimates had it), giving forewarning that the bank would be unable to meet withdrawal demands. Watch for reclassification of bonds to held-to-maturity status in advance of anticipated interest rate increases. In 2022 as bond prices fell, 48 percent of securities held by U.S. banks were classified as held to maturity, up from 34 percent in 2021, according to Federal Deposit Insurance Corporation (FDIC) data. Charles Schwab Bank reclassified $188.6 billion, PNC Financial Services Group, Inc. $82.7 billion, JPMorgan Chase & Co. $78.3 billion, and Wells Fargo & Company $50.1 billion. The total bank unrealized losses on these held-to-maturity securities was $341 billion in 2022. ( Wall Street Journal report, March 30, 2023.) Also watch for borrowing against fair values. That increases leverage risk but there is a possible double whammy: If the fair values to meet debt repayments subsequently collapse, bankruptcy risk is higher. In 2023, several private equity funds resorted to “net asset value financing” (borrowing against net asset value in their investment funds) as they struggled to exit investments. According to a Financial Times report on July 19, 2023, the global group Carlyle arranged more than $1 billion of financing against assets in one of its funds to return money to its limited partners. In 2021, SoftBank Group borrowed more than $4 billion against funds in its Vision Fund 2 to pay money to itself. These borrowings were done at high interest rates. Watch for dividends and bonuses paid out of fair value earnings. With fair values unrealized, that requires borrowing. The failed Enron Corporation is a stark example. These points warn us to handle fair values with care. They are risk in the balance sheet; they might come back and haunt you later. The vignettes in the box below illustrate this. Fair value accounting is not necessary to convey potential problems. There is an alternative: Impairment on the downside, but no write-up on the upside. That warns of liquidity problems but does not bring market prices (and potential bubbles) into the financial statements, as happened with the fair values of bank assets in the 2008 financial crisis that subsequently crashed. SVB Bank’s accounting problem was its failure to write down its held-to-maturity Treasury holdings from cost. This impairment accounting would have conveyed the same information as fair valuing them, but with fair value accounting applied only on the downside. That is conservative accounting. Prudent accounting for banks. A Vignette Fair Value Accounting for Speculative Cryptocurrencies Cryptocurrencies are speculative assets, subject to wild price gyrations. Their price is based largely on speculation about what others might pay for them in the future. Some forms are seen as a (dubious?) store of value, an alternative to fiat currencies that can lose value with inflation and a protection against governments debasing their currencies by printing money. Crypto is also seen as a medium of exchange for making payments that avoid the banking system and credit cards with their relatively high fees. But these are not rationales for solid fundamental value, and there are thousands of competing cryptocurrencies. Even “stablecoins” like LUNA and Terra proved to be anything but stable when their prices plummeted in 2022. As of 2023, firms holding these “assets” could use fair value accounting that recognizes holding gains and losses in the income statement, or they could carry them at cost subject to impairment. The former brings swings in prices into the income statement and speculative values in the balance sheet. Not a balance sheet to anchor on. The latter records only losses. In 2023, the FASB announced that fair value accounting is required for crypto, effective 2025. Fair value assets at FTX Trading Ltd. Struggling to raise cash to survive, the crypto exchange FTX listed four crypto coins on its balance sheet at a “fair value” of $6.4 billion in 2022. The fair values collapsed, as did the firm. Cascading effects of fair value accounting for crypto Celsius Network LLC, Voyager Digital Ltd., and Genesis Global Holdco, LLC and two subsidiaries filed for bankruptcy in 2022 and early 2023 after the collapse of crypto prices. These firms were lenders to crypto trading firms against the (fair) value of their crypto holdings. Signature Bank, also a crypto lender, borrowed $10 billion from its local home loan bank, a Federal Home Loan Bank (FHLB), under a U.S. government scheme to provide cheap funding to banks involved in housing lending (with implicit government backing). Silvergate Capital Corporation, another crypto lender, similarly borrowed $3.6 billion. (Source: Wall Street Journal reports.) Both Signature and Silvergate collapsed. Will this cascade to the taxpayer? The value investor (and value lender) is not a speculator, so avoids these assets. They have no underlying fundamental value to be analyzed. Gemini Trust Company, LLC, a lender to Genesis and other crypto firms, attracted deposits from investors with a promise of high interest rates, but these investors had their money locked up as the loans soured. That presumably had a cascading effect on these investors’ lives. Vignettes Fair value collapses When calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price into the calculation . Tech company holdings during the 1990s price bubble During the tech bubble, large tech firms invested in other tech firms. Here are unrealized gains and losses (in parentheses) booked from marking these investments to market (in $millions): 1998 1999 2000 2001 2002 Cisco Systems 28 234 3,240 (3,812) 224 Intel 545 3,188 (3,596) (163) (19) Microsoft 627 1,052 (283) (1,460) 5 Note : As the three firms had different fiscal years, the drop in the prices of their technology holdings (at about the same calendar time) affected them in different fiscal years. For all three companies, the fair-value gains during the bubble were subsequently wiped out as the bubble burst. Fair-valued assets at Enron Corporation, 2001 Enron gained permission from the SEC to fair value long-term energy contracts before the energy was delivered. The accounting was also applied to power plants, with fair value estimates based on projected revenues. With no cash flow as yet, Enron had to borrow to pay dividends and the handsome bonuses to executives, increasing the risk. As the value of the contracts fell, so did Enron. Enron was a fair value accounting house of cards. Fair value of mortgage assets during the financial crisis, 2007–2009 Banks fair value mortgage loans if they are “available for sale.” They also do so with securitized packages of these mortgages. The value of a residential mortgage asset increases as the borrower has more equity in his home, as happens when home prices increase. During 2005–2007, home prices increased significantly in a real estate bubble. So did fair-valued mortgage assets and mortgage-backed securities on bank balance sheets, often with Level 3 estimates: A bubble came onto these balance sheets. When the bubble burst and high-risk borrowers defaulted, these values collapsed; the water in these assets vaporized. A financial crisis with bank bailouts ensued. Speculative fair value in investments, 2021 Amazon.com , Inc. (AMZN) invested in Rivian Automotive, Inc. (RVN), a firm planning to produce EV trucks. The firm went public in 2021 amid much speculation about EV, even though it had produced few trucks. Amazon recognized a gain of $11.8 billion on marking the investment to the IPO price. The price popped, with Amazon reporting a loss of $8.2 billion on the investment in the next quarter. A Vignette Fair Values are a Risk to the Economy Prior to the financial crisis of 2008–2009, firms became more levered with the promise of good times. Creditors holding their debt often bought credit default swaps (CDS) that are effectively insurance against default. A major supplier of these CDS was American International Group, Inc. (AIG), an insurance company. AIG had a large investment portfolio fair-valued at $695.8 billion to back up potential claims. As the financial crisis hit, these fair values collapsed with AIG unable to meet the CDS claims. That cascaded in the economy with the holders of these CDS now stressed. To avoid systemic collapse, the U.S. government stepped in to bail out AIG with an initial $85 billion that increased to $180 billion, a potential cost to taxpayers. Because the bailout was in exchange for a 79.9 percent equity interest in AIG, the government ultimately turned a profit of $17.7 billion on the sale of its equity stake, with the Federal Reserve Bank making another $5 billion. Now to return to Delta. As indicated in the discussion of the business, Delta has investments in the equity shares of other airlines, and these are at fair value in table 9.2 . Table 9.2 Fair value of equity investments for Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL), 2020 2020 2019 Hanjin KAL (13%) 512 205 Air France–KLM (9%) 235 418 China Eastern (3%) 201 258 948 881 Other investments 469 218 1,417 1,099 Level 1 Level 2 Level 3 Leveling (2020) 948 38 431 Note : In $millions. Percentage interests shown in parentheses. From footnote 5. How does the investor deal with this fair value footnote? Level 3 values in 2020, 30 percent of the total, are based on a valuation model; this is a concern. But there is also the issue of possible mispricing in Level 1. Is this a period of excessive speculation in airline stock prices? If so, give these valuations a haircut to be safe. In fact, the values are down from 2019, as expected during a pandemic. But if you have concerns about the viability of any of these carriers, mark them down on your safe balance sheet. Fair Valuing-Derivatives Derivatives that pay off contingently are typically fair-valued under U.S. GAAP and IFRS. This works well when the derivative is a hedge against other prices. Delta has both currency hedges and hedges against aircraft fuel prices. If the price of fuel goes up, adding to Delta’s costs, so does the mark-to-market on the derivative, providing an offsetting gain. That is how a hedge is supposed to work: No problem. However, if the derivative is a naked speculation on prices, there is danger: any drop in the price of the derivative will not be offset. These derivatives are called WMS, weapons of mass destruction, for a good reason. The Fair Value Option Fair value accounting is applied to assets, but U.S. GAAP and IFRS also give firms an option to apply it to their debt liabilities. The option, once invoked, is binding in subsequent years. This is perverse accounting: The fair value of debt goes down when the firm becomes riskier, so the firm recognizes a gain when it becomes riskier. Gains are recognized all the way to bankruptcy. And, on the balance sheet, it looks like the firm has less to pay back. See the vignette below on gains and losses at Goldman Sachs Group, Inc. during the financial crisis. Re-mark these liabilities back to the amount owed to creditors at maturity. The trouble with the fair value option is that the assets that become riskier are not fair-valued along with the debt. If they were, the gain on the debt would be offset by the loss on the assets. This is called running a matched book, and is the only case where fair-valuing liabilities makes sense. A Vignette Fair Value Option at The Goldman Sachs Group, Inc. (GS), 2008–2009 The following table sets forth the gains and losses (in parentheses) included in earnings for fiscal years 2008 and 2009 as a result of the firm electing to apply the fair value option to own-debt financial liabilities, as described in Note 2 in the firm’s 10-K. Figures are in $millions. Goldman Sachs Group, Inc: Gains (Losses) on Borrowings 2008 (down year) 2009 (up year) Unsecured long-term borrowings 915 (884) Other secured financings 894 (822) Unsecured short-term borrowings 266 (182) Other (20 ) ( 135) Total 2,055 (2,024) The gains were reported in 2008 when the firm became riskier. Losses were reported in 2009 as it recovered: Perverse. Long-lived Assets Under U.S. GAAP and IFRS, long-lived assets, including plant and equipment, booked intangibles and goodwill, must be evaluated annually for impairment. But it is good to ask: Is the firm slow to impair? Is an impairment thus more likely in the future? Pay particular attention to goodwill if a firm’s stock price has declined: Are there are indications that the firm overpaid for acquisitions? Mergers and acquisitions (M&A) activity increases in soaring stock markets. In 2021, there was $5.1 trillion in “deals” when stock market speculation was high, with high assumptions about future cash flows in acquisition prices. In 2022, with stock prices falling, considerable market value in those acquisitions vaporized. The investor adjusts the safe balance sheet to anticipate goodwill impairments (that indeed followed in 2022 and 2023). See the following Carillion vignette. Vignette The Collapse of Carillion plc, 2018 In 2011 Carillion acquired business services group Eaga plc, a company that helped consumers take advantage of government clean energy subsidies. Eaga was renamed Carillion Energy Services. The acquisition price was £309 million, but Carillion booked £329 of goodwill on its balance sheet on completion of the deal. Over the next five years no goodwill impairment was made, despite a series of cuts to government subsidies that shrunk Carillion Energy Services revenues. By 2016, Carillion’s goodwill amounted to £1.6 billion, around 35 percent of total assets. In 2017 the company wrote off its investment in Carillion Energy Services entirely, as well as £845 million surpluses it had aggressively anticipated on its long-term contracts. But by then it was too late. Carillion appeared solvent prior to 2017 due to its inflated balance sheet. However, the company finally collapsed in January 2018 with £1.3bn of debt, having paid out generous executive compensation and dividends of £376m between 2012 and 2017 while generating only £43 million in net operating cash flow over the same period. Watch for impairments to plant and equipment if depreciation rates have been too low. Delta’s fleet has estimated lives for depreciation ranging from 20–32 years: Too long? Are Delta’s indefinite-lived intangible assets (not subject to amortization) in table 9.3 likely to be impaired? Table 9.3 Indefinite-lived intangibles, Delta Air Lines, Inc., 2020, footnote 7 Our December 2020 quarter quantitative impairment tests of goodwill and intangibles concluded that there was no indication of impairment as the fair value exceeded our carrying value: Goodwill and indefinite-lived intangible assets by category Carrying value at Excess fair value at 2020 testing date (in millions) 31-Dec-20 31-Dec-19 Goodwill (1) $ 9,753 $ 9,781 >100% International routes and slots 2,583 2,583 10% to 30% Airline alliances (2) 1,863 1,005 20% to >100% Delta tradename 850 850 >100% Domestic slots 622 622 60% to >100% Total $ 15,671 $ 14,841 (1) The reduction in goodwill relates to the combination of Delta Private Jets with Wheels Up in the March 2020 quarter.
