(2) As part of our strategic alliance with and investment in LATAM, we have recorded an alliance-related indefinite-lived intangible asset of $1.2 billion, which was not reflected in the December 31, 2019 balance. Program Accounting With long-term contracts to build planes, ships, and infrastructure assets, manufacturing firms accrue revenues before delivery, thus booking an associated unbilled receivable on the balance sheet. In 2020, The Boeing Company (BA) had $7.995 billion in such unbilled receivables from airlines struggling during the Covid pandemic, with the following caveat: “Certain commercial airplane customers are experiencing liquidity issues and seeking additional capital. Should these customers fail to address their liquidity issues, accounts receivable, unbilled receivables and certain inventory could become impaired. In addition, we would have to remove contracts related to these customers from backlog and remarket any undelivered aircraft.” Will these unbilled receivables have to be impaired because of the effect of the pandemic on airlines? Inventory Accounting Inventory accounting in the balance sheet typically works well: Inventory is recorded at cost but impaired under the lower of cost or market rule (LCM), if the historical cost is above what the inventory can be sold for. However, the Boeing caveat points to a case with long-term contracting that requires attention. Just as revenues and unbilled receivables are accrued over the years for these contracts, so are inventory costs. Table 9.4 takes you to the inventory footnote for Boeing where the 737-MAX program ran into trouble after two fatal air crashes. The footnote indicates that inventory in commercial aircraft programs was written down by $6.493 billion in 2020. A further $3.460 billion of “reach-forward losses” was written off in 2021. And in 2022, $2.8 billion was written off on military aircraft programs. Had the investor anticipated these losses, she would have worked with a harder balance sheet. Table 9.4 Inventory accounting for The Boeing Company, 2020 Inventories at December 31 consisted of the following in millions: 2020 2019 Long-term contracts in progress $823 $1,187 Commercial aircraft programs 70,153 66,016 Commercial spare parts, used aircraft, general stock materials and other 10,739 9,419 Total $81,715 $76,622 Long-term contracts in progress includes Delta launch program inventory that is being sold at cost to United Launch Alliance (ULA) under an inventory supply agreement that terminates on March 31, 2021. The inventory balance was $17 and $176 at December 31, 2020 and 2019. See indemnifications to ULA in note 14. Commercial spare parts, used aircraft, general stock materials and other includes capitalized precontract costs of $733 at December 31, 2020 and $711 at December 31, 2019 primarily related to KC-46A Tanker and Commercial Crew. See note 13. Commercial aircraft programs The increase in commercial aircraft programs inventory during 2020 reflects the large number of undelivered aircraft due to the 737 MAX grounding, lower wide-body deliveries driven by the impacts of the COVID-19 pandemic and 787 production issues and associated rework, partially offset by a reach-forward loss of $6,493 on the 777X program. At December 31, 2020, commercial aircraft programs inventory includes approximately 425 undelivered 737 MAX aircraft and 80 undelivered 787 aircraft. We are currently remarketing certain aircraft and may have to remarket additional aircraft in future periods. If we are unable to successfully remarket the aircraft, determine further production rates reductions are necessary, and/or contract the program accounting quantities, future earnings may be reduced and/or additional reach-forward losses may have to be recorded. At December 31, 2020 and 2019, commercial aircraft programs inventory included the following amounts related to the 737 program: deferred production costs of $2,159 and $1,313 and unamortized tooling and other non-recurring costs of $480 and $521. At December 31, 2020, $2,560 of 737 deferred production costs, unamortized tooling and other non-recurring costs are expected to be recovered from units included in the program accounting quantity that have firm orders and $79 is expected to be recovered from units included in the program accounting quantity that represent expected future orders. At December 31, 2020 and 2019, commercial aircraft programs inventory included the following amounts related to the 777X program: unamortized tooling and other non-recurring costs of $3,295 and $2,914. During the fourth quarter of 2020 we determined that estimated costs to complete the 777X program plus costs already included in 777X inventory exceed estimated revenues from the program. The resulting reach-forward loss of $6,493 was recorded as a reduction to deferred production costs. As a result, 777X deferred production costs were immaterial at December 31, 2020. The level of profitability on the 777X program will be subject to a number of factors. These factors include continued market uncertainty, the impacts of COVID-19 on our production system as well as impacts on our supply chain and customers, further production rate adjustments for the 777X or other commercial aircraft programs, contraction of the accounting quantity and potential risks associated with the testing program and the timing of aircraft certification. One or more of these factors could result in additional reach-forward losses on the 777X program in future periods. Defined-benefit Pension Plans Firms with defined-benefit pension plans recognize the present value of estimated pension commitments as a liability on their balance sheets along with assets in the pension investment fund to cover the liability. The difference between the two is the net pension obligation (or funding status). Delta’s pension footnote reports on the two in table 9.5 . There are two components of retirement benefits: the pension, and other post-employment benefits (OPEB) for promised medical and education benefits in retirement. Delta was in deficit on its pension obligations by $6.085 billion, with an additional deficit of $4.270 billion for OPEB, raising the possibility of the shareholders having to contribute more to the pension fund to shore up the obligations. (Delta no longer has a plan for current employees, so the liability is for pension promises for past service from employees.) Table 9.5 Pension footnote for Delta Air Lines, Inc., 2020 Pension benefits Other postretirement and postemployment benefits Dec 31, Dec 31, (in millions) 2020 2019 2020 2019 Benefit obligation at beginning of period $ 21,199 $ 19,809 $ 3,379 $ 3,225 Service cost — — 96 83 Interest cost 700 833 120 137 Actuarial loss 2,051 1,678 247 226 Benefits paid, including lump sums and annuities (1,233) (1,107) (356) (315) Participant contributions — — 20 23 Special termination benefits — — 1,260 — Settlements (91) (14) — — Benefit obligation at end of period $ 22,626 $ 21,199 $ 4,766 $ 3,379 Fair value of plan assets at beginning of period $ 15,845 $ 13,459 $ 607 $ 637 Actual gain on plan assets 1,973 2,485 76 134 Employer contributions 47 1,022 189 159 Participant contributions — — 20 23 Benefits paid, including lump sums and annuities (1,233) (1,107) (396) (346) Settlements (91) (14) — — Fair value of plan assets at end of period $ 16,541 $ 15,845 $ 496 $ 607 Funded status at end of period $ (6,085) $ (5,354) $ (4,270) $ (2,772) There are two issues on the quality of the net obligation number. First, the fund assets are fair-valued, raising the fair value accounting issue: What if these fair values collapsed, requiring the firm to contribute more to the pension fund? Second, the liability is the present value of the future payouts estimated by actuaries. Are these estimates sound? What discount rate is Delta using? You will not get an answer to the first question, but Delta is using a 3.40 percent discount rate compared to 2.5 percent for Boeing, 2.72 percent for United Airlines, and 2.61 percent for General Electric. A higher discount rate yields a lower liability. Another measure, the matching gap, is applied to assess whether a firm can meet its pension obligations: Matching Gap = Expected Growth in Obligations − Expected Return on Plan Assets The expected growth in obligations is given by the interest rate plus expected actuarial changes (longevity assumptions) plus increases in obligations for additional employees or changes to plan benefits. Both this and the expected return on plan assets are speculative numbers, of course. Deferred Tax Assets Deferred tax assets arise if taxes are paid in advance of income reported in the income statement. When that income is reported, taxes on that income will have to be matched to the income, but those taxes will already have been paid, an asset. Included in the deferred tax asset is the benefit of future reductions to taxes because current net operating losses (NOL) can be carried forward to reduce taxable income in the future. Deferred tax assets also arise because allowable tax deductions are less than the corresponding expense in income statements. So, if pension expenses are deductible for tax purposes only when pensions are paid, a deferred tax asset arises because the pension expense is accrued in advance on the income statement. But there is a qualification. If the firm assesses that “it is more likely than not” that the tax benefit will not be realized, it must reduce the deferred tax asset by a valuation allowance. That valuation allowance is applied if there is a 50 percent or greater probability that the firm will not earn taxable income in the future; that is, it will not recover from its losses and so will not be able to use the tax benefits. In 2020, Delta had deep losses that were likely to continue, so the question of the valuation allowance arises. Reducing deferred tax assets with a valuation allowance might be prudent. Delta’s deferred tax footnote is in table 9.6 . You can see that while total deferred tax assets increased by over $3 billion, the valuation allowance increased by only $400 million over 2019. Here is Delta’s explanation: Although we are in a three year cumulative loss position as of December 31, 2020, we have a recent history of significant earnings prior to the onset of the COVID-19 pandemic. We expect to return to profitability as the effects of the pandemic subside and to generate sufficient taxable income to utilize our federal net operating loss carryforwards before any expire. Our federal net operating loss carryforwards generated before 2018 do not begin to expire until 2027. Under current tax law, federal net operating losses generated in 2020 do not expire. Therefore, we have not recorded a valuation allowance on our deferred tax assets other than the capital loss carryforwards and state net operating losses that have short expiration periods. Table 9.6 Deferred tax footnote for Delta Air Lines, Inc., 2020 Deferred taxes Deferred income taxes reflect the net tax effect of temporary differences between the carrying amounts of assets and liabilities for financial reporting and income tax purposes. The following table shows significant components of our deferred tax assets and liabilities: Significant components of deferred income tax assets and liabilities December 31, (in millions) 2020 2019 Deferred tax assets: Net operating loss carryforwards $ 1,495 $ 560 Capital loss carryforward 483 – Pension, postretirement and other benefits 2,956 2,241 Deferred revenue 1,797 1,667 Lease liabilities 2,185 1,510 Other 611 380 Valuation allowance (460) (58) Total deferred tax assets $ 9,067 $ 6,300 Deferred tax liabilities: Depreciation $ 4,507 $ 5,190 Operating lease assets 1,324 1,298 Intangible assets 1,076 1,049 Other 172 99 Total deferred tax liabilities $ 7,079 $ 7,636 Net deferred tax assets (liabilities) $ 1,988 $ (1,336) Leases The FASB and IASB now have this right. Prior to 2018, firms were required to report capital lease assets and obligations on their balance sheets. So-called operating leases were off-balance sheet. The financial engineers whose job involves structuring “form over substance” transactions to avoid accounting treatments had a field day writing lease contracts that kept long-term commitments off-balance sheet. The issue is particularly sensitive for airlines that often lease aircraft rather than purchasing them. As David Tweedie, the former chair of the IASB, said: “I look forward to the day I fly in a plane that is on the balance sheet.” In 2017, Delta had an off-balance sheet lease liability present valued at $12.595 billion, distorting its leverage ratio read from the balance sheet. But now, all leases over one year’s duration must be booked to the balance sheet. $525 billion of lease liabilities were added to U.S. corporate balance sheets in 2018. Chapter 5 on leverage takes you through the accounting. A couple of issues remain, however. See the following vignette. A Vignette Issues with Lease Accounting Variable lease obligations If lease payments are variable, the obligation does not have to be booked to the balance sheet. A space for financial engineers to work their machinations? Obligations for airport leases are often variable: 48 percent of all lease obligations for Delta Air Lines, Inc., 54 percent for American Airlines Group Inc., 69 percent for United Airlines Holdings, Inc., and 77 percent for Southwest Airlines Co. Omitted liabilities. Carrying values for leases Lease liabilities are booked at the present value of the future lease payments at the firm’s borrowing rate. This means a high-risk firm (with a high borrowing rate) will have a lower liability. It will have to earn on its lease assets at a rate higher than the borrowing rate to reduce the matching gap and cover the lease obligation. J. C. Penney Co. reported second-quarter operating-lease liabilities of $1.2 billion in 2019. That is based on an 11 percent discount rate, which is well above the 4 percent average for S&P 500 companies. (Source: Calcbench). If JCPenney had used a 5 percent rate, its reported lease liability would increase some $400 million, to about $1.6 billion, according to financial technology firm New Constructs. “It's a loophole,” said David Trainer, chief executive of New Constructs. “It creates potential for a pretty significant understatement of liabilities by struggling companies.” Sales-type leases with residual values Firms sometimes make sales with a lease agreement signed by the buyer. The transaction is recognized as a sale in which the buyer effectively pays for the product with regular lease payments rather than on the spot. The practice is common in automobile sales. These leases are often made for a set number of years, with the car reverting to the seller at the end of the lease period. At this point, it has a residual value (as a secondhand car) and this residual value is included in the value of the lease receivable on the seller’s balance sheet. But these residual values might not be realized. Xerox Holdings Corporation sold copiers under its famed brand name, many in the form of sales-type leases. But Xerox was slow to move with the times as digital copiers began to displace analog machines. Accordingly, the estimated residual values on Xerox analog copier sales declined due to obsolescence, requiring large write-downs and a torpedo to its stock price. Risk in the Balance Sheet An anchoring balance sheet should be a safe balance sheet, but also one that reveals risks to which we are exposed. Here are risks conveyed by Delta’s balance sheet along with the footnotes. We take these risks to heart in setting our hurdle rate. Exposed Risks On-Balance Sheet: Financing Risk The action box below analyzes the financing risk and its possible effects. Note the effect on the required return. Do you want to up your hurdle rate for investing in Delta? Your forecast of future RNOA and the risk to that RNOA will determine this. Exposed Risks On-Balance Sheet: Risk Hedged and Not Hedged Footnotes report that the derivatives on Delta’s balance sheet serve as hedging instruments. Delta hedges fuel prices (but only partially); it also hedges currencies, which is significant as many of its ticket sales are in currencies other than dollars. Phew! There are no WMD, weapons of mass destruction. The risk disclosures in the 10-K indicate other operational risk factors, though they often read like boilerplate. Exposed Risks Off-Balance Sheet We trust you have booked Delta’s off-balance sheet liabilities for outstanding stock options and those U.S. Government warrants. Does the off-balance sheet liability for $13.88 billion in aircraft purchase agreements worry you? The $7.73 billion in guarantees to the regional carriers? Do you want to bring some portion of these on-balance sheet? Do you want to increase your required return? Risk from Fair Values Do you want to trim the fair values on investments in other airlines? Will some of them not survive? You see that these questions about risk are being turned back to you. This is because risk and its pricing is a personal matter, as we emphasized in the last chapter. Of course, the questions may prompt you to gain more information. What are the conditions in the aircraft purchase agreements? Is there an escape from this risk? Mitigating “Rainy Day” Cash Delta has $14.096 billion of cash and short-term investments on its balance sheet. Much of this is insurance against cash shortfalls in bad times. Downside: For every 1 percent decrease in RNOA and spread, ROE decreases by 14.05 percent. Add pension risk Pension deficit 10,355 Net debt 21,548 31,903 Market leverage = 31,903/30,850 = 1.034 For a spread of r Firm of 8 percent over an after-tax borrowing rate of 2.89 percent, leverage adds 5.28 percent to the required equity return. Managing Risk The investor decides what risk he wishes to be exposed to and which he wants to avoid. He looks at the balance sheet and accompanying footnotes to see these exposures. He notes Delta’s currency hedge: He is not bearing currency risk. He wants to buy an airline but does not wish to be exposed to fuel prices. He sees that Delta is only partially hedged, so buys the hedge on personal account. Or he notes that Southwest Airlines is much more hedged, saving the company $1.2 billion during the oil price spike after the Russian invasion of Ukraine. So he buys Southwest instead. He notes the financing leverage and wishes he could buy an airline with much less financing risk. So, he buys the firm’s debt as well and thereby reverses the leverage on personal account. This hedging on personal account requires instruments that provide the hedges, what the economists call a complete market for sharing risk. It also depends on the cost of those instruments. Perhaps a cheaper way to avoid leverage risk is to buy a less-levered airline. Alternative Accounting in the Balance Sheet Asset-based Valuation Asset-based companies are those where the value is in natural resources that they intend to exploit: oil and gas, minerals, forests harvested for timber. If these firms are producing, value can be ascertained from their balance sheets and the added value from the earnings they produce, just like other firms. However, that valuation can be complemented and compared with as asset-based valuation of the resources under the firm’s control. For an oil and gas firm, that is estimated by proven reserves in the ground multiplied by a price per barrel of oil or per cubic feet of gas, less extraction costs. Similarly so for mining companies. The price is the estimated future price on extraction, often approximated by current prices. For timber forests, the value increases each year as trees grow and add board feet, subject to a discount for risk from forest fires and other uncertainties. Recognizing that firms have an option to wait for extraction until prices improve, a so-called real option, option value can be added to the valuation. Replacement Cost Accounting in the Balance Sheet The accounting book anchors on a prudent balance sheet and then adds value from business operations. That is the mode of traditional value investing that adds “earnings power” to the balance sheet. However, our accounting book differs in one important respect from the book in traditional analysis. In that traditional analysis, the balance sheet is adjusted to what is called replacement cost or reproduction cost. That is the cost that would have to be expended to replicate the net assets of the business. It is said to indicate the barriers to entry from competitors, the amount they would have to pay to replicate the business. Our approach is a departure from this traditional one for several reasons. First, the determination of reproduction cost is highly speculative, which goes against the principle: When challenging speculation in the market price, do not put speculation into the calculation. Perhaps such calculations could be made with some integrity in the days when most assets were in plant and equipment. But even then, what is the replacement cost of a 50 percent depreciated plant? And would the competitor invest in the same (obsolete) plant? In the modern context, what is the cost to reproduce the largely intangible assets of Apple and Microsoft? That is very much a guess. In reviewing the reproduction adjustments to balance sheets by some value investors, we question the wild estimates that are often made: they are putting the speculation we are trying to avoid back into the balance sheet. Goodwill, for example, is adjusted to capture intangible assets—treating it as a guess, rather than an investment cost to be charged at the required return to assess value added. Second, value is not found in individual assets but in combining them in an entrepreneurial way to generate value. Replacement cost does not convey this value. Replacement cost is said to indicate a barrier to entry for competitors to replicate a business, but competition comes from entrepreneurs with new ideas: not just for assembling assets, but for replacement products for consumers that may require different assets to compete. The challenge to a pharmaceutical company is not from a competitor building factories to produce the same drug line, but from new drugs that displace the company’s product lines. The cost of reproducing the assets of a brick-and-mortar retailer like Macy’s, Inc. is irrelevant when the competition is from e-commerce companies like Amazon.com , Inc. The reproduction cost idea is for a static world, hardly suited to our modern world of changing technologies and continual innovation that forms the true competitive challenge. Third, our accounting for value recognizes that value in assets is captured by earnings, so adjustment of the balance sheet to capture value is not necessary. See the section on assets missing from the balance sheet and the Coca-Cola example above. A common adjustment for reproduction cost is the value of land that typically is worth more than the carrying value at cost on the balance sheet. But the value of the land is in the earnings it produces, and that is already in the income statement. Fourth, to the replacement-cost balance sheet, the traditional analysis adds an “earning power” calculation. That is a good idea in principle, but balance sheets and income statements articulate; what is reported in the income statement depends on the accounting in the balance sheet. As the discipline of investing by the book has emphasized, balance sheets and income statements work together to convey value. (See the section Protection from Paying Too Much for Profitability in chapter 6 .) But they must do so under the same accounting system. (See also Protection from Paying for Earnings Generated by Accounting in that same chapter.) If profitability—the return on investment to indicate earnings power and value from growth—is determined on the investment in the historical cost balance sheet, but part of the indicated value is on a replacement cost balance sheet, there is double counting. In short, for valuation by a book to have integrity, an accounting consistency condition for both balance sheet and income statement must be satisfied. A depreciation adjustment in “earnings power” calculations to estimate expenditure necessary to maintain replacement cost over a set period provides some amelioration. But with replacement cost itself a fuzzy number, so is this adjustment: More speculation. Value Investing Principles Here are the principles from this chapter to add to your collection: Be prudent: Anchor on a safe, conservative balance sheet. A safe balance sheet is a low-risk balance sheet that cannot come back and hit you later. Beware of off-balance-sheet liabilities. Beware of fair value accounting. Building a Value Investing Engine There is not much in this chapter to add to your analysis and valuation tool. Much of the insight comes from reading the financial statements and their footnotes for accounting issues rather than from quantitative analysis. A textual analysis with keyword searches might help, but nothing substitutes for a keen reading of the documents. Of course, you will modify your reformulated balance sheet because of that reading. You might also add a check list for accounting issues to your engine. The Exercise Book The exercises test your accounting knowledge and ask you to adjust to accounting issues raised. The Case Book Case 16: Microsoft Corporation: Accounting for Stock Options and Contingent Equity Securities An Omitted Liability In the 1990s, Microsoft’s stock price soared from $1.36 in 1991 to $58 by 2000 on a split-adjusted basis, with revenues increasing from $2.8 billion to $23.0 billion. Microsoft was credited with having “knowledge capital,” as evidenced by its dominance in product development and market positioning. Yet that “knowledge” was acquired by hiring talented individuals who were usually rewarded with stock options that paid off handsomely. However, the implicit payments did not appear in the financial statements: It appeared that the shareholders owned the “knowledge” without having to pay for it. Besides stock options that paid employees, Microsoft issued convertible securities and sold put options on its own stock. Yet the financial reports did not fully account for these instruments. Microsoft employees gained considerable value in the 1990s, but did the shareholders? Microsoft’s shares subsequently declined when the tech bubble burst, trading under $30 for the decade after 2000 and as low as $18. Did the accounting contribute to the bubble prices? Would the crash have been avoided with better accounting? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 10 Keeping the Book Chapter 10 Keeping the Book The Anchoring Income Statement T his chapter continues the quality analysis of the last chapter, this time with a focus on the income statement. The balance sheet must be a safe anchor, but earnings that add value to the balance sheet must also be of good quality. Value added is in future earnings; current earnings are the starting point to project those earnings. Are current earnings a good anchor for those projections? Quality Earnings Quality earnings are those that forecast what future earnings will be if the firm and its business remain the same as at present. With this anchoring, the investor then adds other information that tells her how future earnings will be different from the present. Poor quality earnings are those that give a misleading indication of the future because of the accounting measurement involved. The investor takes the current revenue and adds a projection of future revenue growth because she sees higher consumer demand. But, if current revenues are overstated, that is a problem. And so too for overstated or understated profit margins. We call a quality income statement a predictive statement, one that gives an unbiased prediction if the firm’s business remains the same in the future as at present. The Predictive Income Statement: How to Get There There are three steps to get to a quality, predictive income statement: Separate operating activities from financing activities. Been there, done that: See chapter 5 . Identify sustainable income that can repeat in the future. Been there, done that: See chapter 7 . Account for the accounting underlying the sustainable income. It is the third point that is the subject of this chapter. We will deal with Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) in 2020, as with the balance sheet in the previous chapter. First, let’s get the income statement on a basis that satisfies the first two steps. Table 10.1 does so with the loss for the year of $12,385 million. Of course, the business is not sustainable if it continues to make losses, but a base is still needed to determine whether that will be the case or whether recovery is being forecasted. The sustainable income statement below the published income statement reports an operating loss of $11,669 million after tax, increased by after-tax net interest expense of $716 million. After sorting out the detailed unsustainable items, the sustainable operating loss is $8,067 million, which has drawn a tax benefit of $1,480 to report a sustainable after-tax operating loss of $6,587 million. We see that there are $6,591 million of unsustainable losses reduced by $3,946 million in government grants (yes, this is booked as income). With a tax allocation to this net loss and net interest, the tax on sustainable income is −$1,480 million, yielding the after-tax sustainable income of −$6,587 million, a loss. Note the negative taxes (that is, income): Losses draw a tax benefit, a net operating loss (NOL) that can be carried forward against future taxable income.
