interest expense 929 Tax benefit at 22.9% ( 213 ) (716) NET INCOME (LOSS) ( 12,385 ) Now we are ready to deal with the third point, the accounting for sustainable income. Is it of good quality? The accounting can be of poor quality for two reasons: The quality of GAAP or IFRS accounting. The quality of the application of GAAP or IFRS accounting. We deal with these in order. Again, keep an accounting book open beside you to get more detail as you read. The Quality of GAAP and IFRS Accounting Mind the GAAP, to play on the warnings in London Underground stations. There are five issues. Stock Option Accounting We dealt with the effect of stock option accounting on the balance sheet in the last chapter. The recognition of the loss on exercise of options is not reported in the income statement, as vividly shown in the Microsoft case on stock options. The vignette box below adds a further example for stock warrants granted to Warren Buffett.. However, this loss is not part of sustainable income; the loss from the exercise of options in a given year does not forecast those for subsequent years. Here again, the balance sheet substitutes. If the safe balance sheet includes the updated option overhang liability, the expected loss from the future exercise of options is accounted for. Accounting for Convertible Securities This accounting, too, was covered in the last chapter. In booking the expected loss on conversion as a liability to the balance sheet, the loss is also recorded in the income statement by double entry. Thus the omitted financing cost comes into the income statement. But this has no effect on sustainable operating income. And for valuation purposes the liability is already taken care of in the adjusted balance sheet. A Vignette Buffett’s Goldman Warrants In 2008, in the midst of the financial crisis, Goldman Sachs invited Warren Buffett to contribute much-needed equity capital to the firm. Buffett got a very good deal. For a $5 billion cash infusion, he received perpetual preferred shares with a 10 percent dividend (redeemable by Goldman Sachs with a $500 million bonus to Buffett), plus warrants to buy 43.5 million common shares at $115 each. Goldman redeemed the preferred stock in 2011. Buffett exercised the warrants in 2013 when the stock was trading at about $150. What was the loss to Goldman Sachs shareholders when Buffett exercised? Market price of common stock issued on exercise (43.5m × $150) $6,525 million Exercise price (43.5m × $115) $ 5,003 Loss (not tax deductible) $1,522 million This loss was not reported in Goldman’s income statement. Accounting for Pensions While pension accounting for the balance sheet was covered in the last chapter, it requires more attention here. There are five components to the pension expense charged to the income statement, described in the following Accounting Primer. For Delta, these components are analyzed are in the pension footnote in table 10.2 on the next page. Table 10.2 Pension expense and other postretirement employee benefits (OPEB) for Delta Air Lines, Inc., 2020 Pension benefits Other postretirement and postemployment benefits Year ended Dec 31, Year ended Dec 31, (in millions) 2020 2019 2018 2020 2019 2018 Service cost — — — $ 96 $ 83 $ 85 Interest cost 700 833 781 120 137 126 Expected return on plan assets (1,373) (1,186) (1,318) (44) (47) (67) Amortization of prior service credit — — — (9) (9) (24) Recognized net actuarial loss 300 291 267 44 37 36 Settlements 38 5 4 — — — Special termination benefits — — — 1,260 — — Curtailment — — — — — (53) Net periodic (benefit) cost $ (335) $ (57) $ (266) $ 1,467 $ 201 $ 103 Accounting Primer Accounting for Pensions in the Income Statement The pension expense has the following components: Service cost: The expense of additional pension benefits credited to employees for working in the current year. See it is as wages for this year’s work. Interest cost: The cost that accrues as the future payouts come closer and thus the present value of the pension liability increases. It is similar to interest recognized on a zero-coupon bond. You can also see it as interest the firm must pay employees for paying them compensation later rather than now. Expected return on plan assets: This is the fair value of the plan assets multiplied by their expected rate of return. Amortization of prior service cost: When changes are made to plans that add benefits for past employment, the cost is amortized over subsequent years. Changes in actuarial estimates: The cost of actuarial changes (for example, changes in longevity assumptions). As Delta has terminated its pension plan, there is no service cost except for other post-employment benefits (OPEB). Interest accrues and there is an additional cost for changes in actuarial assumptions. But the important point here is the inclusion of the expected return on plan assets in the net pension expense, with the net expense negative and thus income. The net pension expense (or income benefit), typically reported as part of other income in the income statement, confuses expenses from running the business with returns from running a pension fund. Unless separated out, profit margins from sales in the business are increased by returns on the pension fund: Poor quality margins. See the following vignettes. Vignettes Expected Return for Plan Assets, General Motors Company (GE) and The Boeing Company (BA) General Motor’s expected return on plan assets for 2022 was $3,534 million (30.5 percent of earnings before tax). That yielded a negative net pension expense, a gain of $1,415 million. Boeing reported an expected return on plan assets of $3,789 million in 2022. Its net pension expense was a gain of $878 million. The reported loss of $5,022 million before tax is higher excluding this amount. Recognize that these firms run two businesses: a core business and a pension investment fund . There is another point: The expected return on plan assets is an estimate, and we have seen in this book that estimating an expected return is not trivial. Firms can decrease their net pension expense and increase income by setting a high expected return. Indeed, Delta’s expected return is high at 8.97 percent, compared with 7.31 percent for United Airlines, 6.50 percent for Boeing, 6.25 percent for GE, and 4.50 percent for IBM. A quality red flag: Is this an overestimate or is the higher number due to investing in higher-yielding (and higher-risk) investments? Accounting for Operating Leases With operating leases now on the balance sheet, the off-balance sheet issue is resolved. But an income statement issue remains. Unfortunately, the annual rentals on these leases go into the income statement rather than being split into the implicit interest portion (financing expense) and depreciation (operating expense). This treatment affects both operating income (by incorrectly including interest) and also the calculation of the firm’s borrowing rate. Separate the two by applying the firm’s borrowing rate on other long-term debt to the beginning balance of the operating lease liability and moving that number from operating income to interest expense. Accounting for Equity Investments with No Significant Control These investments with less than a 20 percent interest in other firms are marked to fair value, with unrealized gains and losses going to the income statement. (Under IFRS, the unrealized gains and losses are included in other comprehensive income if the investment is part of a long-run strategy.) Review the accounting in chapter 7 where a warning flag is raised. The last chapter pointed to issues with fair value accounting, with price bubbles coming onto the balance sheet along with errors in estimating Level 3 fair values. As a consequence, bubble gains and bubble-bursting losses and Level 3 errors enter the income statement. This compounds the problems in chapter 7 . The investor strips them out to get to sustainable income. But there is another solution. See the following vignette. See-through earnings (or look-through earnings) effectively applies the equity method (below) to holdings with less than 20 percent interest. It brings in fundamentals and throws out prices. Again, when calculating value to challenge the price, do not put price in the calculation . There is a caveat: If see-through earnings replace fair value gains and losses in the income statement, the investment cannot be carried at fair value in the balance sheet, or else there is double counting. A Vignette See-through earnings J.P. Morgan analyst Sarah DeWitt calculates that Berkshire Hathaway’s share of undistributed earnings from its stock portfolio is $12 billion, or about $5.19 per Berkshire B share. But the market doesn't always include this in its analysis. Ignoring the contribution of the portfolio, Berkshire's Class B shares look expensive at roughly 20 times EPS, DeWitt said. But including the calculation tells another story. DeWitt’s estimate for 2019 EPS is $10.25, and she says total see-through earnings are $15.44. The B shares trade around $209, or 13.6 times 2019 see-through earnings. The 13.6 is low for Berkshire’s B shares. Its historic average price-to-see-through earnings is closer to 17, the analyst noted. Her B-share December 2019 price target is $250, about 19 percent above where it currently trades. Take Apple as an example. Berkshire includes in its reported earnings only the dividends that it receives on its stake of 258 million Apple shares based on accounting rules. Those dividends are running at an annual rate of $2.92 a share. The see-through earnings would give Berkshire credit for Apple’s earnings, which at the time were expected to top $13 a share. Source : Yahoo Finance, October 17, 2018, as reported by CNBC. Accounting for Equity Investments Under the Equity Method Under the equity method with 20–50 percent control in a subsidiary, the see-through earnings of the subsidiary are included in the holding company’s income statement. In the balance sheet, the investment is carried at cost, with those recognized earnings added, less any dividends paid by the subsidiary. Thus, the balance sheet number is the original cost plus the holding company’s share in the change in the retained earnings of the subsidiary since acquisition. (The balance-sheet number is also reduced by any impairment of goodwill on purchase.) There are three issues in dealing with equity method reporting. First, equity income should be separated from other operating income in calculating profit margins: equity income is not income generated by top-line sales. Correspondingly, equity method investment in the balance sheet should be separated from other net operating assets so that a clean asset turnover (ATO) measure of sales relative to the net assets that generate them is calculated. Second, equity income is after tax (in the subsidiary), so no tax is allocated to it. A third issue arises because the earnings number from the subsidiary is a net income number that includes both operating activities and financing activities and does not have the detail of revenues, expenses, and net operating assets in the subsidiary that are necessary to analyze its value generation. If the “share of income in subsidiaries” is a large component of earnings, that presents a problem. At one time, The Coca-Cola Company held 49 percent equity in its bottling subsidiaries, the so-called “49 percent solution,” making analysis of value drivers difficult. In 2021, Coke’s equity income still stood at 13.95 percent of total operating income before tax. The vignette below demonstrates for Coke the difference between RNOA from sales and RNOA from equity investments. This issue illustrates how a lack of disclosure can add to information risk. If the subsidiary is publicly listed, the investor can go to its own filings to carry out the desired analysis, but not so in the United States if the subsidiary is a private company. In Europe, both private and public companies are required to file financial statements. To be more formal, one can perform proportional consolidation, that is, carry out the consolidation accounting applied for interests greater than 51 percent on these 20–50 percent interest firms, with line items in their income statements and balance sheets coming into the holding company’s financial statement at the percentage interest after eliminating intra-group transactions. Value Investing in Action Reformulated Financial Statements for The Coca-Cola Company (KO) to Accommodate the Equity Method, 2021 ($millions) In the income statement, equity income is separated from income from sales to calculate operating profit margin (PM) from sales. No tax is allocated to equity income. In the balance sheet, equity method investments are separated from other net operating assets to calculate ATO with sales compared to the net assets involved in generating the sales. Reformulated income statement Reformulated balance sheet Sales revenue 38,655 Net operating assets for sales 37,398 Operating expenses 28,347 Equity method investments 17,598 Sustainable income from sales before tax 10,308 Net operating assets 54,996 Tax expense allocated 2,471 Net debt 30,136 Sustainable income from sales 7,837 Common equity 24,860 Equity income in subsidiaries 1,438 Sustainable income 9,275 [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn01.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn02.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn03.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn04.png] [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn05.png] Segment Reporting Firms are required to report revenue and operating profits by segment, though not balance sheets. When revenues and profit margins vary over products, product-line information can be very helpful to understand what drives operating income. However, disclosure quality is often a problem. Some firms disclose segments by country or region rather than by product lines. For example, Coke gives segment results by regions across the world but, in times of changing tastes from fizzy drinks to other beverages, one would like to see comparative product information. Segment definitions can also sometimes change over time for the same firm, further reducing comparability. The Quality of the Application of GAAP and IFRS Accounting The quality of reported earnings varies with how firms apply GAAP or IFRS, with both estimation error and manipulation in play. Manipulation and error not only affect current earnings but also affect future earnings in the opposite direction when they reverse. The manipulation can go in both directions: Borrowing income from the future: Overstate income now, with lower income later. Lending income to the future: Report lower income now and higher income in the future. Here is a quote that should pull you into this discussion: Even the operating earnings figure that we favor can easily be manipulated by managers who wish to do so. Such tampering is often thought as sophisticated by CEOs, directors and their advisors. Reporters and analysts embrace its existence as well. Beating “expectations” is heralded as a managerial triumph. That activity is disgusting. It requires no talent to manipulate numbers: Only a deep desire to deceive is required. “Bold imaginative accounting,” as a CEO once described this deception to me, has become one of the shames of capitalism. —Warren Buffett, 2023 Berkshire Hathaway shareholders’ letter In valuation, we are interested in future residual earnings, and these are determined in part by RNOA. Figures 10.1A and B depict the issue in terms relevant to valuation for a no-growth firm. [IMAGE: ../images/fig10-1.png] 10.1 Effect of poor-quality earnings on return on net operating assets, RNOA. In the first figure, the firm is consistently earning operating income of 12 on NOA of 100, a 12 percent RNOA. An investor looking at the RNOA in Year 0 would forecast an RNOA of 12 percent for Year +1 if the firm’s business remained unchanged. In the second figure, the manager decides to manipulate the accounting to report income of 22 in Year 0, by booking more revenue or by reducing the allowance for bad debts or warranties, for example. (This must be his bonus year!) But that means lower revenues or higher bad debts or warranty expense in year +1, with income of only 2 as these numbers reverse. There is also a balance sheet effect: The higher income in Year 0 means higher NOA that becomes the base for the RNOA in Year +1, yielding an RNOA of only 1.8 percent. Chapter 6 showed how accounting by the book protects us from such manipulation, but the point here is the lower quality of earnings in Year 0 for predicting earnings in Year +1. The headings in the two figures indicate the examples are with no growth. If there is growth in income in Year +1, RNOA will increase over the 1.8 percent. This is what the manager is hoping for, partly to conceal his manipulation. But the manipulation is still there. And if growth does not materialize, the maneuver is starkly revealed, and the stock is hit by a torpedo. Growth is risky but is magnified with manipulation. Follow the Trails Manipulated earnings leaves footprints in other financial statements. A diligent analyst follows the trails to the Balance Sheet and the Cash Flow Statement to detect manipulation. Follow the Trail to the Balance Sheet The depiction in figure 10.1 demonstrates that manipulated earnings leaves a trail, showing up in net operating assets in the balance sheet. This is because accounting is a double-entry system: One cannot increase or decrease earnings in the income statement without affecting the balance sheet. So, for example, a firm cannot increase revenue without also increasing accounts receivable. There is an accounting equation that captures this: Operating Income = Free Cash Flow + Change in NOA In Figure 10.1B , free cash flow, not manipulated, stays at 12. But the manipulation of operating income shows up in the increase in NOA, one-for-one. Manipulation of earnings leaves a trail in the balance sheet. Follow that trail. Follow the Trail to the Cash Flow Statement Cash flows are objectively measured but accruals are typically estimates. Firms can therefore manipulate earnings with biased accrual estimates: bad debt estimates, loss provisions for banks and insurance companies, revenues allocated to deferred revenues, estimated warranty expenses, and many more. But, just as in the balance sheet, they leave a trail in the cash flow statement when the indirect method is applied (as it is with almost all firms). That calculates cash flow from operations by separating out the non-cash flow components of earnings. Follow that trail. We will do so. The following paper abstract spurs us on: The Misrepresentation of Earnings Ilia D. Dichev, John R. Graham, Campbell R. Harvey, and Shiva Rajgopal Abstract. The authors conducted a survey of nearly 400 chief financial officers on the definition and drivers of earnings quality, with an emphasis on the prevalence and detection of earnings misrepresentation. The respondents believe that the hallmarks of earnings quality are sustainability, absence of one-time items, and backing by actual cash flows. However, they also believe that in any given period, a remarkable 20 percent of companies intentionally distort earnings, even while adhering to GAAP. The magnitude of the misrepresentation is large: 10 percent of reported earnings. Financial Analysts Journal , vol. 72, no. 1, 2016 So, the analyst needs to have antennae up. However, in most cases, no quality issues are discovered. Auditors and board members are usually doing their job, and many managers have integrity. And red flags are often explained by operational factors; the flags are just prompts for further investigation. But there are situations where manipulation is more likely, as we will see in the following list. Situations where Manipulation is More Likely The firm is in the process of raising capital or renegotiating borrowing. Watch IPOs. Debt covenants are likely to be violated. There is a change in management. There is a change in auditor. Management rewards (like bonuses) are tied to earnings. Management is repricing executive stock options. There is a weak governance structure: inside management dominates the board. Regulatory requirements (like capital ratios for banks and insurance companies) are likely to be violated. Transactions are with related parties rather than at arm’s length. Special events such as union negotiations and proxy fights are imminent. The firm is “in play” as a takeover target. The firm engages in exotic arrangements (structured off-balance-sheet vehicles). A thorough earnings quality analysis involves investigating individual line items in the income statement. But, before delving into the line items, there are high-level diagnostics giving “red flag” indications that quality issues might be present. The IBM case at the end of this chapter applies these high-level diagnostics. If these diagnostics fail to raise flags, the investor might be content to go without the tedious analysis of individual line items. That said, a full set of diagnostics can be built into your spreadsheet engine for ready calculation at the press of a button. High-level Red Flags Follow the Trail to the Balance Sheet : Revenue accruals add to net operating assets (NOA) with an asset, accounts receivable. Expense accruals reduce NOA through asset reductions (depreciation and amortization) and liabilities accruing expenses to the income statement. An example of the latter is an accrued warranty expense that results in a warranty liability on the balance sheet, and so too for accrued wages, rent, insurance, and so on: An expense not yet paid for is a liability. If NOA increases more than sales revenue, there is an indication that expenses are underreported. With Δ indicating changes, the first red-flag diagnostic is: Diagnostic: ΔSales/ΔNOA A supporting metric captures expenses booked: Diagnostic: %Growth in OI relative to %Growth in Sales If you see a large increase in operating income (OI) on zero or negative sales growth, questions must be asked. Most likely this is due to operating factors—for example, cost cutting—but it’s good to check: Sales incur expenses, so where are the reduced expenses coming from? These two diagnostics affect the asset turnover (ATO) and the profit margin: An increase in ΔNOA relative to the increase in sales decreases ATO, and an increase in OI relative to an increase in sales increases the profit margin (PM). This introduces a third high-level diagnostic, the change in PM relative to the change in ATO: Diagnostic: ΔPM relative to ΔATO See figure 10.2 . Watch for the red flags off the diagonal. An increase in profit margin accompanied by an increase in ATO indicates increasing sales adding to both. An increase in profit margin but a decrease in asset turnover indicates a profit margin possibly increased by lower expenses: a red flag. A decrease in profit margin with a decrease in ATO indicates lower sales and lower profits from those sales, a negative indicator but not a manipulation flag. A decrease in profit margin with an increase in ATO flags reduced expenses that reduce the decrease in profit margins. [IMAGE: ../images/fig10-2.png] 10.2 Combinations of ΔPM and ΔATO flagging earnings quality. Follow the Trail to the Cash Flow Statement : Accruals are estimated, so net income relative to cash flow, reported in the cash flow statement, is to be challenged. The red-flag metric is: Diagnostic: Net Income/Cash Flow from Operations As Net Income = Cash Flow from Operations plus added Accruals, the diagnostic can be expressed 1+Accruals/Cash Flow. An increase in the measure indicates that a higher component of income is from accruals relative to cash flows, with the accrual component subject to manipulation. There is a little slippage with this metric: Cash flows can also be manipulated, for example, by factoring receivables or delaying payment of payables, so check for this explanation when evaluating the red flag. These diagnostics can be benchmarked against those for the firm in the past to gauge whether they are unusual, provided that past operations are like the present. Diagnostics for similar firms also provide another benchmark. With these high-level red flag diagnostics, we now venture into the detail in the income statement line items. Challenging Revenue Quality Revenue leaves a trail in the balance sheet. Sales on credit booked as revenue are also booked to accounts receivable, and sales not booked but deferred to the future are booked as a deferred revenue liability. The latter are sales for which the customer has paid or has been billed but the revenue recognition criteria of chapter 8 have not been satisfied. An accounting equation captures the accounting: Sales revenue = Cash Sales + Δ Gross Accounts Receivable − ΔDeferred Revenue That prompts two diagnostic red flags for misreported revenues: Diagnostic: ΔSales/ΔGross Accounts Receivable That is, if change in accounts receivable (before allowance for bad debts) is significantly greater than that for sales, there is an indication of poor-quality sales: The accounts receivable are not being paid off by customers. This can be from sales incorrectly booked or sales to low-quality customers who are less likely to pay. Diagnostic: ΔSales/ΔDeferred Revenue The action box for Microsoft on the next page elaborates. Deferred revenue is revenue that will be recognized as revenue in the income statement in a future period once the firm has delivered on its performance obligations. So, if a firm defers too little revenue in the current period, current revenue is too high and future revenue will be lower—borrowing from the future. If a firm recognizes too much deferred revenue now, it must report higher revenue later—lending to the future. The latter is called “cookie jar” accounting: Deferred revenue is a cookie jar that can be dipped into to create more revenue in the future. The Microsoft box tracks revenue recognized in the income statement against deferred revenue. The trails in the balance sheet and cash flow statement are followed: the deferred (unearned) revenue liability in the balance sheet, and the change in the deferred revenue reported between earnings and cash flow from operations in the cash flow statement. The flag years are 2017 and 2018: The higher revenue in 2018 is due to revenue in 2017 that was treated as deferred revenue. An analyst looking at revenue (growth) in 2018 should conclude that a good portion of that revenue is not from new customers booked. And those looking at the increased deferred revenue in 2017 should understand that revenue will increase in 2018. The recognition of earlier deferred revenue as part of revenue confuses revenue from signing up customers in the past with revenue gained by signing new customers. The latter is of keen interest: How many new customers is the firm winning? The first vignette on the next page involves Apple Inc. during the introduction of the iPhone. See also the vignettes for Cisco Systems and Tesco plc. Value Investing in Action Deferred (Unearned) Revenues for Microsoft Corporation (MSFT) Deferred revenues are recorded when a customer has performed but the firm has not entirely fulfilled the contract. That is typically the case when the contract covers several periods with revenue apportioned to periods as the firm delivers. Consider the case common to technology companies like Microsoft: Revenue recognition allocated to multiple parts of contracts: [IMAGE: ../images/ch10eqn12.png] For Microsoft, the trail runs to the balance sheet and cash flow statement. The balance sheet: Trail to unearned revenue liability (in $ millions) 2021 2020 2019 2018 2017 2016 Revenue 168,008 143,015 125,843 110,060 96,751 85,320 Unearned revenues 41,141 39,108 37,206 32,710 44,479 33,909 % of Revenue 26.3% 27.3% 29.6% 29.7% 45.9% 39.7% The cash flow statement: trail to change in deferred liability 2020 2019 2018 2017 2016 Recognition of unearned revenue NA NA NA 57,735 48,498 Deferral of unearned revenue NA NA NA 67,771 57,072 Change in unearned revenue 2,212 4,462 5,922 −10,036 −8,574 % of revenue 1.5% 3.5% 5.4% 10.4% 10.0% (The change in unearned revenue was not broken down in 2018–2021 cash flow statements.) For 2022, Microsoft’s unearned revenue was $48,408, 24.4 percent of revenue of $198,270. Vignettes Deferred Revenue for Apple Inc. (AAPL), 2006–2009 Apple introduced the iPhone in 2007, increasing revenues substantially. However, as the firm promised to update the phone’s operating system in the future, it deferred a significant portion of the revenues as indicated below (in $millions). Analysts recognized that these updates cost the firm little and claims on the liability were unlikely. So they treated the deferred revenue as current revenue. That is the number from selling phones, the base for projecting future sales. They also understood that, in recognizing revenue from deferred revenue with little cost incurred in updating the operating system, subsequent future profit margins are higher. But that is due to deferred revenue recognized, not lower costs per dollar of sales. 2009 2008 2007 2006 Revenue 36,537 32,479 24,006 19,315 Deferred revenue 10,305 7,882 2,240 1,101 % of revenue 28.2 24.3 9.3 5.7 Gross margin % 36.0 34.3 34.0 29.0 Operating PM % (before tax) 21.0 19.3 18.7 12.7 Deferred Revenue for Cisco Systems, Inc. (CSCO) Cisco Systems , producer of important components of the Internet infrastructure, reported revenue of $4,816 million for its second quarter of 2002, up from the $4,448 million in the preceding quarter and exceeding projections. It looked like the revenue decline after the bursting of the internet bubble was over. However, the firm pointed out that, for the first time, deferred revenue had reversed: The firm had recognized an unusually large amount of revenue on conditional shipments from prior periods. Deferred Revenue for Tesco PLC (TSCO.L) Tesco plc is a leading UK retailer operating a wide network of supermarkets and superstores. Commercial income accounts for a large proportion of its operating profits. Commercial income comes from suppliers, who make payments in advance of expected purchases and sales-related activities (e.g., promotions) that Tesco is expected to deliver in the future. The cash received from suppliers should be treated as deferred income (a liability), until such time as Tesco makes the purchases and delivers on any other performance obligations. Commercial income essentially provides a discount on list prices that is related to the Tesco’s efforts and success in selling the supplier’s products, but the discount is packaged as a separate transaction. This presents an opportunity for aggressive earnings management. Booking deferred income as revenue early, before it is earned, has the effect of reducing the cost of goods sold and therefore increasing operating profits. In the accounting periods from 2012 to the first half of 2014, Tesco was alleged to have recognized commercial income of £263 million prematurely in its foods business, overstating operating profits and understating the deferred income liability by this amount. Other Revenue Issues Other factors impinge on revenue quality. These are illustrated in a series of vignettes in the nearby box: FX effects . Revenue is determined in part by currency exchange rates that may not be indicative of currency effects in the future (as with Apple in the vignette). Firms are increasingly reporting “constant currency earnings” or “organic sales” to convey the effect of exchange rates. For its quarter ended September 2, 2022, Adobe Inc. reported revenue of $4.43 billion, up 13 percent but up 15 percent on a constant currency basis. Salesforce, Inc. reported revenue was up 22 percent but 26 percent with a constant currency. With $7.72 billion of revenue for the quarter, that is a significant exchange-rate effect. In 2023, Kimberly-Clark Corporation, the maker of Kleenex tissues and Huggies diapers (among many other household products), with annual revenue of $19.44 billion, reported revenue for its recent fourth quarter was flat but up 5 percent on a constant-currency basis. Channel stuffing : Booking revenue by shipping goods not required (Krispy Kreme) or booking revenue from goods on consignment (Valeant Pharmaceuticals). Also shipping goods early or late to include in the income statement for the year desired. Customer incentives : Recognizing the cost of providing these incentives (Dentsply Sirona) Grossing up revenues (Lyft, Uber, and Broadridge Financial Solutions) Related party transactions (Luckin Coffee) Inventory build-up indicating declining revenues: The firm cannot sell its inventory (Cisco Systems) Reversals of inventory write-downs (Carvana) Revenue Quality Issues: Vignettes FX Effects: Apple Inc. (AAPL), 2015 Sales revenue is determined by P × Q, the price per unit sold times the number of units sold. The analyst tries to get information on both, recognizing that a higher P usually results in a lower Q, depending in the elasticity of demand for the product. But, for international companies, another factor is involved: exchange rates. In 2015, two-thirds of Apple’s revenue was from outside the United States in currencies other than dollars. At the end of 2015, the average price of an iPhone in USD was $642 down from $691 at the beginning of the year. Every dollar of sales outside the United States lost USD 0.15. The effect on Apple’s revenue for 2015 was $10,750 million, or 4.6 percent of total revenue. Source : New York Times , March 27, 2016 As the U.S. dollar rose 16 percent against a basket of other currencies in 2022, large tech firms like Microsoft, Alphabet (Google), and Meta Platforms (Facebook), which generate 58 percent of their revenue outside the United States, saw the stronger dollar taking 5 percent off their revenue growth rate for the year. Channel Stuffing: Krispy Kreme, Inc. (DNUT) and Valeant Pharmaceuticals (renamed Bausch Health (BSC) since 2018) Krispy Kreme, with its much sought-after donuts, went IPO as a hot stock in 2000. But it then faltered upon discovery of channel stuffing: The firm shipped high-margin donut-making equipment to franchisees long before they needed it, booking the revenue. In 2005, the firm was required to restate profits going back to 2000. Its share price, once at a high of $49.37, dropped to $7.30. An accounting torpedo. Valeant Pharmaceuticals booked revenues in 2014 for drugs shipped, but the revenue should have been booked when prescriptions were written. Earnings were subsequently restated. Customer incentives: Dentsply Sirona Inc. (XRAY) In 2022, Dentsply was under SEC investigation for its accounting for customer incentives, the consideration to customers for placing orders in the form of volume discounts, rebates, and free goods. These incentives are common in the healthcare industry. Booking them involves estimates of future sales that are variable and require the recognition of a liability. Inspect footnote disclosures. Grossing up revenues: Lyft, Inc. (LYFT), Uber Technologies Inc. (UBER), and Broadridge Financial Solutions, Inc. (BF) At the time of their IPOs in 2019, Lyft and Uber were reporting losses, so significant weight was given to their revenues. The firms added discounts and incentives to passengers to their revenue numbers, with the amount also added to marketing expenses. Lyft added $338.4 million of rider incentives to revenue in 2018; revenue was 16 percent lower without it. Uber’s revenue would have been 12 percent lower than the $11.27 billion reported in 2018. Broadridge Financial Solutions, a distributor of proxy materials, added reimbursement for postage to revenue. Related party transactions: Luckin Coffee, Inc. (LKNCY) Luckin Coffee, a wannabe Starbucks in China, went IPO in May 2019 and doubled its valuation to $12 billion eight months after the IPO. But in April 2022 the company admitted that its staff had faked sales equivalent to $310 million in 2021, causing the stock price to fall by 75 percent. This confirmed reports from prominent short sellers earlier in the year. Luckin had been selling vouchers redeemable for millions of cups of coffee to employees and companies linked to the company’s chairman, booking those voucher sales as revenue. At the same time, it had been fabricating purchase records. Challenging Cost of Goods Sold (CGS) Impairment Effects on Cost of Goods Sold : Write-downs of inventory under the lower of cost or market rule are often charged to cost of goods sold (CGS), a temporary effect that needs to be accommodated in arriving at sustainable earnings. However, the write-down can also affect future CGS and gross margins: Inventory becomes future CGS, so if inventory is written down too much, future CGS are lower and profits higher: Lending income to the future. The below vignette reported an impairment of inventory at Cisco Systems, Inc. in the third quarter of 2001. For the fourth quarter of 2001, the firm reported a higher gross margin with lower cost of goods sold. Was the firm becoming more effective in controlling costs? No: The lower CGS was largely due to the earlier write-down of inventory. Inventory and Cost of Sales Quality Issues Inventory build-up: Cisco Systems, Inc. (CSCO) Cisco Systems was a major supplier of routers for the internet with revenues and stock price soaring in the 1990s. But, with the bursting of the internet bubble and a consequent drop in demand for its products, Cisco’s inventory-to-sales ratio increased from 16.9 percent in early 2000 to 37.5 percent by the second quarter of 2001. That not only forecasted a revenue decline but also an impairment of inventory of $2.7 billion in the third quarter of 2001. Reversing inventory write-downs: Carvana Co. (CVNA) In its first quarter of 2023, the used-car dealer reported a loss of $1.51 per share, beating analysts’ forecasts of a loss of $2.03. The firm had reversed an charge against inventory made in the previous quarter, boosting EPS by $0.48 per share. FIFO CGS The First-In-First-Out inventory method brings the oldest inventory purchases into CGS first of all at their purchase prices. Thus, if inventory prices are rising, CGS is lower than it would be if costed at current prices. Gross margin, then, is a biased indicator of future gross margin with CGS at the higher prices. Speed of inventory turnover mitigates. LIFO CGS The Last-In-First-Out method (only used in the United States) records CGS at more recent (last-in) prices, leaving inventory purchased at older prices on the balance sheet. However, that introduces a problem: By reducing inventory purchases, the older inventory on the balance sheet enters CGS. So, if these are at lower costs (as they are with rising inventory costs), a firm reduces CGS and increases gross margin. This is known as LIFO liquidation or LIFO dipping. See the vignette below. Absorption Costing for Inventory and Cost of Goods Sold When producing inventory, firms are required to use absorption costing for inventories. Under absorption costing, inventory units are costed at the direct (materials and labor) costs involved plus a share of factory overhead costs like depreciation, power, maintenance, and supervisor salaries. The amount of overhead absorbed into each unit is the total overhead cost for a period divided by the number of units produced. So, firms can reduce per-unit inventory cost and CGS by overproducing. That yields higher gross margins but also the prospect of lower sales prices in the future to clear the excess inventory. The practice has been observed with automobile manufacturers. Look for a buildup of cars in their lots. A Vignette LIFO Dipping: Applied Industrial Technologies, Inc. (AIT) The firm increased gross margin by 1.2 percent in 2010 by LIFO dipping. Firms are required to report dipping in the inventory footnote. Here is AIT’s explanation in its 10-K filing (in thousands of dollars): Inventories consist of the following: June 30, 2010 2009 U.S. inventories at current cost $ 268,021 $ 367,836 Foreign inventories at average cost 48,403 53,742 316,424 421,578 Less: Excess of current cost over LIFO cost for U.S. inventories 143,171 166,888 Inventories on consolidated balance sheets $ 173,253 $ 254,690 In fiscal 2010, the Company undertook an inventory management program which resulted in a significant decrease of certain U.S. inventories from the June 30, 2009 levels. These reductions resulted in the liquidation of LIFO inventory quantities carried at lower costs prevailing in prior years. As a result, a LIFO benefit reduced cost of goods sold by $23.5 million for the year ended June 30, 2010, equating to a $0.33 earnings per share benefit. The LIFO reserves were reduced by the same amounts. If inventory levels had remained constant with the June 30, 2009 levels, instead of recording this benefit, the Company would have recorded LIFO expense of $19.2 million for the year ended June 30, 2010. Therefore, the overall impact of LIFO layer liquidations in fiscal 2010 increased gross profit by $42.7 million. LIFO layer liquidations also increased gross profit in fiscal year 2009 by $4.4 million and in 2008 by $626,000. Inventory Buildup A buildup in the dollar value of inventory can indicate lower profits in the future. This can be due to increasing inventory costs, to absorption costing when production volumes fall, or to the inability of the firm to sell its inventory. The latter forecasts lower future sales but also lower dollar sales, as selling prices are discounted to move the inventory. The relevant diagnostic is Inventory-to-Sales. See the next vignette. A vignette Inventory buildup at Target Corporation (TGT) Target is a leading low-price U.S. retailer, selling food products, consumer staples, and durables, including home decor products, electronics, toys, and household essentials. It has been successful, drawing value investors to its shares with its sustained sales growth and profit margins. The COVID pandemic cut some of its supply chains, inducing the firm to build up inventory to protect against shortages. However, inventory became excessive, exacerbated by general inflation and higher inventory costs when suppliers raised prices as shortages developed. The quarterly numbers below show the consequences (sales in $ millions) 2021 2022 Q1 Q2 Q3 Q4 Q1 Q2 Q3 Sales 23,879 24,826 25,290 30,616 24,830 25,653 26,518 Sales growth 4.0% 1.9% 21.1% −18.9% 3.3% 1.03% Operating PM (b/4 tax) 9.81% 9.80% 7.84% 6.84% 5.35% 1.3% 3.9% Inventory/Sales 44.1% 45.4% 59.2% 45.4% 60.8% 59.7% 64.5% You see profit margins declining over time, even though sales are increasing, but with increasing Inventory/Sales as inventory costs rose and the firm built up inventory in anticipation of supply chain disruptions. (The higher sales and lower Inventory/Sales in Q4 2021 are due to the pre-Christmas spike that retailers typically experience in their peak season.) By Q1 2022, the operating profit margin had dropped to 5.35 percent, with Inventory/Sales up to 60.8 percent against 44.1 percent in the same quarter in 2021. Sales increased slightly in Q2 and Q3 2022 but, with cost of goods sold increasing by 16.6 percent in Q2, the operating profit margin dropped to 1.3 percent. Cost of goods sold dropped by 5.0 percent in Q3, indicating a reversal, with profit margins correspondingly increasing. Bad Debt Expense We have seen the role of gross receivables in diagnosing quality revenues, but gross receivables are then reduced by an allowance for bad debts, and that determines the bad debt expense in the income statement. This is good accounting in principle because both the accounts receivable and earnings are at the expected cash to be received from customers. But underestimation of the allowance overstates net receivables and earnings while overestimation understates them. Any overstatement or understatement will reverse in the future as the actual bad debts become apparent. See the vignette. A diagnostic raises the red flag: Diagnostic: Net Receivables/Gross Receivables A Vignette Allowance for Bad Debts: Gateway, Inc. In 2000, Gateway Inc., a manufacturer of personal computers and peripherals, began to finance computer sales to high-risk customers shunned by financing companies. Gateway’s consumer finance receivables, net of allowances for bad debts, increased from 3.3 percent of sales to 7.3 percent of sales. In the first quarter of 2001, the firm wrote off $100 million of receivables. Gateway was acquired by the Taiwanese company Acer Inc. in 2007. Loan Losses for Banks Under the so-called CECL (Current Expected Credit Loss) method promulgated by the Financial Accounting Standards Board (FASB) in 2016, banks make provisions for loan losses based on estimates of future defaults that might come over the full life of loans. IFRS implemented an alternative version of this “prospective method” accounting. The method was seen as a remedy for banks under-reserving in bad times. However, the estimate is subject to abuse, just like bad debt allowances on receivables. Even unbiased estimates introduce a problem, for an over- or underestimate relative to subsequent actual defaults means a reversal in the future. In bad times, banks book higher loan loss reserves, reducing profits, but these reverse later as conditions improve and loss estimates decline. That creates profits, but not profits from new business. “We don’t consider these as earnings,” says the CEO of a leading bank. “It’s ink on paper.” See the vignette. A Vignette Loan Loss Provisions for Banks Post-financial crisis and into the COVID pandemic, banks increased their loan loss reserves on the prospect of significantly increased defaults. In the first quarter of 2020, banks’ year-on-year profits dropped 70 percent with these provisions. However, as governments introduced their stimulus relief packages and the economy improved, defaults were lower than estimated, so these reserves were reversed, with bank profits jumping 300 percent in the first quarter of 2021. In the fourth quarter of 2020, J.P. Morgan Chase released $3 billion of reserves, amounting to 24.8 percent of profits for the quarter, and another $5.1 billion in the first quarter of 2021. “It’s ink on paper. We’re cheering that America’s doing better, but we don’t consider that earnings,” said Jamie Dimon, CEO of JPMorgan Chase. And later, “What kind of accounting is that? I think it’s crazy.” Thank you, sir, for honest reporting! Later, in the first quarter of 2022, J.P. Morgan Chase increased reserves by $900 million with a 42 percent drop in profits on prospects of a recession. ( Wall Street Journal , March 16, 2021 and June 4–5, 2022). Watch for reversals. Can we really understand how much banks are earning when so much of their profits are based on speculation about the future? Warranty Expenses The obligation to service warranties (on home appliance sales, for example) are booked as an expense against sales, with an associated warranty liability booked to the balance sheet. The red flag is similar as that for deferred revenue: Diagnostic: ΔSales/ΔWarranty Liability Schedule II in the 10-K tracks the change in warranty liabilities, comparing the accrual of the warranty expense with cash actually expended in servicing liabilities. Advertising and Promotion Firms can increase earnings by reducing advertising and promotion. But reducing these expenditures reduces the future earnings they generate. Track advertising expense as a percentage of sales. If lower relative to the past or to competitors, earnings management is suggested. However, it can also indicate that advertising is more successful in yielding sales. Understand the business with more granular data to resolve the question. Research and Development As with advertising, reduced R&D as a percent of sales can indicate earnings management; a firm can reduce R&D expenditures to increase current earnings but with a detrimental effect on future earnings. However, the ratio can also indicate the success of past R&D in gaining sales. The following vignette underscores the difficulty in interpreting the measure. A Vignette R&D for Pfizer Inc. (PFE) ($billions) 2021 2020 2019 2018 2017 2016 2015 2014 2013 2012 Sales 81.29 41.91 41.17 40.83 52.55 52.82 48.85 49.61 51.58 54.66 R&D expense 13.83 9.41 8.39 7.76 7.66 7.87 7.69 8.39 6.67 7.48 R&D/Sales 17.0% 22.4% 20.4% 19.0% 14.6% 14.9% 15.7% 16.9% 12.9% 13.7% From 2018 to 2020, R&D/Sales increased, indicating either declining success in gaining new revenue from R&D or increased R&D to gain future sales revenue. That revenue materialized in 2021 with a further increase in R&D, reducing R&D/Sales back towards its historical average. But was that a one-time effect from the Pfizer vaccine against Covid? Understanding the science will help here. In the first nine months of its 2023 fiscal year, Apple’s revenue declined 3.4 percent over the same period the previous year. Operating income declined more, by 7.3 percent However, the decline in operating income was partly due to R&D, which was up 16.0 percent, from 6.4 percent of sales in 2022 to 7.7 percent in 2023. This is a firm with innovation as a key driver. The 2023 drop in revenue must be evaluated considering the increase in R&D intended to generate future revenue from new products. Depreciation Annual depreciation expense depends on the investment cost of an asset, the estimated disposal value at the end of its productive life, the estimated productive life over which cost is depreciated, and the pattern of actual depreciation over that life. If the estimated productive life is too long or the rate of depreciation is calculated too slowly, depreciation is too low: Expect an (unsustainable) asset write down. Watch for earnings affected by a change in estimated lives. See the vignette on the next page. There is a further issue. Depreciation is based on the cost of investments. This is typically lower than costs to replace assets currently. When replacement is imminent, expect higher depreciation going forward. See that in the vignette. A common method for dealing with this replaces the accounting depreciation with “Maintenance CapEx.” This is the capital expenditure (CapEx) required to maintain assets at their productive level. It involves breaking out current CapEx into that required to maintain productive capacity and that for growth, Growth CapEx. It works when production involves a stable technology continuing into the future. But it is less applicable when products are changing: changing production technologies require different replacement assets with different useful lives and other productivity features. Maintenance CapEx is difficult to estimate and is often a speculative number: What is Maintenance CapEx for Apple, Google, and Amazon? A Vignette Change in Estimated Life for Depreciation American Airlines Group Inc. (AAL) In 2000 before-tax operating income of American Airlines increased from $1,156 million to $1,381 million. But notes to the financial statements revealed that the firm had changed the useful lives of planes from 20 years to 25 years, reducing annual depreciation by $158 million. Alphabet Inc. (formerly Google, GOOG) In 2023, the firm reported in an 8-K filing: “In January 2023 we completed an assessment . . . and adjusted the estimated useful lives of our servers from four years to six years and the estimated lives of certain network equipment from five years to six years. The effect for the three months ending March 31, 2023 was a reduction of depreciation expense of $988 million and an increase in net income of $770 million.” Modeling Asset Replacement Depreciation With aging assets, current deprecation may not indicate future depreciation when assets are replaced at higher cost. The following models show operating income for a hypothetical airline replacing aging planes with new ones ($millions): Airline with old fleet Airline with new fleet Revenues $37,254 $43,372 Depreciation 7,452 12,864 Fuel costs 12,982 9,723 Maintenance 3,142 23,576 2,045 24,632 OI before tax 13,678 18,740 Depreciation increases due to higher cost of replacement aircraft. But, due to innovations, the new aircraft are more fuel efficient, reducing fuel costs. They also require lower maintenance expenditures. And, by adding more revenue seats per mile and load factors by attracting customers through a more pleasant flying experience, they also add to revenues. Of course, higher NOA with more expensive aircraft also affects RNOA, and thus value-added residual income. Restructuring Charges When firms change the direction of their business, they often book restructuring charges for the costs of closing down the old business, along with a liability to pay for the restructuring. However, the costs and the liability are estimated. If the estimate is subsequently found to be too high, it is bled back to income to reduce the liability that no longer exists. These “bleed backs” create income, but not from business. Again, it’s ink on paper. The cash flow statement will render a red flag if it reports the change in the estimated liability among the accruals. Notice the restructuring charges in IBM’s cash flow statements in table 10.3 below. Income from a restructuring charge? Watch when new management arrives and takes a business in a new direction. Their incentive is to take an excessive restructuring charge, called a “big bath,” and then bleed it back to income later. That makes the restructuring look more profitable than it was and management more successful. Note that more recent accounting rules have made it more difficult to play this game. Deferred Tax Asset Valuation Allowance We saw in the last chapter that deferred tax assets are reduced by a valuation allowance if it is “more likely than not” that the deferred tax asset will not result in tax benefits. When firms change their assessment to indicate that the tax benefits will now be more likely to be realized, they reduce the valuation allowance, with a corresponding reduction in the tax expense in the income statement. See the following Delta Air Lines vignette. A Vignette Reversal of Valuation Allowance: Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) Tax expense, 2012–2014: ($millions) 2014 2013 2012 Income before tax 1,072 2,527 1,025 Tax expense 413 ( 8,013 ) 16 Net income 659 10,540 1,009 The tax expense in 2013 is negative, income rather than an expense. Effective tax rate 38.5% −317.1% ?? 1.6%?? What gives?? The explanation from the income tax footnote in the 10-K for fiscal year 2015: We periodically assess whether it is more likely than not that we will generate sufficient taxable income to realize our deferred income tax assets. We establish valuation allowances if it is not likely we will realize our deferred income tax assets. In making this determination, we consider all available positive and negative evidence and make certain assumptions. We consider, among other things, projected future taxable income, scheduled reversals of deferred tax liabilities, the overall business environment, our historical financial results and tax planning strategies. We recorded a full valuation allowance in 2004 due to our cumulative loss position at that time, compounded by the negative industry-wide business trends and outlook. At December 31, 2013, we released substantially all of the valuation allowance against our net deferred tax assets, resulting in an $8.3 billion benefit in our provision for income taxes. During 2014 and 2015, we continued our trend of sustained profitability. After considering all available positive and negative evidence, we released additional valuation allowances related to net operating losses and capital loss carryovers in each of those years. As a result, at December 31, 2014 and 2015, we retained valuation allowances of $46 million and $56 million, respectively, primarily related to state net operating losses, state credits and unrealized losses on investments, which have limited expiration periods. At that high level, the focus is total accruals relative to total cash flows. However, the cash flow statement gives more detail on the accruals (very occasionally in footnotes), so specific accruals can be investigated. Table 10.3 presents the income statement for International Business Machines Corporation (IBM) for years 1996–2000, years during the boom when considerable earnings management was going on in U.S. corporations. You see operating income and net income growing steadily over the years, but that is growth to be challenged: To what extent is it sustainable growth? That involves the question of how much of earnings are due to the accounting employed. Mind the GAAP In recent years, firms have increasingly referred to earnings numbers that adjust those in financial statements. Called non-GAAP earnings or street earnings, these measures omit selected components of earnings. Typically these are expenses so that the measures make the firm look more profitable. Cynics have labeled these numbers EBS (Everything but the Bad Stuff) to contrast with EPS. Clearly, one has to be careful in handling these earnings numbers. However, often the numbers added back to earnings are one-time items, so the measures convey sustainable earnings. In the United States, the SEC requires the numbers to be reconciled to the GAAP earnings so that one can see exactly what goes into them. The vignettes below give some examples. Beware also when firms refer to measures other than earnings. Companies generating sales through websites might refer to “clicks” or “eyeballs on the site” to proxy for revenue. Retailers mention Net Promoter Score® (NPS) based on surveys at checkout. These may be leading indicators but are typically not highly correlated with current sales. And they are not under the discipline of accounting that ensures that bad accounting for earnings leaves a footprint in the balance sheet. Vignettes Mind the GAAP In its 2019 IPO filing, Uber Technologies, Inc. (UBER) reported “Core Platform Profit” of $940 million compared to a GAAP loss of $3.7 billion. Core Platform Profit strips out marketing, R&D, and customer acquisition expenses. In its IPO in the same year, Lyft, Inc. (LYFT) reported Core Platform Profit of $921 million against a GAAP loss of $911 million. In 2022, Uber reported adjusted quarterly earnings of $560 million against a net loss of $1.6 billion. WeWork Inc. (WE) reported what it calls “contribution margin” of $467 million in its 2019 IPO filing. We have already pointed to the misrepresentation in excluding depreciation and amortizations with an EBITDA number, but this number also excluded lease expenses, marketing and sales, and salaries of building staff. The GAAP operating loss that included these expenses was $1.69 billion. The Kraft Heinz Company (KHC), in its 2021 10-K, reports “adjusted EBITDA” that adds back not only depreciation and amortization but other items to get from GAAP Net Income of $1,024 million to $6,371million and from an EPS of $0.82 to $2.93: Dec 25, 2021 Dec 26, 2020 Net income/(loss) $ 1,024 $ 361 Interest expense 2,047 1,394 Other expense/(income) (295) (296) Provision for/(benefit from) income taxes 684 669 Operating income/(loss) 3,460 2,128 Depreciation and amortization (excluding restructuring activities) 910 955 Divestiture-related license income (4) — Restructuring activities 84 15 Deal costs 11 8 Unrealized losses/(gains) on commodity hedges 17 (6) Impairment losses 1,634 3,413 Certain non-ordinary course legal and regulatory matters 62 — Equity award compensation expense (excluding restructuring activities) 197 156 Adjusted EBITDA $ 6,371 $  6,669 Embracer Group, a Swedish company, became the largest gaming group in Europe through rapid acquisition of interests in developmental studios, comics publishers, and intellectual property in The Lord of the Rings franchise, among others. It reports a metric called Operational EBIT that excludes the “earn outs” of pay to executives of acquired companies if they stay in their jobs and meet financial milestones. That turned losses in 2021–2022 into a profit of SKr9.9 billion. ( Financial Times report, April 3, 2023.) Here’s a trick: Rent the Runway, Inc. (RENT) does not book the cost of clothes it rents against the revenue from those rentals. Rather, it books the costs to the balance sheet and depreciates them. It then appeals to adjusted EBITDA that excludes this depreciation as a profit measure. So the costs of clothes rented is not incorporated. In the second quarter of 2022, its GAAP loss was $33.9 million while it reported adjusted EBITDA (profit) of $1.8 million. Stock compensation is an expense commonly left out of adjusted EBITDA. Robinhood Markets, Inc. (HOOD) excluded $1.57 billion in stock compensation to report an adjusted EBITDA of $34.1 million against a GAAP loss of $3.68 billion for its 2021 year. Non-GAAP earnings can convey sustainable earnings. Here are those for Delta Air Lines whose 2020 earnings we have examined. The add-back items do appear to be genuine unsustainable items. MTM refers to mark-to-market on derivatives. Pre-tax (loss)/income, adjusted reconciliation Year Ended Dec 31, (in millions) 2020 2019 Pre-tax (loss)/income $ (15,587) $ 6,198 Less: Restructuring charges 8,219 — Less: Government grant recognition (3,946) — Less: Impairments and equity method losses 2,172 — Less: Pension settlement charges 36 — Adjusted for: MTM adjustments and settlements on hedges 10 14 Equity investment MTM adjustments (19) (14) MTM adjustments on investments 119 13 Delta Private Jets adjustment — 3 Pre-tax (loss)/income, adjusted $  (8,996) $ 6,214 Accounting Arbitrage We have depicted the diagnostic exercise as a defensive one for the value investor. But discovery of low-quality earnings can also be an opportunity: Take positions in stocks where the market appears not to recognize the quality issues and thus is mispricing stocks. That is called accounting arbitrage. There are several behavioral theories behind accounting arbitrage: Investors, collectively, have limited ability to penetrate the accounting reports, they are fixated on bottom-line earnings, and they have limited attention to the flow of financial information. Those better informed and more diligent therefore have an advantage. Figure 10.3A tracks RNOA with an S-score that ranges between zero and one and indicates the degree to which current RNOA is sustainable. A sustainable income analysis goes into this S-score as well as the high-level diagnostics introduced in this chapter. Year 0 is the year in which S-scores are calculated, and this S-score forecasts differences in RNOA in subsequent years. While the change in RNOA in figure 10.1 is hypothetical, this one is real, with an analysis of the sustainability of RNOA providing the discrimination. [IMAGE: ../images/fig10-3a.png] 10.3A Return on net operating assets (RNOA) for firms scored on the sustainability of their RNOA at Year 0. The S-score indicates the probability that RNOA will increase after Year 0 based on information on the sustainability of RNOA that includes an earnings quality analysis. S = 0.5 indicates sustainable profitability. High S identifies firms with the top 1/3 of S-scores. Low S identifies firms with the bottom 1/3 of S-scores. Source : S. Penman and X. Zhang, “Modeling Sustainable Earnings and P/E Ratios Using Financial Statement Information,” 2005, https://papers.ssrn.com/sol3/papers.cfm?abstract_id=318967 . Figure 10.3B reports returns from investing based on the S-score, investing long in firms with a high S-score and shorting firms with a low S-score for zero net investment before transactions costs. In all but three years, the returns are positive. They come with two disclaimers. First, the investment period is some time ago. Second, returns can also be explained by risk; the risk analysis of chapter 8 should accompany this exercise. [IMAGE: ../images/fig10-3b.png] 10.3B Returns from investing long in firms with high S-scores and short in firms with low S-scores, by year. Returns are adjusted for returns associated with firm size. Risk in the Income Statement Income statements serve to indicate sustainable income for the future. But they also serve to report income under different scenarios. That informs the sensitivity analysis of risk to value in chapters 7 and 8 . Delta’s 2020 year is analyzed, for Delta took a hit to sustainable income and to value. So, its income statement conveys how value-added earnings and its drivers are affected in extreme circumstances. And it can be studied to see how Delta adapted to those circumstances to sustain itself. One year is just one outcome for input into scenario analysis, but this is an important year to learn about risk. That risk is to value-added residual income from operations and to its drivers. In chapter 7 , that is risk to operational metrics and the corresponding accounting metrics that connect these metrics to residual income and value. Table 10.4 compares these measures in 2020 to those in recent years. The decline in revenue passenger miles, load factors, and revenue per available seat explain the 63.63 percent decline in revenues. This is a firm with a big fixed-cost operation including, in this period, the retention and payment of employees during the pandemic. So, the decline in sales goes directly to bottom-line operating income, declining from 10.89 percent and 8.95 percent in 2019 and 2018 to −38.53 percent in 2020, relieved by lower fuel costs and no profit sharing with employees. And, with a largely fixed assets base, the revenue drop goes into a large drop in ATO. One counter is the government assistance of $3,946 million (in the income statement in table 10.1 ). These driver effects combine to reduce sustainable residual operating income to −$9,857 million from $2,672 million in 2019. Base forecasts of earnings on a quality, predictive income statement. Manipulated income reverses in the future. Follow the trail to the balance sheet and cash flow statement to detect manipulated income. Turn quality analysis into an opportunity for accounting arbitrage. Building a Value Investing Engine The diagnostics in this chapter can be incorporated in your engine with the metrics then automatically calculated. Start by accommodating the GAAP deficiencies and then calculate the high-level diagnostics followed by those for individual line items. An ambitious programmer might also build in the calculation of an S-score. The Exercise Book The exercises are there for you to calculate and apply various quality diagnostics. The Case Book Case 17: IBM: Challenging Growth Beware of Paying Too Much for Growth Generated by Accounting IBM was one of the great growth companies of the twentieth century. Founded as the Computing-Tabulating-Recording Company in 1910, annual sales grew to $88 billion by 2000. IBM was one of those “growth companies” that Benjamin Graham avoided with his reluctance to pay for growth. But he missed something: The firm delivered substantial value from growth. In the early 1990s, like now, the firm’s growth prospects were challenged. Long a leader in hardware with its large mainframe computers, the nature of the business was changing to decentralized computing with emphasis on software and information services. IBM’s early leadership with the personal computer in the 1980s proved to be short-lived. That became a low margin business, with more nimble producers of IBM PC-based computers such as Dell gaining advantage and the alternative technology offered by Apple intruding. Under the leadership of Louis Gerstner Jr., the firm transformed itself into an information technology company, providing technology, system software, services, and financial products in addition to hardware. Mr. Gerstner’s book Who Said Elephants Can’t Dance? Inside IBM’s Historical Turnaround , published in 2002, gives you the play-by-play. During the turnaround, IBM took large restructuring charges, reporting losses of $2.86 billion, $4.97 billion, and $8.10 billion for 1991–1993. The turnaround worked, with substantial earnings growth from 1995–2000. But was the growth all that it was made out to be? Was it good-quality growth that one would anchor on in evaluating the prospect of further growth in the future? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 11 Investing by the Book Chapter 11 Investing by the Book Special Situations Y ou have seen value investing in action in many contexts in this book. This chapter takes you to special settings where the same principles apply, but with wrinkles for the particular situation. The chapter covers the valuation of property and casualty insurers, and value added in mergers and acquisitions (M&A). A case in the Case Book also takes you into a reorganization. M&A and reorganizations, along with turnarounds, are sometimes referred to as asset conversion activities, as opposed to the going-concern activities we have been involved with in the book to this point. Investing in a Property and Casualty Insurer In the first quarter of 2022, Berkshire Hathaway acquired a $620 million stake in Markel Corporation (MKL), buying 420,000 shares amounting to 3 percent of Markel. Markel is primarily a property and casualty (P&C) insurer but, like Berkshire Hathaway itself (and also Alleghany Corporation, which Berkshire recently acquired), Markel also invests in businesses, particularly early-stage and private businesses, including a luxury handbag maker, a farm business, a homebuilder, construction, and transportation. Markel’s insurance subsidiaries have a financial strength rating (FSR) of A from both Best’s and Standard & Poor’s. Let’s evaluate Markel at the time of the share purchase and, in so doing, demonstrate how one applies value investing principles when investing in a P&C insurer. It’s time for you to digest Markel’s 10-K on the SEC’s Electronic Data Gathering, Analysis, and Retrieval (EDGAR) website. Understanding the Business As with any investment, we don’t venture very far without understanding the business. 1 1 . This discussion benefited from comments by Professor Tom King at Case Western Reserve University. Insurance is at the center of Markel’s business, selling insurance contracts and settling claims by insurees. Revenue is generated from the insurance premiums and expenses from insurance losses on claims and the administrative costs of the business. P&C insurance insures against property losses, marine losses, loss of profits, professional liability, credit risk, and transaction risk (among other scenarios), and can involve reinsurance of other insurers’ exposures. Though there are some similarities, P&C insurance contrasts with life insurance that insures against death and sells investment and tax-mitigation products. A particular feature of the insurance business model is the generation of a float. The lag between premiums received and payouts on insurance policies provides cash that the insurer can deploy. The float depends on the frequency and severity of claims (high frequency/low severity like car insurance, versus low frequency/high severity like nuclear plant insurance) and the time to settlement of claims (a short tail, as with the brief time between hurricanes and settlement on homeowners insurance, versus a long tail, as with medical malpractice liability). Typically, the float is invested in securities, but some of it is invested in businesses. This underlies the Berkshire Hathaway business model. Warren Buffett and his team are good at insurance, in Geico automobile and general liability insurance, reinsurance, and even catastrophe insurance. Buffett is also a good equity investor but, rather than appealing to other investors to invest in Berkshire Hathaway to provide the funds to invest, he uses the float from the insurance business to do that. Similarly with Markel: The firm invests the float in investment securities, both fixed income and equity securities, but also in businesses grouped into an entity called Markel Ventures. This is sometimes referred to as using other people’s money (OPM). The box on the next page depicts the business model. It is tempting to see this float as free money, unlike money from investors who require a return in compensation. However, that is a mistake and a point to be recognized in valuing an insurer. Who would provide money for free? One typically charges for the use of one’s money. Hark back to the discussion of operating liability leverage in chapter 6 . The supplier allowing deferred payment with accounts payable charges a higher price for goods supplied than if cash is on delivery. Customers providing cash in advance, a deferred revenue liability, demand a lower sale price for the use of their money. And insurees providing a float require a price for using their money. You might object, saying: I can’t negotiate a lower insurance premium on my car insurance. But the forces of competition take care of it, another example of the welfare improvement of competitive markets. Insurance firms compete for the float and, to do so, lower insurance premiums to get the float, giving the benefit to you. Of course, monopoly in the insurance market may impinge on this mechanism, with loss to the insurees. Sadly, insurance regulators can support the insurers rather than you. That aside, the insurer has a key strategic decision to make: Lower premiums, resulting in lower insurance profits from lower premiums relative to insurance losses, but gaining a higher float; or raise premiums and forego the float. This, we will see, is an important point in evaluating an insurer. Again, as always, understanding the business model, the strategy, is critical. Have you noticed that insurers typically don’t make much from their insurance business: Some good years but some bad years, with just a small profit overall? That is how it should be: If they are using OPM, they are charged for it. However, insurers make it up from profits from investing the float. The Business Model for a P&C Insurer Underwriting casualty losses: – Earn premiums to cover losses – Pay out on claims [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn01.png] Invest the float in an investment portfolio and operating businesses: Using OPM (Other People’s Money) Reformulated Financial Statements As always, an investment analysis begins with the reformulation of the financial statements. Table 11.1 gives the 2021 balance sheet and table 11.2 the income statement for Markel. Refer to the 10-K or an internet glossary if you have any doubt about what each item contains. If you use the tagged eXtensible Business Reporting Language (XBRL) versions of the financial statements in EDGAR, they provide definitions directly. The formal statements are followed by the reformulated statements in the second part of each table. Those reformulated statements not only distinguish operating activities from financing activities, but also break up operating activities into the different businesses. For a P&C insurer, that involves insurance activities, investment in securities, and investment in businesses, with the last two involving investment of the float.
