You see these three components in Markel’s reformulated balance sheet. In the insurance operations, operating liabilities are greater than operating assets. Thus, net operating assets in the insurance business is a negative $11,723 million, extreme operating liability leverage. This is the float: The unpaid claims and prepaid premiums in operating liabilities provide cash to invest in securities and Markel Ventures businesses. The security investment portfolio, comprised of fixed income (debt) securities and equities measured at fair value, totals $23,411 million. Markel Ventures’ net assets are $4,466 million. The combined net operating assets of these operations is $16,154 million. A reduction by $1,436 million in net debt and a small $23 million in noncontrolling interests explains the common shareholders’ equity of $14,695 million in the reported balance sheet. As it should, the reformulated balance sheet mirrors how an insurance business is run: Generate a float by maximizing operating liabilities relative to operating assets, then get that float to work with investments in securities and other business operations. The reformulated income statement in table 11.2 reports how those activities performed in 2021. As usual, the reformulated statement distinguishes sustainable and nonsustainable income, with taxes allocated to components of each (at the end of the table) as well as to the financing activities. Note that, while the investment activity is an ongoing business, its income is designated as nonsustainable: It is determined by security sales and the unrealized gain during the year, neither of which are indicative of the future. So these items do not come into sustainable income, nor a valuation. No worries: Valuation uses both an income statement and a balance sheet, and these securities are already fair-valued in the balance sheet. In effect, the current performance of the portfolio is irrelevant. The numbers are, of course, relevant to judging the performance of the investment managers—to compare it to benchmark returns, for example. Note that the profits from insurance underwriting are quite low, 6.8 percent of premiums (and this was a “good year”). The low profitability reflects the cost to generating the float, the cost of using OPM. The Accounting Quality Analysis As always, the value investor examines the accounting quality in the financial statements before proceeding. There are four main quality issues for a P&C insurer: 1. The quality of fair value accounting Fair value accounting is applied to the investment portfolio. See the investment footnote 4 in the 10-K for details of these investments. Our concerns, enumerated in chapter 9 , are twofold. First, if the fair values are Level 1 (marked-to-market) and those market prices are bubble prices or depressed (fire-sale) prices, we are worried that the investment portfolio is over- or undervalued. Second, if the fair values are estimated Level 3 values, we are concerned about estimation bias from using aggressive or conservative assumptions. The fair value footnote 5 informs that most fixed income securities are fair-valued at Level 2 and equity securities at Level 1. Our concerns about Level 3 investments are thus mitigated, though we must worry about possible mispricing of the Level 1 equities. Are we buying an overvalued investment portfolio? If so, there is danger. Are we buying an undervalued portfolio? If so, we might be buying securities cheap, and more so if the portfolio manager is a good value investor. That is a reason for buying an insurance company—provided, of course, the insurance operation is not overvalued. Indeed, buying an insurance company is an alternative to buying an active investment fund if the market pricing of the insurance operation also looks cheap. This we will investigate. 2. The quality of estimated unpaid insurance losses The liability for unpaid claims, $18,179 million for Markel, is an estimate of amounts to be paid eventually on insurance policies, so is subject to error and bias. Under-reserving for expected claims reports higher insurance profits now but lower profits in the future as the actual losses occur. And, with those higher profits, under-reserving leads to incentives to underprice insurance. Markel details the additions to these reserves along with the subsequent settlement payments on claims. Table 11.3 explains the $18,178.9 million number. From 10-K footnote 9, the first panel compares the additions to the reserve each year with the actual payments made. The two should align over time, though with some yearly variation. Markel increased reserves by $3,582 million in 2021 and $3,467 million in 2020, more than payments for claims in those two years. That indicates more conservative loss provisions or an extension of time from claims to payment that increases the float. expenses, net of reinsurance $ 7,588.0 Footnote 9 to the financial statements goes into the reserving methodology and provides a much longer record of reserving and insurance payments in what is known as the diagnostic triangle. That is displayed in table 11.3 , which reports the total of incurred but not estimated insurance liabilities, complemented with a second triangle that reports amounts ultimately paid on claims. Reading down the columns in the first triangle, you can see that of the $19,803 million in total losses recognized at December 31, 2021, $1,315 million relates to claims in 2012 while $3,128 is from claims in 2021, and so on. The second triangle reports the payments made against claims. In 2021, Markel had paid out $12,451 million of the estimated incurred losses of $19,803 million in the first diagnostic triangle, with the years the claims were made detailed. Footnote 9 has commentary of these diagnostic triangles. 3. The quality of reported returns on the investment portfolio You notice that Markel presents a combined income statement and comprehensive income statement (as do most insurance companies) and that the reformulated statement is footed to comprehensive income, not net income. This is in response to the practice of “cherry-picking.” With only realized gains and losses for fixed-income securities going into net income and the unrealized going into other comprehensive income (OCI), firms could report higher net income and earnings per share (EPS) by selling securities that have appreciated rather than depreciated, with the unrealized loss on the latter in OCI, thus excluded from net income and EPS. This is more likely when net income needs a boost in a year with large insurance losses. In strong investment years, firms can recycle the accumulated losses in OCI into net income, thus smoothing EPS over time. Beware of EPS for firms with investment portfolios. The recent requirements to report in net income all unrealized gains and losses for equity investments mitigates, but that does not cover debt investments. For Markel, there are significant investment gains in the income statement in 2021 but unrealized losses of $355 million on debt securities in comprehensive income. So the income statement overstates the performance of the investment portfolio that year, and net income is correspondingly overstated. The reformulated statement reports the overall investment performance. Note, however, that accumulated comprehensive income in the balance sheet is positive, indicating that, to the extent this is due to investment performance, there are gains that can be booked to net income in the future. 4. The fair value option Chapter 9 warned about firms taking the option to fair value liabilities: Strip out the fair valuing and associated gains and losses, and report the actual amounts a firm is obligated to pay. Mercifully, Markel does not exercise the fair value option. Buying Markel Let’s proceed according to the discipline of investing by the book. After reformulating financial statements and investigating the accounting quality in those statements, the discipline requires us to understand the expectations built into the market price. With the warnings about paying too much for growth, we are interested in the market’s growth forecast, as with all firms we have looked at. The Value Investing in Action box establishes the market value of each of Markel’s operations in the reformulated balance sheet by assigning the total market capitalization to these components. To do so, it first sets the market value of the investment portfolio at its fair value on the book, and the net debt at book value. The fair value for the investment portfolio is subject to the fair value quality analysis above: If there are indications that the fair values are too high, give them a haircut. With little disclosure in the United States on the private firms in which Markel Ventures invests, its value is simply its after-tax earnings in the reformulated income statement in table 11.2 , multiplied by the average P/E ratio at the time of 22. Clearly this is a noisy estimate, adding to information risk due to lack of disclosure that could be reduced if one were to obtain more data on the enterprises consolidated into Markel Ventures. In our final analysis, we will subject this estimate to a sensitivity analysis. In the European Union and for many companies, financial statements of private companies are publicly available. The noncontrolling interest is small, so is just valued by applying Markel’s P/B ratio to the balance sheet number. The value for the insurance business is a plug number and it is negative: −$6,666 million. This might surprise you, but the insurance operation generates a liability, as the negative NOA of $11,723 million in the balance sheet indicates. Put another way, the insurance business generates a float; that is, OPM that must ultimately be paid back to insurees with the payment of claims. That negative value is countered by the positive value of the activities in which the float is invested, value that can be called upon to satisfy insurance obligations. If we are happy with the values of the other components of the market price, it is the −$6,666 million market price for the insurance business that we must challenge. Again, ask yourself if you think the value of the investment portfolio needs to be written down or up from the fair value on the balance sheet. You are protected by being conservative, particularly in an uncertain period like the first quarter of 2022 with both inflation and recession looming, conditions that might damage Markel Ventures also. That creates the margin of safety that value investors desire. A lower value for the investment portfolio means a higher value (less negative) for the insurance plug number, a higher hurdle to buy the stock. Value Investing in Action Market Price of Markel Corporation’s Operating Segments Market equity price = $1,476 x 13.570 million shares = $20,029 million (The per-share price is that paid by Berkshire Hathaway) Composition of market price with unknowns (in $millions): Value of investment activities (at fair value) 23,411 Value of insurance activities ? Challenging the Market Price The challenge, as always, is to the growth expectations in the market price. That is elicited by reverse engineering from the market price in our standard valuation model. For the insurance operation, [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn02.png] This looks a little different from the model we have been using with other firms, for r Firm it contains two discount rates: r Invest = The after-tax expected return in investment securities and business ventures. r Combined = The required return for investing in all the businesses, including insurance, investments in securities, and business ventures NOA is negative for an insurance operation, the float. That float is invested in the securities portfolio and Markel Ventures, so r Invest in the numerator of the valuation is the (after-tax) expected return from investing the float in those operations. With this return applied to negative NOA, the subtraction from OI yields a positive number: value added (a negative times a negative is positive). So the numerator in the formula says: To the operating income (OI) from the insurance operation, add the expected return from investing the float. That is the value added (residual income) from the insurance operation. However, the risk to this value added involves both the risk of not getting the forecasted OI in the insurance operations and the risk of not achieving the expected return from investing the float. That is the risk in r Combined at which the value added is discounted in the denominator. Those two risks can compound. 20th Century Insurance Company had a concentration of homeowners insurance risk in Southern California, leading to substantial insurance claims from the 1994 Northridge earthquake just as bond prices in its investment portfolio fell when the Fed raised interest rates. Reinsurance risk also enters the combined required return; that is, the risk that the counterparty will not pay on claims. Risk is in the management of premium pricing, loss reserving, investing, and the use of reinsurance, and “good management” is judged on the management of these risks. The growth rate ( g ) is the growth in the value added to NOA in the insurance operation. As in our discipline, elicit this from the market price by entering the required inputs for the reverse-engineering calculation: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn05.png] We have the set the expected (after-tax) return for investing the float at 5 percent per year, equivalent to a 6.32 percent before-tax return. From the reformulated financial statements, the (after-tax) return for 2021 was 6.5 percent, but that was a good year. The combined required return with insurance risk is set to 12 percent (conservative?). You see the negative NOA adding value, as with Apple Inc. in chapter 6 . Insurance is an example of using operating liability leverage to add value. That leaves the forecast of operating income for 2022, OI 2022 , to be inserted. In other businesses, we might insert the sustainable income for 2021. But, always aware of transitory income, we understand that insurance companies can have good years and bad years; what you lose on the swings, you pick up on the roundabouts. So the past year may not be a good forecast of the forward year. What we need is the average experience in the insurance business. Helpfully, insurance firms provide information on loss and expense ratios. See the Value Investing in Action box below. That box returns a forecast of operating income of $244 million for 2022. Thus, [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn06.png] The solution: g = −4.42%. A negative growth rate: Now, there’s something to think about! Are you as pessimistic as the market appears to be? The Berkshire Hathaway folks clearly were not. Value Investing in Action Combined Loss and Expense Ratios Aid the Forecast of Insurance Profits and Losses Combined ratios : A loss ratio is the amount of reported insurance losses to insurance premiums, and an expense ratio is the amount of expenses incurred in the operation relative to premiums. The combined loss and expense ratio is the ratio of losses plus expenses to premiums. The combined ratio indicates profit or loss, with a ratio of 100 percent for zero profits, over 100 percent for losses, and under 100 percent if the firm is making profits from insurance. Firms often break these ratios down into contributing factors (hurricanes and the COVID pandemic, for example). Here are the combined ratios for Markel from 2016 to 2021 (in percent), compared to those for Chubb Limited (CB) and W. R. Berkley Corporation (WRB). 2021 2020 2019 2018 2017 2016 Average Markel 90.3 97.7 94.0 98.0 105.0 92.0 Chubb 89.1 96.1 90.6 90.6 94.7 88.3 W. R. Berkley 89.6 94.9 93.8 95.3 96.7 94.3 The average for Markel is 96.2%. For comparison, the average for Markel from 2010–2015 was 96.0%. Applying average combined ratio to forecasting 2022 operating income (in $millions): Current premiums, 2021 6,503 Average loss and expense (96.2%) 6,256 Expected profit before tax 247 Other insurance income, 2021 92 Expected insurance profit before tax 339 Tax (at 2021 tax rate) 95 Expected OI 2022 244 Insert this operating income into the reverse engineering task. Challenging the Market’s Growth Rate The negative growth rate implies that the market believes that the value added by the insurance business is in decline from the $830.15 million estimated for 2022. Fundamental analysis tells us to go to the drivers to challenge that forecast. What are the drivers and what could be the market’s beliefs about these drivers that explains its forecast? That begins our “negotiation with Mr. Market” about Markel, forcing us to come up with a reply. Here are the value drivers for a P&C insurer: Profitability in insurance: The loss and expense ratios that translate into insurance gain or loss The float Value of investments The investment return driver, r Invest The overall risk driver, r Combined So the negotiation goes something like this: The insurance profitability driver: The market thinks insurance profits will be significantly lower in the future than the $244 million we forecasted for 2022. That means lower insurance premiums, but these have been growing. Or it means a combined loss and expense ratio higher than the historical average of 96 percent. Do we see more competition for premiums, or does Markel have low churn of insurance clients? Do we see insurance calamities on the horizon? The float driver: The market thinks that Markel will lose its value-adding float. That can come from competitors lowering premiums to gain float. If Markel responds by also lowering premiums, it with have lower insurance profits. That is the tension in the insurance business in a competitive market: Higher (lower) premiums mean lower (higher) float. Is Markel faced with the prospect of losing the float by maintaining premiums, or must it lower premiums to maintain the float? The valuation of investments: The market attributes a lower value than the balance sheet fair values. The return on investments driver: The market thinks that the investment portfolio and Markel Ventures’s businesses will earn a lower return than the 5 percent we have estimated. The investor digs into these investments to continue the negotiation. Are the equities in the investment portfolio overvalued? The fixed income (debt) securities are sensitive to increase in inflation. Is this in Mr. Market’s thinking? The risk and required return driver: The market thinks the firm is riskier than the 12 percent r Combined . It thus adds a further discount to the stock price. What is your view of the risk and what is your personal discount rate for that risk? Is the 12 percent not conservative enough? Sensitivity Analysis These varying scenarios can be evaluated with a sensitivity analysis. Scenarios that explain the −$6,666 market price are of particular interest: If these scenarios are low probability, the investor is more assured in buying the stock. Consider one such scenario: Premiums dropping to $5,000 million per year A combined loss and expense ratio of 99 percent. With the drop in premiums and the same loss and expense ratio, this implies an after-tax profit from insurance of $36 million. Expected return on investments of 4 percent Growth in the float of only 2 percent per year A required return for the combined risk of the business of 12 percent The consequent valuation is: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn07.png] This is approximately the current market value of the insurance operation. If the investor sees these scenarios as unlikely, then a BUY decision seems comfortable. More so if these scenarios are conservative. Other scenarios can be entertained. The fair value of the investment portfolio, based on Level 1 and Level 2 market values, is $23,411 million, with the implied market value of the insurance operation then −$6,666 million. What if one concluded that the $23,411 million was an overvaluation and shaved it down to $20,000? The plug value of the insurance operation is then −$3,255, a higher hurdle to meet, requiring higher drivers than those justifying the −$6,666 valuation. Interestingly, market overvaluation of the investment portfolio implies an undervaluation of the insurance operations at Markel’s current stock price, making those operations look cheap. The same thinking applies to the estimated value of Markel Ventures: If we have priced that too low, then the hurdle to buy the insurance operations is too high. Subject this to sensitivity analysis also. Insurance is about sharing risk and some of that risk falls on shareholders. So remember the effect of being conservative: Setting a lower valuation for the investment portfolio or Markel Ventures implies a higher market valuation for the insurance operations, the plug, and thus a higher hurdle to meet. If a conservative analysis says that higher value still looks cheap, all the better: The conservatism has built in the desired margin of safety. That follows the principle: When facing risk, be conservative: Build in a margin of safety. The Markel case at the end of this chapter takes you there. What driver scenario did Berkshire Hathaway see to justify the share purchase? Value Added in Mergers and Acquisitions An article titled “A Deal Too Far” ( The Economist , October 7, 2017) reported on a study by S&P Global Market Intelligence on the impact of acquisitions from 2001–2017 on the acquiring firms’ share price. The study concluded: Shares of the acquiring firm underperformed the market and industry average Profitability fell Net profit margins fell EPS grew more slowly Debt increased Interest expense increased There is something wrong with this picture. This section will show that these consequences can be expected even if the acquiring firm adds value with the acquisition. The metrics are not indicators of value added. Our analysis applies to the shareholders of both the acquiring firm and the acquired firm. Think of yourself as a valuation expert giving a fairness opinion on a proposed acquisition, or a board member of either firm being asked to approve the proposed merger or acquisition: Will it add value for my shareholders? Will the acquiring firm gain and the target (acquired) firm lose? Will the acquired firm gain and the acquiring firm lose? Or will value be added for both? Shareholders are anxious about mergers and acquisitions, aware of managements’ inclination toward empire building. Acquiring looks like decisive action; better to be the hunter than the prey. But it can distract from other issues: Making acquisitions in the capital market is easier than dealing with the challenges in the product market. Investment bankers, with the prospect of fancy fees, cheer the process. So any deal requires careful examination and due diligence. Warren Buffett, for one, is skeptical about how diligent CEOs tend to be: In some mergers there are truly major synergies—though often times the acquirer pays too much to obtain them—but at other times the cost and revenue benefits that are projected prove illusory. Of one thing, however, be certain: if a CEO is enthused about a particular foolish acquisition, both his internal staff and his outside advisors will come up with whatever projections are needed to justify the stance. Only in fairy tales are emperors told that they are naked. Warren Buffett, Berkshire Hathaway Annual Report, 1997 Buffett has made many value-adding acquisitions, though he will confess to making some mistakes. Let’s look into it. We do so by looking at the acquisition of The Gillette Company by Procter & Gamble Company (PG) in 2005, a merger of two consumer brand companies favored by value investors. Founded by King Camp Gillette, the inventor of the safety razor in the late 1800s as men’s beards began to go out of fashion, Gillette developed the razor into its modern version and, by the time of the merger, were also marketing a number of consumer products such as Duracell batteries, Braun electric shavers, Oral-B dental products, and Right Guard deodorant. P&G had more than 300 brands to complement these, including Crest toothpaste, Ivory soap, Head and Shoulders shampoo, Bounty paper towels, Pringles snacks, baby care products including Pampers diapers, laundry products, and beauty and feminine care products. This looks like a good combination of similar firms. But let’s check; let’s value by the book. Understanding Value Added in M&A To do so, we first “understand the business” of M&A. How do firms add value (or destroy value) in acquisitions? There are three ways: Synergies. This is the standard explanation: By combining, firms gain efficiencies in marketing, product distribution, production, supply sourcing, or management. But beware of talk of “synergies,” a word bandied about without much specificity. Require the offered synergies to be spelled out: Where will the efficiencies be achieved? In marketing? In production? An investor valuing by the book prepares pro forma financial statements for the proposed combined firm to test if the synergies can be realized, and then converts those statements to a valuation (which we will do). The P&G–Gillette merger was championed as a merger of brand companies with efficiencies in gaining shelf space in retail stores and marketing, but was that a value-added proposition? And there is another question: How will the value of these synergies (if they exist) be divided between the two firms? Buying the target cheap. As with any investment, the acquirer adds value by buying a company underpriced by the market; shareholders of the acquiring company can buy the cheap shares on personal account (if traded) but can also benefit if their firm buys those cheap shares on their behalf. Correspondingly, the acquirer loses and the target gains if the acquirer buys an overpriced firm. This requires both parties to evaluate the target as a going concern, by the book. Using the acquirer’s shares as currency. By using its overpriced shares to buy the target, the acquiring shareholders gain, the target shareholders lose. This is just the point (made in chapter 5 ) that shareholders gain if a firm issues shares at greater than fair value. Target shareholders beware if the acquirer makes a share offer rather than a cash offer. On the other hand, if the acquirer issues underpriced shares in the acquisition, the target shareholders gain and the acquiring shareholders lose. See the next vignette. A Vignette The AOL Acquisition of Time Warner In 2000, AOL (America Online Inc.) and Time Warner Inc. merged in what was celebrated as a synergistic melding of content (Time Warner) and distribution (AOL). Time Warner was the home of Warner movie and television production, HBO, CNN, TBS, Time Warner Cable, and Time Magazine, while AOL was a dial-up service for internet access with 30 million subscribers. An era of new media was on the horizon, it was said. Gerald M. Levin, CEO of Time Warner, waxed eloquent according to the New York Times : The internet had begun to “create unprecedent and instantaneous access to every form of media and to unleash immense possibilities for economic growth, human understanding and creative expression.” The combined company, AOL Time Warner, was valued at $350 billion, with AOL shareholders owning 55 percent and Time Warner shareholders owning 45 percent. AOL was the acquiring firm, offering $182 million in stock. The combination occurred at the height of the “internet bubble” with AOL’s shares trading in the sky so, sensibly, it brought its overpriced stock to the deal. The proffered synergies were not realized. In 2002, the firm reported a quarterly loss of $54 billion on a massive write-off of goodwill from the acquisition. The merger was widely judged as a “disaster.” It was indeed a disaster for Time Warner shareholders. But was it a disaster for AOL shareholders? No: They used their overpriced shares as currency to acquire the valuable assets in Time Warner. Those overpriced shares would have collapsed anyway as the internet bubble subsequently burst. And AOL as a stand-alone enterprise was in doubt as the internet developed and other companies entered. (AOL was spun off in 2009 at a fraction of its 2000 price and eventually sold to Verizon Communications Inc. for $4.4 billion, 2.3 percent of the value shareholders acquired in the combination.) For a blow-by-blow on this episode, see https://www.nytimes.com/2010/01/11/business/media/11merger.html Understanding the Terms of the Deal Here is the structure of the Gillette acquisition by Procter & Gamble announced in January 2005 and consummated in October of that year: Gillette shareholders receive PG stock, no cash. Gillette shareholders surrender their shares with an exchange ratio of 0.975 PG shares for each Gillette share. The terms are ex-dividend to PG shareholders; that is, PG shareholder receive all dividends paid by P&G up to the consummation of the deal. The price paid to Gillette shareholders is $53.94 per share, based on the price of PG shares at the time of the agreement. This is an 18 percent premium over the price of Gillette shares at the time. 999 million shares were issued by Procter & Gamble, resulting in 3,976 million shares outstanding for the merged company. The Accounting Quality Analysis Review the accounting for M&A in chapter 6 : The purchase price is allocated to the fair value of tangible net assets acquired and the fair value of definite- and indefinite-lived intangible assets acquired, with the plug going to goodwill. You see the effect in the Procter & Gamble balance sheet in table 11.4 . All assets and liabilities increased from 2005 to 2006, due to the inclusion of Gillette’s net assets restated to fair value, but the big increases are in intangible assets and goodwill, resulting in a 168 percent increase in net operating assets. Table 11.4 Reformulated Balance Sheets for Procter & Gamble Company (PG), 2005–2008 Procter & Gamble (PG) Reformulated Balance Sheets 2008 2007 2006 2005 Operating Assets: Operating cash 120 120 120 120 Accounts receivable 6,761 6,629 5,725 4,165 Inventories 6,416 6,819 6,291 5,006 Deferred income taxes 2,012 1,727 1,611 1,081 Prepaid expenses and other 3,785 3,300 2,876 1,924 Property, Plant and Equipment 38,086 34,721 31,881 26,325 Accumulated depreciation (17,446) (15,181) (13,111) (11,993) Goodwill 59,767 56,552 55,306 19,816 Other intangibles 34,233 33,626 33,721 4,347 Other assets 4,837 4,265 3,564 2,703 140,571 132,578 127,989 53,494 Operating Liabilities: Accounts payable 6,775 5,710 4,910 3,802 Accrued liabilities 10,154 9,586 9,587 7,531 Taxes payable 945 3,382 3,360 2,265 Deferred taxes 11,805 12,015 12,354 1,896 Other liabilities 8,154 5,147 4,472 3,230 37,833 35,840 34,685 18,724 Net Operating Assets (NOA) 102 738 96 73 8 93 306 34,770 Financial Obligations: Debt due in one year 13,084 12,039 2,128 11,441 Long-term debt 23,581 23,375 35,976 12,887 Less ESOP reserve (1,325) (1,308) (1,288) (1,259) Preferred stock 1,366 1,406 1,451 1,483 36,706 35,512 38,267 24,552 Financial Assets: Cash equivalents 3,193 5,234 6,573 6,269 Investment securities 228 202 1,133 1,764 3,421 5,436 7,706 8,033 Net Financial Obligations 33,285 30,076 30,561 16,519 Common Equity 69,453 66,662 62,745 18,251 The main quality issue is the allocation of the purchase price to indefinite-lived intangibles and goodwill. Tangible assets and definite-lived intangibles must be depreciated or amortized against future earnings, making the acquisition look less profitable. Indefinite-lived intangibles and goodwill are not amortized but rather are impaired if their carrying value is subsequently deemed greater than their carrying value (as with the impairment for AOL Time Warner in the vignette). So, reporting incentives are to allocate more of the purchase price to the non-amortizable accounts. Indeed, research has shown that an overallocation to goodwill and indefinite-lived intangibles results in subsequent impairments. So, inspect the allocations. Overallocation is hard to assess, but at least be aware of the possibility of future impairments. A pro forma analysis like the one below gives an indication of the success of a merger. If that analysis indicates value lost, expect a future impairment in recognition that purchase price was too high. (As it happened, Procter & Gamble took a $1.3 billion impairment charge on the Gillette acquisition in 2023.) Steps for Evaluating an Acquisition The key point in evaluating an acquisition: Shareholders of both firms are buying a share in future balance sheets and income statements of the consolidated company. Calculate that value and then divide it between the shareholders of the acquiring and acquired firms according to the terms of the deal. This is done in three steps: Estimate the value of the combined company based on pro forma balance sheets and projected income statements after the combination. Calculate the per-share value of combination on the new shares outstanding. Compare value received with shares in the combination with value given up: value added for both acquiring and target shareholders Step 1 The timeline of events gives the dates at which the combined balance sheet and income statements are reported. PG has a June 30 fiscal year: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn08.png] Table 11.4 gives the first reformulated consolidated balance sheet for year ending June 30, 2006 and for subsequent years. Your authors did not develop pro forma income statements at the time, so table 11.5 gives actual sales and operating income for the same years and compares the operating income to net operating assets in an RNOA calculation. Treat these actual numbers as those one would have with perfect foresight. Using these numbers has the advantage of showing actual outcomes to the acquisition. Table 11.5 Sales, Operating Income, and Return on Net Operating Assets for Procter & Gamble Company (PG), 2005–2008 2008 2007 2006 2005 Sales 79,257 76,476 68,222 56,741 Operating income (after tax) 11,999 10,584 9,095 8,980 NOA (beginning of year) 96,738 93,306 78,672 * 32,459 RNOA 12.40% 11.34% 11.56% 27.67% Residual OI (7.0%) 5,227 4,053 3,588 6,363 * Combination was at beginning of 2 nd quarter of 2006, so NOA is a weighted average of 2006 and 2005 NOA These are actual outcomes. In evaluating an acquisition, one would model expected outcomes based on expected synergies etc. That is what should be submitted to the respective Boards. Oh no! In table 11.5 , profitability as measured by RNOA declined to 11.56 percent in 2006 from 27.67 percent in the year before the acquisition (2005), and it never recovered! A disaster! Well, no. Return to chapter 6 which explained that profitability is not a measure of value added. Residual income is the value-added measure. But, oh no! Residual operating income (calculated with rFirm = 7 percent) declined from $6,363 million in 2005 to $3,588 million in 2006. A disaster! Well, no. Valuation by the book says to use both income statements and balance sheets in assessing value added; value added is value added to the balance sheet. The Value Investing in Action box below, similar to one in chapter 6 , demonstrates that value is not lost in this acquisition. By 2008, the value is $270,971 million: Value added. Following the discipline of using both income statements and balance sheets, the next action box completes step 1 with the (pretend) forecasts of balance sheet and income statement numbers from 2006–2008. The combined value is $221,820 million. One might adjust this valuation for any change in the risk and discount rate resulting from the combination. Value Investing in Action Value of The Procter & Gamble Company Before and After Acquisition Balance sheets before and after the acquisition (in $millions): BEFORE AFTER Net operating assets 34,770 93,306 Net debt 16,519 30,561 Equity 18,251 62,745 Operating income, RNOA, and ReOI before and after the acquisition: BEFORE, 2005 AFTER, 2006 Operating income 8,980 10,730 RNOA (on beginning NOA) 25.8% 11.6% Residual operating income (7.0%) 6,363 3,588 Enterprise valuation before acquisition (with a 4% GDP growth rate): [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn09.png] Enterprise valuation after acquisition (with a 4% GDP growth rate): [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn10.png] Residual income is lower on a higher book value. But adding the higher book value in the valuation compensates. The big drops in RNOA and ReOI do not indicate a big loss of value. The 2005 and 2006 valuations are not quite the same but can be explained by integration costs for the merger in 2006. The value in 2008 is: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn11.png] Always use both balance sheets and income statements in valuation . Value Investing in Action Step 1 for Evaluating the P&G and Gillette Merger Valuation of the combined firm: The combined value starts with the initial consolidated balance sheet from the combination in October 2005, then adds value from subsequent pro forma consolidated income: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn12.png] A growth rate at the GDP growth rate of 4 percent is used, but that can be modified. Step 2 The second step calculates the per-share value of the combination for the 3,976 million shares outstanding after the combination: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn13.png] Step 3 This step compares the value received in the combined firm to value given up to assess value added, for both the acquiring and acquired shareholders. Value received by Gillette shareholders = $53.01 Gillette shareholders received shares worth $55.79 at consummation in October 2005. The present value at January 2005 (with a discount rate of 7% for 9 months) [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn14.png] Value given up by Gillette shareholders per share (before 18% premium offer): [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn15.png] Value added for Gillette shareholders = $6.13 per share = $53.01 − $46.88. Value received by P&G shareholders, ex-dividend = $53.01 But P&G received the dividend between agreement and consummation. So, Value received by P&G shareholders, cum-dividend = $53.01 + 0.87 = $53.88 Value given up by P&G shareholders = Price per share before deal = $53.94 Value added for P&G shareholders = −$0.06 per share = $53.88 − $53.94. There is another way to challenge the value in the merger. That follows the challenge to the market price in chapter 4 by calculating the implied growth rate in the acquisition price with the pro forma for the consolidated firm: [IMAGE: ../images/ch11eqn16.png] Setting the total price for the combined firm to $53.94 × 3,976 million shares = $214,465 million, g = 3.85% If an investor sees a growth rate greater than 3.85 percent from the merger, it adds value. That, of course leaves the division of value between the acquiring and acquired shareholders open. Evaluating the Success of an Acquisition This section opened with an Economist report giving metrics for evaluating the success of a merger (after the fact). Our analysis has cast some doubt on these metrics. Here, again, are the metrics offered as indicators of a failed acquisition, with retorts: Shares of acquiring firms underperformed the market and industry average. Retort: A declining share price can be due to poor performance but, if the firm has used its overpriced shares in the acquisition, shares would have declined anyway as they reverted to value. A share price decline post-acquisition may just be that. The relevant metric is the share price decline relative to what it would have been without the acquisition: A successful acquisition reduces the decline. Profitability fell. Retort: It must fall, as we have seen with RNOA. Higher income on a yet higher base means a lower RNOA. Profitability is not a measure of value added. Net profit margins fell. Retort: This can be telling, but not a sufficient statistic. Revenue and asset turnovers come into the picture. Profit margins post acquisition are a weighted average of those of the two firms plus synergistic income. The acquired firm’s profit margin can be low, affecting this average, but with high asset turnovers and revenue growth potential. EPS grew more slowly. Retort: Share issues can decrease EPS ( chapter 5 ) so, if the acquisition was with shares, EPS can decrease even if value is added. On the other hand, a cash acquisition increases net debt and leverage, increasing EPS if the leverage is favorable. Debt increased. Retort: Debt increases if a firm borrows to finance a cash acquisition, but that has nothing to do with value added in operations. (It does increase leverage risk, however.) Interest expense increased. Retort: Interest expense increases with borrowing to finance a cash acquisition. If an acquirer uses its own cash for the acquisition, interest expense might not increase, but net interest expense will. Value Investing Principles Generating a float (negative net operating assets) adds value, but at a cost. When facing risk, as with an insurance company, be conservative: Build in a margin of safety. Value added in a merger involves both balance sheets and income statements. Building a Value Investing Engine If you are interested in investing in insurance companies, build in the template that follows the analysis of Markel Corporation in this chapter. The Exercise Book There are exercises in the book involving insurance companies and acquisitions. The Case Book Case 18: Buying A P&C Insurer: Markel Corporation Buying with a Margin of Safety Buying Markel Corporation (MKL) at $1,476 per share in the first quarter of 2022 would have caused some short-term pain: The share price dropped to $1,178 by August of that year, a 20.2 percent decline. In its second quarter report, for the half year ending June 30, 2022, the fair value of Markel’s investment portfolio had dropped from $23,411 million to $21,169 million. Redo the analysis in this chapter with the value of the investment portfolio set to $21,169 million. Would you have bought MKL in the first quarter of 2022 at $1,476 per share with this value for the investment portfolio, a number you estimate would give you a margin of safety? In answering this question, you might conduct a sensitivity analysis with the valuation of Markel Ventures, again building in a margin of safety. Would you buy Markel at a price of $1,178 per share? Case 19: An All-Cash Acquisition Tapestry and Capri: A Luxury Powerhouse? In August 2023, the U.S. luxury house, Tapestry, Inc. (TPR) announced its acquisition of Capri Holdings Limited (CPRI) for $8.5 billion, including Capri’s debt. Tapestry is home to the Coach, Kate Spade, and Stuart Weitzman brands; Capri’s brands included Michael Kors, Versace, and Jimmy Choo. The $57 per share cash offer was a 64.7 percent premium to Capri’s stock price of $34.61 on the day of the announcement. The 40 percent drop in Capri’s stock price in 2023 may have made the acquisition more attractive. The merger was due to be consummated in April 2024, but the U.S. Federal Trade Commission (FTC) sought to deny the deal on anti-trust grounds. The merger of two luxury brands was hailed as a “transformational deal,” creating a U.S. “powerhouse” with combined revenues of $12 billion across 75 countries to match the large European luxury houses such as LVMH, Kering, and Hermès in the $200+ billions global market for handbags, accessories, footwear, and apparel. Synergies of $200 million in cost reductions within three years were promised, building on Tapestry’s customer engagement platform and data analytics. Double-digit EPS accretion and a “compelling” return on invested capital was indicated. Tapestry approved a 17 percent increase in dividends per share to $1.40. There was some skepticism, however. With an all-cash offer, Tapestry was going into debt, raising its debt-to-EBITDA ratio to 4.5 from 1.0. Michael Kors, a not-so-high-end brand, comprised 70 percent of its revenue. Versace, the only true high-end brand in Capri’s portfolio, provided only 20 percent of its revenue. But perhaps this was a synergistic merger of mid-range brands. It is to be noted that Tapestry does not have a great M&A record. It acquired Kate Spade just before the brand fell out of favor and has had difficulty expanding the Stuart Weitzman brand. Tapestry’s shares dropped 16.5 percent on announcement of the acquisition agreement. That suggests value lost. Carry out an analysis to assess the value added (or otherwise) for shareholders of both companies. That may involve evaluating scenarios for the future that justify the price paid and received. Case 20: Coke Light Adding Value by Restructuring the Balance Sheet? We have seen how companies like Apple and property-casualty insurers generate value in the way they structure their balance sheets. Increasingly, firms that traditionally have been asset heavy are going light. The Coca-Cola Company (KO) in 2017 is a recent example. A long-time favorite of value investors (that brand is a moat, they say), its performance had raised some doubts. There had been little change in the stock price in the four years up to 2017, leaving shareholders only the dividend yield of 3 percent as their return. Sales and profit margins had been flat and asset turnovers were declining. While the prospect of consumers moving to more “healthy” beverage alternatives is an issue, Coke apparently also sees enhanced value in going light in the balance sheet. Will that restructuring add value? Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 12 Investing by the Book Chapter 12 Investing by the Book Investing in Style I nvestment funds market themselves as following a particular style. Indeed, funds registered with the Securities and Exchange Commission must convey their investing style in their offering documents and any departure from that style (style creep) brings sanctions from that regulator. In this chapter, we again compare the common value versus growth and size investing styles in the Morningstar style box. In chapter 2 , these styles were viewed as being opaque: The investor does not know what she is getting into. Therein lies a trap. What are the underlying fundamentals and what is the risk that they convey? Can an understanding of these fundamentals protect the investor from falling into a trap? We bring the analysis in this book to answer these questions. Investing by the Book: Value versus Growth Investing and the Value Trap We saw in chapter 2 that so-called value investing in firms with low price-to-book value (P/B), low price-to-earnings ratio (P/E), and other price multiples has historically earned higher returns than investing in so-called growth firms with high multiples. The panel A in table 12.1 expands on this. It reports returns to a strategy trolled many times by investors: trading on both P/E and P/B. This strategy obeys the value-investing dictum we have stressed: Both balance sheets (book value) and income statements (earnings) must be used in valuation. The returns across the top row of the first panel are the spread from investing in five portfolios based on earnings yield (E/P). Then, for each of these E/P portfolios, the returns (down columns) are for five portfolios from adding book-to-price (B/P) to the strategy. Returns are higher for higher E/P (across rows) and higher B/P (down columns). The difference in buying high E/P and high B/P versus low E/P and low B/P, across the diagonal in the panel, is large: 27.1 percent − 1.9 percent = 25.2 percent per year. Hey, this looks like easy money! A fifth grader could do this! Why buy this book? Beware . Table 12.1 Annual returns to investing in value versus growth, 1963–2015 Each year, firms are formed into five portfolios based on a ranking on their trailing E/P. Then, within each E/P portfolio, firms are formed into five portfolios based on their B/P. The coverage is all U.S.-listed firms except financial firms and those with share prices less than 20 cents. Loss firms with negative E/P are included but negative book value firms are excluded. The returns for these portfolios are the average from implementing the strategy each year. The returns are increasing in E/P (decreasing in P/E) and increasing in B/P (decreasing in P/B). H-L is high minus low. t-statistics indicate statistical significance. A. Penman and F. Reggiani, Fundamentals of Value vs. Growth Investing and an Explanation for the Value Trap. Financial Analysts Journal 74, no. 4 (December 2018): 102–19. However, the two other panels of the table tell us that this can flip. That is the value trap; the strategy turns against the investor. Indeed, we saw in chapter 2 , that this strategy has not performed well in years after 2006. Our risk-reward economics tells us that we cannot earn the returns in the first panel without taking on risk, perhaps a point that a fifth grader has not yet appreciated. A spread of 25.2 percent seems too large: Can so much money be left on the table with a simple scheme that a fifth grader is capable of understanding? But what explains the risk? Can our value investing explain it? E/P and B/P are Accounting Phenomena Given price, E/P and B/P are accounting phenomena; they depend on how earnings and book value are measured. Is there something about the accounting that would tell us that E/P and B/P indicate risk and warn us that we are falling into a trap? The Risk in E/P In chapter 2 , we posed a simple E/P model to demonstrate how E/P embeds risk: [IMAGE: ../images/ch12eqn01.png] The r here tells us that the price in E/P (or P/E) has a discount for risk. So a higher E/P might be associated with higher risk and return. But growth is also involved, and growth is risky, so r can increase with higher expected growth; if so, r increases with g with no effect on E/P because r − g remains the same. So, a given E/P in table 12.1 could be one with high growth and risk, or low growth and risk. The investor trading on E/P must sort out the extent to which she is buying risky growth. We will do so. The Risk in B/P Chapter 2 also explained why the price in B/P (and P/B) has a discount for risk, and our valuation platform incorporates it explicitly: P/B is based on expected ROE in the future but, the higher the risk to that ROE, the more the price and P/B is discounted. The effect of the accounting in conveying this risk is illustrated as follows. Consider a money market fund investing in “risk-free” U.S. government bonds. Consider also a risky fund investing in equities. What is the B/P of these two funds? Well, for both, B/P = 1.0 because they apply a particular form of accounting, fair value accounting, where the book value of the fund, net asset value (NAV), is equal to the price of the securities in the fund. So B/P cannot indicate the different risk in these funds. Now consider the firm that applies historical cost accounting—what we might call earnings deferral accounting—rather than fair value accounting. Here, B/P differs from 1.0 and is typically less than 1.0, with a historical median of about 0.6 (a P/B of 1.7). The accounting explains why: Under the realization principle, value is not added to the balance sheet until risk is resolved with realized earnings; the firm must have customers committed and able to pay before earnings are booked. Until that point, expected earnings to be added to book value are at risk of not being realized, and price in the B/P ratio discounts for this risk, yielding a higher B/P. E/P and B/P Working Together E/P and B/P are not two separate measures to evaluate stocks. Rather, they are generated jointly in a double-entry accounting system: The calculation of earnings affects the balance sheet and vice versa. Thus they should be employed jointly in an investment strategy, capturing the complementary information from the double-entry system. A value versus growth investing strategy optimizes by employing both E/P and B/P, as table 12.1 indicates. We have seen that E/P is determined by risk and growth: E/P = r − g . As growth is risky, r can increase with g , leaving r − g (and the E/P ratio) unchanged. Thus the question: In buying a given E/P, are we buying high growth with high risk or are we buying low growth with low risk? As B/P and E/P are jointly determined by double entry, can B/P sort this out? If the returns down columns in the first panel of table 12.1 are returns to risk, then the B/P is sorting on the risk: B/P is telling us whether the given E/P is high risk or low risk. Is there something in the accounting for E/P and B/P that is telling us that this is so? The answer is: Yes. The following relationship connects B/P to E/P and introduces return on equity (ROE) that combines the bottom-line earnings on the income statement with the bottom-line book value in the double-entry balance sheet: [IMAGE: ../images/ch12eqn02.png] Setting the forward E/P ratio, [IMAGE: ../images/ch12eqn03.png] , and recognizing that [IMAGE: ../images/ch12eqn04.png] , [IMAGE: ../images/ch12eqn05.png] Thus, For a given E/P = r − g , a ranking on B/P is an inverse ranking on ROE . That is, B/P is higher if ROE is lower, holding r − g constant. For a given E/P = r − g , a higher B/P must be associated with higher g if a lower ROE is associated with higher g . That is, a lower ROE means a higher B/P so, if a lower ROE means a higher g , then a higher g also means a higher B/P. For a given E/P = r − g , a higher B/P must indicate a higher r if a higher B/P is associated with higher g. That is, if r increases with g such that r − g does not change, then B/P increases with r because a lower ROE is associated with the higher r and g . So, let’s see if these relationships play out in the data. Point 1 does not have be tested since it is just math. Nevertheless, table 12.2 confirms: For a given positive E/P a ranking on B/P (down columns) is a ranking on ROE. So a high B/P is a low ROE and vice versa, though in the opposite direction for negative E/P firms (low E/P) with the negative sign operating. Do these statements pan out in the data? Table 12.3 gives a nod to point 2: For a given E/P, a lower ROE (and thus a higher B/P) indicates higher future earnings growth (down columns). (Loss firms in the lowest E/P column work differently with considerable transitory, non-sustainable losses.) The growth rates are for two years ahead, but one sees similar patterns for one and three years ahead (with a consideration of survival rates). This seems strange, for people often say that a higher ROE, not a lower ROE, begets earnings growth. An understanding of the accounting explains it, as we will see. Table 12.4 says: Yes. In the first panel, the standard deviation of growth rates increases with B/P (and decreases with ROE) for all E/P portfolios: Higher risk. The standard deviations over portfolios is correlated with the standard deviation of the stock returns for the portfolios, as indicated at the bottom of the panel. The interdecile ranges (the 90th percentile minus the 10th percentile of outcomes) in the second panel measure the extent to which growth falls in the tails of the outcome distribution. With high B/P, the investor is more likely to get either very low or very high earnings growth: Higher downside risk compensated with upside potential. The final panel of table 12.4 reports the fundamental earnings betas introduced in chapter 8 where risk is discussed: The earnings of high B/P stocks are more sensitive to market-wide earnings affected by the economy’s ups and downs: Higher systematic risk. In short, point 3 is validated: B/P indicates the risk in an E/P ratio, distinguishing an E/P = r − g with high r from an E/P with low r due to the risk to growth. That is because, for a given E/P in each column of the tables, ROE indicates growth and a lower ROE means a higher B/P. And, as growth is risky, a lower ROE (and thus a higher B/P) means higher risk and a higher required return ( r ). The numbers indicate that returns to value versus growth in table 12.1 are due, in part at least, to risk. Beware . But can that risk be understood in advance of taking positions in stocks? As it is ROE that indicates growth at risk, that is the number to look at. Accounting for ROE and Risk in Value versus Growth Investing At this point, it might be good to hark back to the Accounting and Risk section in chapter 8 . That laid out two accounting principles for measuring earnings: the realization principle and conservative accounting for investment. Both affect ROE via both its numerator and denominator. For a given price, current earnings, the numerator of ROE, is lower when the earnings expected in the price are still at risk; they are deferred to the future until realized. Higher future unrealized earnings on lower current earnings in ROE are risky earnings growth. Conservative accounting for investment, by expensing that investment, further reduces current earnings, but yields higher expected future earnings (growth), not only from the investment but also because those earnings will not be reduced by depreciation or amortization in the future—the investment has already been expensed. As with the realization principle, this accounting is applied because of risk: The expected earnings growth is risky earnings growth. Accordingly, ROE is lower via its numerator when this accounting is applied. That is the low ROE (high B/P) associated with risky earnings growth and higher returns in the tables above. The vignettes on the next page illustrate. Vignettes ROE Informs About Risk Twitter, Inc. (TWTR) Twitter, Inc. went to IPO in November 2013, closing on its first trading day priced at 26 times estimated 2014 sales, a price embedding significant growth expectations. The firm was reporting losses (and a negative ROE) due largely to the expensing of research and development (R&D), advertising that amounted to 80 percent of revenue. These expenditures were made to generate revenue and earnings growth, but there was uncertainty about whether the expected revenues and earnings would be realized. In 2018, profitability continued to be low with the expensing of investments: ROE = 0.3 percent. In December 2021, ROE(ttm) = −2.4 percent. This is a risky company. Will the risky investments ever pay off or will Twitter’s stock price drop because the promised earnings growth in the price is not realized? (The company went private in 2022.) Facebook, Inc. (FB) now Meta Platforms, Inc. (META) Facebook, Inc. traded in 2013 with significant growth prospects built into its market price. However, the firm was reporting an ROE of only 4 percent, due to the expensing of development costs to foster the growth. Should that growth be realized, Facebook will have significant earnings growth, not only from the revenues but because only variable costs will have to be covered: most fixed costs have already been expensed. The low ROE due to the expensing of these investments indicates potential earnings growth, but growth that is uncertain. Fast forward to December 2021: Facebook has had considerable success in generating those earnings with ROE(ttm) at 32.1 percent. That success was accompanied by high stock returns that always accompany realized earnings. But it could have gone the other way. With ROE at 35.1 percent, the firm was significantly lower risk than in 2013. However, with R&D and marketing at 43 percent of revenue in 2022 with the venture into the metaverse, risk increased. The stock dropped 65 percent with the risk to this venture in 2022. Amazon, Inc. (AMZN) Amazon.com , Inc. regularly reported losses from its formation in 1994 up to 2014. These losses were on continually rising sales. The losses were attributed to “spending on technology and content, such as video streaming and grocery delivery to mobile devices” and the firm’s “willingness to win customers by losing money,” says the firm. Add expenditure on software to develop e-commerce platforms and cloud services, and the venture into drone development. These investments were being expensed directly to the income statement, yielding a negative ROE. While high expectations were built into the share price, the results of these investments are uncertain; added customers had yet to be realized. Perhaps drones will deliver toothpaste through your bathroom window in the future, but don’t wait for this to clean your teeth. Amazon is now realizing revenue and is profitable. In December 2021 ROE(ttm) was 25.8 percent. But it still has considerable expensed investments: $88.6 billion in technology development and marketing annually. It is a firm that is potentially more profitable, but that profitability is at risk. Indeed, 2022 saw a shock to that growth with a reported loss with $115.5 billion expenditure on technology development and marketing, up 30.4 percent from 2021, while sale revenue increased only 9.6 percent. Starbucks Corporation (SBUX) During the 1990s, Starbucks was a hot stock. With a P/E of 51 in 1999, considerable growth expectations were built into its market price. However, the firm was reporting a book rate of return on its operations of only 9 percent. Starbucks was expanding stores aggressively, expensing store-opening expenses, advertising, employee training, and its development of coffee supply chains. This expensing depressed the book return (ROE), an indication that the growth strategy was risky. As it happened, the strategy paid off with the book rate of return rising to 52 percent by 2018. But the risky strategy could have gone the other way. The low book return projected growth, but risky growth. The Coca-Cola Company (KO) Coca Cola had a ROE(ttm) of 41.0 percent in 2022, due largely to a brand investment that is omitted from the balance sheet but one that actually delivers sales and earnings in the numerator of the ROE. This is a low-risk ROE, for the risk taken with the brand building investment has been resolved. Coke had a low market beta of 0.6. Microsoft Corporation (MSFT) For its 2022 fiscal year, Microsoft reported an RNOA of 88.8 percent. Considerable investment in R&D and marketing had paid off. With these investments not on the balance sheet but with the realized earnings from the investments flowing through the income statement, the RNOA was very high: Risk resolved. But the firm was continuing to invest in R&D into new ventures: Risk added. In these vignettes, ROE increases as earnings are realized. This is, of course, is partly due to those earnings coming into the numerator of ROE. But conservative accounting for investment also explains a high ROE because the investments that generated the earnings are not on the balance sheet and thus not in the denominator. Thus, a high ROE indicates lower risk: Earnings realized from very risky (expensed) investments is risk resolved; the firm took on risk and kept the investment off the balance sheet, but it paid off yielding a handsome ROE. The firms with low ROE in table 12.2 are high risk, and those with higher ROE are lower risk. A start-up biotech with little revenue as yet but a lot of R&D has a low ROE and is high risk. A mature pharmaceutical firm where the R&D has paid off is lower risk. It has a high ROE because the R&D investment is not on the balance sheet. In short, for a given price in E/P and B/P, ROE indicates risk to both value and growth if the ROE is determined by the accounting. In the investment business, there is a tendency to put labels on things, even if the phenomena are not well-understood, and the very labels “value” and “growth” are suspect. Indeed, we have seen here that, while a low B/P is said to be growth, it is high B/P (value) that forecasts growth. That is risky growth, so a trader buying into a high B/P can be buying into risk. There is the value trap. Investing by the book protects you. We have focused on ROE here because the investing style refers to E/P and B/P. But the same analysis applies to RNOA. Indeed, more so, for that is where the relevant accounting is applied. ROE is affected by leverage, as chapter 5 explains, and leverage risk increases ROE rather than reducing it. Nothing here says that all the return to value versus growth is due to risk: Some of the returns in table 12.1 could be due to mispricing. But the investor pursuing those returns is warned to tread carefully. The question of what part of the returns is for risk and what part is gravy is open. Value investing of the previous chapters will guide you. A retort would go as follows: The spread of returns in the first panel in table 12.1 is just too large to be reward for risk; they must be returns to mispricing that even a fifth grader can pick up on. Well, these are returns to U.S. firms in a very successful economy. The period from 1963 to 2015 was one of continuing growth (with just a few hiccups) over an extended period rarely before seen. It was “The American Century,” they say. Buying stocks is buying risky earnings growth, so buying U.S. stocks in the 1960s was a bet on growth. That growth materialized over the next 60 years; the risk on buying growth paid off, and the large returns in the table are returns to that bet on risk. It could have been otherwise. Will the next century be the American century? Maybe the Chinese century? Well, there is potential growth there, but we don’t want to upset our Chinese readership! It must be seen as risky growth. It’s all about buying risky earnings growth. Investing by the Book: Buying Firm Size Value versus growth investing is combined with investing by firm size in the Morningstar style box. Small-cap investment funds target small firms because, historically, small firms have outperformed large firms. In 2022, when the value-weighted return for the S&P 500 was −18.11 percent, the return for the equally weighted version of the index (weighted more towards small firms) was nearly 7 percent higher according to S&P Global research. However, at times, that has flipped, as in 2020 and 2021. Therein lies a trap for size-focused investors. We show here that small firms outperform large firms because they typically have higher expected earnings growth, but that growth is at risk and risk yields higher returns on average: The small-firm investor is buying risk, and risk can result in lower returns. That’s a trap. However, large-cap firms sometimes outperform small firms when those large firms have higher growth at risk. That’s a further trap. The investor is thus advised to assess growth at risk rather than investing on the amorphous feature of “size.” Payoffs to Firm Size Table 12.5 gives the average returns for portfolios formed each year on size as measured by market capitalization of equity. The returns are negatively related to firm size, with a difference of 19 percent between the average return for the lowest and highest size portfolios. But that difference is associated with risk: The standard deviation of returns, range of returns, the interdecile range, and return kurtosis indicate that small firms are more risky and more likely to deliver returns in the tails of the return distribution. That’s downside risk compensated with upside potential. Small firms also have higher return betas, both up-market and down-market betas, with the latter particularly high. The message: Buy smaller firms for higher expected returns but appreciate that those returns are at risk. Table 12.6 shows that small firms have higher forward earnings growth than large firms, with a difference of 11.0 percent in annual growth rates between the lowest and highest size portfolios. However, the table also shows that the higher growth comes with risk: Buy smaller firms for higher earnings growth but appreciate that the growth is at risk. The risk is manifest both in higher earnings betas (the sensitivity of earnings to market-wide earnings) and in earnings growth betas (the extent to which earnings growth is related to shocks to economy-wide earnings). These betas are higher for small firms in both up-markets and down-markets. These metrics mirror the risk measures for returns in table 12.5 : Growth at risk is returns at risk. Numbers are portfolio means. So, by observing these features in a fundamental analysis, the investor gains an appreciation of the risk he is taking on. Table 12.7 Fundamentals that convey risk to earnings growth and returns Size portfolio Change in revenue to net operating ssets R&D+ advert to revenue SG&A to revenue Return on equity (ROE) 1 (Small) 0.184 0.232 0.297 −0.126 2 0.345 0.239 0.264 −0.059 3 0.420 0.234 0.248 −0.025 4 0.483 0.262 0.237 0.004 5 0.539 0.199 0.218 0.037 6 0.558 0.178 0.210 0.062 7 0.619 0.153 0.199 0.086 8 0.623 0.118 0.191 0.116 9 0.623 0.087 0.181 0.145 10 (Large) 0.587 0.066 0.173 0.180 As in chapter 8 , sales (revenue) realization shows (at least some) risk is resolved and thus indicates lower risk; larger firms in the table are associated with higher revenue realization. That makes sense: Large firms have become larger by getting customers and the consequent revenues, while small firms are those yet to become large (or fail!) because they have not yet gained those customers. Small firms promise revenue, but it is yet to be realized, still at risk. Conservative accounting expenses particularly risky investment. That investment conveys more earnings (growth), but growth that is more likely not to be realized. In the table, the categories of R&D, advertising, and SG&A (selling, general, and administrative expenses, where some of this risky investment is contained) is higher for smaller firms. ROE is lower for small firms in table 12.7 . As in the discussion of value versus growth investing, a lower ROE conveys risk when it is determined by accounting for risk that is indicated in the other columns in the table. In short, these tables show that firm size, so commonly seen as an investing style, is fundamentally an indicator of earnings growth at risk; buying firms by size is buying risk and the underlying fundamentals inform about this risk. Larger firms were once small firms where the risk paid off, growing them into large firms but making them less risky. The (small) start-ups and (large) technology firms illustrate this. In the vignette we saw earlier in this chapter, Apple, Microsoft, Cisco, Meta, and Amazon were once small firms that became successful by realizing revenues and profits. The Large Firm Size Trap However, those large firms can continue to make risky investments with the growth they generate at risk. Think of Amazon with a market capitalization over $1 trillion in January 2023, and with $115.5 billion in technology development expenditures and advertising and promotion in its 2022 income statement. That is an increase of 30.3 percent from $88.6 billion in 2021, while revenue was up only 9.6 percent. This is a large firm with growth at risk. So one must be careful in buying large firms as a refuge from risk. Indeed, the relationship between size and risk flips at times with large firms sometimes those with growth at risk. An investor buying large firms deemed to be less risky might be caught in a trap. As well as Amazon, the large technology and social media firms of the 2010’s were priced with high earnings expectations, but those earnings were at risk: Netflix, Twitter, Meta, Microsoft, Apple. The large declines in their stock prices in 2022 warned of this. Netflix’s stock price was down 48.7 percent in 2022 from its peak in 2021, Amazon down 41.2 percent, Meta Platforms down 60.7 percent, and Microsoft down 26.3 percent. These drawdowns were larger than the 15.9 percent for the Russell 2000 index of small firms. In the period 1963–2020, a small-firm return premium was earned from 1973 to 1977, 1978 to 1982, 1998 to 2002, 2008 to 2012, and 2018 to 2020, with small firms returning an average annual return of 41.1 percent versus 21.3 percent for large firms. In the periods from 1988 to 1992, 1993 to 1997, and 2003 to 2007, there was little difference between the returns to small versus large firms. However, from 1968 to 1972, 1983 to 1987, and 2013 to 2017, the returns to large-firm investing were higher than those for small firms. Table 12.8 documents the returns during each of these latter periods, but for each firm size category it also gives the mean expected returns conveyed by fundamental information that conveys growth at risk. That information is aggregated in the summary measure expected return (ER), the fundamental risk measure introduced in chapter 8 . If the higher returns to large firms in these periods are a reward for risk, the table shows that ER gave an advance indication of this risk and the higher compensation returns, warning of the large-firm size trap. The years 1968 to 1972 were down years (as indicated by negative returns for those years for all size groups) where higher risk firms should have lower returns, The higher ER for small firms with the worst performance so indicates. The period from 1983 to 1987 had up years; ER indicates the higher returns received. The numbers for 2013 to 2017 are more ambiguous, but the returns for ER within size groups indicate that this was a period when return for risk did not pay off. Nevertheless, ER does not signal that the investor should invest in small firms in these years. Table 12.8 The large size trap: Annual returns to investing on size in periods when the size premium flips Actual returns % Expected returns, ER % Period Indicator Small Medium Large Small Medium Large 1968–1972 −8.7 −6.0 −0.6 22.8 22.0 21.4 ER Low −6.7 −5.8 1.1 2 −6.5 −4.3 −1.6 3 −13.5 −2.2 9.3 ER High 5.3 −6.8 1.1 1983–1987 5.0 11.0 16.4 20.2 22.0 23.2 ER Low −4.8 −3.0 6.0 2 −1.4 9.6 11.4 3 1.2 12.4 14.3 ER High 21.8 19.9 22.0 2013–2017 4.8 8.1 9.3 12.0 11.7 11.3 ER Low −2.9 7.8 9.9 2 8.2 8.2 11.7 3 9.8 7.6 11.0 ER High 0.2 8.5 7.4 The returns in the table from investing in four portfolios based on ER within each size group show that in most cases, ER forecasts actual returns within these groups. Indeed, one sees that ER differentiates returns much more than size does. The message for the investor: Invest on fundamentals that indicate growth at risk, rather than on the opaque size attribute. Value Investing Principles Simple investing styles expose the investor to traps. Discover risk and return with fundamental analysis to avoid these traps. Accounting metrics convey risk to investing. Building a Value Investing Engine There is not much in this chapter to add to your analysis engine. By applying your analysis with the engine, you will avoid the value and size traps. The Exercise Book Exercises in the Exercise Book deal with P/E and P/B ratios and small and large firms. The Case Book There are no cases for this chapter. Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.
