Chapter 13 Keeping a Book on Liquidity and the Analysis of Credit Risk Chapter 13 Keeping a Book on Liquidity and the Analysis of Credit Risk T o this point, we have had little to say about cash flow. Indeed, what we have said is negative: Free cash flow is not a value-added measure to work with. Rather, the focus is on buying earnings and earnings growth. However, firms borrow to lever earnings growth. If all works well, value is added and those earnings generate cash flows to service the debt. But if those cash flows are not realized, the firm will have trouble meeting its debt obligations. With the resulting default, shareholders pass control of the firm to the debtholders, typically losing all. That is the worst case impact of the leverage risk introduced in chapter 5 . Even if cash flows are expected to be realized eventually, debt payments may come due before that point, forcing default and possible loss of that future value. This compounds the duration risk introduced in chapter 8 . So default risk must be evaluated by the shareholder in addition to the going-concern risk in operations. That evaluation must also be made by debtholders, of course, for they might not receive full repayment in default. As with value generation, the analysis of liquidity involves keeping a book. This chapter lays out the design of that book and shows how it is applied to evaluate the risk of default. There are conditions where liquidity is important even for a going concern with little danger of default. In a leveraged buyout where investors borrow large amounts to finance the buyout, they must be sure that the business generates cash in time to meet repayment schedules even if the buyout is successful. So both value generation and liquidity are in focus. And private equity (PE) investors typically redeem out of a PE fund at a promised date. So the fund’s manager must ensure that liquidity is there to meet those calls. The Default Point The default point occurs when the cash available to service debt is less than the cash required to service the debt, whether in the form of interest payments or principal repayments. Cash available to service debt and cash required to service debt are identified by the cash conservation equation, and this equation governs the design of the book on liquidity: Free Cash Flow (FCF) = Cash from Operations (C) − Net Cash Investment (I) = Net Dividends to Shareholders (d) + Payments to Net Debtholders (F) We’ll use simple notation to state this: FCF = d + F C − I = d + F Net dividends, d , are net cash to shareholders; that is, cash dividends + share repurchases − share issues. As net debt = debt obligations − debt assets, C ‒ I = d + F O + F A where F O is cash that services debt obligations and F A is cash invested in debt assets. This accounting equation shows that the net cash flow from the operations (free cash flow, or FCF) is shared between shareholders and net debtholders. It also explains that, if FCF is negative, it must be made up with cash either from shareholders (reducing payout or issuing shares) or from net debtholders (issuing debt or selling debt assets). It is called a conservation equation because it must balance: All cash generated from operations must go to the two claimants; none is left over. As we have seen many times in this book, an equation that must balance is very helpful for designing an accounting book. Rearranging the equation, the default point is where required payments to service debt is less than the cash available to service the debt: F O > C ‒ I − d ‒ F A Debt obligations can be satisfied by positive free cash flow from the business. As Free cash flow = cash from operations − cash investment, cash from operations can be applied to reduce debt, but reducing investment also provides cash for debt service. And, as investment is net of liquidation of investments, that can involve selling off assets. Net dividends to shareholders ( d ) = cash dividends + share repurchases − share issues, so debt obligations can also be met by reductions in dividends and share repurchases or by a new share issue. Obligations can also be serviced by selling off debt assets ( F A ). If the cash from all these sources is less than the cash required to meet debt obligations, the firm is in default. To summarize, the equation tells the firm’s treasurer, the CFO, and the board what their alternatives are for servicing debt: If cash from operations is insufficient: Liquidate debt assets, if any. Reduce dividends or share repurchases. Issue shares. Reduce investment. Sell off assets. If these actions are not taken, the firm is in default, with the bankruptcy administration then taking the action of selling off assets. Some of these sources of cash are less palatable than others. Issuing shares in distress is not desirable; as we have seen, issuing equity at fire-sale prices destroys shareholder value (and there may be no takers). New debt can be issued to satisfy current debt obligations, but again there may not be takers when a firm is in distress. However, as the debt is net debt (that is, debt obligations minus debt assets, as in chapter 5 ), debt assets can be liquidated to service debt obligations. Of course, there is a limit to that; see the nearby vignette. These are the elements that come into the treasurer’s strategy, and which are also the inputs to a book for planning for liquidity to satisfy future debt obligations. A Vignette Cash Burn Increases the Risk of Default With a decline in free cash flow, firms can finance their business by liquidating their debt assets held in cash and short-term investments. But this “cash burn” increases default risk, leaving less cash to satisfy debt obligations. During the COVID pandemic and its aftermath, with cruises curtailed, cruise lines burned cash to keep their business afloat (literally). They also issued a significant amount of debt to replenish the cash. The following tracks the declining liquidity for Carnival Corporation & plc (CCL) and Royal Caribbean Cruises Ltd. (RCL) in 2020 and 2021 against their debt commitments. Bed Bath & Beyond Inc. (BBBY) is added, a retailer in similar circumstances. Debt Assets are cash and cash equivalents plus short-term investments, net of fresh debt issued to replenish cash expended. The generation of liquidity is described by a cash flow statement, and it is this statement which, in pro forma (for the future), the treasurer models to understand her ability to meet debt obligations. Unfortunately, the GAAP and IFRS cash flow statements are not up to the task, so must be reformulated. Keeping a Book on Liquidity Table 13.1 presents the cash flow statement for Bed Bath & Beyond Inc. (BBBY), one of the firms in the vignette in danger of default. Identified as a so-called “meme stock,” BBBY was in focus in mid-2022. Extraordinarily, for a firm in danger of default, its stock price rose 350 percent in the first two weeks of August of that year, only to fall over 50 percent in one day. By that time, cash at the heavily indebted retailer had fallen below $100 million compared to $1.1 billion a year earlier. is divided into three sections that explain cash added to (or taken from) the cash account: Cash flow from operations ‒ Cash investment − Cash distributed in financing activities = Increase (decrease) in cash The cash flow in any section can be negative. So, for example, cash received in financing activities from issuing shares or debt adds to cash in financing activities. There are issues with the statement, largely because the statement confuses operating and financing activities. As with the balance sheet and income statement, the separation of the two is essential to get insights. Here are the problems with the GAAP and IFRS cash flow statements: Cash flow from operations includes cash disbursed for interest on debt and cash interest received on debt assets. This is OK if the interest is for a bank or financing operation that generates earnings from the spread between lending and borrowing rates. But, more generally, this is cash flow in financing activities, not from the business. Take it out and put it in cash from financing activities. As interest is taxed, the appropriate number is the after-tax amount: (Interest paid − Interest received) × (1 − tax rate). For BBBY, the tax rate is 23 percent, so number for 2022 is $64.7 million × (1 − 0.23) = $49.8 million, yielding a corrected cash flow from operations of $17.9 + 49.8 = $67.7 million. For 2021, the after-tax net interest is $59.2 million and the corrected cash flow from operations is $327.3 million. The investment section includes net cash invested in debt assets, usually short-term investments. That is cash invested in debt assets minus cash from liquidating debt assets. For BBBY in 2022, there is little difference in the purchases of these investments and their liquidation, but in 2021 there is a sale (liquidation) of $386.5 million. This is not cash generated in the business, so cash flow from investing activities is misstated by this amount, as is free cash flow read directly from the GAAP statement. Remove the net number to the financing section, yielding a corrected cash in investing activities of $737.9 − 386.5 = $351.4 million. The corrections in (1) and (2) yield the correct free cash flow number for 2021, C − I: $327.3 + 351.4 = $678.7 million. (Notice that the investing activities are generating cash rather than using cash because of the sale of assets in the distressed circumstances.) The decrease in cash of $936.3 million for BBBY in 2022 is the remainder in standard cash flow statement but really is cash generated by liquidating debt assets, little different from selling short-term investments that we have included in the cash financing section. So it should be classified as such in the financing section. In 2021 and 2020, cash increases so this is an investment of surplus cash in debt assets, again added to the financing section. The confusion in (2) is particularly pernicious: Liquidation of debt investments reports lower cash expended in investing activities and higher free cash flow, as with BBBY, making the firm look more able to generate cash to meet debt obligations than it is. With these corrections, the format of a reformulated statement follows the cash conservation equation we started with: C − I = d + F F = net interest payments ( i ) + net debt principal payments ( P ), where net interest payments are interest paid on debt obligations net of interest received on debt assets, and principal payments can be payments to buy and sell debt assets or repay and issue debt obligations. Thus, C − I = d + i + P This expression tells us how free cash flow, C − I , is disbursed; or, if free cash flow is negative, how financing flows on the left-hand side make up the shortfall. The latter is the case for BBBY in 2022. The reformulated statements for BBBY in this format are in table 13.2 . You see that the free cash flow equals the flows in financing activities: Cash generated equals cash distributed. In 2020, BBBY generated $630.7 million in the business, distributed some of that in net dividends and interest payments, and invested the rest in cash and other debt assets. In 2022, the firm had a shortfall of $281.5 million in cash from the business so, to satisfy the shortfall and pay dividends and interest plus a small debt repayment, the firm had to pay out cash. Table 13.2 Reformulated cash flow statement for Bed Bath & Beyond (BBBY), 2020–2022 (in $millions) 2022 2021 2020 Cash flow from operations (C) 67.7 327.3 640.8 Cash flow in investing activities (I) ( 349.2) 351.4 ( 10.1) Free cash flow , C − I (281.5) 678.7 630.7 Cash flow in financing activities: Net dividend to shareholders 590.2 403.1 182.8 Net interest payments (after tax) 49.8 59.2 55.8 Repayment of debt 15.8 460.8 0.0 Issue of debt 0.0 (236.4) 0.0 Increase (decrease) of debt assets 0.0 (386.5) (101.5) Increase (decrease) in cash (936.3) 383.6 493.7 Effect of exchange rates on cash (1.0) ( 5.1) ( 0.1) d + i + P (281.5) 678.7 630.7 The Treasurer’s Rule This reformulated statement is one the treasurer can work with. With this format, the treasurer follows a rule: If C − I − i > d , buy debt ( P ) If C − I − i < d , sell debt ( P ) That is, if the free cash flow remaining after paying net interest and dividends is positive, she buys debt, either by buying debt assets to be booked to cash and short-term investments or by buying back the firms debt obligations. If free cash flow after paying net interest and dividends is negative, she sells debt by liquidating debt assets or issuing more debt obligations. The action boxes below demonstrate the application of the Treasurer’s Rule for two firms we have already seen: Delta Air Lines, Inc. in the distress year 2020 ( chapter 9 ); and Microsoft Corporation (a firm not in distress) in the same year ( chapter 5 ). He did so by issuing $28,357 million of new debt, but with $8,559 million of it applied to pay off maturing debt and $12,042 million invested in debt assets: rainy-day funds against possible future free cash flow shortfalls and debt repayments. The Board of Directors authorized dividends of $15,137 million and share repurchases of $22,968 million, and there was $1,343 million in cash received from the issue of shares in employee compensation plans. That left the treasurer with $4,710 million to trade in debt. He did so by paying off debt obligations of $8,935 million, supplemented with $4,225 million from the liquidation of debt assets. The Liquidity Book in Pro Forma Having made financing decision for the current year, the treasurer turns to anticipating financing needs in the future. To do so, she keeps a book: a pro forma liquidity book that follows the Treasurer’s Rule. With a forecast of free cash flow and with the dividend and share repurchase policy set by the board, she anticipates the sale or purchase of debt going forward in preparation for consultations with the firm’s commercial or investment bankers. The action box below lays out such a pro forma for Bed Bath & Beyond Inc. (BBBY), whose ability to satisfy debt obligations came into question in the earlier vignette. It is initialized on the actual 2022 reformulated cash flow statement, with the projected numbers for subsequent years then added. These are hypothetical numbers, in no way coming from diligent forecasting for BBBY. They merely demonstrate how the pro forma is developed. As the treasurer is particularly anxious about default, the bottom lines in the pro forma compare the two measures determining a default point: cash available to service debt and cash required to service the debt. Default is a pressing issue for BBBY. Rules Governing the Liquidity Book The numbers for a given year in the pro forma are governed by the cash conservation equation underlying the reformulated cash flow statement. But there are additional accounting equations that govern the evolution of the numbers over time, and these are applied to prepare the evolving pro forma. As always, keeping a book under accounting rules ensures integrity of the numbers and protects us from mistakes. In the BBBY pro forma, reformulated balance sheets and income statements are first modeled as an input to the pro forma. This responds to the feature that lenders lend against value because value-generating firms are less likely to default, and reformulated balance sheets and income statements convey value as a starting point to the liquidity analysis. However, modeling balance sheets and income statements is also done with another purpose: Free cash flow falls out from these statements following an accounting equation: Free Cash Flow = Operating income − Change in Net Operating Assets FCF = OI − ΔNOA For 2022, free cash flow for BBBY is −$510 − (–$228) = −$282 million, and for 2023, −$943 − ($2,324 − $2,839) = −$428 million. So, one does not have to forecast free cash flow directly! Further, the forecast of free cash flow has integrity. When you think of it, forecasting free cash flow is difficult, and that difficulty has led to use of rough approximations—EDITDA and Cash from operations minus Capex are often applied, for example. These measures leave out some relevant features; the calculation by the book does not. The treasurer’s forecasted amount of net debt to be covered in future is also supplied by an accounting equation: Net Debt t = Net Debt t-1 + Net Interest t − (Free Cash Flow t − Net Dividend t ) Net Debt t = Net Debt t + i t − (FCF t − d t ) This also just drops out under the accounting for the book. It comes from the cash conservation equation we started with: Interest adds to debt obligations but free cash flow after paying net dividends reduces the debt. For BBBY’s forecast net debt at the end of 2023, Net debt = $2,665 + $49 − (−$428 − $0) = $3,142 million, as in the pro forma balance sheet. A final equation, quite familiar to you by now, confirms the integrity of the book: Equity t = Equity t-1 + Earnings t − Net Dividend t For BBBY in 2023, that is $174 + −$992 − 0) = −$818 million. That must satisfy Equity t = NOA t − Net Debt t = $2,324 − $3,142 = −$818. Protection in forecasting, by the book. Forecasting is easier and accurate when done under the discipline of keeping a book. Default Points The BBBY pro forma indicates negative cash available to meet debt payment obligations in both 2022 and 2023 and a deficiency in the $286 million to meet repayments of $346 million in 2024. Thus potential default is indicated. Aside from annual lease payments, the firm’s next repayment requirements (from the debt footnote to financial statements) are $300 million in 2034, and $900 million in 2044. Thus the investor is looking for how the firm will fill the immediate cash flow deficiency. Dividends and share repurchases are set to zero in the pro forma, so reducing these is not an option. A share issue is, but that is problematic, an option that the investor does not like given its likely dilutive effects. Potential sales of assets have been built into the NOA number, but further asset sales might be made, potentially damaging future profits. Further debt might be entertained, but that adds to debt obligations and risk. BBBY has a letter of credit agreement with a bank and can draw on it up to $1,000 million. But that also adds more debt, so the critical question, as always, is whether the firm can generate significant operating income and cash flow in the future. That is solvency rather than temporary liquidity, and solvency is assessed by extending the pro forma into the future. Again, it is value from earnings that covers debt. With continuing losses, the bank, JPMorgan Chase & Co., subsequently sent a default notice to the firm. Default Prediction How likely is a firm to default? We have seen the indicators: Sustained low profits or losses, and declining cash flow from operations and free cash flow. Add to these the leverage measures of chapter 5 : [IMAGE: ../images/ch13eqn11.png] There are refinements on these measures. See the vignette below. As with other financial statement scores like the S-score and C-score introduced earlier, these and other metrics can be combined by weighting them into one number that indicates the probability of default. Common scores are the Altman Z-score and the Ohlson O-score. The calculations are on Wikipedia and Investopedia. As always, an understanding of the business and where it is headed combines with these metrics to predict default. A Vignette Balance sheet and income statement predictors of default for Jack in the Box, Inc. (JACK) Jack in the Box Inc. (JACK), the fast-food, hamburger chain, acquired Del Taco Restaurants, Inc. in March 2022. It then decided to sell most of the Del Taco outlets to franchisees from whom it earns franchise revenues. Why? The company said the aim was to get assets off the balance sheet, giving it access to lower-cost secured debt. But aren’t balance sheet assets security for debt? Well, debt secured against revenue streams is deemed more secure than debt secured against assets. That tells us that, in evaluating debt, decide whether the balance sheet measure, [IMAGE: ../images/ch13eqn12.png] , or the income statement measure, [IMAGE: ../images/ch13eqn13.png] , is the appropriate measure. This is particularly important when firms turn themselves into “asset-lite” corporations where the value securing the debt is in the income statement flows rather than the balance sheet. Income statement default measures can be refined. For example, if the debt is secured on franchise royalties, as in the JACK strategy, then the ratio of those royalties to the secured debt is informative. Better yet, consider the ratio of expected future royalties to debt. Value Investing Principles Free cash flow is irrelevant to going-concern valuation but important for evaluating liquidity and default probability. The accounting book monitors liquidity. The accounting book in pro forma predicts default. The Treasurer’s Rule governs debt financing. Building a Value Investing Engine Your engine can be supplemented with a reformulated cash flow statement and a template for a liquidity pro forma that applies the Treasurer’s Rule to plan for future financing needs and identify potential default points. Build in the accounting equations that govern this pro forma so that components enter automatically from your reformulated balance sheets and income statements. The Exercise Book There are exercises in the Exercise Book that deal with liquidity and default. The Case Book Case 21: Delta Air Lines Locked Down Will Delta return to profitability or default? Because of their operational structure, airlines are at risk in market downturns: They have fixed costs with fixed fleets and landing slots, but empty seats bring losses and negative cash flows. As this chapter shows, that raises the probability of default. Indeed, most U.S. airlines have gone through bankruptcy at some time in their history. In several chapters in the book, we have already looked at Delta Air Lines, Inc. (DAL) both before and during the pandemic. We observed the airline was in grave danger during the pandemic. We know that the firm got out of it, though “with a little help from your friends,” the U.S. government. This case takes you back to that period and asks you to make an evaluation of the likelihood of default. Carry out some forecasting scenarios and look at the default points under those scenarios after considering the alternatives to avoid default. The action box applying the Treasurer’s Rule to DAL in this chapter cues you. Your Library For each chapter, the library gives you a list of readings that add to the material in the chapter and presents some of the research that underlies the analysis in the chapter. These readings will fortify the good thinking that gives you the confidence for investing securely and will increase your appetite to become an intelligent investor.

Chapter 14 Closing the Book Chapter 14 Closing the Book I t’s time to close this book, although we trust that you will keep the accounting book very much open. The book has prepared you to become Benjamin Graham’s intelligent investor . Value investing by the book has made you feel more secure in investing, but you may be still apprehensive. And well you might. You have protected yourself from the risk of paying too much, but risk cannot be eliminated. This chapter adds some further thinking to boost your confidence as you go into action. But first, let’s summarize what we have done and review the main themes of the book. Negotiation with Mr. Market We have taken the position that the main risk in equity investing is the risk of paying too much, and that the risk is in paying too much for growth. So we have characterized investing as a matter of understanding the market’s speculation about growth and challenging the price that the market is putting on that growth. Chapter 4 saw this as the start of a negotiation with Mr. Market (to use Benjamin Graham’s characterization). What do you think about growth, Mr. Market? Does that agree with my thinking? To do this, we must understand the business and how it generates value. But we also keep an accounting book that tracks the generation of value, so the business is represented in the same dollar numbers (or other currency) as the market price; the book gets us from the business to value. That book is disciplined by accounting principles honoring the value investing principles of chapter 1 and the principles of modern finance presented in chapters 2 and 3 . The book thus embeds thinking about value, and so guides you in your thinking about value generation and how much to pay for it. After understanding the market’s pricing of growth, we then conduct a fundamental analysis with the book that uncovers the (dollar) value generation. It is this fundamental analysis that is brought to the task of challenging Mr. Market’s pricing of growth. Does this analysis indicate his thinking is correct? Does it lead to a thinking that differs? This inquiry follows the chapter headings in the book. First, financing leverage is dangerous, so its effects need to be understood. Indeed, it increases earnings growth but, except in special circumstances, that growth is risky. So don’t pay for it. Always strip out financing leverage and focus on value added in the business operations. And beware of the danger in levered accounting numbers such as ROE, levered P/E, and levered P/B, as well as (levered) earnings growth. If you keep the accounting book appropriately, you have a set of leveraging equations that show you the effects of leverage on these numbers as well as on value. They also identify the appropriate leverage measure, net financing debt-to-common equity, not the ubiquitous debt-to-equity ratio. See chapter 5 . Second, profitability adds value in operations, measured by return on net operating assets, RNOA. Focus on this measure, not the many other profitability measures offered in textbooks and by some analysts, like return on assets or return on tangible assets. RNOA leads you to an understanding of how after-tax operating income adds value and exposes the false trail of measures like EBIT and EBITDA. It also leads to an understanding of net operating assets in value creation and, unlike financing leverage, operating liability leverage potentially adds value. You understand that assets can be missing from the balance sheet and thus from the RNOA measure, but this does not affect the value implied by the RNOA: The missing assets don’t matter if earnings from the missing assets are coming through the income statement. This is the first encounter with a theme throughout the book: Income statements and balance sheets work together to convey value. See chapter 6 . Third, it is not just RNOA that adds value but also the amount of net operating assets that earn at the RNOA. So a complete analysis of value-added and its growth involves an analysis of the drivers of both RNOA and net operating assets. These drivers are analyzed in a nested hierarchy so that the effect of lower-level drivers on those higher in the hierarchy, and ultimately on value generation, can be ascertained. That supplies the full analysis to challenge the market’s pricing of growth. Can scenarios for these drivers be constructed that justify that price? Are those scenarios feasible? Or do other feasible scenarios lead to contrarian thinking? See chapter 7 . Fourth, buying growth is risky, so understand this risk. And understand what works to convey risk and what does not. Standard factor models like the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) don’t work in practice. Indeed, though considerable research effort has been given to the task, we must be honest and recognize that we don’t know how to calculate the “cost of capital.” A risk model cannot bail us out, so it is up to the investor to understand the risk. That risk is in the business but is captured by the accounting value drivers. So the value investor can understand fundamental risk by modeling upside and downside scenarios with outcomes that differ with variation of the drivers in the hierarchical driver analysis. See chapter 8 . Fifth, to challenge the market price with this accounting analysis, the integrity of the accounting must also be challenged. The balance sheet must be a safe anchor, with nothing that can come back and hit the investor later. The income statement must be a predictive income statement with no reversals in the future because of the current accounting. See chapters 9 and 10 . With this accounting for value, the investor ventures into active investing, including the special situations of chapter 11 . She brings a critique to shallow investing styles such as “value versus growth investing” and “size investing” and understands the traps in those styles, as in chapter 12 . The investor also understands the traps with investing on the basis of cash flows, but keeps the book that accounts for cash flow if liquidity is a concern. See chapter 13 . Principles In addition to the value investing principles of chapter 1 and the finance principles of chapters 2 and 3 , further principles emerge as the investor develops the accounting book in successive chapters. It is well to hold to them tenaciously. They are summarized at the end of each chapter, but here is a restatement that brings the most important ones together. There are investment traps if these principles are not adhered to. Earnings add value only if they cover the required return for risk. Thus, value-added earnings are those in excess of book value (net assets) charged at the required return, also known as residual earnings. Applying this measure protects the investor from the trap of buying earnings and earnings growth that do not add value. It protects from buying earnings growth from investments that do not add value. Dividend payout is irrelevant to value. Dividends are the distribution of value, not the generation of value. Buying dividend yield is a trap: A dividend reduces price, so the investor is no better off cum dividend. Free cash flow is not a value-added measure. It treats investment as a loss of value, a trap: Investment typically adds value. Anchor on the balance sheet, then add value to the balance sheet from expected earnings after covering a required return for risk. That is at the heart of accounting for value. Income statements and balance sheets work together to convey value. RNOA does not convey value without the balance sheet. Buying profitable firms or eschewing low RNOA firms falls into a trap. Using balance sheets and income statements together also protects from the trap of paying for earnings generated by accounting manipulations. It is best to think there is no such thing as intrinsic value. It is too elusive to estimate with any precision. Thinking that value can be calculated with a valuation formula leads to the trap of building speculation into a valuation. But the investor does not have to know the intrinsic value, for investing is a game against other investors, not a game against nature. Thus, investing starts with understanding the market price that other investors set, and specifically the price that the market puts on growth. Financing leverage typically does not add value, but leverage from operating liabilities can. Beware of buying levered accounting numbers, like ROE, P/E, and P/B. They might look good, but that could be a trap: You are buying leverage risk but might not be buying value. Financing leverage increases expected earnings growth, but that is not growth to be paid for, a trap. Do not buying earnings growth generated by financing leverage. Separate operating activities from financing activities in financial statements and proceed to value the operating activities. That will keep you from falling into the leverage traps. Beware of paying too much for profitability. It’s an accounting measure, not a measure of real return on investment. Anchoring on the balance sheet protects from falling into the trap of paying too much for profitability. A growth company is one that can grow value-added (residual) earnings from operations. Sustainable earnings are the base for growth. Value-added (residual) operating income is driven by value drivers identified in a decomposition analysis. These drivers aggregate to residual operating income so that the effect on value when a driver changes can be ascertained. Abandon the question: What is the cost of capital? The required return is a personal matter. Diversification protects from risk but embeds a trap. In down markets, stock returns tend to move together more than usual, taking away the benefits of diversification. To be an anchor, a balance sheet must be a safe balance sheet with no feature that can come back and hit the investor later. Beware of fair values in balance sheet. Beware of off-balance liabilities. And more. Beware of an income statement manipulated by accounting such that the manipulation reverses in the future. Knowing Mr. Market In any negotiation, it is good to understand your opposite, how he or she is thinking about the issue at hand. With the approach in this book, we identify Mr. Market’s pricing of growth and challenge that growth pricing with fundamental analysis. But we might get further traction by understanding where his price came from. Fundamental analysis drives the disciplined investor’s thinking about growth, but what drives the market’s thinking? If we have the answer to that question, we might have a better answer to the question of whether the price in misguided or not. Behavioral Finance Traditional value investors characterized Mr. Market as a “moody fellow” subject to fear or overexcitement. Detecting those moods, the investor takes advantage. In what has been called behavioral finance or behavioral economics, research has brought more of a scientific approach to the behavior of investors and how that affects stock prices. After all, it is humans who trade, and humans can be driven by factors other than an understanding of value. The underlying disciplines are psychology and sociology. Psychology attempts to explain why individuals might not be entirely “rational” in pricing or, to be more precise, why they might bring a different rationality to the matter than maximizing returns for the risk taken. Sociology deals with collective behavior that potentially explains group thinking and the herd mentality that moves prices. You may have run into some of these ideas in books that popularize behavioral thinking: Thaler’s “nudge,” Kahneman’s “thinking, fast and slow,” and Gladwell’s “blink.” More widely, investor behavior has been attributed to traits such as overconfidence, overreaction to news, fixation on bottom-line earnings, limited attention, a reluctance to sell after losses, and investor sentiment. Some research even suggests that these traits differ between males and females. Herding instincts induce individuals with similar traits to move together. Your authors confess to not being experts in these matters. The science of behavioral investing is young with some hypotheses still conjectural. But it is important to understand that there are two legs to stand on when investing. One is fundamental analysis that understands rational value that might differ from the market price, and the other is an understanding of why the market price might differ from rational price implied by fundamental information. Fundamental investors adhere to the idea that prices ultimately gravitate to fundamentals as mispricing corrects itself. However, the behavior that causes price to deviate from fundamentals can perpetuate itself, with prices deviating even more from fundamentals, at least for some time. That is referred to as price momentum: Prices that have gone up go up more, and prices that have dropped drop further. That goes against the value investor trading against prices. Upward momentum adds risk to the short seller when he sees stocks as overpriced: The resulting margin calls as the price increases are very uncomfortable. Downward momentum is a frustration to a fund manager who must report continuing losses while holding the long position. The appropriate reaction may be to double down on the investment, but if investors leave the fund because of losses, the fund manager must sell stock she deems underpriced to satisfy the redemptions. That is a shame; it takes away the potential returns to value investing. The quote “the market can remain irrational longer than you can remain solvent” is credited to John Maynard Keynes, though some say it was coined in 1986 by Gary Shilling, a Wall Street investor skeptical of the market pricing. Whatever, it is wise. Keynes characterized investment as “an activity of forecasting the yield over the life of the asset” and speculation as “the activity of forecasting the psychology of the market.” The value investor does not speculate, but can benefit by understanding how others do. But also from Keynes: “A conventional valuation which is established as the outcome of the mass psychology of a large number of ignorant individuals is liable to change violently as the result of a sudden fluctuation of opinion due to factors which do not really make much difference to the prospective yield since there will be no strong roots of conviction to hold it steady” ( chapter 12 of The General Theory of Employment, Interest and Money , Palgrave Macmillan 1936). The American Association of Individual Investors publishes a sentiment index based on a survey of their members. A Google search reports other sentiment indexes. There is a CNN Business Fear and Greed Index based on seven features of stock market behavior and a State Street Investor Confidence Index®. Put/call ratios from the stock option market and short interest are also said to indicate investment sentiment. We have little experience with these metrics and cannot vouch for their effectiveness. Mr. Market as a Sustainable Investor In recent decades, the environmental, social and governance (ESG) performance of companies have grown in importance for investment managers. ESG is now often labelled sustainability by regulators, consultants and investors. Companies are unsustainable if their policies and business activities fail to meet minimum performance standards in each of the three ESG areas. And institutional investors are being required to demonstrate that their investment decisions and engagement with company management encourage sustainable corporate behavior. The ESG performance of companies can have a major impact on investor demand, and hence stock prices. What does this mean for the fundamental investor whose decisions are driven by whether prices reflect value? Are socially responsible investors a distinct clientele of investors who ignore the fundamentals, one that value investors can arbitrage against? We do not see things quite like that. Here is a way of viewing it. You are an investor who invests to generate wealth for your retirement, but you are also concerned with the quality of life during your retirement and for your children and grandchildren. So, you look for investments that will create that wealth but also not damage the planet or investments that promote a better society for yourself or your children to whom you might leave your wealth. Some investors might be at one extreme of protecting the planet whatever the cost to wealth creation, others at the extreme of wealth creation without consideration of ESG consequences. However, the choice between wealth creation and ESG benefits is not as stark as one might believe. ESG factors are also fundamentals, and any group of investors will not survive for long if they ignore the fundamentals. Investing in a “polluter” potentially brings regulatory penalties that affect value and loss of profits from customers who object to such behavior. Investing purely on the basis of ESG outcomes erodes capital. Socially responsible investors will survive and succeed if they think about value and how their ESG concerns can affect value. Investors in wealth creation will achieve their goal only if they think how ESG factors contribute to that wealth creation. Our framework deals comprehensively with how all factors, including ESG factors, impinge on value creation. Socially responsible investors might ignore the interplay of ESG factors on fundamentals, trading “green” only. It they are a big enough group, they might depress “dirty” (or “brown”) stock prices, providing a trading opportunity for other investors. But the latter must have a good grasp of the fundamentals to which ESG factors contribute. If a firm’s management adopts a green agenda without regard to profits, the investor must be aware of the effect on fundamentals. He or she also understands how to trade against those who price on potential profits without the consideration of ESG factors that affect those profits. The “E” in ESG refers to environmental performance: What is the impact of the firm’s business on the natural environment? How does a firm contribute towards climate risk, and what are the risks related to climate change that challenge the firm? Do a company’s products, production process, production inputs, or supply chain cause environmental damage? Damage might be in the form of carbon or other harmful emissions, natural resource depletion, or destruction of the natural habitat and damage to biodiversity. Is the firm taking steps to reduce negative environmental impact by investing in cleaner production technology or developing cleaner new products or products that mitigate damage elsewhere in the supply chain? Firms with weak environmental performance face the risk of direct regulation to prevent harmful production processes, loss of environmentally conscious customers, taxes and tariffs, and divestment by socially responsible investors. The “S” in ESG refers to social performance—this captures the company’s relationships with, and treatment of, a broad set of stakeholders, including employees, customers, supply chain partners, and the broader communities within which it operates. Weak social performance is often associated with management of the workforce and investment in the firm’s human capital, but issues such as workforce diversity and equality of treatment, inclusion, and modern slavery are increasingly prominent. Firms with weak social performance again risk suffering regulatory penalties, loss of customer goodwill and product demand, a discontented workforce, and divestment by socially responsible investors. The “G” in ESG refers to the quality of a company’s governance structure—the policies it has in place to ensure that corporate decision-making is in shareholders’ interests, but at the same time complies with legal obligations and ethical constraints. Firms with high-quality governance have effective mechanisms in place to ensure that corporate insiders cannot misappropriate shareholder value through excessive cash or stock-based compensation, insider trading, related party transactions, fraudulent activities, or other forms of corrupt behavior. Good governance is also important for the quality of financial reporting; failures in financial reporting are often linked to ineffective audit committees and dysfunctional remuneration policies that incentivize misreporting. If investors perceive weak governance that will increase the likelihood of managers expropriating shareholder wealth, they will expect lower future profitability and hence shareholder value will be discounted. Historically, many investors paid attention to G much earlier than S and E, partly because corporate scandals and collapses have been linked to weak governance. But now, high-quality governance is considered by many to be a prerequisite for firms to rank high on S and E. Taken together, investors now think of sustainable firms as those with high performance on all dimensions of ESG. Note that here the word “sustainable” is not necessarily being used by investors in the narrow sense of sustainable operating income encountered in chapter 7 , although we expect that sustainable firms will usually produce more sustainable operating performance. You can now start to work through how ESG performance can be accommodated in our framework by thinking about some of the potential consequences of strong (or weak) ESG performance just outlined. You can think about ESG performance in one or more areas (E, S, or G)—and keep in mind that they do not necessarily move together. Demand from large investment funds classified as socially responsible investors can increase stock prices of green, socially responsible firms. The opposite will happen with dirty, socially irresponsible firms. In terms of the discussion in chapter 8 , higher prices for green, socially responsible firms suggests that investors have a lower implied required return. And the expected return for a given growth rate will be lower. You might not think of yourself as a socially responsible investor who focuses on green, socially responsible stocks, and based on the fundamentals you might think that the firm should have a higher required return (a lower price). You might decide to sell the stock or sell it short. But important socially responsible investors create a new risk that can threaten your strategy of trading against them. If future events such as new regulations and taxes benefit high-performing ESG firms and penalize the low performers, you are taking on an additional risk in shorting ESG. Do you then require additional compensation for that risk (higher r )? If so, perhaps you are starting to agree with Mr. Market. There is another way of thinking about this ESG risk. You can try to anticipate the likely regulatory and tax costs that could affect the dirty, socially irresponsible firms. You can also try to estimate how much self-interested managers will extract from the firm at your, the shareholder’s, expense. Then build these expectations into your predictions of future profitability and profitability growth. Either way, the model can accommodate your thinking. Similarly, if you see a firm with weak governance, you might think that the financial statement accruals are being manipulated by managers for their own short-term gain, perhaps to ensure they meet a short-term bonus performance target. You can then try to build this expected excess compensation into your future operating income and profitability estimates ( chapter 7 ), you can adjust the numbers to get a harder balance sheet ( chapter 9 ), or you can increase your required return to compensate for the additional weak governance risk ( chapter 8 ). Or you can just run away from that firm. All of this takes us back to the principle of chapter 1 : When buying a business, know the business . Knowing the business requires you to know the firm’s current and prospective performance with respect to E, S, and G. It also requires you to understand how ESG performance shows up in the fundamentals that determine value. Knowing Yourself In any negotiation, it is good to know yourself. Self-deception is a problem in many aspects of life. If you tend toward overconfidence or pessimism, are overly influenced by others’ opinions, or have any other of the foibles the behavioralists talk about, you might check yourself. Don’t feel bad; we all are moved by the enthusiasms of the moment (your authors certainly confess to “irrational” behavior at times). Without any pretense of being behavior counselors, here are some attributes that are said to characterize value investors. Momentum investors have a different disposition. Discipline. Value investors are disciplined in how they approach the task. That checks emotions of the moment. The discipline of value investing in this book functions as a discipline against our suspect instincts, a way of checking our self-deception. Starting presumptions. The presumptions by which we enter the game can be important to success. If (with overconfidence) you see yourself as having a natural advantage, easily making money, you will be disappointed. It is best to be humble in the endeavor. As in chapter 1 , it is best to start with the presumption that the market is efficient; you can’t beat it. That puts the onus on to you to demonstrate otherwise. That is different from assuming other investors are wrong and you are right. Reserve. Be a person apart, but not too far apart. Keep a scrutinizing distance from current trends and popular opinion. Curiosity. Being curious spurs you along and leads to new insights. Skepticism. Oscar Wilde said that the skeptic knows the cost of everything but the value of nothing. But, for an investor discerning value different from price, the opposite is true. An investor is skeptical of ideas until they are tested with the discipline of value investing. Honesty. Value investors are honest people, requiring integrity in the conduct of business. Just as importantly, they are also honest with themselves, recognizing what they know and don’t know. Self-deception is our weak point, so challenging our own thinking is as important as challenging the market price. Humanity. That involves understanding humans and their behavior, but it also means empathy for your fellow humans: being outgoing, understanding, and generous; being critical yet also sympathetic. A reader. The value investor is a scholar who reads widely on investing, sifting our insights from noise. But reading even yet more widely rounds out the whole person, adding wisdom and perspective. Read the writings of the Enlightenment with its perspective on society, liberty, and our coexistence as humans. Indeed, read widely in the humanities: the great novels, the great poems, Shakespeare, Goethe, and more. You’ll find much of life’s experience there. A love of the game. Investing is stimulating, and involves always looking for opportunities, always testing ideas, always thinking constructively. It can be fun. In the end, it’s a matter of having an inner peace in investing, and these attributes get you there. Think of yourself as being in a pool of investors. There is the average investor, but some must be above the average, some below. These traits, along with the investing discipline in this book, can put you above the average. If so, someone must be below it. Who are you trading against? That brings an understanding of yourself and an understanding of other investors in the market. Know Mr. Market and know yourself. As a value investor, you can feel you are not just engaged in a selfish, self-rewarding strategy. You are part of a competitive enterprise that, as Adam Smith instructs, brings benefits to society, not only by adding to the wealth of nations but also by developing the “moral sentiments” by which we relate to each other. Your research and trading make the market more efficient, benefiting others that can trade with some assurance even though they don’t do the in-depth work. By bringing information to price formation, you are enabling an efficient allocation of capital to its best productive use in the economy. You are also acting as a monitor of the stewardship of management, watching when they might attempt to take some of the value for themselves, with inefficient prices the consequence. The true meaning of life is to plant trees under whose shade you do not expect to sit. Nelson Henderson Your Library Readings in the library expand on points in this chapter.
